See Law 10 (Covering the pitch). \\n2.3.16 any special conditions of play affecting the conduct of the match. \\n2.3.2  inform the scorers of agreements in 2.3.1.2, 2.3.1.3, 2.3.1.4,  and 2.3.1.6. \\n"'b'b"2.4 The wickets, creases and boundaries \\nBefore the toss and during the match the umpire shall satisfy themselves that \\n2.4.1 the wickets are properly pitched See Law 8 (The wickets) . \\n2.4.2 the creases are correctly marked See Law 7 The creases) . \\n2.4.3 the boundary of the field of play complies with the requirements of Laws    \\n19.1 (Determining the boundary of the field of play), 19.2 (Identifying and \\nmarking the boundary) and 193 (Restoring the boundary). \\n \\n2.5 Conduct of the match, implements and equipment  \\nBefore the toss and during the match the umpires shall satisfy themselves that \\n \\n2.5.1 \\nthe conduct of the match is strictly in accordance with the Laws. \\n2.5.2 \\nthe implements used in the match conform to the following  \\n  \\n2.521 Law 4 (The ball). \\n  \\n25.22 externally visible requirements of Law 5 (The bat) and Appendix B  \\n  \\n2.5.23 either Laws 8.2 (Size of stumps) and 8.3 (The bails) or if applicable. Law 8.4 \\n(Junior cricket). \\n2.5.3 \\n no player uses equipment other than that permitted See Appendix A2 Note \\nparticularly therein the interpretation of protective helmet. \\n2.5.4 \\nthe wicket-keeper\'s gloves comply with the requirements of Law 27.2 \\n(Gloves). \\n \\n2.6 Fair and unfair play \\nThe umpires shall be the sole judges of fair and unfair play. \\n \\n2.7 Fitness for play \\n2.7.1 \\n It is solely for the umpires together to decide whether weather conditions of \\nground weather or light or exceptional circumstances mean that it would be \\ndangerous or unreasonable for play to take place Conditions shall not be regarded as \\nthe dangerous or unreasonable merely because they are not ideal. The fact that the \\ngrass and the ball are wet does not warrant the ground conditions being regarded as \\nunreasonable or dangerous \\n \\n"'b'b"2.7.2 \\nConditions shall be regarded as dangerous if there is actual and foreseeable \\nrisk to the safety of any player or umpire \\n2.7.3 \\nConditions shall be regarded as unreasonable if although posing no risk to \\nsafety, it would not be sensible for play to proceed. \\n2.7.4 \\nIf the umpires consider the ground is so wet or slippery as to deprive the \\nbowler of a reasonable foothold the fielders of the power of free movement, or the \\nbatsmen of the ability to play their strokes or to run between the wickets, then these \\nconditions shall be regarded as so bad that it would be dangerous and unreasonable \\nfor play to take place \\n \\n2.8 Suspension of play in dangerous or unreasonable circumstances \\n2.8.1 \\nAll references to ground include the pitch See Law 6.1 (Area of pitch) \\n2.8.2 \\nThe Umpires shall immediately suspend play, or not allow play to start or to \\nrecommence, if either umpire considers that the conditions of ground, weather or \\nlight, or any other circumstances are either dangerous or unreasonable \\n2.8.3 \\nWhen there is a suspension of play it is the responsibility of the umpires to \\nmonitor conditions. They shall make inspections as often as appropriate. \\nunaccompanied by any players or officials. Immediately the umpires together agree \\nthat the conditions are no longer dangerous or unreasonable they shall call upon the \\nplayers to resume play. \\n \\n2.9 Position of umpires \\nThe umpires shall stand where they can best see any act upon which their decision may be \\nrequired. Subject to this over-riding consideration, the bowler\'s end umpire shall stand in a \\nposition so as not to interfere with either the bowler\'s run-up or the striker\'s view. The \\nstriker\'s end umpire may elect to stand on the off side instead of the on side of the pitch, \\nprovided he/she informs the captain of the fielding side, the striker and the other umpire \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"2.10 Umpires changing ends \\nThe umpires shall change ends after each side has had one completed innings. See Law 13.3 \\n(Completed innings) \\n2.11 Disagreement and dispute \\nWhere there is disagreement or dispute about any matter, the umpires together shall make \\nthe final decision. See also Law 31.6 (Consultation by umpires). \\n \\n2.12 Umpire\'s decision \\nAn umpire may alter any decision provided that such alteration is made promptly. This \\napart, an umpire\'s decision, once made, is final. \\n \\n2.13 Signals \\n2.13.1 The following code of signals shall be used by umpires: \\n2.13.1.1 Signals made while the ball is in play. \\n  \\nDead ball-by crossing and re-crossing the wrists below the waist. \\n  \\nNo ball - by extending one arm horizontally.  \\n  \\nOut - by raising an index finger above the head. (If not out, the umpire shall call Not \\nout.)  \\n  \\nWide - by extending both arms horizontally. \\n2.13.1.2 When the ball is dead, the bowler\'s end umpire shall repeat the signals in 2.13.1.1,       \\nwith the exception of the signal for Out, to the scorers.  \\n2.13.1.3 The signals listed below shall be made to the Scorers only when the ball is dead. \\n  \\nBoundary 4 - by waving an arm from side to side finishing with the arm across the \\nchest \\n  \\n Boundary 6 - by raising both arms above the head \\n  \\nBye - by raising an open hand above the head. Commencement of last hour - by \\npointing to a raised wrist with the other hand. \\n  \\nFive Penalty runs awarded to the batting side - by repeated tapping of one shoulder \\nwith the opposite hand. \\n  \\nFive Penalty runs awarded to the fielding side - by placing one hand on the opposite \\nshoulder. \\n  \\n \\n"'b'b"  \\nLeg bye - by touching a raised knee with the hand. \\n  \\nNew ball - by holding the ball above the head. \\n  \\nRevoke last signal - by touching both shoulders, each with the opposite hand  \\n  \\nShort run - by bending one arm upwards and touching the nearer shoulder with the \\ntips of the fingers. \\n  \\nThe following signals are for Levels 3 and 4 of the player conduct offences. Each \\nsignal has two parts, both of which should be acknowledged separately by the \\nscorers. \\n  \\nLevel 3 conduct \\n  \\nPart 1-by putting one arm out to the side of the body and repeatedly raising it and \\nlowering it. \\n  \\nPart 2 - by raising both hands, all fingers spread, to shoulder height, palms facing \\ntowards the scorers.  \\n  \\nLevel 4 conduct \\n  \\nPart 1 - by putting one arm out to the side of the body and repeatedly raising it and \\nlowering it. \\n  \\nPart 2 - by raising an index finger, held at shoulder height, to the side of the body. \\n  \\n \\n2.13.1.4 All the signals in 2.13.13 are to be made by the bowler\'s end umpire except that  \\nfor Short run, which is to be signalled by the umpire at the end where short \\nrunning occurs. However, the bowler\'s end umpire shall be responsible both for \\nthe final signal of Short run to the scorers and, if more than one run is short, for \\ninforming them as to the number of runs to be recorded. \\n \\n2.13.2 The umpire shall wait until each signal to the scorers has been separately  \\nacknowledged by a scorer before allowing play to proceed.  \\n  \\nIf several signals are to be used, they should be given in the order that the events \\noccurred. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"2.14 Informing the umpires \\n \\nThroughout the Laws, wherever the umpires are to receive information from captains or \\nother players, it will be sufficient for one umpire to be so informed and for him/her to \\ninform the other umpire. \\n \\n2.15 Correctness of scores \\n \\nConsultation between umpires and scorers on doubtful points is essential. The umpires \\nshall, throughout the match, satisfy themselves as to the correctness of the number of runs \\nscored, the wickets that have fallen and, where appropriate, the number of overs bowled \\nThey shall agree these with the scorers at least at every interval, other than a drinks \\ninterval, and at the conclusion of the match See Laws 3.2 (Correctness of scores), 16.8 \\n(Correctness of result) and 16.10 (Result not to be changed). \\n \\nAppointment and attendance \\n \\nAn umpire may have little or no involvement with either of the teams, or may have been \\nappointed by the captain of one of them. In some recreational cricket, they may even be a \\nplayer as well as an umpire. Whatever the situation, this Law makes it clear that both \\numpires must act completely fairly, treating both teams equally. \\nUmpires have many duties - all vital for the match to run smoothly - which they are required \\nto carry out before play begins. The Law insists that umpires arrive at least 45 minutes \\nbefore the scheduled start, although it must be noted that this is a minimum - many \\numpires will want to arrive long before that. \\nChange of umpire \\n \\nIt should be rare for an umpire to be changed. If, however, he/she has become ill or injured, \\nor there is another reason for a change, it is permitted. The convention has always been for \\nthe other umpire to decide on a replacement - although this is not necessary under the \\nLaws. The replacement will normally be limited to standing at the striker\'s end every over. \\nHe/she can, however, take a full part as an umpire if both captains agree to his/her doing \\nso. The remaining, originally appointed, umpire may need to explain this to the captains. \\n"'b'b"Duties before the toss \\n \\nBefore the toss, the umpires must meet with the captains and determine a number of \\nthings. Special Regulations and Playing Conditions affecting the match will include \\ncompetition rules, such as a limit on the number of overs any one bowler can deliver, \\nlimitations of time or number of overs for each innings, and similar regulations. There may \\nbe special conditions specific to that particular ground. Whether standard or unusual, all \\nmust know and understand them - particularly the umpires and scorers! \\nMatters that have to be determined on the day should, where possible, be settled in \\nagreement with both captains and the umpires. If that is not possible, then it is for the \\numpires to have the final say. \\nAs some of the items listed in Law 2.3 will have been agreed before the day of the match, \\n\'determining\' them will mostly be checking between umpires and captains that they all have \\nthe same understanding as to the details involved. Some may seem obvious, but it is still \\nimportant to go through the process. It is essential that everyone knows what is expected, \\nso that embarrassing misunderstandings do not happen later, during the game. \\nThe hours of play will cover the number of days for the match, as well as the times for start \\nof play on each day and for close of play on each day. They are not to be confused with \\ntimes and durations of intervals. Those are temporary breaks in play during the day. The \\nduration of intervals are to be determined at this point, though there are instances (to be \\naddressed in Law 11) when the timings of those intervals may be changed. Clearly all these \\ntimes are essential information for the scorers. \\nThe actual balls to be used should be available for captains to inspect, but it is for the \\numpires to determine which should be used. Law 4.2 specifies that, having been \\ndetermined, the balls are to be given to the umpires and are to remain under their control \\nfrom that point onwards. In most one-day recreational cricket there will be only one ball for \\neach innings. The scorers need to know if more than one is to be available, and if so, when a \\nnew one can be taken. They do not need to know which actual balls are to be used.  \\nThe determination of boundaries, and allowances for them, must not be forgotten, and care \\nmust be taken to \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"ensure they are agreed. Often, it will be assumed that these will be the standard 4 and 6. \\nOften this will be true, but everyone needs to know that this is the case, and, if not, if there \\nare any local customs that will change them - this is covered in more detail in Law 19, but it \\nis worth including here because an umpire must remember to make sure everyone - players, \\ncaptains and scorers - know all the relevant details before play begins. \\nAn \'obstacle within the field of play\' is a vague description this is not accidental. Examples \\ninclude a tree, an \'immovable\' roller, a concrete strip for the run up to a practice net etc. It \\nis for the umpires to decide whether such an object is to count as a boundary or not. Many \\ngrounds will have local customs, and, where possible, these should be observed. The \\nplayers, through their captains, need to know, as do the scorers. Moveable \'obstacles\' - such \\nas supporters or animals running onto the field, are dealt with by the umpires as and when \\nthey enter the field and/or make contact with the ball. That is covered in more detail under \\nLaw 19. \\nAccurate time-keeping plays an important role at several stages in the match. A visible, \\nworking, pavilion clock is the most suitable time piece. The provision of a back-up time piece \\nis a necessary precaution against the failure of the one agreed as the master. It is usual for \\none of the umpires watches to be the back-up - although this may need to be the primary \\ntime piece if there is not a public, easily readable one. In that case the other umpire\'s watch \\nwould serve as a back-up. It is good practice for the umpires to make sure the times on their \\nwatches are exactly the same before the start of play. \\n \\nCrease markings, wickets, implements \\n \\nBefore the pre-match meeting with the captains, umpires will need to make a complete \\ninspection of the field of play. This must include the boundaries and how they are marked, \\nand what obstacles there are which may have to be designated as boundaries. \\nIt is also essential that, before the game, umpires check that the creases are correctly \\nmarked and the wickets correctly pitched. This will include seeing that stumps and bails are \\nof the correct size. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Left: Check that your \\ncolleague at the far end has \\nthe stumps upright and \\nequally spaced. He/she can \\ndo the same for you. \\n \\nRight: Check from the side as \\nwell as from in front to make \\nsure the sumps are properly \\naligned  \\n \\nWhile play is in progress, the umpires are responsible for seeing that the players use only \\nthe equipment that is allowed and that balls, bats, and wicketkeepers were all meet the \\nstipulations outlined in the Laws. These are all covered elsewhere in the Laws, and in the \\nAppendices, and umpires need to be aware of all of them-items such as a bat gauge and a \\nball gauge can be carried onto the field, but without a detailed knowledge of what \\nequipment should look like, no umpire would know when to use thee implements. \\n \\nFair and unfair play \\n \\nFair and unfair play are discussed in much greater details the Preamble, Laws 1, 41, and 42. \\nHowever, Law 2.6 is not to be overlooked, as it emphasises that the umpires have the \\nresponsibility of judging whether any action is fair or not. It is stated clearly that they are \\nthe sole judges of fair and unfair play. Specific acts of unfair play are mentioned in a number \\nof Laws, and actions are laid out for the umpires to follow in those cases. There may, \\nhowever, be occasions when one umpire sees something which he/she considers unfair, \\nwhich is not specifically covered in the Laws. In that situation, the umpire must intervene to \\nstop play by calling and signalling Dead ball and then immediately inform his \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"her colleague of the reason for this action. The umpire who has seen it may also want \\nclarification from his/her colleague if they too have seen the incident. The bowler\'s end \\numpire will then carry out the procedure of Law 41.2, and together the umpires will report \\nthe incident after the match. \\n \\nFitness of ground, weather and light \\nIt used to be the case that, if conditions were not very good, but not dangerous, the batting \\nside could be offered the light that is the batsmen at the wicket would decide whether or \\nnot play should continue. That was removed from the Laws in 2010, and it is crucial to \\nremember that decisions on whether conditions are fit for play are solely down to the \\numpires - whatever certain players may think. If either umpire considers that conditions are \\ndangerous or unreasonable then play will not start will be suspended or will not restart. \\nThe single most important consideration is the safety of those on the field of play, players \\nand umpires alike. The umpires are the only judges of whether the conditions of the ground \\nunderfoot of the weather overhead or of the surrounding light is safe for play to take place. \\nIt is not to be deemed dangerous merely because the weather is dull and overcast. If, \\nhowever, the light is so poor that players or umpires are unable to pick up soon enough the \\nflight of a ball coming towards them then that is dangerous. This can be made worse by a \\ndark background of, for example, trees - that must be taken into account. Equally, if it is a \\nbit wet and it is necessary to dry the ball fairly often, it is not dangerous. If the ground is so \\nwet and soft that players cannot maintain a reasonable foothold - when bowling. when \\nplaying the ball, when running between wickets or when fielding the ball - then that is \\ndangerous. It may be that, if rain has interrupted play, it is possible to resume whilst there is \\nstill some drizzle. So long as the ground is safe (particularly the bowler\'s run-ups and the \\npitch itself) and reasonable to play on, and the continued rainfall is unlikely to cause \\nsignificant damage to the pitch, play could well restart. \\nUmpires must not allow play to take place in dangerous conditions, whatever the wishes of \\nthe captains may be. The state of the game should never be a consideration. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"If the conditions are dangerous, they are dangerous however close a result is. If they are not \\ndangerous, then play should continue. Whilst not suspending play unless conditions require \\nit, the umpires must, if in doubt, err on the side of caution, and suspend play earlier rather \\nthan later. \\nThere could be other situations where it would be dangerous for play to take place, such as \\na large swarm of bees Invading\' the field of play. Similarly, it may be unreasonable, but not \\ndangerous for play to take place - for example, waiting for paramedics to come to treat a \\nplayer who has collapsed on the field of play. The same considerations apply to such \\nsituations as to ground, weather and light. If conditions are dangerous or unreasonable, \\numpires must suspend play. \\nThe umpires\' first duty in this respect is to check the conditions before the day\'s play. \\nAlthough the selection and preparation of the pitch before the toss is entirely a matter for \\nthe Ground Authority, umpires have authority to judge at any time, even before the toss, \\nthe fitness of conditions and must do so. After the toss they take sole responsibility for the \\nground and conditions. When play is to resume after an interval or interruption, again, both \\nthe umpires must agree that conditions are safe for this to happen. \\nAlmost everywhere in the Laws, the umpires are encouraged, indeed required, to discuss \\nmatters between them and reach an agreement (more on this to follow in Law 2.11 and Law \\n31.6). Where possible, it is expected that the umpires will agree on whether play should \\ntake place and every step should be taken to ensure that this is the case. However, if there \\nis a disagreement between the two umpires, then one umpire deciding that it is \\nunreasonable or dangerous to play is enough to stop the game, or prevent it from starting. \\nOnce the umpires have decided that play is not to take place, they must keep a watch on \\nconditions to see if they improve. The state of the game may influence the teams differently \\nabout wanting to start play. The umpires must not take that into consideration. They will \\ncarry out inspections at suitable intervals, concerned only with whether conditions are safe \\nfor play, and without any player or team official joining them. Once the umpires \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"agree between themselves that conditions are no longer dangerous, play is to resume \\nregardless of the wishes of the players. Again, both umpires must agree that the conditions \\nare safe and reasonable for play to resume \\n \\nPosition of umpires \\nThe ability to see the action that is taking place is always the most important consideration \\nfor an umpire. If a bowler requests that an umpire stands closer to or further from, the \\nstumps, the umpire should agree only as far as is possible without making it impossible for \\nthe umpire to do his/her own job properly, and make sure the new position is still safe. \\nWhere the umpire stands may vary for different types of bowlers. Many umpires will want \\nto stand closer to the stumps for slower bowlers, and slightly further back for faster ones. \\nAn umpire\'s position should allow him/ her to see the popping crease clearly, and then \\nadjust to watching the batsman, without movement of the head. Only the umpire\'s eyes \\nshould move as they go from watching the bowler\'s foot to picking up the flight of the ball. \\nIf a bowler in his/her follow-through runs in front of the umpire, he/she may or may not be \\nguilty of breaching various Laws - more pertinently, he/she will certainly block the umpire\'s \\nview. The umpire must not move to get a better view - keeping the head still is vital for \\njudging any decision, and an umpire who is not in line with the stumps will be not much \\nbetter placed to judge an LBW appeal than one who has a bowler in the way. If it is not \\npossible to judge LBW decisions as a result of his/her view being blocked then the bowler \\nshould be informed and a decision cannot be answered with anything other than Not out. It \\nis a bowler\'s right to follow through in front of the umpire, so long as they do not enter the \\nprotected area (this is unlikely to be possible for a fast bowler, but a spinner\'s follow-\\nthrough may well be short enough to allow them to fairly block the umpire\'s view) - \\nhowever, most will prefer to move out of an umpire\'s way once they realise it is likely to \\ncost them the chance of a wicket. \\nThe same applies to the umpire standing at the striker\'s end when a fielder crosses his/her \\npath. However, it will usually be possible for the striker\'s end umpire to move slightly to one \\nside or the other and still be able to see everything necessary. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"When the batsmen are running, the bowler\'s end umpire must move round quickly to get a \\nside-on view of the creases, of the putting down of the wicket, and, if necessary, of the \\ncrossing or otherwise of the batsman. He/she needs to be as far from the wicket as possible \\nin the time allowed. The umpire must not get in the fielders\' way, nor in the way of the \\nthrow in - not only to avoid being hit, but to make sure the fielding side are not \\ndisadvantaged. Most umpires move to the same side as the ball - which tends to allow for a \\nbetter view of the stumps being broken. However, there are exceptions - if the ball is hit \\ndown the ground, or to a point in line with the popping crease at the non-striker\'s end, it \\nwill be better to be on the other side, so as to avoid being hit by a throw. If an umpire places \\nhim/herself between the ball and the stumps, he/she may possibly obstruct or impede a \\nfielder\'s attempt to field the ball or have a clear throw at the wicket. \\nThe decision on which side to move is a personal one, and umpires will develop their own \\npreference with experience. What is key is to always remain aware of where the ball is, and \\nsimultaneously know when the batsman is about to touch down at the non-striker\'s end. \\nStanding side on with quick glances back and forth is generally the best way to be aware of \\nboth of these. When a batsman who has a runner is on strike, the bowler\'s end umpire will \\nalways move to the same side as the striker\'s end umpire, so as to avoid getting in the way \\nof the runner and to be able to see where he/she is. \\nThe umpire at the striker\'s end should stand on the popping crease, and can be any distance \\nfrom the wicket - but usually between 20 and 30 yards is suitable, close enough to see \\neverything necessary, but far enough away from the bat to be safe and not interfere with \\nplay. The umpire should generally be at square leg, although the position of fielders, the \\nsun, or a runner may require the umpire to move to the off side. If the two batsmen are left \\nand right-handed, it may require moving often - the umpire should anticipate this, and if the \\nbatsmen have changed ends, be ready to move quickly and immediately, so as not to hold \\nup the game. If the bowler has an extremely short run-up - likely to be a spinner - and the \\nbatsmen are taking regular singles, then it may be better for the striker\'s end \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"umpire to stay one side, rather than switching every ball - so long as the umpire can still see \\nall that is necessary. \\n \\nUmpires changing ends \\nUmpires will not change ends during a one innings match. They will change ends only when \\nboth sides have had one completed innings (see Law 13). \\n \\nUmpires consulting \\nNot only is it allowed to ask a colleague for help, the Law insists that umpires should consult \\nin a number of situations. For example, it may be that the bowler\'s end umpire cannot see \\nhow the wicket was broken because the striker has remained in front of it, completely \\nblocking the wicket from view. The striker\'s end umpire may be able to supply this \\ninformation. Consulting is not weakness on the part of an umpire; it is essential to ensure \\nthat decisions are made only on the most accurate and complete evidence available. It is \\nsometimes assumed that there are some matters that umpires can consult on, and others \\nwhich they may not. This is not the case - if a colleague is in a better position to see, on any \\ndecision, consultation is encouraged on any point of fact. \\nConsulting when unnecessary does little good. An umpire who feels the need to talk to their \\ncolleague about a decision they should be able to make on their own will lose the players\' \\nconfidence. However, there are many occasions where it is necessary, and important to \\nconsult. \\nThere are two main ways to consult: obviously, and subtly. The obvious consultation, of two \\numpires coming together to discuss a decision, has the advantage of being clear to players. \\nThe decision has then, obviously, been taken based on all the possible information and is \\neasier to \'sell\' to the players. \\nHowever, sometimes this will not be necessary. An effective consultation will be a single \\nquestion requiring the response of either yes or no - this may simply be achieved by a look \\nor a nod. Eye-contact between the umpires after every ball will allow consultation, often \\nunseen by players or spectators. Sometimes an obvious mime - for example to ask if the ball \\nhas carried - may also be necessary. Signals may be pre-arranged between the umpires \\nbefore the game for subtle consultation to work best. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Changing a Decision and Revoking a call  \\nAt umpire may realise that he/she has made a mistake. Perhaps he/she gave a batsman out \\nCaught not aware that the other umpire had called No ball. Perhaps the mistake is his her \\nown. There may be embarrassment but there Is no disgrace in admitting it and changing the \\ndecision as allowed in Law 2.12. It is much better than allowing a Poor decision to stand. The \\numpire will be more respected for it than for sticking to a decision he/she knows to be \\nwrong. The reversal of decision must be made \'promptly and certainly before any progress \\nat all with the game. Once an umpire has made a decision, and has not altered it, that \\ndecision will stand in all circumstances. It should be noted that this applies to all decisions, \\nexcept the call of Dead ball Playing Regulations at the highest levels, for challenging umpires \\ndecisions (known as UDRS), do not apply in Law The changing of an umpire\'s decision is \\nseparate, in Law, from a captain withdrawing an appeal (Law 31.8)- which requires the \\numpire\'s permission, but is not the umpire\'s decision. \\n \\nSignals to scorers \\nThese are fully explained in Law and illustrated on pages 61-66. Signals should be made as \\nstated. They are the means by which the umpire informs the scorers of vital facts about the \\nprogress of play. They must be entirely clear, without personal variations or flourishes. \\nThe Law also lays down when signals are to be made Four of the signals should be made \\nwhen the ball is in play, and then three of these are repeated when the ball becomes dead \\nall other signals are simply made once the ball is dead. Initial signals made while the ball is in \\nplay (Out, No ball, Wide ball and Dead ball) are merely to inform the players - they are all, \\nwith the exception of Out, to be accompanied by a call to further inform the players. VIDEO \\nthere is a call alongside a signal, this should be loud and clear - it does not need to be \\nshouted for the scorers to hear but should at least be audible to any fielder in the in-field \\nWhere Apart from the signal for Out, all of these initial signals a to be repeated to the \\nscorers by the bowlers end umpire when the ball is dead. \\nThe other signals are not made at all until the ball is dead. They are all to be made by the \\nbowlers end umpire. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"with the one exception of Short run. This is not made until the ball is dead, but may be by \\neither umpire and is accompanied by a call. It will depend on the end, or ends, at which \\nshort running happened - although the bowler\'s end umpire will then repeat it for the \\nscorers. If both umpires have seen a short run, they must confer to see whether two \\nseparate runs were short or whether the short running was in one and the same run. If \\nmore than one run is short, the bowler\'s end umpire should, as well as repeating the signal, \\ntell the scorers the actual number of runs scored. \\nIf there are multiple signals required, they should be made in the order the action occurred. \\nFor example, a No ball that goes for four Leg byes was a No ball first, then struck the \\nbatsman\'s person, then crossed the boundary. The umpire should signal them in that order - \\nNo ball, Leg byes, boundary four. Separate acknowledgements for each signal by at least \\none scorer are essential if the umpire is to be satisfied that the information has been \\nreceived in full. It should also be noted that, when making the signal for a Level 3 or Level 4 \\nbreach of conduct under Law 42, the umpire should wait for acknowledgement between \\nPart 1 and Part 2. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nDead ball \\nCross and re-cross the wrists below the waist. \\n \\nNo ball \\nExtend one arm horizontally. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b"b' \\nOut \\nRaise an index finger above the head. \\n \\nWide \\nExtend both arms horizontally. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nBoundary Four (4) \\nMove one arm back and forth as shown, starting and \\nending with the arm across the chest. \\n \\n \\n \\nBoundary Six (6)  \\nRaise both arms above the head. \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b"b' \\nBye \\nRaise an open hand above the head. \\n \\n \\nCommencement of last hour \\n \\nPoint to a raised wrist with the other hand. The \\nwrist should be well away from the body to be \\nvisible to the scorers. \\n \\n \\nFive Penalty runs to the batting side \\nTap one shoulder with the opposite hand \\nseveral times. The patting movement must be \\nexaggerated to make it clear - turning side on to \\nthe scorers will help this. \\n \\nFive Penalty runs to the fielding side \\nPlace one hand on the opposite shoulder. The \\nhand remains on the shoulder. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'Leg bye \\nRaise a knee and touch it with the hand. This is made side on to the scorers so that they can \\nclearly see it. \\n \\nNew ball \\nThe ball is held above the head so it can be clearly seen.   \\nRevoke last signal \\n Touch both shoulders with the opposite hand. \\n \\nShort run \\nTouch a shoulder with the tips of the fingers of the same hand, by bending that arm \\nupwards.  \\n \\n'"b"b' \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nLevel 3 offence \\n \\nFirst, put one arm out to the side of \\nthe body and repeatedly raise and \\nlower it as shown. \\nThen raise both hands, with fingers \\nspread, at shoulder height. \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b"b' \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nLevel 4 offence \\nFirst, put one arm out to the side \\nof the body and repeatedly raise \\nand lower it as shown. \\nThen raise an index finger at \\nshoulder height to the side of the \\nbody. \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Correctness of scores \\n \\nThe umpires\' responsibility is to satisfy themselves, throughout the game, that the score is \\ncorrect. The Law requires that they check with the scorers at intervals, and any opportunity \\npresented by interruptions in play. They do not, however need to keep score - that is the job \\nof the scorers. Some umpires will choose to keep score themselves - perhaps using a \\nmechanical counter - and then make a note at the end of every over. However, this is not \\nnecessary. \\nAt different levels of the game it will be more or less important - usually depending on how \\nexperienced the scorers are. An umpire who knows that both scorers are experienced and \\naccurate will not need to keep score almost at all. In the lower levels of the recreational \\ngame, where members of the batting team are taking turns to score, the umpires may well \\nneed to keep a fuller record. \\nThe minimum expected at all levels is for umpires to make a note of anything that the \\nscorers may have missed - Short run, for example, or the correct number of runs in the case \\nof an overthrow - and then consult the scorers at the next suitable break in play. \\nHowever, the umpires must check with each other at the first opportunity if either of them \\nthinks the scoreboard is incorrect. If they suspect that it may not be right, and it is not put \\nright in the next over or so, the problem must be sorted out. As far as is possible, play \\nshould not be held up to do so, although sometimes it may be necessary - for example, in a \\nclose game, the scores will need to be verified before the last over of a match. Aside from \\nthis, the fall of a wicket or a bowling change may provide the necessary pause in play. What \\nis critical is for the umpires to make a note of when the score appeared to be wrong and by \\nhow many runs. \\nAt the end of each innings and, of course, at the end of the match, the umpires and scorers \\nmust agree as to the final scores, wickets and, if relevant, overs. If there is any discrepancy, \\nthis must be sorted out between the umpires and scorers. If agreement cannot be reached, \\nit is the umpires who decide what is to be recorded. For more on what to do if there has \\nbeen a mistake which affects the result, see Law 16.9. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 3 THE SCORERS \\n \\n3.1 Appointment of scorers \\n  \\nTwo scorers shall be appointed to record all runs scored, all wickets taken and, \\nwhere appropriate, number of overs bowled. \\n \\n3.2 Correctness of scores \\n  \\nThe scorers shall frequently check to ensure that the records agree. They shall agree \\nwith the umpires, at least at every interval, other than drinks intervals, and at the \\nconclusion of the match, the runs scored, the wickets that have fallen and, where \\nappropriate, the number of overs bowled. See Law 2.15 (Correctness of scores). \\n \\n3.3 Acknowledging signals \\n  \\nThe scorers shall accept all instructions and signals given to them by the umpires and \\nshall immediately acknowledge each separate signal. \\nThere should be no difficulty in understanding this Law. As with umpires, there needs to be \\ntwo scorers. They should constantly check with each other that their records agree, \\nnormally at least at the end of each over. Just as it is important that umpires give them clear \\nsignals, it is important that they acknowledge those signals, each separately. \\nAs has been mentioned elsewhere, for umpires and scorers to work effectively together, \\nthey must meet before play. The umpires must be sure of the position of scorers - If they are \\nsitting outside, there is a possibility that they may move if light rain begins. The umpires \\nmust know where they will go if that happens. \\nAs well as the checks laid down for each interval (except drinks), the scorers should take the \\nearliest opportunity to clarify with the umpires any doubtful points. For example, that a \\nbatsman has been dismissed will be obvious. The method of dismissal may not be. \\nUmpire may want to agree some \'unofficial\' signals with the score to help them. In \\nparticular, good practice would be to arrange a signal between the scorers and umpires to \\nconfirm that the match is completed and that both scorers agree the result-this would save \\nthe embarrassment of leaving the field and having to return under Law 16.9. \\n \\n"'b"b'The scorers are all too often taken for granted, or overlooked. While this Law is very simple, \\nthe art of scoring is not. That art is covered in far more detail in the Scoring section of this \\npublication. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"LAW 4 THE BALL \\n 4.1 Weight and size \\nThe ball, when new, shall weigh not less than 55 ounces/155.9 g. nor more than 5.75 \\nounces/163g and shall measure not less than 8.81 in/22.4 cm, nor more than 9 in/22.9 cm in \\ncircumference. \\n \\n4.2 Approval and control of balls \\n4.2.1 \\nAll balls to be used in the match, having been determined by the umpires, \\nshall be in the possession of the umpires before the toss and shall remain under \\ntheir control throughout the match. \\n4.2.2 \\nThe umpire shall take possession of the ball in use at the fall of each wicket, \\nat the start of any interval and at any interruption of play. \\n \\n4.3 New ball \\nUnless an agreement to the contrary has been made before the match, either captain may \\ndemand a new ball at the start of each innings. \\n \\n4.4 New ball in match of more than one day\'s duration \\nIn a match of more than one day\'s duration, the captain of the fielding side may demand a \\nnew ball when the number of overs, excluding any part overs, bowled with the old one is \\nequal to or greater than 80 overs The umpire shall inform the other umpire and indicate to \\nthe batsmen and the scorers whenever a new ball is taken into play. \\n \\n4.5 Ball lost or becoming unfit for play \\nIf, during play, the ball cannot be found or recovered or the umpires agree that it has \\nbecome unfit for play through normal use, the umpires shall replace it with a ball which has \\nhad wear comparable with that which the previous ball had received before the need for its \\nreplacement. When the ball is replaced, the umpire sha Inform the batsmen and the fielding \\ncaptain. \\n4.6 Specifications \\nThe specifications as described in 4.1 shall apply to men\'s cricket only. The following \\nspecifications shall apply to: \\n \\n"'b'b"4.6.1 Women\'s cricket \\n  \\nWeight: from 4.94 ounces/140 g to 5.31 ounces 151g Circumference: from 8.25 \\nin/21.0 cm to 8.88 in/22.5 cm \\n4.6.2 Junior cricket - Under 13 \\n  \\nWeight from 469 ounces/133 g to 5.06 ounces/144 g Circumference from 8.06 \\nin/20.5 cm to 8.69 in/22.0 cm \\n \\nThis Law is concerned with two major areas - the measurements of the balls used, and the \\ncontrol of them during the match. \\nLaw 4.1 gives very specific regulations on the size and weight of the ball, while 4.6 provides \\nthe same information for women\'s and junior cricket. At higher level matches. ball gauges \\nare often used to check the size of balls. Gauges consist of two rings. One prevents too large \\na ball from passing through: the other will not prevent one that is too small from going \\nthrough. At lower levels, umpires without such devices may have to trust the manufacturers \\nto produce balls conforming to Law. Fortunately, this is usually the case - although it is not \\nideal, and where possible umpires should perform their own check. \\n \\nApproval and control \\nBefore the match, umpires must decide which type and how many balls are to be used, and \\nif relevant, make sure they know when a new ball may be taken. It is normal in a one-day \\nmatch for there to be a new ball for each side\'s innings. This is often set out in Playing \\nConditions for the match but is otherwise not required in Law. It may be agreed that the \\nsame ball will be used in both innings. Unless there is such an agreement, however, if either \\ncaptain asks for a new ball at the start of an innings, the other captain cannot prevent this. \\nBefore the match, the umpires must check, as far as they can, that the balls to be used are \\nwithin the Law. Most importantly, the umpires must then take possession of these. Ideally, \\nthere should also be a supply of used balls. in case the ball in use has to be replaced at any \\ntime. The relevant Playing Conditions will usually specify that these should be the same \\ncolour and brand as the ball in use. These should also be determined, and then kept, by the \\numpires - though for convenience they are often left with the scorers. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'During play, the umpire must take possession of the ball at the fall of every wicket, and at \\nevery interval or interruption. It should be the umpire who will be at the end from which the \\nnext delivery will be bowled. These should not be the only occasions when an umpire looks \\nat the ball. Law 41.3.1 instructs that they should make frequent and irregular inspections of \\nthe ball. The fact that irregular inspections are specified should help to prevent players from \\ntampering with the ball. Frequent inspections mean that umpires will know how much wear \\na ball has received if it has to be replaced with one of similar wear. It is also sensible to \\ncheck the ball after a boundary has been scored where the ball has struck a hard object \\noutside the boundary - any damage to it can be noted then, and its cause will thus be \\nobvious. \\n \\nReplacing the ball in use \\n \\nBalls can often be lost at any level of the game, particularly on smaller grounds. It can \\nbecome unfit for play if it loses its shape, becomes too wet to use, or some mishap damages \\nthe seam or the surface. It will be the umpires jointly who decide, independently of the \\nplayers, that the ball is unfit for play. The umpires will together choose, from those \\navailable, a replacement ball. The condition of the replacement should be as near as \\npossible to the condition of the original ball before it was lost or became unfit. If, however, \\nthe lost or unfit ball is wet, they will replace it with a dry one of similar wear. If the ball is \\nlost, or the damage is accidental, no action is taken against any players. However, if the \\numpires consider that the condition of the ball has been deliberately \\n \\n \\n \\nIf a ball becomes damaged  \\naccidentally, as this one has been \\nthe umpires must replace it, but need \\nnot take any action against  \\neither team. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"and unfairly changed it is dealt with under Law 41.3. The non-offending captain will then be \\nable to choose whether the ball is replaced or not. \\nShould only a newer ball be available as a replacement. umpires should not scuff it up or \\nbounce it to attempt to make it a similar condition to the original. They should simply \\nchoose the most suitable replacement available, and use that replacement ball in the \\ncondition it is in. \\nIn longer games, a new ball is available after 80 overs. The new ball does not have to be \\ntaken after 80 overs - the fielding captain may wait longer - nor does it have to be taken at \\nthe start of an over. However, a subsequent new ball will only be available 80 full overs after \\nthe previous one was taken - that means that another new ball will not necessarily be \\navailable after 160 overs. It will be later if the second new ball\'s taking was delayed. For \\nexample, if a side bowled 80.3 overs before they took the 2nd new ball, the 3rd new ball \\nwould not be available until 161 complete overs had been bowled. \\nWhen a ball is changed, the fielding captain and the batsmen must be informed. When a \\nnew ball is taken, the same people must be informed with the addition of the scorers. \\n \\nField technique \\n \\nIt can be useful for each umpire to carry a used spare ball on the field, particularly at a \\nground where balls are likely to be lost. If there are two that have received different wear, it \\nis more likely to be possible to match the condition of the ball in use if it has to be replaced. \\nIt will certainly save time if a ball can be replaced without the umpires having to go off the \\nfield. At higher levels, where there may be an extra official able to bring the spare balls out \\nto the pitch, umpires will be less likely to carry spares with them. \\nBalls should not be changed unnecessarily. The ball can move in and out of shape early in \\nthe innings. Umpires should be sure to check it regularly, but not necessarily replace it at \\nthe first sign of trouble - it may be back to normal a few balls later. It is also worth being \\naware that some players - likely to be bowlers - may ask for a ball to be changed because \\nthey feel it will benefit them tactically. Umpires must not be swayed by these requests. The \\nball should be changed if it is not fit for play, not because the fielding side feel it isn\'t \\nswinging! \\n"'b"b' Here, use of the ball rings (pictured below) may prove useful. In Law, the ball only needs to \\npass through the gauge when new - but some umpires will find it helpful to demonstrate \\nthat a ball is either in or out of, shape, through use of a gauge. This provides a simple, \\nmeasureable, answer to whether the ball is out of shape, and cannot be debated. That said, \\nthe Law does not require the umpire to change the ball simply because it does not fit \\nthrough the gauge - it is for the umpires to exercise their best judgment. \\n \\nA standard ball gauge. This \\nhas two holes, one which \\nthe ball should be able to \\npass through and one \\nwhich it should not be able \\nto pass through. Note that, \\nby Law the ball only has to \\nfit through a ball gauge \\nwhen new. \\n \\n \\nOnce the umpires decide a ball is no longer fit for use they must treat any subsequent balls - \\nincluding those in other Innings - in the same way It must be the same for both sides \\nIn matches of more than one day, in which a new ball is to be taken into use at some point, \\nIt  will be prudent for one umpire to take a new ball on to the field at the start of a session in \\nwhich the new ball becomes due. \\nBoth umpires should also carry with them a cloth for drying in case the ball should get wet. \\nSee also Law 41.3.2. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'LAW 5 THE BAT \\n5.1 The bat \\n5.1.1 \\nThe bat consists of two parts a handle and a blade . \\n5.1.2 \\nThe basic requirements and measurements of the bat are set out in this Law \\nwith detailed specifications in Appendix B. \\n \\n5.2 The handle \\n5.2.1 \\nThe handle is to be made principally of cane and/or wood. \\n5.2.2 \\nThe part of the handle that is wholly outside the blade is defined to be the \\nupper portion of the handle It is a straight shaft for holding the bat. \\n5.2.3 \\nThe upper portion of the handle may be covered with a grip as defined in \\nAppendix B.22. \\n5.3 The blade \\n5.3.1 \\nThe blade comprises the whole of the bat apart from the handle as defined \\n5.2 and in Appendix B3. \\n5.3.2 \\nThe blade shall consist solely of wood. \\n5.3.3 \\nAll bats may have commercial identifications on the blade. the size of which \\nmust comply with the relevant specification in Appendix B 6. \\n5.4 Protection and repair \\nSubject to the specifications in Appendix B.4 and providing 55 is not contravened, \\n5.4.1 \\nsolely for the purposes of \\neither protection from surface damage to the face. sides and shoulders of the blade \\nor repair to the blade after surface damage material that is not rigid, either at the \\ntime of its application to the blade or subsequently, may be placed on these \\nsurfaces. \\n5.4.2 \\nfor repair of the blade after damage other than surface damage \\n5.4.2.1 solid material may be inserted into the blade. \\n5.4.2.2 The only material permitted for any insertion is wood with minimal essential \\nadhesives  \\n \\n \\n5.4.3   to prevent damage to the toe, material may be placed on that part of the blade      \\nbut shall not extend over any part of the face, back or sides of the blade. \\n'"b'b"5.5 Damage to the ball \\n5.5.1 \\nFor any part of the bat, covered or uncovered, the hardness of the \\nconstituent materials and the surface texture thereof shall not be such that either or \\nboth could cause unacceptable damage to the ball. \\n5.5.2 \\nAny material placed on any part of the bat, for whatever purpose, shall \\nsimilarly not be such that could cause unacceptable damage to the ball. \\n5.5.3 \\nFor the purpose of this Law, unacceptable dom any change that is greater \\nthan normal wear and tear caused by the ball striking the uncovered wooden surface \\nof the blade. \\n \\n5.6 Contact with the ball \\nIn these Laws, \\n5.6.1 \\nreference to the bat shall imply that the bat is held in the batsman\'s hand or \\na glove worn on his/her hand unless stated otherwise. \\n5.6.2 \\ncontact between the ball and any of 5.6.2.1 to5.6.2.4 . \\n5.6.2.1 \\nthe bat itself . \\n5.6.2.2 \\nthe batsman\'s hand holding the bat. \\n5.6.2.3 \\nany part of a glove worn on the batsman\'s hand holding the bat. \\n5.6.2.4 \\nany additional materials permitted under 54 shall be regarded as the ball \\nstriking or touching the bat or being struck by the bat. \\n5.7 Bat size limits \\n5.7.1 \\nThe overall length of the bat, when the lower portion of the handle is \\ninserted, shall not be more than 38 in/96.52 cm. \\n5.7.2 \\nThe blade of the bat shall not exceed the following dimensions: \\nWidth: 4.25in/10.8 cm \\nDepth: 2.64in / 6.7 cm \\nEdges: 1.56in / 4.0 cm.  \\nFurthermore, it should also be able to pass through bat gauge as described in \\nAppendix B.8. \\n5.7.3 \\nExcept for bats of size 6 and less, the handle shall not exceed 52% of the \\noverall length of the bat.  \\n5.7.4 \\nThe material permitted for covering the blade in 5.4.1 shall not exceed 0.04 \\nin/0.1 cm in thickness \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"5.7.6 \\nThe maximum permitted thickness of protective material placed on the toe \\nof the blade is 0.12 in/03 cm. \\n5.8 Categories of bat \\n \\n5.8.1 \\nTypes A Band Care bats conforming to 51 to 5.7 inclusive. \\n5.8.2 \\nType A bats may be used at any level of cricket. \\n5.8.3 \\nThe specifications for Type D bats are described in Appendix B.7 and are for \\nuse by junior players in junior cricket only. \\n5.8.4 \\nBats of Type B. Type C. Type D and any other bats may be used only at or \\nbelow levels determined by the Governing Body for cricket in the country concerned.  \\n5.8.5 \\nBats that do not quality for any of the four categories A to D are not \\nrecognised in the Laws. \\nEverything about the cricket bat, from its size and shape to the materials used, has become \\nmore regulated in recent years. Major changes were made in 2008, and then again in 2017, \\nso that Law 5, and Appendix B, are now extremely clear on what is, and isn\'t allowed. \\n \\nDimensions of the bat (or \'Compliance of the bat\') \\nIt is clearly impractical for the umpire on the field to check all the complex details of the bat \\nfor every player. Bat manufacturers ought only to produce bats that meet the regulations - \\nhowever this cannot be assumed. \\nA bat more than 38 inches (96.52cm) long is rarely, if ever, found. There have, however, \\nbeen bats more than 4.25 inches (10.8cm) wide, even without any binding. A bat should be \\nmeasured if either umpire suspects it may be too wide, but there is certainly no need to \\nmeasure every bat brought to the crease. \\nThe size of the bat\'s edges, and the overall depth of a bat. are now regulated. Therefore, if \\numpires suspect that a bat looks too large, they should not be afraid to check it, using a bat \\ngauge. These are neither expensive nor heavy, and fit in an umpire\'s pocket. A player may \\nplead ignorance - and they are not to be penalised for having an improper bat by any on-\\nfield sanction, save for not being permitted to use that bat. Nonetheless, it is each player\'s \\nresponsibility that their bat is within the limits, and if it is not, they simply may not use it. \\nThere is no specified sanction for using an \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"illegal bat, aside from being prevented from using it further However, umpires should work \\nwith Governing Bodies to ensure a player does not repeatedly attempt to get away with\' an \\nillegal bat. This may mean providing a report if umpire consider this is a deliberate unfair \\naction. \\nThe suggested method for \\nusing a bat gauge: the bat is \\nheld in one hand, away from \\nthe body and in mid-air, while \\nthe other hand is used to pass \\nthe gauge along the blade\'s \\nlength, ensuring that the \\ngauge is held square to the \\nface of the bat. \\n \\nSee also the diagram in Appendix B.8 When testing the legality of a bat and its ability to pass \\nthrough a bat gauge, the umpire should only apply a minimal amount of force to get it \\nthrough. The suggested testing method is for the bat to be held in one hand, away from the \\nbody and in mid-air (i.e. not pressed against the ground or the body), while the other hand \\nis used to pass the gauge along the blade\'s length, ensuring that the gauge is held square to \\nthe face of the bat. The gauge\'s measurements are such that a bat with legitimate tape! \\nanti-scuff covering on the blade should still be able to pass through it. \\n \\nOther features \\nUmpires should also check, as far as is practical, if the blade is solely of wood and that any \\ncovering is not likely to damage the ball. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Types of bat \\n \\nIn most non-professional cricket, any of the three types A, Band C will be allowed, though \\ncloth-covered bats (Type C) may not be allowed at higher levels of the recreational game. \\nThe Type of a bat is not dependent on the quality of the willow - rather it is the materials it \\nis made of which define its Type. This is explained in Appendix B. Type D bats, which are \\nallowed to be laminated, cloth-covered and/ or coloured as explained in Appendix B.7, are \\nrestricted to use by junior players in junior cricket where the particular regulations allow. If \\nthose bats are used, the umpires should check that the material used to colour the bats \\ndoes not cause unacceptable damage to the ball. \\n \\nBat or person \\n \\nIn a number of Laws, whether the ball struck the striker\'s bat or his person is an important \\nfactor. Law 5.6, together with the definitions in Appendix A, make it clear that a hand (bare, \\nor wearing a glove) holding the bat is to be regarded as part of the bat. Similarly the whole \\nof a glove worn on the hand equally counts as part of the bat, as long as the hand is \\ntouching the bat. If a glove is held rather than worn, or the hand is not holding the bat, then \\neither will become part of the batsman\'s person. To be holding the bat, the batsman has to \\nhave some part of a glove he/she is wearing, or his/ her bare hand, in contact with the bat. \\n \\nAppendix A2.6 and Law 6, taken together, explain that the hand, if it is touching the bat, is \\nto be considered as part of the bat. Therefore, if one hand is holding the bat and the other \\nhand, though not touching the bat directly, is touching the hand holding the bat, that hand \\nis effectively touching the bat and so it also is to be considered as part of the bat. This does \\nnot apply to other parts of the batsman\'s person. \\n \\nDamage to the ball \\n \\nLaw 5.5 sets out what is considered unacceptable damage to the ball, and states that a bat \\nmust not cause this. That includes all parts of the bat. A striker is perfectly permitted to use \\nthe back of the bat, deliberately, to play the ball. That means that there cannot be any \\nmaterial on the back of the bat which might cause unacceptable damage to the ball. \\n"'b'b\'LAW 6 THE PITCH \\n6.1 Area of pitch \\nThe pitch is a rectangular area of the ground 22 yards/20.12 m in length and 10 ft/305 m in \\nwidth is bounded at either end by the bowling creases and on either side by imaginary lines, \\none each side of the imaginary line joining the centres of the two middle stumps, each \\nparallel to it and 5 ft/1.52 m from it If the pitch is next to an artificial pitch which is closer \\nthan 5 ft/1.52 m from the middle stumps, the pitch on that side will extend only to the \\njunction of the two surfaces. See Laws 8.1 (Description, width and pitching), 8.4 (Junior \\ncricket) and 7.2 (The bowling crease). \\n6.2 Fitness of pitch for play \\nThe umpires shall be the sole judges of the fitness of the pitch for play. See Laws 2.7 (Fitness \\nfor play) and 2.8 (Suspension of play in dangerous or unreasonable conditions). \\n6.3 Selection and preparation \\nBefore the match, the Ground Authority shall be responsible for the selection and \\npreparation of the pitch. During the match the umpires shall control its use and \\nmaintenance. \\n6.4 Changing the pitch \\nThe pitch shall not be changed during the match unless the umpires decide that it is \\ndangerous or unreasonable for play to continue on it and then only with the consent of both \\ncaptains. \\n6.5 Non-turf pitches \\nIn the event of a non-turf pitch being used the artificial surface shall conform to the \\nfollowing measurements \\nLength - a minimum of 58 ft/17.68 m  \\nWidth - a minimum of 6 ft/1.83 m \\nSee Law 9.8 (Non-turf pitches) \\n \\nNote that the area of ground defined in Law 6 is the pitch. not the wicket. The wicket is the \\nset of three stumps and two bails. In cricket jargon, \\\'wicket\\\' is often mis-used to mean the \\npitch - such as "it\\\'s a good batting wicket\\xe2\\x80\\x9d. \\n \\n\''b'b"Umpires must be clear about the difference between them. The wicket is a set of three \\nwooden sticks with two bits of wood on top. The pitch is the closely specified piece of \\nground between the two wickets. \\n \\nMeasurements \\nThere is an excellent diagram in Appendix C, showing all the measurements and how they \\nrelate to the crease markings. These are the subject of Law 7. It will not often be necessary \\nfor umpires to measure the length of the pitch. With experience, the umpire will develop \\nthe ability to judge the length of the pitch without needing to measure. After a few matches \\nin the season, markings on previously used pitches can be a help. Ir, however, it is thought \\nthat the length of the pitch may be incorrect, proper measurement should be made. Pacing \\nit out is not a proper measurement - if there is enough doubt to measure, then it should be \\ndone accurately. \\nAny errors should be corrected if possible before the start of the match. If it is not possible, \\nboth captains should be made fully aware of the situation. \\nNote that in junior cricket, Law 8.4 allows Governing Bodies to determine the dimensions of \\nstumps, bails and the length of the pitch. \\n \\nNon-turf or Artificial pitches \\nCompared to a natural, grass cricket surface, artificial cricket pitches are low maintenance \\nand are often found in areas where growing grass is challenging, or the cost of maintaining a \\ngood pitch is too high, or there isn\'t enough space for multiple pitches. Where competitions \\nallow use of non-turf pitches, umpires need to be familiar with variations to the relevant \\nLaws. \\nThe join between an artificial pitch and turf is unlikely to be perfectly flat, and can cause \\nseriously uneven bounce. Therefore, if an artificial strip is not the full 10 feet wide, the edge \\nof the artificial pitch is deemed to be where the artificial pitch meets the natural turf. \\nSimilarly, if a turf pitch, which is being used, is next to an artificial one, and the artificial one \\nis closer than five feet from middle stump, the join is taken as the edge of the turf pitch. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Selection, preparation and maintenance  \\nBefore the match, selection and preparation of the pitch is entirely up to the Ground \\nAuthority. Once the toss has been made, however, the umpires must control its use and \\nmaintenance. The ground staff will still carry out the mechanical tasks of mowing and \\nrolling, but the umpires are to supervise, to make sure that this is done within the Laws as \\nset out in Law 9. Even though this responsibility does not pass to the umpires until the toss, \\nthey should still inspect the pitch well before the match is to start to check both that it \\nmeets all the requirements of the Law and that it is fit for play - that is, that it would not be \\nunreasonable or dangerous for play to take place on it, as discussed under Law 2. \\n \\nChanging the pitch during a match \\nThis is not allowed, unless the umpires pronounce the one in use to be unfit - that is, \\nunreasonable or dangerous, for play. Even then, the two captains must agree to continue \\nthe game on another pitch. It is unlikely that there will be another pitch properly prepared, \\nalthough on some grounds there may be an artificial pitch which can be used. \\nIf either or both captains decide that they do not wish to use another pitch, this would not \\nbe considered a refusal to play. The options then would be either that the match is \\nabandoned or that it is decided to wait in the hope that conditions will improve sufficiently \\nfor play to restart on the original pitch. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'LAW 7 THE CREASES \\n7.1 The creases \\n  \\nThe positions of a bowling crease, a popping crease and two return creases shall be \\nmarked by white lines. as set out in 7.2, 7.3 and 7.4, at each end of the pitch. See \\nAppendix C. \\n7.2 The bowling crease \\n  \\nThe bowling crease, which is the back edge of the crease marking is the line that \\nmarks the end of the pitch as in Law 6.1 (Area of pitch. It shall be 8 ft 8 in/2.64 m in \\nlength. \\n7.3 The popping crease \\n  \\nThe popping crease, which is the back edge of the crease marking, shall be in front of \\nand parallel to the bowling crease and shall be 4 ft/1.22 m from it The popping \\ncrease shall be marked to a minimum of 6 t/1.83 m on either side of the imaginary \\nline joining the centres of the two middle stumps and shall be considered to be \\nunlimited in length. \\n7.4 The return creases \\n  \\nThe return creases, which are the inside edges of the crease markings, shall be at \\nright angles to the popping crease at a distance of 4 ft 4 in/132 m either side of the \\nimaginary line joining the centres of the two middle stumps Each return crease shall \\nbe marked from the popping crease to a minimum of 8 ft/244 m behind it and shall \\nbe considered to be unlimited in length. \\nThe umpires should always check the crease markings before the pre-match conference. \\nEven experienced groundsmen have been known to make occasional errors, both in \\nmeasurement and in alignment. As with the pitch itself and the wickets, umpires will, with \\nexperience, become better able to judge visually if the creases are not correct. The most \\nfrequent errors are the return creases not being marked to their full 8 feet back from the \\npopping crease and the wickets not being centred between the return creases. Any mistakes \\nshould be corrected if possible before the match starts. If it is not possible then the captains \\nmust be made fully aware of the difficulty. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Often groundsmen will add marks for the position of the Protected Area at the edge of the \\npitch and either side of the stumps. Whilst these marks are not required by the Laws, if they \\nare present it is important to check that they are accurate. They can help Umpires in \\nassessing both that the bowler\'s follow through does not encroach under Law 4113 and that \\nthe Striker does not take his guard within or close to the Protected Area as under Laws \\n41.14 and 41.15. The same is true of wide guidelines, which are often painted on pitches \\nand can be useful in limited overs cricket. Umpires may choose to use or ignore these \\nmarks, but if they are going to use them, they must first be sure they are accurate. \\nCreases and crease markings \\nThe crease markings are the lines painted on the ground. The creases are the back or inside \\nedges of those lines. They are clearly marked on the diagram in Appendix C. The Law makes \\na clear distinction between the two. The creases are the exact lines to be used in judgments \\nunder Law 21 (No ball) and Law 30 (Batsman out of his/her ground). \\nFor the crease markings, although the Law states that the lines must be white, it imposes no \\nrestrictions on their width. The thickness of the lines does not matter, as long as the inside \\nor back edges of the lines are in the correct place Whilst the bowling crease is to be 8ft 8 \\nin/2.64m long, for the popping crease and the return creases the stated lengths are only the \\nminimum that must be marked. They actually extend to the boundary on either side and the \\nboundary behind the wicket. This must not be forgotten by umpires judging the position of \\nthe feet in Law 21. \\n \\nOne end of a fully marked pitch. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"The bowling crease, perhaps confusingly, no longer has any significance for bowling. Its \\nimportance is in relation to the wicket. The centres of the stumps are to be on the crease \\n(the back of the marked line, in accordance with Law 8.1), not in the middle of the crease \\nmarking. \\nRe-marking of creases \\nSections of the popping crease are often quickly wiped away by the bowler\'s front foot. It is, \\nhowever, essential that there is a clear line for the umpire to judge the position of the \\nbowler\'s front foot for No balls. Although in Law 9 it will be seen that creases may be re-\\nmarked when either umpire considers it necessary, umpires should take any possible \\n \\nStumps correctly \\npitched Note that \\nthe centre of the \\nstumps are on the \\nback edge of the \\nbowling crease \\nmarking. \\n \\n"'b"b'action to restore the popping crease temporarily, until it is possible for the ground staff to \\nrepaint them fully, without holding up play unnecessarily. Marking a line with the edge of a \\nboot or a bail is effective but may have to be done for every delivery. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'LAW 8 THE WICKETS \\n8.1 Description, width and pitching \\n  \\nTwo sets of wickets shall be pitched opposite and parallel to each other in the \\ncentres of the bowling creases. Each set shall be 9 in/22.86 cm wide and shall consist \\nof three wooden stumps with two wooden bails on top. See Appendix D. \\n8.2 Size of stumps \\n  \\nThe tops of the stumps shall be 28 in/71.12 cm above the playing surface and shall \\nbe dome shaped except for the ball grooves. The portion of a stump above the \\nplaying surface shall be cylindrical apart from the domed top, with circular section of \\ndiameter nat less than 1.38 in 3.50 cm nor more than 1.5 in/3.81 cm. See Appendix \\nD. \\n8.3 The bails \\n8.3.1 \\nThe bails, when in position on top of the stumps. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 shall not project more than 0.5 in/1.27 cm above them. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 shall fit between the stumps without forcing them out of the vertical. \\n8.3.2 \\nEach bail shall conform to the following specifications (see Appendix D): \\n  \\nOverall length: 4.31 in/10.95 cm \\n  \\nLength of barrel: 2.13 in /5.40 cm  \\n  \\nLonger spigot: 1.38 in/3.50 cm \\n  \\nShorter spigot: 0.81 in/2.06 cm \\n8.3.3 \\nThe two spigots and the barrel shall have the same centre line. \\n8.3.4 \\nDevices aimed at protecting player safety by limiting the distance that a bail \\ncan travel off the stumps will be allowed, subject to the approval of the Governing \\nBody for the match and the ground authority. \\n8.4 Junior cricket \\n  \\nThe Governing Body for Cricket in the country concerned shall determine the \\ndimensions of the stumps and balls and the distance between the wickets. \\n8.5 Dispensing with balls \\n  \\nThe umpires may agree to dispense with the use of bails, if necessary. If they so \\nagree then no bails shall be used at either end. The use of bails shall be resumed as \\nsoon as conditions permit. See Law 29 4 (Dispensing with bails). \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'This Law defines the shape and size of the stumps and of the bails, as well as the dimensions \\nof the wicket as a whole. These are all clearly illustrated in Appendix D. Umpires must be \\nsatisfied that all implements and equipment used in the match is correct under the Laws, \\nbut they are not expected to have to check all measurements with a tape measure. If there \\nare any items which appear to be incorrect to the naked eye, then further inspection will be \\nneeded. \\nMatters that umpires should always check are:  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 that the wickets are aligned end to end and parallel to each other. A not uncommon \\nerror by umpires is a failure to check that the stumps are perfectly upright  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 that stumps are of the correct shape and are correctly placed, with the back edge of \\nthe marking through the centres of the stumps - this is of particular importance, as it \\nis often, wrongly, assumed that the stumps should be in the middle of the marked \\nline. It is the Ground Authority, not the umpires, who will usually make the holes for \\nthe stumps - but should an umpire notice they are incorrect they should attempt to \\ncorrect them, or get the ground staff to do so \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 that the stumps are secured firmly in the ground, and are equally spaced, upright \\nand parallel to each other Watering of the stump holes will give extra firmness if the \\nground is crumbling or better grip if the ground is hard  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 that the bails fit into the grooves and do not force the tops of the stumps apart  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 that, if possible, the bails at each end are a matching pair \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 that the ball will not pass between the stumps.  \\nBoth umpires should carry a spare bail in case one breaks. Some will carry a spare pair, to \\nensure having a matching pair if replacement is necessary. \\n \\nTethered bails \\nIn the act of putting down the wicket, the bails can be sent flying, which is occasionally \\ndangerous. In response to injuries sustained by wicket-keepers in particular, Law \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"8.3.4 allows specially designed mechanisms which tether the balls to the stumps, thereby \\nrestricting the distance that they can fly off the stumps, but without limiting their ability to \\nbe dislodged. Such devices would need the approval of the appropriate Governing Body, \\nwhich may be done nationally or locally. \\n \\nDispensing with bails \\nIf the bails are continually being blown off during play, not only is this irritating, unfairly \\nbreaking concentration for both batsmen and fielders, but it slows play. Heavy bails, made \\nof a heavier type of wood, are often a solution. If they are available, they are often enough \\nto fix the problem, if it is caused by a strong wind. It is not always that the difficulty is \\ncaused by a high wind. It may be the result of poor bail grooves. Nonetheless, if heavy bails \\ndo offer a solution it is far better to use them, and continue with a \'normal\' game, if \\npossible, than to abandon bails altogether. \\n \\nThere is no \\ndifference in size \\nand appearance \\nbetween regular \\nbails (left) and \\nheavy bails \\n(right). \\n \\nHowever, if heavy bails do not solve the problem, the umpires should agree to dispense \\nwith bails altogether. This decision, which is for the umpires alone to make, should not be \\ntaken lightly. It creates extra responsibility for the umpires in judging whether or not the \\nwicket has been put down. That problem is discussed in Law 29. Here it should be noted \\nthat if bails are not used at one end, then they must not be used at either end. If conditions \\nimprove, bails should be replaced at both ends as soon as possible. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 9 PREPARATION AND MAINTENANCE OF THE PLAYING AREA \\n9.1 Rolling \\nThe pitch shall not be rolled during the match except permitted in 9.1.1 and 9.1.2 \\n9.1.1  Frequency and duration of rolling \\n  \\nDuring the match the pitch may be rolled at the refuge of the captain of the batting \\nside, for a period of not more than 7 minutes before the start of each innings other \\nthan the first innings of the match and before the start of each subsequent day\'s \\nplay. See 9.1.4.  \\n9.1.2  Rolling after a delayed start \\n  \\nIn addition to the rolling permitted above, if after the toss and before the first \\ninnings of the match the start is delayed, the captain of the batting side may request \\nthat the pitch be rolled for not more than 7 minutes However, if the umpires \\ntogether agree that the delay has had no significant effect on the state of the pitch \\nthey shall refuse such request for rolling of the pitch. \\n9.1.4  Choice of rollers \\nif there is more than one roller available the captain of the batting side shall choose which \\none is to be used  \\n9.1.4  Timing of permitted rolling  \\nThe rolling permitted (maximum 7 minutes) before play begins on any day shall be started \\nnot more than 30 minutes before the time scheduled or rescheduled for play to begin. The \\ncaptain of the batting side may, however, delay the start of such rolling until not less than \\n10 minutes before the time scheduled or rescheduled for play to begin. \\n9.2 Clearing debris from the pitch \\n9.2.1 The pitch shall be cleared of any debris \\n9.2.1.1 \\nbefore the start of each day\'s play. This shall be after the completion of \\nmowing and before any rolling, not earlier than 30 minutes nor later than 10 minutes \\nbefore the time or any rescheduled time for start of play. \\n9.2.1.2 \\nbetween innings. This shall precede rolling if any is to take place. \\n9.2.1.3 \\nat all intervals for meals. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'9.2.2  The clearance of debris in 9 21 shall be done by sweeping, except where the umpires \\nconsider that this may be detrimental to the surface of the pitch. In this case the debris \\nmust be cleared from that area by hand, without sweeping. \\n9.2.3 In addition to 9.21, debris may be cleared from the pitch by hand, without sweeping, \\nbefore mowing and whenever either umpire considers it necessary. \\n9.3 Mowing \\n9.3.1 Responsibility for mowing \\n9.3.1.1 \\nmowings which are carried out before the match shall be the sole \\nresponsibility of the Ground Authority. \\n9.3.1.2 \\nAll subsequent mowings shall be carried out under the supervision of the \\numpires. \\n9.3.2 The pitch and outfield \\n  \\nin order that throughout the match the ground conditions should be as nearly the \\nsame for both sides as possible, both the pitch and the outfield shall be mown on \\neach day of the match on which play is expected to take place, if ground and \\nweather conditions permit. \\n  \\nIf, for reasons other than conditions of ground or weather complete mowing of the \\noutfield is not possible, the Ground Authority shall notify the captains and umpires \\nof the procedure to be adopted for such mowing during the match. \\n9.3.3 Timing of mowing \\n9.3.3.1 \\nMowing of the pitch on any day shall be completed not later than 30 minutes \\nbefore the time scheduled or rescheduled for play to begin on that day, before any \\nsweeping prior to rolling. If necessary, debris may be removed from the pitch before \\nmowing, by hand, without sweeping See 9.2.3. \\n9.3.3.2 \\nMowing of the outfield on any day shall be completed not later than 15 \\nminutes before the time scheduled or rescheduled for play to begin on that day. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"9.4 Watering the pitch \\n  \\nThe pitch shall not be watered during the match. \\n9.5 Re-marking creases \\n  \\nCreases shall be re-marked whenever either umpire considers it necessary. \\n9.6 Maintenance of footholes \\n  \\nThe umpires shall ensure that the holes made by the bowlers and batsmen are \\ncleaned out and dried whenever necessary to facilitate play. In matches of more \\nthan one day\'s duration, the umpires shall allow, if necessary, the re-turfing of \\nfootholes made by the bowlers in their delivery strides or the use of quick-setting \\nfillings for the same purpose. \\n \\n9.7 Securing of footholds and maintenance of pitch \\n  \\nDuring play, umpires shall allow the players to secure their footholds by the use of \\nsawdust provided that no damage to the pitch is caused and that Law 41 (Unfair \\nplay) is not contravened. \\n9.8 Non-turf pitches \\n  \\nWherever appropriate, the provisions set out in 9.1 to 9.7 shall apply. \\n \\nMuch of this Law will apply only in higher grade matches, where play extends to more than \\none day. However, even at fairly low levels of the game, rolling of the pitch is often available \\nand, in addition to the pre-match rolling by the ground staff, will take place between innings \\nif requested. \\nResponsibility of umpires \\nUntil the toss, decisions about mowing, sweeping, watering and covering the pitch are \\nentirely the responsibility of the Ground Authority - whoever that may be. This does not \\nmean that the umpires have no interest until the toss. Not only do they have a responsibility \\nto check that the creases and wickets are in accordance with Law but they are also the sole \\njudges of the fitness of the pitch for play. It is not necessary for the toss to have taken place \\nfor them to decide that it would be unreasonable or dangerous for play to take place and so \\nto forbid play to start. \\nOnce the toss has taken place, the management of all the activities listed above, although \\nstill carried out by the Ground Authority, is transferred to the umpires. The Law lays down \\n"'b"b'both the restrictions imposed and what is permitted. This is what should happen, but in \\nmany one day games there will be neither the equipment nor the personnel to achieve \\nthese conditions. \\n \\nAfter the toss the pitch is \\nthe responsibility of the \\numpires. Therefore, while \\nthe ground staff may carry \\nout sweeping (top) or other \\nmaintenance (bottom) it \\nshould be under the \\nsupervision of the umpires. \\n \\nUmpires must see that what is possible does take place and that no action of pitch or \\nground maintenance contravenes the Law. \\nThe chart on page 96 summarises the points of the Law. The captain of the side winning the \\ntoss will have based the decision whether to bat or to field partly on his/her reading of the \\npitch.
