This is the reason that no rolling is allowed before the first innings of the match, \\nunless there is a delay between the toss and the call of Play. If there is a delay and the \\ncaptain of the side due to bat then requests that the pitch be rolled, the umpires must \\ndecide together \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"what effect that delay has had on the state of the pitch. If there has been a sudden heavy \\ndownpour before the call of Play, this may change its condition - particularly if covers are \\nnot available or are not immediately used - and rolling could be allowed. If, however, one of \\nthe opening batsmen injures his/her ankle on the way to the crease, there would be a short \\ndelay in which nothing significant happens to the pitch. In this case, the umpires would \\nrefuse the request for rolling. \\nDamage to the pitch \\nSince the players cannot avoid walking or running on the pitch in appropriate circumstances, \\nsome damage is unavoidable, especially in damp conditions when the ground is soft. \\nSpecific, or even deliberate, damage is dealt with in Law 41. Here, umpires should note their \\nresponsibility for minimising this unavoidable damage in several ways under this Law. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The use of sawdust will help bowlers and batsmen avoid sliding. This should help \\nthem keep their footing, boosting their performance, and help keep the ground in \\nbetter condition. However, the use of sawdust should not be excessive in situations \\nwhere play might otherwise be suspended under Law 2.7.4, and must not damage \\nthe pitch in any way. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The batsmen may prod down divots on which the ball could land but must not be \\nallowed to bang the bat on the ground with unnecessary force. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If, in dry conditions, the surface is breaking up the umpires will not allow sweeping, \\nwhich might worsen the condition. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The bowlers\' footholes should be cleaned out and dried as far as possible. In \\nmatches of more than one day\'s duration, the ground staff will be able to fill them \\novernight with quick setting fillings, or even turf. \\n \\nMowing not possible \\nThe Law makes provision for difficulties in mowing the outfield every day, such as the \\nbreakdown of the machinery required. The umpires and captains must know what \\nalternative arrangements - perhaps for partial mowing - have been made. The umpires \\nshould check that this information is readily available for all. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"In multi-day matches, quick-set \\nfillings like this can help repair \\ndamage caused by bowlers\' \\nfootmarks. \\n \\nIn some parts of the world it may be more suitable to mow the outfield at the end of a day\'s \\nplay - rather than the beginning of the next one, when dew may be a factor. The Laws do \\nnot allow for this, however many Playing Conditions do alter matters so it is permitted. \\nUmpires should make sure they are aware of any such Playing Conditions. \\nRe-marking creases \\nCreases are important for many judgments that the umpires have to make. It is therefore \\nessential that the markings are clear. There is no restriction in Law on when they may be re \\nmarked. It is to be done whenever either umpire considers it necessary. It is likely that both \\nsets of creases will need re-marking at the same time, but the Law does not insist on this. \\nThey should, if possible, be re-marked between innings. There are other opportunities when \\nre-marking can be done without delaying the progress of play. If necessary an umpire can, \\nas explained in Law 7, do a makeshift marking him/herself, until such an opportunity arises. \\nThe chart summarises the main points. It does not include the initial preparation by the \\nground staff, except for the time by which rolling and mowing must finish before the match. \\nNon-turf pitches \\nClearly regulations about mowing the pitch will not apply. but re-marking of creases and \\nclearing of debris might well be relevant. If the under surface is hard earth, rather than \\nconcrete, even rolling might be appropriate. Umpires must apply this Law, taking into \\naccount the relevant context. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'PREPARATION AND MAINTENANCE OF THE PITCH \\n \\nBefore time for start of play \\n \\nStart \\nFinish \\n \\nFirst Day \\nSubsequent \\ndays \\nNot Earlier \\nthan \\nNot later than \\nNot later than \\nMOWING \\nOutfield \\n- \\neach morning \\n(If possible) \\n- \\n- \\n15 mins \\nPitch \\n- \\neach morning \\n- \\n- \\n30 mins \\nROLLING THE PITCH \\n(if the batting captain requests it. Maximum of 7 minutes for each occasion) \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nPitch \\nnot before first \\ninnings \\n \\nbetween toss \\nand start of \\nplay, if  start \\ndelayed* \\n \\nbefore \\nsubsequent \\ninnings \\nEach morning \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nbetween \\ninnings \\n30 mins \\n10 mins\\xe2\\x80\\xa0 \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n- \\nSWEEPING \\n(may be used clear debris as below, but not before mins \\n10 mins \\n- \\nPitch \\nAdditionally, by hand, before \\nmowing and whenever umpires \\nconsider necessary \\n- \\n- \\n- \\nRE-MARKING CREASES \\n Whenever either umpire considers necessary \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa0 Batting captain can request that start of rolling be delayed until not less than 10 minutes before start time \\n'"b'b"LAW 10 COVERING THE PITCH  \\n10.1 Before the match \\n  \\nThe use of covers before the match is the responsibility of the Ground Authority and \\nmay include full covering if required. \\n  \\nHowever the Ground Authority shall grant suitable facility to the captains to inspect \\nthe pitch before the nomination of their players and to the umpires to discharge \\ntheir duties as laid down in Laws 2 (The umpires), 6 (The pitch), 7 (The creases), 8 \\n(The wickets) and 9 (Preparation and maintenance of the playing area). \\n10.2 During the match \\n  \\nUnless determined otherwise before the toss, on each night of the match and in \\ninclement weather at any time during the match \\n \\n10.2.1 \\nthe whole pitch and a minimum of 4 ft /122 m beyond it at each end shall be \\ncovered \\n10.2.2 \\nthe bowlers\' run-ups, where possible, shall be covered. \\n10.3 Removal of covers \\n10.3.1 \\nafter the toss the pitch is covered overnight, the covers shall be removed as \\nsoon as practicable on each day that play is expected to take place. \\n10.3.2 \\nif covers are used during the day as protection from inclement weather or if \\ninclement weather delays the removal of overnight covers, they shall be removed \\npromptly as soon as conditions allow. \\nAgreement to the use of covers \\nOnce a rarity in club cricket, most grounds can now offer some form of covering for the \\npitch. The Law change in 2017 reflected this, making the use of covers standard practice - if \\nthey are available, they will be used unless determined otherwise. \\nUmpires will discover, in their general inspection of the ground on arrival, what covers, if \\nany, are available. What covering is done before the match is, as with all pitch preparation, a \\nmatter for the Ground Authority. The only restriction on the extent and timing of its use is \\nthat players, and umpires must be granted access before the toss, to Inspect the pitch. Once \\nthe toss has taken place, the umpires are responsible for overseeing covering throughout \\nthe match. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Covering bowlers\' run ups \\nThe point of covering the pitch is to maximise playing time once conditions allow for play. \\nThe Law therefore requires that the whole pitch, and the area four feet beyond it at each \\nend (Le to the end of the marked return creases). are covered. After that, the next priority, if \\npossible, is the bowlers\' run-ups. Even at Test Match grounds, the covers may not be long \\nenough for the complete run-up of some bowlers At other grounds they may well fall very \\nfar short! The most important part that needs covering is the last few strides before delivery \\nclose to the bowling and popping creases which is covered by the Law. After that it is \\nobviously better to cover as much of the rest of the run-ups as possible. \\n \\n       ================     Minimum required covers \\n \\nRemoval of covers \\nTo keep loss of playing time to a minimum, covers should be removed as soon as the \\numpires decide the weather has improved sufficiently. It is for the umpires to make this \\ndecision, but the action will be taken in conjunction with the Ground Staff. In some \\ncompetitions there are Playing Conditions giving clear direction what is expected. In \\nmatches of more than one day, covers may well be used to protect the pitch from possible \\nrain overnight. These, too, should be removed as early as practicable providing the weather \\nis fine. \'Practicable\' means at a time at which it is reasonable to expect ground staff to be \\navailable. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Field technique \\nIf there is a sudden downpour, getting the covers on to the pitch quickly will be important \\nfor keeping the pitch as dry as possible, and maximising the time for play once conditions \\nallow for a restart. In their pre-match inspection, umpires should not only find out what \\ncovers are available, they also should find out how they are to be brought on to the pitch \\nand who will do it, so that everyone is ready to act quickly if need arises. \\nIn deciding a time for resumption of play after an interruption for rain, umpires should allow \\ntime for removal of covers. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"LAW 11 INTERVALS \\n11.1 An interval \\n11.1.1  The following shall be classed as intervals: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The period between close of play on one day and the start of the next day\'s play. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Intervals between innings. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Intervals for meals. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Intervals for drinks. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Any other agreed interval. \\n11.1.2  Only these intervals shall be considered as scheduled breaks for the purposes of Law \\n24.2.6. \\n \\n11.2 Duration of intervals \\n11.2.1   An interval for lunch or tea shall be of the duration determined under Law 2.3 \\n(Consultation with captains) taken from the call of Time before the interval until the call of \\nPlay on resumption after the interval. \\n11.2.2    An interval between innings shall be 10 minutes, commencing from the close of an \\ninnings until the call of Play for the start of the next innings. See, however 11.3, 11.5 and \\n11.6. \\n11.3 Allowance for interval between innings  \\nIn addition to the provisions of 11.5 and 11.6. \\n11.3.1   if an innings ends when 10 minutes or less remains before the time agreed for close \\nof play on any day there shall be no further play on that day. No change shall be made to \\nthe time for the start of play on the following day on account of the 10 minute interval \\nbetween innings. \\n11.3.2    if a captain declares an innings closed during an interruption in play of more than \\n10 minutes duration provided that at least 10 minutes remains of the interruption, no \\nadjustment shall be made to the time for resumption of play on account of the 10 minute \\ninterval between innings, which shall be considered as included in the interruption If less \\nthan 10 minutes remains of the interruption when the captain declares the innings closed, \\nor forfeits an innings, the next innings shall commence 10 minutes after the declaration or \\nforfeiture is made. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'11.3.3    if a captain declares an innings closed during any interval other than an interval for \\ndrinks, provided that at least 10 minutes remains of the interval, the interval shall be of the \\nagreed duration and shall be considered to include the 10 minute interval between innings if \\nless than 10 minutes remains of the interval when the captain declares the innings closed, \\nor forfeits an innings, the interval shall be extended as necessary and the next innings shall \\ncommence 10 minutes after the declaration or forfeiture is made. \\n \\n11.4 Changing agreed times of intervals \\nIf, at any time during the match, \\neither Playing time is lost through adverse conditions of ground, weather or light or in \\nexceptional circumstances, \\nor \\nthe players have occasion to leave the field other than at a scheduled interval, the time of \\nthe lunch interval or of the tea interval may be changed if the two umpires and both \\ncaptains so agree, providing the requirements of 112 and 115, 116, 11.7 and 118 3 are not \\ncontravened. \\n11.5 Changing agreed time for lunch interval  \\n11.5.1   If an innings ends when 10 minutes or less remains before the agreed time for \\nlunch, the interval shall be taken immediately. It shall be of the agreed duration and shall be \\nconsidered to include the 10 minute interval between innings. \\n11.5.2   if because of adverse conditions of ground, weather or light, or in exceptional \\ncircumstances, a stoppage occurs when 10 minutes or less remains before the agreed time \\nfor lunch, then, whether or not agreement is reached in the circumstances of 11.4. the \\ninterval shall be taken immediately It shall be of the agreed duration Play shall resume at \\nthe end of this interval or as soon after as conditions permit. \\n11.5.3   If the players have occasion to leave the field for any reason when more than 10 \\nminutes remains before the agreed time for lunch then, unless the umpires and captains \\ntogether agree to alter it, lunch shall be taken at the agreed time. \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'11.6 Changing agreed time for tea interval \\n11.6.1    If an innings ends when 30 minutes or less remains before the agreed time for tea \\nthe interval shall be taken immediately It shall be of the agreed duration and shall be \\nconsidered to include the 10 minute interval between innings. \\n11.6.2n   If, when 30 minutes remains before the agreed time for tea, an interval between \\ninnings is already in progress, play shall resume at the end of the 10 minute interval if \\nconditions permit. \\n11.6.3 If, because of adverse conditions of ground, weather or light or in exceptional \\ncircumstances, a stoppage occurs when 30 minutes or less remains before the agreed time \\nfor tea, then unless \\neither there is an agreement to change the time for tea, as permitted in 11.4 \\nor \\nthe captains agree to forgo the tea interval as permitted in 11.9 \\nthe interval shall be taken immediately. The interval shall be of the agreed duration Play \\nshall resume at the end of the interval or as soon after as conditions permit. \\n11.6.4   If a stoppage is already in progress when 30 minutes remains before the agreed \\ntime for tea, 114 shall apply \\n11.7 Lunch or tea interval - 9 wickets down  \\nFor the lunch interval and for the tea interval if  \\nEither 9 wickets are already down when 3 minutes remains to the agreed time for the \\ninterval  \\nor \\nthe 9th wicket falls within this 3 minutes, or at any time up to and including the final ball of \\nthe over in progress at the agreed time for the interval, \\nthen the provisions of Law 12.5.2 shall not apply and the interval will not be taken until the \\nend of the over that is in progress 30 minutes after the originally agreed time for the \\ninterval, unless the players have cause to leave the field of play or the innings is completed \\nearlier For the purposes of this section of Law, the retirement of a batsman is not to be \\nconsidered equivalent to the fall of a wicket. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'11.8 Intervals for drinks \\n11.8.1   If on any day the umpires determine that there shall be intervals for drinks, the \\noption to take such drinks shall be available to either side. Each interval shall be kept as \\nshort as possible and in any case shall not exceed 5 minutes. \\n11.8.2     Unless, as permitted in 11.9, the captains agree to forgo it, a drinks interval shall be \\ntaken at the end of the over in progress when the agreed time is reached. If, however, a \\nwicket falls or a batsman retires within 5 minutes of the agreed time then drinks shall be \\ntaken immediately. \\nNo other variation in the timing of drinks intervals shall be permitted except as provided for \\nin 118.3. \\n11.8.3    If an innings ends or the players have to leave the field of play for any other reason \\nwithin 30 minutes of the agreed time for a drinks interval, the umpires and captains \\ntogether may rearrange the timing of drinks intervals in that session. \\n11.8.4    Intervals for drinks may not be taken during the last hour of the match, as defined \\nin Law 126 (Last hour of match - number of overs). Subject to this limitation, the umpires \\nshall determine the times for such intervals, if any, before the toss and on each subsequent \\nday not later than 10 minutes before play is scheduled to start. \\n11.9 Agreement to forgo intervals \\nAt any time during the match, the captains may agree  to forgo the tea interval or any of the \\ndrinks intervals The umpires shall be informed of the decision When play is in progress, the \\nbatsmen at the wicket may deputise for their captain in making an agreement to forgo a \\ndrinks interval in that session. \\n11.10 Scorers to be informed \\nThe umpires shall ensure that the scorers are informed of all agreements about hours of \\nplay and intervals and of any changes made thereto as permitted under this Law. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"This Law identifies which breaks in play are to be classed as intervals, how intervals may be \\nchanged or cancelled and the specific arrangements governing certain intervals. Intervals \\nare also known as scheduled breaks and are different to interruptions and stoppages. Many \\nmatches will be governed by different Playing Conditions, which will define when intervals \\nmay be taken. Where intervals are not specifically mentioned in those conditions, they must \\nbe determined by the umpires before the toss, as stated in this and other Laws. In either \\ncase, it is essential that umpires, captains and scorers all know, before the toss, the timing \\nand duration of all intervals. The slight exception to this is in matches of more than one \\nday\'s duration, when drinks intervals on days other than the first day are decided not later \\nthan 10 minutes before the start of play on those days. \\n \\nDuration of intervals \\nThe umpires should make every effort to ensure that intervals are limited to the length of \\ntime agreed, measured from the call of Time to start the interval up to the call of Play for \\nresumption of play after the interval. There are many situations, discussed below, where the \\ntime for starting an interval is, or can be, changed. In no circumstances can the duration of \\nan agreed interval, if taken, be changed. \\nAlthough the timing of a declaration or forfeiture, or some other cause, may mean that the \\n10 minute interval between innings is not long enough for everything that needs to be done, \\nonce the 10 minutes has expired, the interval should be at an end (although it has not \\nactually finished until the next call of Play). The remaining time is playing time, which cannot \\nbe made up unless the delay is caused by the umpires investigating a possible refusal to play \\nor a Law 42 offence. \\n \\nInterval between innings \\nThe interval between innings is not a matter of agreement No flexibility is allowed. It has to \\nstart as an innings ends and must be 10 minutes long. Law 2.3.1.2 provides that in a one-day \\nmatch, no specific time for the tea interval needs to be agreed. Instead it may be taken at \\nthe close of the innings and the normal 10 minute interval between innings will then be \\ntaken as part of the tea interval, which does \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"not change in length. There are other occasions when the 10 minute interval, or part of it, is \\nto be included in another break in play. These are \\n(a)    an innings ending within 10 minutes of time for close of play. There will be no further \\nplay that day. Play will resume at the scheduled time on the following day - subject to \\nconditions being safe for play. \\n(b)   the batting captain declaring his/her side\'s innings closed or forfeits an innings \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 during an interruption of more than 10 minutes duration \\nIn this case the new innings will start no earlier than 10 minutes from the declaration or \\nforfeiture and as soon as umpires consider that conditions are no longer unreasonable or \\ndangerous. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 during any interval other than a drinks interval. In this case the new innings will start \\nat the time agreed for the end of the interval or 10 minutes from the declaration or \\nforfeiture - whichever is later. In both these cases, if there is less than 10 minutes of \\nthe interruption or interval remaining, the next innings will begin 10 minutes after \\nthe declaration or forfeiture. \\n \\nAlteration of time for play and intervals \\nThe hours of play are times for start of play and for close of play on each day. Start of play \\nmay have to be delayed if conditions are unreasonable or dangerous (see Law 2.8.2). There \\nare special provisions in Laws 16.3 and 16.9 for altering time for close of play. These \\nsituations apart, hours of play are not to be altered. \\nThe timing of lunch, tea and drinks intervals can be changed in one of two ways: first by \\nagreement between the captains and the umpires, and secondly in accordance with specific \\nprovisions of Laws 11.5 to 11.8. In no case, however, may the duration of lunch or tea \\nintervals be changed. \\nLunch and tea intervals \\n \\nLaw 11.4 sets out how the time of either the lunch interval or the tea interval may be \\nchanged. It requires that not only both captains but also both umpires agree to the \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'change. This prevents one captain exploiting a situation to disadvantage the other side - \\nalthough it is usually the umpires who will suggest the rescheduling of an interval to \\nmaximise the possible playing time, and then the captains who will agree to it. Umpires will \\nsee that the agreed change is not only sensible, but is possible for everyone, Including the \\ncatering staff at the ground, who will need to make such a change at short notice. \\nFor example, suppose lunch has been arranged for 40 minutes at 1-30 p.m. At 12-30p.m. \\nthere is heavy rain. which is obviously going to continue for some time. It would clearly \\nprevent a waste of playing time if lunch could be taken straight away, in the hope that by 1-\\n10p.m. conditions will have improved. It might, however, be Impractical to expect that at \\nshort notice lunch could be ready an hour before it was due - particularly in the recreational \\ngame. But it might be possible, in consultation with the catering staff, to have it brought \\nforward to say, 1 p.m. \\nChanging the time of the lunch interval \\nMany of the principles around changing the timing of an interval apply to both lunch and \\ntea, but there are also differences, so it is useful to take them separately. For lunch the \\ncritical time is 10 minutes before it is scheduled. Any stoppage, or the end of an innings at \\nthat time, or closer to the agreed time than that, will mean that lunch is to be taken at once. \\nThis is not subject to agreement, but must be complied with. The 10 minute interval \\nbetween innings will be absorbed into the lunch interval. \\nPlayers leaving the field, at the end of an innings for a stoppage, or for any other reason, \\nearlier than 10 minutes before the agreed time for lunch means that lunch is to be taken at \\nthe agreed time. In this case, however, agreement between captains and umpires to change \\nthis, as described above, is allowed. \\n \\nChanging the time of the tea interval \\n - end of innings and stoppages \\nIn every case the captains can agree to forgo the tea interval.  In every case the critical time \\nfor decisions about altering the timing of the tea interval is 30 minutes before tea is due A \\nstoppage may occur before this time. If the stoppage \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'is still in progress at the 30 minute deadline then an agreement between captains and \\numpires as detailed above is permitted. Otherwise tea will be taken at the agreed time. \\nIf an innings ends before this time, such an agreement is not permitted. Play will resume \\nafter the 10 minute interval, even if that interval is already in progress at the 30 minute \\ndeadline. Tea will be taken at the agreed time. \\nThe following two provisions apply to times at or after the 30 minute deadline. If at exactly \\n30 minutes before tea is due, or between then and the end of the over in progress at the \\ndue time \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 players have to leave the field for any reason other than the end of an innings - for \\nexample a stoppage for weather - tea will be taken immediately but captains and \\numpires are allowed to agree otherwise. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 an innings ends, the tea interval will be taken at once and will absorb the 10 minute \\ninterval between innings. Captains and umpires are not permitted to agree \\notherwise. \\n \\nIntervals when nine wickets are down \\nIf nine wickets are down when a session before lunch or tea is about to end, the interval is \\ndelayed to reduce the likelihood of two stoppages in a short period of time. This applies to \\nboth lunch and tea, and delays the interval if the ninth wicket is down, or falls, when there \\nare three minutes before the interval is scheduled. Play continues for another 30 minutes, \\nending at the end of the over in progress 30 minutes after the originally agreed time for the \\ninterval, unless the players have cause to leave the field, or the innings is completed, in \\nwhich case the interval will be taken immediately.  \\nNote that this section of Law applies only to 9 wickets actually falling. A batsman retiring \\ndoes not count as one of the 9 wickets. Moreover it must be 9 wickets. If the team is one \\nplayer short, it does not apply to 8 wickets falling. \\nNote that how early the 9th wicket falls is immaterial. If, for example, lunch is due at 1 p.m., \\nthe 9th wicket falling at 11.30 a.m. will qualify. It is only required that it should either be \\nalready down when 3 minutes remains, or fall before the completion of the over in progress \\nwhen the agreed time for lunch or tea is reached. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"So, for example, lunch is due at 1 p.m. Lunch will be deferred in the following cases \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the 9th wicket falls at 12 p.m. A new over starts at 12-58 and is completed at 1-01. \\nThe 10th wicket has still not fallen. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 a new over starts at 12-57. Only 8 wickets are down. The 9th wicket falls at 12-59 on \\nthe 4th ball of the over. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 a new over starts at 12-59. Only 8 wickets have fallen. At 1-02 the 9th wicket falls on \\nthe 5th ball of this over \\nPlay will continue for up to 30 minutes and lunch will then be taken, or will be taken when \\nthe innings ends - the fall of the 10th wicket is most likely but maybe the captain will \\ndeclare, or even the winning runs will be scored! Lunch would also have to be taken if the \\nplayers are forced off the field by a sudden thunderstorm or other incident. However, the \\ninterval is not to be postponed for ever. The deadline is not exactly 30 minutes after the \\noriginal time, but the end of the over then in progress. If in the examples above play is still \\nin progress at 1-30 and 2 balls of an over have been bowled, then the very latest that lunch \\ncan be taken is the end of that over. (Note that this also applies to the tea interval. So, \\nsimilarly, if tea were due to be taken at 3-40p.m. it would be deferred if the ninth wicket is \\ndown at that time, and would be taken when the 10th wicket falls or at the end of the over \\nin progress at 4-10p.m.) \\nIt is also worth repeating that all the above provisions are subject to the captains\' right to \\nagree to forgo the tea interval (see below). \\n \\nDrinks intervals \\nUnlike other intervals, which are decided before the match takes place, the decision \\nwhether or not to have drinks intervals must be determined by the umpires on each day of \\nthe match. If drinks breaks are to be taken: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The same arrangement must be made for both sides. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The drinks intervals should be as short as possible - 5 minutes is the maximum. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The break should be taken at the end of the over in progress when the time is \\nreached or the end of the relevant over unless a wicket falls or a batsman retires \\nwithin 5 minutes of that time when, to save playing time, it should be taken \\nimmediately. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If an innings ends or players are off the field within 30 minutes of the break, it is not \\ntaken. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 There should be no drinks intervals in the last hour of the match. \\nNote also that a drinks break can be cancelled only if the captains both wish to do so. The \\nbatsmen at the wicket are allowed to take the place of their captain in making this decision, \\nif the captain is not on the field of play. \\nUmpires should see that the time limits set out in Law 11.8.1 are observed to avoid \\nunnecessary delays. Note also that, if there is an interruption outside of the 30 minute limit, \\nthe rearrangement of a drinks interval can be made only by captains and umpires together. \\n \\nForgoing intervals \\nThe captains are permitted to agree between themselves to forgo the tea interval on any \\nday, or one or more of the agreed drinks intervals. This must be jointly agreed. If either \\ncaptain does not wish to cancel any particular interval, then that interval will take place. If \\nthe captain of the batting side is not on the field, his/her team-mates at the wicket can \\nmake the agreement about drinks on his/her behalf- though not about the tea interval. They \\nmust of course notify the umpires if they come to such an agreement. The umpires should \\nensure that the scorers also know. The captains are not permitted to forgo the lunch \\ninterval, even if they both wish to do so. \\nScorers \\nThe whole of Law 11 is about the possible alteration of timings relating to the match. It is \\nessential that at every stage the scorers are kept fully informed. \\n \\nField Technique \\nIn a number of the situations in this Law, the responsibility for the decision lies with the \\ncaptains. However, in practice it may be that the umpires will remind the captains that they \\ncan make such a decision. For example, a match may clearly be about to finish early, with \\nonly a few runs for the batting side to get with several wickets in hand and the last hour not \\nyet started. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'If a drinks interval had been arranged to be taken just before the start of the last hour the \\numpires might ask the captain of the fielding side and the batsmen at the wicket, if they still \\nwant to take the interval for drinks or not. If they both agree to play on without the break \\nthen the umpires simply do not call Time for that interval. If one or other team wants to \\ntake the drinks then the interval must be taken. \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"LAW 12 START OF PLAY; CESSATION OF PLAY \\n \\n12.1 Call of Play \\nThe bowler\'s end umpire shall call Play before the first ball of the match and on the \\nresumption of play after any interval or interruption. \\n12.2 Call of Time \\nThe bowler\'s end umpire shall call Time, when the ball is dead, at the end of any session of \\nplay or as required by the Laws. See also Law 20.3 (Call of Over or Time). \\n12.3 Removal of bails \\nAfter the call of Time, the bails shall be removed from both wickets. \\n12.4 Starting a new over \\nAnother over shall always be started at any time during the match, unless an interval is to \\nbe taken in the at normal pace, has arrived at the position behind the stumps at the \\nbowler\'s end before the time agreed for the next interval, or for the close of play, has been \\ncircumstances set out in 12.5.2. if the umpire, walking reached. \\n12.5 Completion of an over \\nOther than at the end of the match, \\n12.5.1  if the agreed time for an interval is reached during an over, the over shall be \\ncompleted before the interval is taken, except as provided for in 12.5.2. \\n \\n12.5.2    when less than 3 minutes remains before the time agreed for the next interval, the \\ninterval shall be taken immediately if \\neither a batsman is dismissed or retires \\nor the players have occasion to leave the field whether this occurs during an over or at the \\nend of an over. Except at the end of an innings, if an over is thus interrupted it shall be \\ncompleted on the resumption of play. \\n12.6 Last hour of match - number of overs \\nWhen one hour of Playing time of the match remains according to the agreed hours of play, \\nthe over in \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"progress shall be completed. The next over shall be the first of a minimum of 20 overs which \\nmust be bowled, provided that a result is not reached earlier art provided that there is no \\ninterval or interruption in play The bowler\'s end umpire shall indicate the commencement \\nof this 20 overs to the players and to the scorers. The period of play thereafter shall be \\nreferred to as the last hour, whatever its actual duration When the minimum number of \\novers have been bowled in the last hour, further overs can be started only if the original \\ntime for the close of play has not been reached See 12.7 and 12.8. \\n12.7 Last hour of match - interruptions of play \\nIf there is an interruption in play during the last hour of the match, the minimum number of \\novers to be bowled shall be reduced from 20 as follows:  \\n12.7.1   The time lost for an interruption is counted from the call of Time until the time for \\nresumption as decided by the umpires  \\n12.7.2    One over shall be deducted for every complete 3 minutes of time lost  \\n12.7.3    In the case of more than one such interruption, the minutes lost shall not be \\naggregated the calculation shall be made for each interruption separately.  \\n12.7.4     If, when one hour of Playing time remains, an interruption is already in progress  \\n  \\n12.7.4.1    only the time lost after this moment shall be counted in the calculation. \\n  \\n12.7.4.2    the over in progress at the start of the interruption shall be completed on \\nresumption and shall not count as one of the minimum number of overs to be \\nbowled  \\n12.7.5    If, after the start of the last hour, an interruption occurs during an over, the over \\nshall be completed on resumption of play. The two part-overs shall between them count as \\none over of the minimum number to be bowled. \\n \\n12.8 Last hour of match - intervals between innings  \\nIf an innings ends so that a new innings is to be started during the last hour of the match, \\nthe interval starts \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'with the end of the innings and is to end 10 minutes later. \\n \\n12.8.1   if this interval is already in progress at the start of the last hour then, to determine \\nthe number of overs to be bowled in the new innings, calculations are to be made as set out \\nin 12.7. \\n12.8.2   If the innings ends after the last hour has started, two calculations are to be made, \\nas set out in 12.8.3 and 12.8.4. The greater of the numbers yielded by these two calculations \\nis to be the minimum number of overs to be bowled in the new innings. \\n12.8.3   Calculation based on overs remaining: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 At the conclusion of the innings, the number of overs that remain to be bowled, of \\nthe minimum in the last hour, to be noted. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If this is not a whole number it is to be rounded up to the next whole number. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Three overs, for the interval, to be deducted from the resulting number to \\ndetermine the number of overs still to be bowled. \\n12.8.4    Calculation based on time remaining: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 At the conclusion of the innings, the time remaining until the agreed time for close \\nof play to be noted. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 10 minutes, for the interval, to be deducted from this time to determine the Playing \\ntime remaining. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 A calculation to be made of one over for every complete 3 minutes of the Playing \\ntime remaining, plus one more over if a further part of 3 minutes remains. \\n12.9 Conclusion of match \\n \\n12.9.1 The match is concluded \\n12.9.1.1   as soon as a result as defined in Laws 16.1 to 16.4 and 16.5.1 (The result) is \\nreached. \\n12.9.1.2 as soon as both \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the minimum number of overs for the last hour are completed \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 and the agreed time for close of play is reached, unless a result is reached earlier.  \\n12.9.1.3   in the case of an agreement under Law 13.1.2. as soon as the final innings is \\ncompleted as defined in Law 13.3.5. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"12.9.2   The match is concluded if, without a conclusion having been reached under 12.9.1, \\nthe players leave the fed for adverse conditions of ground, weather or right, or in \\nexceptional circumstances, and no further class possible. \\n12.10 Completion of last over of match  \\n  \\nThe over in progress at the close of play on the final day shall be completed unless \\n  \\nEither    a result has been reached \\n  \\nOr  \\n  \\nthe players have occasion to leave the fees In this case there shall be no resumption \\nof play except in the circumstances of Law 9 (Mistakes in scoring) and the match \\nshall be an end. \\n12.11 Bowler unable to complete an over during last hour of match \\n  \\nIf, for any reason, a bowler is unable to complete an over during the last hour, Law \\n17.8 (Bowler incapacitated or suspended during an over) shall apply The separate \\nparts of such an over shall count as one over of the minimum to be bowled. \\nThe call of Play begins a session of play. The call of Time ends one. These calls have \\nconsiderable significance for the game. For example, there are restrictions, set out in Law 26 \\n(Practice on the field), imposed on players once Play has been called. Although many times \\nduring the course of play the ball becomes Dead, or is called Dead, these do not have the \\nsame finality as the call of Time. Once Time has been called, no incident can have any \\nsignificance for the match apart from some specifically stated exceptions which apply \'at \\nany time during the match. The significance of a call of Time is discussed further in Law 31 \\n(Appeals). Note however the implication of Law 16.9 (Mistakes in scoring) which might \\nmean that play has to resume even though the players had left the field. \\n \\nCall of Play \\nThe first call of Play is not the start of the match - that is the toss. However, the first call of \\nPlay does start the first session of the match, signifying the start of Playing time \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"and the batting innings and the innings of both opening batsmen) and instructs the bowler \\nto bowl the first ball of the match. \\nA call of Play must be made by the bowler\'s end umpire to restart play whenever it has been \\nstopped, for whatever reason, whether an interruption or an interval. An interruption is an \\nunscheduled break in play - usually but not always for conditions of ground, weather or \\nlight. It could, for example, be caused by the umpires having to investigate a delay in the \\nappearance of a new batsman after the fall of a wicket. The definition of an interval is set \\nout in Law 11. Inexperienced umpires sometimes forget that taking drinks is just as much an \\ninterval as the break for lunch. None must be forgotten. \\nThe exact time of the call of Play has implications for the timing of the start of a batsman\'s \\ninnings and must therefore be carefully noted. See Law 25.2 (Commencement of a \\nbatsman\'s innings). \\n \\nCall of Time \\nThis is also the responsibility of the bowler\'s end umpire. Just as Play is called to start any \\nsession and restart after any break in play, so Time is called to end play at the end of any \\nsession of play or as required by the Laws, and at the end of the match. The call of Time \\nshould be made once the ball is Dead - it is not used to make the ball Dead. \'Any break\' \\nincludes interruptions as well as intervals. It is just as important to call Time when everyone \\nis running off the field for a sudden heavy downpour as it is when the break is for lunch or at \\nthe end of an innings. Again, the umpire must not forget to call Time before a drinks interval \\nas well as at other intervals. After Time is called, the bails are to be removed. This is a \\nsymbolic act; it has no significance for the game - indeed, it is often ignored at drinks \\nintervals, although in Law it is clear that the bails should be removed at that point, just as at \\nany other interval. There is nothing wrong with a friendly wicket-keeper bringing them to \\nthe umpire at that end if going to the pavilion takes the player in that direction, as long they \\nwere not removed until Time had been called. The appropriate umpire should also take \\npossession of the match ball (see Field Technique - call of Time). \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Field technique - call of Play \\nWhen taking the field at the start of the first session of the match, the umpires will check \\nthat the playing area is cleared of spectators, practicing players and any other obstructions. \\nThey should walk out a few minutes before the time agreed for play to start, giving \\nthemselves and the players enough time to get in position, and complete all their final \\nchecks, before the agreed time. On some grounds there is a bell which can be rung to alert \\nplayers. If there is no \'5-minute bell\', it will be necessary to make sure in some other way \\nthat the players know that the umpires are about to go out to the pitch. The experienced \\numpire will always make sure both teams are aware that they are on their way out to the \\nmiddle. \\nThe umpires will agree at which ends they will stand, check the alignment of the stumps \\nagain and place the bails in position as the players follow them out.  \\nThere have been several incidents in recent seasons where there has been a \'stalemate\' at \\nthe start of an innings, with the fielding team claiming that they wanted to know which \\nbatsman was on strike before deciding which bowler would bowl the first over, while the \\nbatting side claimed they wanted to know who was bowling before deciding which of them \\nwould take strike. To solve this there is a protocol that is now observed: \\n1. The umpires take to the field first, and choose the end from which they each will officiate. \\n2. The fielding side take to the field next and inform the umpires from which end they will \\nbe bowling the first over and who that bowler would be. \\n3. The batsmen take to the field last and with the above information decide who is going to \\nface the first ball. \\nThe umpire at the end from which the first over will be bowled must then take the following \\nactions: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 find out the bowler\'s mode of delivery - i.e. right handed or left handed, from over or \\nround the wicket. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 notify the bowler\'s action and give guard to the striker. The giving of a guard is often \\ntaken for granted, but umpires must make sure they know exactly what a batsman is \\nasking for. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"The terminology is not identical around the world, but an umpire should make sure they \\nknow what is meant when a batsman asks for each particular guard. The most common \\nguards are middle stump, leg stump (or \'one leg). or \'two legs\' (which is the midpoint \\nbetween the middle and leg stumps and is also known as \'middle and leg\'). Some batsmen \\nwill hold their bat side on for \'two\', some will hold it facing the umpire. That is the \\nbatsman\'s choice, and it is not for the umpire to question them, merely to advise them \\nwhere the bat is. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Give a marker, if necessary, to the bowler. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 give the match ball to the bowler. \\nIf the sight screen needs to be moved, it may be helpful for the umpire to assist by standing \\nwith the appropriate arm upraised in the approximate place which the ball will be delivered. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The umpire must also check that: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 there are no more than 11 fielders on the field  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the fielding captain has finished setting the field \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the striker\'s end umpire is ready \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the scorers are in position and ready \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 both batsmen are ready \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the striker\'s end umpire agrees the time for start of play has been reached. \\nThe experienced umpire at the striker\'s end will be looking for the enquiry that he/she is \\nready, having checked that the field setting does not involve more than two leg side fielders \\nbehind the popping crease. See Law 28. \\nThe listing of these items does not imply that they have to be in that order, though it will be \\nseen that there is a natural logic in it. For example, the point at which to hand the match \\nball to the bowler can be varied to suit the circumstances. However, the check that the \\nplayers and fellow officials are ready must come last. Many umpires will want to check once \\nagain that the striker is ready immediately before calling Play. \\nWhen the checks are completed and the umpires agree that the scheduled time for start of \\nplay has been reached, the bowler\'s end umpire will call Play. The call must be made clearly \\nso that players of both sides are aware that the innings has started and play is about to \\nbegin. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b\'The call of "Play" should be made with confidence - particularly in the first instance in a \\nmatch. On most occasions, it will be the first time the players have heard the umpire speak, \\nso the call should convey the message, i know what I am doing, and I am ready for \\nanything". \\nThe exact time that Play is called should be noted by bot umpires, so that they have an \\naccurate record of what is playing time and what is not. \\nAll these checks are important. Probably the two most often neglected by inexperienced \\numpires are checking that the other umpire is ready and checking that the scorers are \\nready. This can, obviously, lead to major mistakes. A frequently used signal for this check \\nwith the scorers, though not official, is for the umpire to raise one arm high above the head, \\nas if to signal a Bye if the game were in progress. An acknowledgement from the score box \\nthen demonstrates that the scorers are ready. \\nMost of this routine will be necessary at each call of Play There will be small exceptions. For \\nexample after an interval for drinks the umpires and players will already be on the field of \\nplay and there will be no set time for play to resume. It will begin as soon as the checks \\nreveal that everyone is ready. The umpire must do what is appropriate to each situation. \\n \\nField technique - call of Time  \\n1. Before an interval \\nTime is not to be called until the ball is dead. Unless the reason for the call is the end of an \\ninnings, it is necessary for the umpires to check that the moment for the call has arrived. It is \\nclearly inappropriate to check while the ball is in play. It must be done, slightly ahead of \\ntime, when the ball is dead. When an agreed interval is approaching possible checks, which \\ncan be made unobtrusively, are:  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 at the start of an over that the umpires agree there will not be time for more than \\nthis over \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 during an over, while the ball is dead between deliveries, that the 3 minute deadline \\ndiscussed below has been reached \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 if necessary, a final \\\'fail-safe\\\' check, by a small mime of lifting the bails before \\nactually doing so. \\n \\n \\n\''b"b'2. For any break \\nFor a break not at the end of an innings, if play resumes after the interval, it must do so with \\nthe game in exactly the same state as it was when the break began. This is easy to forget if \\nthe interruption is unexpected - a sudden shower of rain - or only likely to be brief - for \\ninstance in order to administer a Law 42 sanction. It is important that umpires make sure \\nthey follow the procedure every time. Umpires should note, agree with each other and \\nwrite down:  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 at which end the bowling will resume; the umpire at that end should take possession \\nof the ball  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 who was bowling and at which ends  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 at which end each batsman (or the not out batsman, as the case may be) should be  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 how many balls remain in the over, if relevant  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the exact time at which the break began. For an agreed interval this will determine \\nwhen it is to end. For an interruption it will be needed to determine how much \\nplaying time is lost. \\nExcept for drinks, at any interval as opposed to an interruption, the umpires are to check \\nwith the scorers that they agree the number of runs scored, the number of wickets that \\nhave fallen and, if relevant, the number of overs bowled. It is therefore prudent for umpires \\nto have checked with each other first. \\nIf the break is an interruption after consultation about the conditions there will be plenty of \\ntime to make these notes. If it is a wild dash, grabbing the bails on the way, because of a \\nsudden cloudburst, the notes can be made when the shelter of the pavilion is reached. \\n \\nCompleting the over in progress  \\n1. During the match \\nThe current over will always be finished unless an innings ends during the course of it. Law \\n12.5 sets out when an over is to be completed before starting an interval and when it will \\nnot be completed until after the interval. If a batsman is out or retires when an interval is \\nnearly due, the umpire should check, and then double check with his/her colleague, \\nwhether the time remaining until the agreed time for the interval is 3 minutes or more, or \\nwhether it is less than 3 minutes. If there is less than 3 minutes remaining (i.e. 2 mins and 59 \\nsecs or less, before the agreed time for \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"an interval) the umpire will call Time and remove the balls (unless it is the ninth wicket, \\nwhen play will continue as discussed in Law 11). The interval will be taken. This is ta happen, \\neven if the wicket falls on the last ball of an over and the other umpire could have been in \\nplace in time for another over to start. Otherwise play will continue. Of course, if an innings \\nends or the players have occasion to leave the field within the 3 minutes, there is no option \\nbutts take either the 10 minute interval, or lunch or tea, or dost of play, as appropriate. \\n \\n2. At the end of the match \\nHere there is no option - the over cannot be completed. If a result is reached, the match is \\nat an end, unless the belle that a result has been reached turns out to be a mistaken one. \\nThat situation is discussed in Law 16. \\nAside from that rare possibility, if the players have to leave the field in mid over, it may \\nalready be clear that this was to be the last over. If that is not the case, then the los of time \\nduring the interruption may mean that it becomes the last over. In either of these two \\nsituations, play will not resume, and this over will be left unfinished: \\nIn all other situations, the over in progress at the end of a match will be completed, in the \\nabsence of a result, even if this means a new batsman coming in after time has been \\nreached. \\nNote also that if the bowler bowling the last over of the match is unable to complete it \\nbecause he/she is incapacitated or suspended then another bowler must still complete this \\nover as required by Law 12.11. \\n \\nStarting a new over \\n1. During the course of play - not at the fall of a wicket  \\nWhen it is getting near the time for an interval at the end of a session, the umpires will \\ncheck with each other how much time is remaining. If, at the end of an over the time for the \\ninterval has already been reached, Time will be called, If, however, there is even a short \\ntime remaining the umpires will walk to their positions for the next over. This is to be done \\nat their normal pace! It follows that a \'normal pace must have been established during the \\ncourse of the match. When the umpire who was at the striker\'s end arrives at his/her new \\nposition behind the stumps, he/she should \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'check the time again. If time has still not been reached, even by a small margin, a new over \\nwill be started. There might be a delay in starting it. For example the captain may decide to \\nchange the field setting. The striker may be facing his/her first ball at that end and need to \\ntake a guard. That makes no difference, neither would fielders rushing (or, moving \\ndeliberately slowly) to get to their new positions. The only thing that matters is whether or \\nnot time has been reached when the umpire arrives at his/her position behind the stumps. \\nThe situation at the fall of a wicket has been dealt with under\\xe2\\x80\\x99 completing the over in \\nprogress\\xe2\\x80\\x99. \\n \\n2. At the end of the match \\nIf the length of the final innings is limited to a number of overs, a new over will always be \\nstarted until all these overs are completed, unless a result is reached earlier, or conditions \\nmake play impossible. \\nEven if the match (not the innings) is one limited by time, during the last hour the number of \\novers becomes the first deciding factor. Unless the match has come to an end for any other \\nreason, the minimum number of overs in the last hour must be bowled, and no question of \\ntime for starting another over or not can arise. Once that minimum number of overs is \\nachieved, if there is still time remaining, then exactly the same routine will be applied as \\nthat described in 1. The first time check will be at the end of the last over of the minimum \\nnumber required. It will be rare for there to be any time remaining at this point, but it could \\nhappen. Note, however, that the fall of a wicket, either during the course of an over, or at \\nthe end of an over, will not alter the decision as to whether another over is to be bowled. If, \\nwhen the umpire arrives at his/her position behind the stumps, the agreed time for close of \\nplay has not been reached, in the absence of a result, another over will be started. \\n \\nThe last hour of the match \\nLaws 12.6, 12.7 and 12.8 deal with this very important feature of the closing stages of a \\nmatch (not an innings) limited by time - the last hour. The last hour begins, not one hour \\nbefore the agreed time for close of play, but at the start of the next over after that moment. \\nOnce the last hour \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"has begun, a minimum of 20 overs is to be bowled, unless the match ends sooner, or time is \\nlost for an interruption or interval. Often it takes significantly longer than one hour to \\ncomplete the overs. Sometimes more than 20 overs are bowled before time is reached. The \\nlatter is rare, but not impossible - especially in competitions that use a lower number than \\n20 for their last hour calculations. However, the Law requires 20, and specifies that, even if \\nthey take an hour and a half to complete, or if 23 overs are achieved in the hour, this final \\nperiod of play is to be known as the last hour. \\nUmpires and scorers need to be clear that the match will not be concluded, unless a result is \\nreached sooner, until the minimum number of overs has been bowled and time for close of \\nplay has been reached. \\nSuppose close of play has been agreed for 6-30p.m. An over is in progress at 5-30p.m., so \\nthe last hour does not begin until 5-32p.m. and there is no interruption or interval after that \\npoint. This does not change the time for close of play. It is still 6-30p.m. If 20 overs have \\nbeen completed by 6-23p.m., then play will continue until the end of the over in progress at \\n6-30p.m. (not 6.32p.m.). If, on the other hand, at 6-30p.m. only 17.4 overs of the minimum \\n20 have been bowled, then, assuming the match is not concluded earlier play will continue \\nuntil the full 20 have been bowled. \\nThat it is time for the last hour to begin should be agreed between the umpires exactly as \\nfor the call of Time before an interval. The umpire who is at the bowler\'s end for the first of \\nthe 20 overs will announce it to the players and signal to the scorers. The scorers will record \\nthe passage of the overs, but the umpires must themselves both keep a check. \\n \\nLoss of time in the last hour \\n \\nIf playing time is reduced, either by interruptions to play or by an interval between innings, \\nthere will be a reduction in the minimum number of overs to be bowled. The principles \\nwhich govern the calculations are set out in detail in the Law and illustrated in the four \\nexamples on pages 124-127. Two of those principles may need emphasising. \\nIt is difficult, sometimes impossible, for umpires to do calculations in their head at the \\nmoment of calling Play. An interval between innings is always to be counted as \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'10 minutes. This will determine in advance the time for resumption. In the case of an \\ninterruption, the umpires must decide the time for resumption before going back on to the \\nfield, so that they can make a calculation before going out, agree it with the scorers and tell \\nthe captains. In deciding this time, they will take account of how long it will take to remove \\nany covers, as well as the usual time taken for all to be ready for the call of Play. After either \\nan interval or an interruption, if for any reason - adjustments to the sight-screen perhaps - \\nthe start of play varies slightly from the time that had been decided, no change to the \\nearlier calculation is to be made. If, of course, the start after resumption is held up because \\nof the conditions, then the loss of time counts as a continuation of the interruption and a \\ncalculation has to be made accordingly. \\nThe second principle to note especially is that starting a new innings during the last hour \\ndoes not of itself change the method of calculation. It is only when an innings ends during \\nthe last hour that the two calculations are to be made. \\nThe exact recording of times at each stage is essential. To minimise the possibility of \\nmistakes, each umpire makes a calculation and agrees it with his/her colleague. \\nThe following examples illustrate the points, though some may not be very realistic. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'Example 1 \\n1. Close of play is agreed for 6:30. \\n2. The first over of the minimum 20 begins at 52 3. At 5:39, 2.2 overs (2 overs and 2 balls) \\nhave been bowled \\n4. There is then an interruption of 5 minutes for rain. \\n5. Play is resumed at 5:44 \\n1 \\n5:30 \\n \\nOne hour remains \\n \\n \\n2 \\n5:32 \\n \\nLast hour starts \\n \\nMinimum of 20 hours remain \\n3 \\n5:39 \\n22 \\novers have been bowled \\n \\nMinimum of 17.4 overs remain \\n4 \\n \\n \\nInterruption of 5 minutes \\nLose 1 \\nover \\n \\n5 \\n5:44 \\n \\nPlay resumes \\n \\nMinimum of 16.4 Overs \\nremain \\n \\n \\n0.4 \\nover bowled to complete \\nunfinished over \\n \\nMinimum of 16 Overs remain \\n \\nNotice that \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 although the last hour does not start until 5:32p.m. this has no effect on any \\ncalculation \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 in column 2, the 2.2 overs and 0.4 overs together make 3 overs achieved, partly \\nbefore the interruption, the rest by completing the unfinished over after the \\ninterruption \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 in column 4 only 1 over is lost. The 5 minutes lost is only one complete period of 3 \\nminutes. The remaining 2 minutes are ignored \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the achieving of 3 overs shown in column 2 and the loss of 1 over shown in column 4 \\ntogether make overs, which tallies with the final statement that 16 overs must still \\nbe bowled. \\n'"b"b'Example 2 \\nExactly the same as above, but now with a second interruption \\n1. Close of play is agreed for 6:30. \\n2. The first over of the minimum 20 begins at 5:32. \\n3. At 5:39, 2.2 overs (2 overs and 2 balls) have been bowled. \\n4. There is then a interruption of 5 minutes for rain. \\n5. Play is resumed at 5:44. The unfinished over is completed and a further 5.1 overs are \\nbowled \\n6. There is then a second interruption of 10 minutes. \\n \\n \\nIn column 2, a total of 9 overs are shown as bowled (2.2 + 0.4 = 3; 5.1 + 0.5 = 6; total 9). In \\ncolumn 4, 4 overs (1, then 3) have been lost. These all total 13 overs. The minimum number \\nremaining out of 20 is thus 7. Note that the two interruptions are not aggregated to make \\n15 minutes. They are treated separately. \\n'"b"b'Example 3 \\nAn interruption is already in progress when the last hour is reached \\n1. Close of play is agreed for 6:30. \\n2. At 5:10 rain holds up play, with 2 balls of an over still to be bowled \\n3. Play resumes at 5:38 \\n \\n \\nThe irregular numbering on the left is intentional. It corresponds with events which are \\ndescribed in a logical sequence but are considered in time sequence. Notice here that  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 although the interruption lasted 28 minutes, only 8 minutes came after 5:30, in the \\nlast hour \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the over in progress at the interruption is still the over in progress when 1 hour of \\nplaying time remains. The last hour does not start until the next over after it. \\nTherefore although this over must be completed on resumption, it does not form \\npart of the minimum 20 overs. \\n'"b"b'Example 4 \\n \\nAn innings ends and a new innings is started during the last hour \\n1. The Close of Play is scheduled for 6:30 and the last hour starts at 5:33.  \\n2. At 5:45 the batting side is all out, after 3.4 overs of the last 20 have been bowled. The \\ncalculation is for the number of overs for the next innings \\n \\nThe larger number is 14. A minimum of 14 overs to be allocated for new innings. \\nNotice that \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the time calculation is needed only because a new innings ended in the last hour \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 if the 16.2 overs remaining at the end of the innings had been exactly 16, it would \\nnot have been rounded up \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 in the time calculation, 35 minutes is 33 + 2 minutes - i.e. 11 overs and 2 minutes. In \\ncontrast to the overs calculation an over is added on for the surplus 2 minutes. \\n'"b'b"Conclusion of match \\nUmpires need to be clear about when a match - as opposed to any one particular innings - is \\nover. The criteria are se out in Law 12.9. There are some situations where it will be clear \\nwhen the match has come to an end - such as a side batting last reaching their target - but \\nnot all are as clear. If a result is not reached in a match limited solely by hours of play, the \\ncriteria for determining whether the match is concluded are discussed above under \'Last \\nhour of match. If, however, the match is one where it has been agreed to limit some or all \\ninnings to a number of overs, or to a specified length of time, deciding that the match is \\nconcluded will involve some of the terms of that agreement Umpires should ensure that \\nthey know what those terms are before the start of the match. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 13 INNINGS \\n13.1 Number of innings \\n13.1.1 A match shall be one or two innings for each side according to agreement reached \\nbefore the match. \\n13.1.2 It may be agreed to limit any innings to a number of overs or to a period of time. If \\nsuch an agreement is made then  \\n13.1.2.1 in a one-innings match a similar agreement shall apply to both innings. \\n13.1.2.2 in a two-innings match similar agreements shall apply: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 to the first innings of each side \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 or to the second innings of each side \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 or to both innings of each side. \\nFor both one-innings and two-innings matches, the agreement must also include criteria for \\ndetermining the result when neither of Laws 16.1 (A Win - two innings match) or 16.2 (A \\nWin - one-innings match) applies. \\n13.2 Alternate innings \\nIn a two-innings match each side shall take their innings alternately except in the cases \\nprovided for in Law 14 The follow-on) or in Law 15.2 (Forfeiture of an innings). \\n13.3 Completed innings \\nA side\'s innings is to be considered as completed if any of the following applies: \\n \\n13.3.1  the side is all out. \\n13.3.2  at the fall of a wicket or the retirement of a batsman further balls remain to be \\nbowled but no further batsman is available to come in. \\n13.3.3 the captain declares the innings closed. \\n13.3.4 the captain forfeits the innings. \\n13.3.5  in the case of an agreement under 13.1.2,  \\neither      the prescribed number of overs has been bowled \\nor            the prescribed time has expired as appropriate \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'13.4 The toss \\n  \\nThe captains shall toss a coin for the choice of innings on the field of play and in the \\npresence of one or both of the umpires, not earlier than 30 minutes, nor later than \\n15 minutes before the scheduled or any rescheduled time for the start of play. Note, \\nhowever the provisions of Law 1.3 (Captain). \\n \\n13.5 Decision to be notified \\n  \\nAs soon as the toss is completed, the captain of the side winning the toss shall \\ndecide whether to bat or to field and shall notify the opposing captain and the \\numpires of this decision. Once notified, the decision cannot be changed. \\n \\nNumber and limitation of innings \\nWhether a match is played over one or two innings for each side will normally be either \\ndetermined by the Playing Conditions governing the match, or will have been agreed well in \\nadvance of the start of the match. It is clearly essential that all concerned have this \\ninformation. The Playing Conditions or agreement may include a limitation on the time or \\nthe overs allowed for any, or all of the innings. The Law states that similar arrangements \\nmust apply to both sides. \\nThe umpires must ensure, before the toss is made, that both captains and they themselves \\nare aware of any limitations that have been laid down, or agreed, together with all the \\npossible consequences. It should not be assumed that everyone has studied the Playing \\nConditions as closely as the umpires will have done, so they must make sure both captains \\nknow exactly what is to happen. \\n \\nAlternate innings \\nIf a match is of two innings for each side, the only two situations in which innings are not to \\nalternate are those of a follow on or a forfeiture. These are discussed in Laws 14 and 15 \\nrespectively, If an innings is forfeited in a two innings match, for the purpose of umpires \\nchanging ends it is considered to have taken place. \\n \\nCompleted innings \\nThe outcome of a match may depend on whether an innings is completed or not. There are \\nother occasions when it is important to know if an innings has been completed. Law 13.3 \\n'"b'b"sets out clearly what is required for an innings to be considered completed. Apart from the \\nside being all out, the situation of there being no further batsmen to come in although \\nfurther balls remain to be bowled, will occur if a batsman has retired at some point in the \\ninnings and either is unable to resume his/her innings, or is not permitted to do so by the \\nopposing captain. The same would be true if the last batsman, having been injured earlier, \\nwas unable to come in at the fall of the ninth wicket. \\nA batsman who has retired hurt will be assumed to be able to return if needed, until the \\nmoment they are required and are unable to. For example, if a team who have had a \\nbatsman retire hurt are eight wickets down and the ninth wicket falls, that team\'s innings is \\nnot completed until the retired batsman is unable to return. If the ninth wicket falls on the \\npenultimate delivery of the match, and the injured batsman cannot come out to bat, then \\nthe innings is completed. However, if the ninth wicket falls on the final delivery of the match \\nthen the injured batsman might have been able to return. Whether they would or would not \\nhave done so is relevant - even if, in extreme cases, they are absent from the ground. The \\ninnings is not completed. \\nIf a batsman is suspended under a Level 3 offence of Law 42, and has not served his/her full \\nsuspension when the ninth wicket falls, the innings is also complete, with the batsman being \\nrecorded as Retired - Not out (see Law 42.4.2.3.5). Similarly if a player has been removed \\nfrom the field for Level 4 offence, their innings is recorded as Retired Out either at the time \\nif the side is batting or at the start of the next innings if they are suspended while fielding \\n(See Law 42.5.3.2.1) \\n \\nThe toss \\nIt is usual for the captains to make the toss for choice of innings on or near the pitch to be \\nused. The Law only requires that it is on the field of play. It is, however, necessary for one of \\nthe umpires to be present at the toss, to see that it is fairly conducted. It is not necessary for \\nboth umpires to be present but this tends to be the common practice. \\nThe Law lays down time limitations for the toss - not earlier than 30 minutes, nor later than \\n15 minutes before the time set for play to start. Umpires must insist that the toss is made \\nwithin these time limits so as to enable a prompt start for the match. \\nIf it has been decided that, for any reason, usually bad weather, it is necessary to delay the \\noriginally agreed time for start of play then, unless the toss has already taken place, the \\ntimings will apply to the new start time. \\nIf either captain is not available when the toss is to be made, the umpires must call upon \\nthat side to appoint a deputy to do this. This deputy must be a nominated player. Although \\n"'b'b"the nomination of players can be made by anyone associated with the team, only a \\nnominated player can deputise at the toss. \\nThe captain who wins the toss will, virtually immediately, have to declare to the other \\ncaptain and the umpire his/ her choice of batting first or fielding first. Once he/she has \\nstated this choice, there are no circumstances in which it can be changed - even if the player \\nconducting the toss is a deputy for the captain, or if there is then a long delay before the \\nstart of play which changes the length of the match. \\n \\nField technique \\nThe same points of technique apply to the toss as to the nomination of teams, as discussed \\nunder Law 1. If the umpires have taken early steps to ensure the nomination is done in time, \\nthen, if required, a deputy will also be ready to make the toss. It may well be necessary to \\nremind captains that the time for the toss is approaching. Team sheets should be exchanged \\nbefore the toss is made. In the case of a delayed start, it may also be necessary to tell the \\ncaptains that a decision to bat or to field cannot be changed, even if it was made some \\nhours before the rescheduled start time. For this very reason umpires should encourage \\ncaptains, where events allow, to delay the toss until 30-15 minutes before the rescheduled \\ntime for the start of play.  \\nThe practicalities of performing the toss are relatively straightforward. Convention states \\nthat the home captain tosses the coin and the away captain calls, but the umpire may toss \\nand simply ask one captain to call if that is preferable. It may be useful to instruct the \\ncaptain who Is calling to call \'heads or tails\' loudly, so as to avoid any confusion. Once the \\ntoss is made, the scorers must be informed of the outcome. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 14 THE FOLLOW-ON \\n14.1 Lead on first innings \\n14.1.1  In a two-innings match of 5 days or more, the side which bats first and leads by at \\nleast 200 runs shall have the option of requiring the other side to follow their innings. \\n \\n14.1.2  The same option shall be available in two-innings matches of shorter duration with \\nthe minimum leads as follows: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 150 runs in a match of 3 or 4 days; \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 100 runs in a 2-day match: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 75 runs in a 1-day match. \\n14.2 Notification \\nA captain shall notify the opposing captain and the umpires of his/her intention to take up \\nthis option Once notified, the decision cannot be changed. \\n14.3 First day\'s play lost \\nIf no play takes place on the first day of a match of more than one day\'s duration, 14.1 shall \\napply in accordance with the number of days remaining from the start of play. The day on \\nwhich play first commences shall count as a whole day for this purpose. irrespective of the \\ntime at which play starts. Play will have taken place as soon as, after the call of Play, the first \\nover has started. See Law 17.2 (Start of an over). \\n \\nEnforcing the follow-on \\nA follow-on can happen only in matches of two innings for each side. It occurs when each \\nside has completed one innings. It requires the side batting first to have scored more runs \\nthan the side batting second, by at least the number stated in 14.1.1 and 14.1.2. In this \\nsituation, the captain of the side batting first may ask the opposing side to \'follow their \\ninnings\' - that is to bat again immediately. The captain does not have to use this power. If \\nhe/she does use it, the Law states that the captain must notify the opposing captain and the \\numpires of the intention to do so. Usually, everyone will be aware that the difference in \\nscores is large enough to make a follow-on possible, so the captain will either notify that the \\nfollow-on is being enforced, or that it is not. \\n \\n"'b"b'The scorers will need to be informed as to whether there is to be a follow-on or not. As with \\nthe Toss (see Law 13), once a decision is made to enforce the follow-on, the captain may not \\nchange that decision. \\n \\nChanging the duration of the match \\nThe lead required for a follow-on to be possible depends on the number of days the match \\nis to last. This number may be affected by loss of playing time. \\nThe moment that play is deemed to have taken place will determine the duration of the \\nmatch. Play will have taken place as soon as the bowler starts his/her run up or, if he/she \\nhas no run up, his/her bowling action for the first delivery of the match. Once play has \\nstarted, that day counts as a whole day, however much playing time there is. The length of \\nthe match for the calculation of the follow on is based on that start time, even if later days \\nare lost. A 5-day match in which play first takes place after lunch on the third day, counts as \\na 3-day match, even if the fourth day is washed out. If, according to the definition above, \\nplay takes place on the first day, the match will be considered as remaining at its originally \\nscheduled length, however many days play are actually achieved. That means that, if a \\nbowler begins running in to bowl on the first morning, but rain falls before the first ball is \\nbowled, the match is still deemed to have started on the first day, even if there is no ball \\nactually delivered until the scheduled third day, and it should be treated as a match of the \\noriginally decided length. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"LAW 15 DECLARATION AND FORFEITURE \\n15.1 Time of declaration \\nThe captain of the side batting may declare an innings closed, when the ball is dead, at any \\ntime during the innings. A declared innings shall be considered to be a completed innings. \\n15.2 Forfeiture of an innings \\nA captain may forfeit either of his/her side\'s innings at any time before the commencement \\nof that innings, A forfeited innings shall be considered to be a completed innings. \\n15.3 Notification \\nA captain shall notify the opposing captain and the umpires of any decision to declare or to \\nforfeit an innings. Once notified, the decision cannot be changed. \\nDeclaration \\nThe captain of the batting side may declare his/her side\'s innings closed at any time during \\nthe innings, whether play is in progress or not. If play is in progress, the ball must be dead. \\nThere are competitions in which Playing Conditions may set restrictions on declaring, or \\neven forbid it in some circumstances. \\nAs soon as a captain takes the decision to declare, and notifies both the other captain and \\nthe umpires, he/she may not change this decision. \\n \\nForfeiture \\nIn a two-innings match either captain may forfeit - that is, choose not to use - either of \\nhis/her side\'s innings at any point before the start of that innings. It may be that some \\nplaying time has been lost to rain and both captains agree not to use one innings, effectively \\nreducing the game to a one-innings match. The captain of the side batting first may decide \\nto risk forfeiting the second innings because his/ her side have not achieved a sufficient lead \\nto enforce a follow-on but the captain believes that it may be possible to dismiss their \\nopponents for fewer runs than the lead that has been established. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'As soon as a captain takes the decision to forfeit, and notifies both the other captain and \\nthe umpires, he/she may not change this decision.  \\nBoth an innings declared closed and a forfeited innings are to be regarded as completed \\ninnings. \\n \\nField technique \\nWhen a captain declares an innings closed, or forfeits an innings, umpires should ensure \\nthat the opposing captain and the scorers are informed. It is essential that the groundsman \\nis told as soon as possible, so that there is minimum delay in rolling the pitch, if the captain \\nrequests this, and re-marking the creases. There will be a ten minute break between the \\ninnings, as outlined in Law 11.2.2, and the rolling must take place in this time. Where a \\nDeclaration or Forfeiture is notified during an interval or interruption, but with less than 10 \\nminutes of that stoppage remaining the next innings will begin 10 minutes after the \\nnotification. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'LAW 16 THE RESULT \\n16.1 A Win - two-innings match \\nThe side which has scored a total of runs in excess of that scored in the two completed \\ninnings of the opposing side shall win the match. See Law 13.3 (Completed innings). Note \\nalso 16.6. \\n \\n16.2 A Win - one-innings match \\nThe side which has scored in its one innings a total of runs in excess of that scored by the \\nopposing side in its one completed innings shall win the match. See Law 13.3 (Completed \\ninnings). Note also 16.6. \\n \\n16.3 Umpires awarding a match \\nRegardless of any agreement under Law 13.1.2 (Number of innings), \\n16.3.1   a match shall be lost by a side which \\n  \\n16.3.1.1     concedes defeat \\n  \\n16.3.1.2   in the opinion of the umpires refuses to play If so, the umpires shall award \\nthe match to the other side. \\n16.3.2 if an umpire considers that an action by any player or players might constitute a \\nrefusal by either side to play then the umpires together shall ascertain the cause of the \\naction. If they then decide together that this action does constitute a refusal to play by one \\nside, they shall so inform the captain of that side. If the captain persists in the action the \\numpires shall award the match in accordance with 16.3.1.
