See also Law 42.6.1 (Captain \\nrefusing to remove a player from the field).  \\n16.3.3 if action as in 16.3.2 takes place after play has started and does not constitute a \\nrefusal to play. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Playing time shall be counted as lost from the call of Time to the call of Play, \\nexcluding intervals (Law 11) and suspensions of play (Law 2.8). \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the time for close of play on that day shall be extended by this length of time. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 if applicable, no overs shall be deducted during the last hour of the match solely on \\naccount of this time. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"16.4 Matches in which there is an agreement under Law 13.1.2 \\nFor any match in which there is an agreement under Law 13.1.2 (Number of innings), if the \\nresult is not determined in any of the ways stated in 16.1, 16.2 or 163, then the result shall \\nbe as laid down in that agreement. \\n \\n16.5 All other matches - A Tie or Draw \\n16.5.1   A Tie \\n  \\nThe result of a match shall be a Tie when all innings have been completed and the \\nscores are equal. \\n16.5.2  A Draw \\n  \\nThe result of a match shall be a Draw when it is not determined in any of the ways \\nstated in 16.1. 16.2 163 16.4 or 16.5.1. \\n \\n16.6 Winning hit or extras \\n16.6.1   As soon as a result is reached as defined in 16.1, 16.2, 16.3, 16.4 or 16.5.1, the \\nmatch is at an end. Nothing that happens thereafter, except as in Law 41.18.2 (Penalty \\nruns), shall be regarded as part of it. Note also 16.9 \\n16.6.2  The side batting last will have scored enough runs to win only if its total of runs is \\nsufficient without including any runs completed by the batsmen before the completion of a \\ncatch, or the obstruction of a catch from which the striker could be dismissed \\n16.6.3 If a boundary is scored before the batsmen have completed sufficient runs to win the \\nmatch the whole of the boundary allowance shall be credited to the side\'s total and in the \\ncase of a hit by the bat, to the striker\'s score. \\n \\n16.7 Statement of result \\nIf the side batting last wins the match without losing all its wickets, the result shall be stated \\nas a win by the number of wickets still then to fall. \\nIf, without having scored a total of runs in excess of the total scored by the opposing side, \\nthe innings of the side batting last has been completed, but as the result of an award of 5 \\nPenalty runs its total of runs is then sufficient to win, the result shall be stated as a win to \\nthat side by Penalty runs. \\n"'b"b'If the side fielding last wins the match, the result shall be stated as a win by runs.  \\nIf the match is decided by one side conceding defeat or refusing to play, the result shall be \\nstated as Match Conceded or Match Awarded, as the case may be. \\n \\n16.8 Correctness of result \\nAny decision as to the correctness of the scores shall be the responsibility of the umpires. \\nSee Law 2.15 (Correctness of scores). \\n \\n16.9 Mistakes in scoring \\nIf, after the players and umpires have left the field in the belief that the match has been \\nconcluded, the umpires discover that a mistake in scoring has occurred which affects the \\nresult then, subject to 16.10, they shall adopt the following procedure. \\n16.9.1 If, when the players leave the field, the side batting last has not completed its innings \\nand  \\nEither     the number of overs to be bowled in the last hour, or in that innings, has not been \\ncompleted \\nor         the agreed time for close of play, or for the end of the innings, has not been reached \\nthen, unless one side concedes defeat, the umpires shall order play to resume. \\n \\nUnless a result is reached sooner, play will then continue. If conditions permit, until the \\nprescribed number of overs has been completed and either time for close of play has been \\nreached or the allotted time for the innings has expired, as appropriate. The number of \\novers and time remaining shall be taken as they were at the call of Time for the supposed \\nconclusion of the match. No account shall be taken of the time between that moment and \\nthe resumption of play. \\n16.9.2 If, at this call of Time, the overs have been completed and no Playing time remains, \\nor if the side batting last has completed its innings, the umpires shall immediately inform \\nboth captains of the necessary corrections to the scores and to the result. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"16.10 Result not to be changed \\n \\nOnce the umpires have agreed with the scorers the correctness of the scores at the \\nconclusion of the match - see Laws 215 (Correctness of scores) and 32 (Correctness of \\nscores) - the result cannot thereafter changed. \\n \\nCorrectness of result \\nThe umpires have a responsibility to ensure that the result is correct. They are required to \\nagree the score with the scorers at intervals and again at the end of the match. As discussed \\nin Law 2, umpires are not required to keep score, but must be able to satisfy themselves \\nthat it is right in order to be able to check the result is correct. If there is no qualified scorer, \\nor only one scorer, the umpires may decide to keep a running tally, making a note at the end \\nof each over. However, this will have an impact on their focus on the other key areas for \\nwhich they are responsible so, if at all possible, should be avoided. At higher levels, where \\nthere are two qualified scorers, they need only not incidents that may need checking with \\nthe scorers, as well as the number of wickets fallen and overs bowled. \\nIf there are any queries that can\'t be resolved by discussion with the scorers then the \\numpires must decide what the score should be. \\nThe result of a match  \\nA win by one side getting more runs than the other is clear It is important to realise that for \\nsuch a win, the losing side must have completed its innings or, in a two innings match both \\nits innings (as defined in Law 13). It should be noted from 16.7 how these results are to be \\nstated in the scoring record, as a win by runs if the winning side batted first. or a win by \\nwickets if the winning side batted last. In a two innings match it is possible for a side to \\nscore enough runs to win in one innings, although the losing opponents must have batted \\ntwice. In this case, it is traditional to state the result as a win by an innings and the number \\nof runs in excess of its opponent\'s two scores put together.  \\nUnless Playing Conditions (most usually the Duckworth Lewis Stern method) apply, if the \\nside batting last does not score more than its opponents, but has not completed itS innings, \\nthen the match is a Draw, even if the scores are equal. When the side batting last have \\ncompleted their final innings, and the final scores are level, the result is a Tie.  \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'The special and rare cases of a win by Penalty runs, of the umpires awarding a match, of a \\nside conceding defeat or even of a match being abandoned with no result are dealt with \\nbelow. \\n \\nWinning hit or extras \\nThe scoring of the winning run ends the match immediately. The ball is dead straightaway \\nand any events after this will not be part of the match (other than the awarding of Penalty \\nruns, more of which below). For example, if the side batting last needs one run to win and \\nWide ball is called, and not revoked, the instant award of a one run penalty for the Wide \\nmeans the desired run is scored and the match is won. Even if the wicket it put down fairly \\nby a member of the fielding side, the batsman cannot be Stumped or Run out. If two runs \\nare needed to win and the batsmen complete two runs, the match is finished and a third run \\nwould not be valid, however much the striker might want it to complete his/ her century. If \\nthe ball reaches the boundary before the batsmen have completed the winning run, the \\nboundary allowance will count. If the winning run is completed before the ball reaches the \\nboundary, only the runs up to the point of winning will count. \\nHowever, if the umpire has occasion to award 5 Penalty runs for some offence on this final \\ndelivery, the Law instructs him/her to do so when the ball is dead. Although this appears to \\nbe too late, these runs will in fact count. Although the penalty is not announced until the \\nball is dead, the offence must have been committed before it became dead. \\nLaw 16.6.2 is a reminder that runs not scored or disallowed over-ride the award of runs. So, \\nif a catch is taken, or obstructed, any runs made before its completion are not scored and \\nthus do not count, so cannot contribute towards a winning score. \\n \\nWin by Penalty runs \\nThis can occur only if the side batting last has completed its innings and the award of 5 \\nPenalty runs at the very end of the match, as described in the last paragraph, makes the \\ndifference between falling short of the target and having \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"enough runs to win. For example, a side is chasing a total of 185 runs and, with nine wickets \\ndown and one ball to go, have reached 183, two short of the opposition\'s total. They need \\nthree runs to win. The batsmen attempt these runs but one of them is run out on the \\nsecond run. They will score only one run, making their total 184 all out. The result would be \\na win by one run to the fielding side. If, however, 5 Penalty runs are awarded to the batting \\nside as a result of a fielding offence on this last delivery, that will bring the batting score to \\n189 all out. The result is a win by Penalty runs to the batting side. \\n \\nUmpires awarding a match \\nLaw 16.3 makes clear that although no appeal is necessary, the awarding of a match is not \\nan arbitrary action by the umpires. First there must be some action by either the fielding \\nside or the batting side that means play cannot start or cannot continue as it should. This \\ncould be a batsman not emerging from the pavilion or a fielding captain refusing to continue \\nafter an interval. The umpires together must discover the reason for this action. If play is in \\nprogress, this will probably mean leaving the field and going to the pavilion. Time must be \\ncalled before doing so and the umpires should take possession of the ball. The umpires \\nshould agree and note the time - taking care to record the time of the incident prompting \\nthe investigation, rather than the time that the investigation itself started - as adjustments \\nto the time for close of play may be needed. If it is apparent to the umpires that there is \\nindeed a refusal to play, they must warn the captain of the side of the consequences of this \\nrefusal. Failure to start or continue after such warning leaves the umpires no option but to \\naward the match to the other side. \\nIf a captain refuses to remove a player from the field as directed by the umpires for a Level 3 \\nor Level 4 offence under Law 42 then the umpires should warn the captain that this will be \\ntaken as a refusal to play. If the captain still fails to remove the offending player then the \\numpires must award the match to the opposing side as directed under Law 42.6.1. \\nIf play start or continue as a result of the warning or because there was no intentional \\nrefusal to play, calculation of the time lost must be made jointly by the umpires. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Umpires abandoning the match \\nA match might be abandoned because the weather or other unforeseen circumstances \\nmake it impossible for there to be enough play to reach a result as agreed under Law 13.1.2. \\nHowever, under Law 42.6.2, refusal by both captains to comply with the instructions of the \\numpires to remove players from the field for the same Level 3 or, more likely, Level 4 \\noffence could, in the extreme, result in the umpires abandoning the match and no result is \\nreturned. \\nFor this to occur at least two players, from different teams, must commit Level 3 or Level 4 \\noffences as part of the same incident (or more than one incident being dealt with by the \\numpires at the same time). Should one captain refuse to remove his/her player for this \\noffence, the match will be awarded to the other team. If both captains refuse, since the \\nmatch cannot be awarded to both teams, and cannot continue, it will simply not be \\nconcluded, and there will be no result. Umpires should work with the captains to avoid such \\na conclusion if at all possible. \\n \\nCaptain conceding a match \\nAlthough it will be extremely rare, the Law allows a captain to decide that it is not worth \\ncontinuing play and to admit defeat. The umpires will then declare that the other side has \\nwon the match. \\n \\nMistakes in scoring \\nEither keeping a tally of the score, or checking throughout play, depending on the number \\nand experience of the scorers as advised above, will mean that any discrepancies are soon \\ndiscovered and can be put right long before the end of the match. It is especially important \\nto keep to this routine as the match draws near to its close. Both sides will need to know \\nhow the scores stand. It is usually good practice to confirm the scores are right more \\nfrequently towards the end of a match, and at the very least ensure they are correct before \\nthe final over, as well as at every interval. \\nHowever, Law 16.9 sets out details of how to proceed if a mistake is discovered after the \\nplayers have left the field, thinking the match was over. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'Action other than informing those concerned is not required unless the mistake affects the \\nresult. There are two possible situations: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 First, if the batting side had completed its innings, or no time or overs remain, then \\nclearly no more play is possible - as indeed would be the case in dangerous \\nconditions of ground, weather or light. The umpires must then alter the score to its \\ncorrect value and tell the captains of the changed result. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Second, if the innings of the batting side is not completed, or time or overs remain, \\nplay should restart, and continue until a result is reached or until time and overs are \\nused up, or until conditions become unreasonable or dangerous. Failing light may \\nwell bring play to an end in such a situation. \\nThis is one of the very rare situations in which a captain might concede defeat. If it is \\ndiscovered that the batting side needs one more run to win and still has five wickets in \\nhand, with half an hour to play in good light and dry conditions the fielding captain just \\nmight concede defeat rather than bring the players back on to the field. \\nIf play is resumed, time will have been lost, but should not be taken out of the match. This \\nloss of time and any associated decrease in the number of overs to be bowled are both to \\nbe disregarded. The clock and the over count are to stop at the original call of Time and will \\nnot start again until the call of Play for the resumption.  \\nIt is much better to be careful that the scores are correct throughout play so that the \\nsituation does not arise. \\n \\nResult not to be changed \\nThere have been reports of Governing Bodies attempting to change the results of matches. \\nThe Law unequivocally states that, once the two umpires have agreed the scores and hence \\nthe result, it may not thereafter be changed. The Governing Body may, in the light of some \\nirregularity or incident of unfair play, award points or whatever for a match without \\nchanging the result, particularly where an award of points is involved. This should not be \\nconfused with changing the actual result. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'LAW 17 THE OVER \\n17.1 Number of balls \\nThe ball shall be bowled from each end alternately in overs of 6 balls \\n \\n17.2 Start of an over \\nAn over has started when the bowler starts his/her run up or if there is no run-up, starts \\nhis/her action for the first delivery of that over. \\n \\n17.3 Validity of balls \\n17.3.1   A ball shall not count as one of the 6 balls of the over unless it is delivered, even \\nthough, as in Law 41.16 (Non- striker leaving his/her ground early) a batsman may be \\ndismissed or some other incident occurs without the ball having been delivered. \\n  \\n17.3.2    A ball delivered by the bowler shall not count as one of the 6 balls of the \\nover. \\n  \\n17.3.2.1  if it is called dead, or is to be considered dead, before the striker has had an \\nopportunity to play it. See Law 20.6 (Dead ball, ball counting as one of over).  \\n  \\n17.3.2.2  if it is called dead in the circumstances of Law 20.4.2.6. Note also the \\nspecial provisions of Law 20.4.2.5 (Umpire calling and signalling Dead ball). \\n  \\n17.3.2.3   if it is a No ball. See Law 21 (No ball). \\n  \\n17.3.2.4   if it is a Wide See Law 22 (Wide ball). \\n  \\n17.32..5 when any of Laws 24.4 (Player returning without permission) 28.2 (Fielding \\nthe ball), 4).4 (Deliberate attempt to distract striker). or 41.5 (Deliberate distraction, \\ndeception or obstruction of batsman) is applied. \\n17.3.3 Any deliveries other than those listed in 17.3.1 and 17.3.2 shall be known as valid \\nballs. Only valid balls shall count towards the 6 balls of the over. \\n \\n17.4 Call of Over \\nWhen 6 valid balls have been bowled and when the ball becomes dead, the umpire shall call \\nOver before leaving the wicket. See also Law 20.3 (Call of Over or Time). \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"17.5 Umpire miscounting \\n  \\n17.5.1 If the umpire miscounts the number of valid balls, the over is counted by the \\numpire shall stand. \\n  \\n17.5.2 If, having miscounted, the umpire allows an over to continue after 6 valid balls \\nhave been bowled, he/she may subsequently call Over when the ball becomes dead \\nafter any delivery, even if that delivery is not a valid ball. \\n17.6 Bowler changing ends \\n  \\nA bowler shall be allowed to change ends as often as desired, provided he/she does \\nnot bowl two overs consecutively, nor bowl parts of each of two consecutive overs, \\nin the same innings \\n \\n17.7 Finishing an over \\n  \\n17.7.1 Other than at the end of an innings, a bowler shall finish an over in progress \\nunless incapacitated or suspended under any of the Laws. \\n  \\n17.7.2 If for any reason, other than the end of an innings, an over is left uncompleted \\nat the start of an interval or interruption, it shall be completed on resumption of \\nplay. \\n  \\n \\n17.8 Bowler incapacitated or suspended during an over \\n  \\nIf for any reason a bowler is incapacitated while running up to deliver the first ball of \\nan over, or is incapacitated or suspended during an over, the umpire shall call and \\nsignal Dead ball. Another bowler shall complete the over from the same end, \\nprovided that he/she does not bowl two overs consecutively, nor bowl parts of each \\nof two consecutive overs, in that innings. \\n \\nThe over \\nUnless there is a Playing Condition or other prior agreement that says otherwise, an over is \\nto consist of 6 valid balls Umpires should study Law 17.3 to be sure they understand what a \\nvalid ball is and they know which balls are valid and which ones are not. 17.3.1 and 17.3.2 \\nlist the deliveries which are not valid balls and so do not count in the over. \\nA ball is not delivered unless the bowler completed his/her delivery action and releases the \\nball towards the striker\'s end. Although in an attempt to run striker a bowler may throw the \\nball at the non-striker\'s wicket before the point of delivery, this is not delivering the ball, \\nsince he/she has not reached the point at which you would expect the ball to be released \\n"'b'b"normally. It therefore will not count as a ball in the over, whether or not the bowler actually \\nreleases the ball. \\nThe two cases of balls that are not valid cited in 17.3.2.2 are those where the striker is \\ndistracted while preparing to receive, or receiving the ball, or not ready when it is delivered. \\nIf the striker is not ready for the ball to be delivered, then the ball will not count as valid \\nonly if the batsman does not make an attempt to play it, and the umpire is satisfied that the \\nstriker had an acceptable reason for not being ready. \\nIf, however, there is a distraction, even if the striker actually tries to play the ball and makes \\ncontact, the delivery will not be a valid ball. \\nIn the cases listed in 17.3.2.5, the fact that the ball is not to count in the over is specifically \\nstated in each of these Laws. \\nThe over starts when the bowler starts his/her run up to deliver the first ball. The Law \\nrecognises that there are bowlers, though not many who do not have a run up. In that case \\nthe starting point for an over is when such a bowler begins the delivery action for the first \\nball. The delivery action would start with the placement of the back foot in \'Delivery stride, \\nor the \'Delivery swing, as defined in Appendix A. \\nIt is vital that umpires count the balls in an over correctly, especially in matches where \\ninnings are limited to a nu ber of overs. Devices such as \'clickers\' are available, but coins, \\npebbles or other small objects are also widely used. Whatever method of counting is \\nemployed, it is essential that the umpire uses the same procedure for every delivery so that \\nit becomes almost automatic. So, if an umpire chooses to count the ball as it is released, he/ \\nshe must always count it then. If another umpire chooses to count when the ball becomes \\ndead, that is another perfectly valid method - so long as that umpire always makes sure to \\ncount when the ball becomes dead. If a No ball or Wide is bowled, or for some other reason \\na delivery is not a valid ball, the umpire must remember not to count it as one of the over. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"The bowler\'s end umpire is responsible for the call of Over when the sixth valid ball to have \\nbeen delivered has become dead. However, both umpires should count, to as to help each \\nother out if needed. It is expected that the umpires will check with each other and agree \\nwhen there are two deliveries remaining. There are some situations when umpires may \\nchoose to check after the completion of both 4 and 5 deliveries as an extra safeguard. Such \\nan arrangement must be clearly understood in agreement between the two umpires before \\nplay begins. \\n \\nUmpire miscounting \\nIf, as suggested, the two umpires work together in checking the number of deliveries, the \\npossibility of miscounting will be minimised. Mistakes do, however, sometimes occur, le \\nmay be possible to rectify the mistake immediately, without any fuss - should the other \\numpire or scorer point it out straight away. However, if that does not happen, no further \\nattempt at correction is to be made. The over stands with the wrong number of valid balls - \\nthose balls cannot be made up later and the umpire should not attempt to fix one mistake \\nwith another.  \\nFurther, if the bowler\'s end umpire accidentally lets the over go on for too long - he/she \\nmay perhaps have allowed seven balls to be delivered. If the seventh delivery is a No ball or \\na Wide, when the umpire realises the mistake he/she should call Over, as soon as the ball is \\ndead for that delivery. Although that ball was not valid, there had already been six valid balls \\nbefore it and so the Over is complete and no further delivery should be allowed. \\n \\nCall of Over \\nThe call of Over does not make the ball dead. The sixth valid ball has to have become dead \\nbefore calling Over - just as the ball should become dead before the call of Time (rather than \\nbeing made dead by the call of Time). However, if an action means the match has been \\ncompleted, the call of Time will indicate this and hence the ball will already have been made \\nautomatically dead, even though it might appear still to be in play. See Law 16.6.1. \\nAs with all calls, the call of Over should be made loud and clearly. While avoiding waste of \\nplaying time by delaying too long, the umpire must be sure that the ball is dead before \\nmaking the call. Dead ball is the subject of Law 20. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Over must be completed \\nThere are several reasons - injury or suspension of a bowler; taking an interval at the fall of a \\nwicket; interruptions for weather and so on - for an over being interrupted. If play resumes \\nafter the break, an innings ending in mid-over is the only situation in which the over will not \\nbe completed. \\nThe completion must be by the same bowler unless that bowler is incapacitated or \\nsuspended. Umpires must note carefully both the current bowler and the previous bowler \\nwhen any break occurs, to ensure that a replacement bowler did not bowl any balls in the \\nprevious over. Further, that bowler will not be allowed to bowl any balls, not even as a \\nreplacement bowler part way through, in the next over. The same embargo of not bowling \\neven a few balls in two consecutive overs applies to a bowler changing ends. These \\nembargoes only apply within the same innings - should a team be bowling in consecutive \\ninnings in the case of a forfeiture or follow-on) then a bowler may be involved in overs that \\nare consecutive, but make up parts of different innings. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'LAW 18 SCORING RUNS \\n18.1 A run \\n  \\n18.1.1 The score shall be reckoned by runs. A run is scored so often as the batsmen, \\nat any time while the hall play, have crossed and made good their ground from end \\nto end. \\n  \\n18.1.2 when a boundary is scored. See Law 19 (Boundaries).  \\n  \\n18.1.3 when Penalty runs are awarded. See 18.6. \\n18.2 Runs disallowed \\n  \\nWherever in these Laws provision is made for the scoring of runs or awarding of \\npenalties, such runs and penalties will be subject to any provisions that may be \\napplicable for the disallowance of runs or for the non-award of penalties. \\n  \\nWhen runs are disallowed, the one run penalty for No ball or Wide shall stand and 5 \\nrun penalties shall be allowed, except for Penalty runs under Law 28.3 (Protective \\nhelmets belonging to the fielding side). \\n18.3 Short runs \\n  \\n18.3.1    A run is short if a batsman fails to make good his/her ground in turning for a \\nfurther run. \\n  \\n18.3.2 Although a short run shortens the succeeding one, the latter if completed \\nshall not be regarded as short. A striker setting off for the first run from in front of \\nthe popping crease may do so also without penalty. \\n18.4 Unintentional short runs \\nExcept in the circumstances of 18.5,  \\n  \\n18.4.1   if either batsman runs a short run, the umpire concerned shall, unless a \\nboundary is scored, call and signal Short run as soon as the ball becomes dead and \\nthat run shall not be scored. \\n  \\n18.4.2  if, after either or both batsmen run short, a boundary is scored the umpire \\nconcerned shall disregard the short running and shall not call or signal Short run.  \\n  \\n18.4.3   if both batsmen run short in one and the same run this shall be regarded as \\nonly one short run. \\n  \\n18.4.4   if more than one run is short then, subject to 18.4.2 and 18.4.3, all runs \\ncalled as short shall not be scored.  \\n  \\n18.4.5   if there has been more than one short run, the umpire shall inform the \\nscorers as to the number of runs to be recorded. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"18.5 Deliberate short runs \\n18.5.1  If either umpire considers that one or both batsmen deliberately ran short at that \\numpire\'s end, the umpire concerned shall, when the ball is dead, call and signal Short run \\nand inform the other umpire of what has occurred and apply 18.5.2 \\n18.5.2   The bowler\'s end umpire shall  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 disallow all runs to the batting side  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 return any not out batsman to his/her original end  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 signal No ball or Wide to the scorers, if applicable  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the fielding side  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award any other 5-run Penalty that is applicable except for Penalty runs under Law \\n28.3 (Protective helmets belonging to the fielding side)  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the scorers as to the number of runs to be recorded  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the captain of the fielding side and, as soon as practicable, the captain of the \\nbatting side of the reason for this action \\n18.5.3   The umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after the \\nmatch to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body responsible for the \\nmatch, who shall take such action as is considered appropriate against the captain, any \\nother individuals concerned and, if appropriate, the team. \\n \\n18.6 Runs awarded for penalties \\nRuns shall be awarded for penalties under 18.5, and Laws 21 (No ball), 22 (Wide ball), 244 \\n(Player returning without permission), 26.4 (Penalties for contravention), 28.2 (Fielding the \\nball), 28.3 (Protective helmets belonging to the fielding side), 41 (Unfair play) and 42 \\n(Players\' conduct). \\nNote, however, the restrictions on the award of Penalty runs in Laws 18.5, 23.3 (Leg byes \\nnot to be awarded), 25.6.5 (Dismissal and conduct of a batsman and his/her runner), 25.7 \\n(Restriction on the striker\'s runner), 28.3 (Protective helmets belonging to the fielding side), \\n34 (Hit the ball twice), 41.14 (Batsman damaging the pitch) and 41.15 (Striker in protected \\narea). \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'18.7 Runs scored for boundaries \\nRuns shall be scored for boundary allowances under Law 19 (Boundaries). \\n18.8 Runs scored when a batsman is dismissed \\nWhen a batsman is dismissed, any runs for penalties awarded to either side shall stand. No \\nother runs shall be credited to the batting side, except as follows. \\n 18.8.1 If a batsman is dismissed obstructing the field the batting side shall also score any \\nruns completed before the offence. \\nIf, however, the obstruction prevented a catch being made, no runs other than penalties \\nshall be scored. \\n18.8.2 If a batsman is dismissed Run out the batting side shall also score any runs completed \\nbefore the wicket was put down. \\nIf, however, a striker who has a runner is himself/herself dismissed Run out under Law \\n25.6.5 (Dismissal and conduct of a batsman and his/her runner), any runs completed by the \\nrunner and the other batsman shall be disallowed. \\n \\n18.9 Runs scored when the ball becomes dead other than at the fall of a wicket \\nWhen the ball becomes dead for any reason other than the fall of a wicket, or is called dead \\nby an umpire, unless there is specific provision otherwise in the Laws any runs for penalties \\nawarded to either side shall be scored. Note however the provisions of Laws 233 (Leg byes \\nnot to be awarded) and 28.3 (Protective helmets belonging to the fielding side). \\nAdditionally the batting side shall be credited with all runs completed by the batsmen \\nbefore the incident or call of Dead ball, \\nand \\nthe run in progress if the batsmen had already crossed at the instant of the incident or call \\nof Dead ball. Note specifically, however, the provisions of Law 41.5.8 (Deliberate distraction, \\ndeception or obstruction of batsman). \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'18.10 Crediting of runs scored  \\nUnless stated otherwise in any of the Laws.  \\n18.10.1  if the ball is struck by the bat. all runs scored by the batting side shall be credited to \\nthe striker, except for the following: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 an award of 5 Penalty runs, which shall be scored as Penalty runs \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the one run penalty for a No ball, which shall be scored as a No balls extra. \\n18.10.2   If the ball is not struck by the bat, runs shall be scored as Penalty runs, Byes. Leg \\nbyes, No ball extras or Wides as the case may be. If Byes or Leg byes accrue from a No ball, \\nonly the one run penalty for No ball shall be scored as such, and the remainder as Byes or \\nLeg byes as appropriate. \\n18.10.3 the bowler shall be debited with: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 all runs scored by the striker \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 all runs scored as No ball extras \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 all runs scored as Wides. \\n18.11 Batsman returning to original end  \\n  \\n18.11.1  When the striker is dismissed in any of the circumstances in 18.11.1,1 to \\n18.11.1.6, the not out batsman shall return to his/her original end. \\n  \\n18.11.1.1  A dismissal in the circumstances of Law 25.6.4 or 25,6.5 (Dismissal and \\nconduct of a batsman and his/her runner). \\n  \\n18.11.1.2  Bowled. \\n  \\n18.11.1.3  Stumped \\n  \\n18.11.1.4  Hit the ball twice. \\n  \\n18.11.1.5  LBW \\n  \\n18.11.1.6  Hit wicket. \\n18.11.2  The batsmen shall return to their original ends in any of the cases of 18.11.2.1 to \\n18.11.2.3. \\n18.11.2.1 A boundary is scored.  \\n18.11.2.2 Runs are disallowed for any reason. \\n18.11.2.3 A decision by the batsmen at the wicket to do so, under Law 41.5(Deliberate \\ndistraction, deception or obstruction of batsmen). \\n \\n'"b"b'18.12 Batsman returning to wicket he/she has left  \\n18.12.1 When a batsman is dismissed in any of the ways in 18.12.1.1 to 18.12.1.3, the not \\nout batsman shall return to the wicket he/she has left but only if the batsmen had not \\nalready crossed at the instant of the incident causing the dismissal. If runs are to be \\ndisallowed however, the not out batsman shall return to his/her original end. \\n18.12.2.1 Caught \\n18.12.2.2 Obstructing the field. \\n18.12.2.3 Run out other than under Law 25.64 or 25.6.5 (Dismissal and conduct of a \\nbatsman and his/her runner). \\n18.12.2 If, while a run is in progress, the ball becomes dead for any reason other than the \\ndismissal of a batsman the batsmen shall return to the wickets they had left, but only if they \\nhad not already crossed in running when the ball became dead. If, however, any of the \\ncircumstances of 18.11.2.1 to 18.11.2.3 apply, the batsmen shall return to their original \\nends. \\nThis Law explains how the batsmen can score a run and details the cases where the runs \\nthey appear to make do not count. It summarises when runs that have not been made by \\nthe batsmen are to accrue to the batting side. It also states what runs, if any, are to be \\nallowed when a batsman is dismissed, and to which end the batsmen should go after a \\nwicket falls. It is therefore at the very heart of the game of cricket. \\nThere are three main ways in which runs are scored. The batsmen run; runs are awarded for \\nboundaries; runs are awarded to a team as penalties, following a contravention of the Laws \\nby the other side. All of these are set out in detail in individual Laws. \\nRuns not scored and runs disallowed  \\nBefore considering the many ways in which runs can be scored or awarded, due note must \\nbe taken of the situations when a run, even though taken, is not scored and when a run or \\nruns are disallowed (See also definitions in Appendix A.11). \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Law 18.2 makes it clear that whenever a Law speaks of the scoring of runs, or penalties to \\nbe awarded the statement is to be understood as accompanied by the phrase \'subject to \\nany disallowance of runs or penalties that may apply. This Law advises that when runs by \\nthe batsmen are to be disallowed, penalties otherwise due may or may not be awarded. It \\navoids the necessity of listing all such occasions, although it is always implicit in the relevant \\nLaw. There are two situations: \\n1. Runs that are not scored are runs that are not illegal but are not recognised as properly \\nexecuted runs. These are not \'runs that are disallowed, so the batsmen are not returned to \\ntheir original ends on that account unless required by other Laws; \\nThe basic principle that a run is scored if the batsmen \'cross and make good their ground \\nfrom end to end\', does not apply if other Laws deem that run not to have been scored \\nalthough the batsmen are not returned to their original ends. These Laws are: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 One or both batsmen run short (Law 18.4) \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 A batsman is dismissed Caught (Law 33.4) \\xe2\\x80\\xa2 A striker is dismissed Obstructing a ball \\nfrom being caught (Law 37.5) \\n2. Runs are disallowed when a breach of Law by one or both batsmen requires the umpire \\nto cancel any completed runs and return the batsmen to their original ends in the following \\nsituations:  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 either batsman deliberately runs short (Law 18.5) \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Leg byes are not awarded, as the striker was neither attempting to play the ball with \\nthe bat nor avoiding injury (Law 23.3)  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 an injured striker with a runner is himself/herself dismissed Run out (Law 25.6.5)  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 a batsman\'s runner leaves his/her ground before the ball reaches the striker or \\npasses the popping crease (Law 25.7) \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the batsmen run after a ball has been lawfully struck more than once (Law 34.4) \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 a second or subsequent instance of any batsman damaging the pitch (Law 41.14) \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 a second or subsequent instance of any batsman taking guard in or close to the \\nprotected area (Law 41.15) \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"When runs are disallowed in these seven situations, the penalty of one run for a No ball or a \\nWide will be awarded if appropriate, as will any other award of 5 Penalty runs except for \\nthose if the ball hits an unworn helmet belonging to the fielding side that has been placed \\non the ground behind the wicket-keeper in line with the stumps (Law 283 Protective \\nhelmets belonging to the fielding side). \\nWhen the batsmen score a run by running  \\nIt is important to understand that a run is not completed until both batsmen have run and \\ncrossed and each has grounded their bat or some part of his/her person behind the popping \\ncrease at the other end. It is this grounding behind the popping crease that is essential for \\nthe completion of the run. \\nWhenever the batsmen run, each umpire must be in a position to watch that every run is \\nproperly completed. This means judging whether the batsmen have both made good their \\nground (See Law 30.1) behind the crease at the end to which they are running and, if more \\nthan one run, before they turn for the next run. The bowler\'s end umpire must move away \\nfrom the wicket to a position where he/she can observe the creases and the wicket from the \\nside and the striker\'s end umpire must be lined up with the popping crease at that end. This \\nis discussed further in Law 38. \\nShort running \\nThe key words in Law 18.3.1 are \'in turning for a further runt If a batsman fails to complete a \\nrun, but does not start out for another one, he/she can be run out, but is not to be \\nconsidered as running short. Equally, if he/she does turn for another run, then whatever \\nmay happen on the next run is not affected by this one. It is the run the batsman fails to \\ncomplete before setting out again that is short and so not to be counted. The Law also \\nstates what is taken for granted by players - the striker receiving the ball in front of the \\npopping crease may run from that point without causing that first run to be short. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"A simple mistake is penalised only by the loss of the uncompleted run, and even that \\nsanction is not imposed if the ball goes to the boundary. Since it is a failed turn that \\nconstitutes a short run, it follows that two runs must be attempted before one can be short. \\nIf in this case both umpires call and signal Short run when the ball is dead, it must be for the \\nfirst run. Only one run will be deducted. The scorers will understand this and no \\nconsultation between umpires is necessary, though it should be checked with the scorers at \\nthe next interval. If, however, three (or more) runs have been attempted, and both umpires \\ncall and signal, consultation between them will be necessary to determine whether it was \\nthe same run short at both ends or whether it was the first at one end and the second at the \\nother. In the former case only one run is to be deducted; in the latter case two will not be \\ncounted. The umpire at the bowler\'s end is responsible for repeating the Short run signal to \\nthe scorers when the ball is dead and for informing them of the number of runs that have \\nbeen scored if more than one run is short. \\nThis is not a case of runs being disallowed. It is an example of the principle that, with certain \\nexceptions, only completed runs count. \\n \\nDeliberate short running \\nDeliberate short running is unfair, and could occur at any time but it is more likely towards \\nthe end of an innings, when it could be important to the batting side both to score runs and \\nfor a particular batsman to keep the strike. The dishonest practice of deliberately running \\nshort leads not only to all runs from that ball being disallowed and the return of the \\nbatsmen to their original ends, but also to a 5-run Penalty and report to the Governing Body \\nfor the match. This is deliberate cheating so, unlike with accidental short running, the \\npenalty applies even if the ball goes to the boundary. If the deliberate short run was from a \\nNo ball or Wide then the one run penalty for this will be awarded, as will any other Penalty \\nruns applicable except for the ball hitting the unworn helmet placed on the ground behind \\nthe wicket-keeper by the fielding side.  \\nThe decision that it was deliberate must be made with due seriousness, but an umpire must \\nnot avoid making it. Help from his/her colleague may not be available; the other umpire will \\nhave been watching the touch-down at his/her \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"own end. Nevertheless, the umpires must act together if a report is to be made. Note also \\nthat while either umpire may have seen and signalled the short running at their own end, it \\nis the bowler\'s end umpire who will issue the penalty. \\n \\nOther scoring of runs \\nScoring of runs for boundaries - or by the award of penalties - are all dealt with under the \\nappropriate Laws. Laws 18.6 and 18.7 list what those Laws are although the numerous \\noccasions under Laws 41 and 42 are not separately listed. \\nLaw 18.10 clarifies who is awarded runs when the ball is struck by, and when not struck by, \\nthe bat. This part of the Law is more useful for scorers than umpires, though it is crucial, for \\nmatters of communication, that umpires are aware of it. The Law changed in 2017, so that \\nByes and Leg byes that are scored off a No ball are no longer scored as multiple No ball \\nextras, but rather one No ball extra and the rest scored as Byes or Leg byes. This change \\ndoes not affect the overall score, or the result, but rather how the runs are recorded. The \\nrecording of only the one run penalty for the No ball against the bowler and the remaining \\nruns, when not off the bat as Byes or Leg byes, is a fairer reflection of the number of No \\nballs bowled than all runs not off the bat off a No ball being scored as No balls against the \\nbowler It is noted here that the scoring of any runs for \'Byes\' off a delivery that has been \\ncalled a \'Wide ball remain as Wides. \\n \\nBatsmen crossing \\nTaken in conjunction with Law 30, it will be seen that when the batsmen cross they \\nexchange ends. In addition to watching the crease for the completion of runs, umpires must \\nalso watch whether the batsmen have crossed or not when they are running. It is not as \\ndifficult as it sounds. \\nWhat, precisely, it means for batsmen to have crossed has not always been well-defined. \\nHowever, it is important to note what it means for batsmen to have \'crossed\' - it requires \\nthe whole of a batsman\'s person, and bat, to have passed the whole of their partner\'s \\nperson and bat. That is to say, if a batsman\'s trailing leg is not closer to the end he/she is \\nrunning to than his/her partner\'s entire body then the batsmen have not yet fully crossed. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b' \\nBatsmen before they have crossed at all (top left), while they are in the process of crossing \\n(top right), when they have nearly (but not quite) completely crossed (bottom left), and \\nwhen they have fully crossed (bottom right). \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Whether the batsmen had already crossed or not at the crucial moment is an important \\nconsideration in the awarding of runs, in deciding which batman is out and in deciding \\nwhether or not batsmen will continue to the ends they were approaching or be sent back. It \\nis obvious in most cases, particularly if it is remembered that batsmen who are level have \\nnot yet crossed. \\n \\nSending batsmen back - or not \\nIf the ball becomes dead for any reason when the batsmen are running, it is important that \\nthey are then directed to the correct ends. This is dealt with in Laws 18.11 and 18.12 and in \\nthe definitions of Original end, meaning the end where the batsman was when the ball \\ncame into play and \'Wicket he/she has left\' meaning the wicket at the end where the \\nbatsman was at the start of the run in progress. There are two situations to consider: \\n1. Ball becomes dead without either batsman being dismissed \\nThere are several situations in which this can occur. Two examples from many are the ball \\nhitting a helmet on the ground, and the umpire calling Dead ball for a case of a possible \\nserious injury to a player or umpire.  \\nThe general principle here, in Law 18.12.2, is that each batsman goes to his/her end. That is \\nif, at the instant the ball becomes dead, the batsmen had already crossed they will continue \\nto the ends they were approaching. If they had not already crossed, they go back to the \\nends they had just left. Law 18.11.2 sets out the only three exceptions to this principle. In \\ntwo of these three cases the batsmen are to go to their original ends - where they were \\nwhen the ball came into play. The third is a special case, with its own instructions. \\n \\nCases for return to original ends  \\nA boundary. Most of the time the ball crosses the boundary and a 4 or a 6 (or other \\nboundary allowances determined before the toss) is scored, the ball becomes dead, and the \\nbatsmen return to their original ends. The unusual situation of the batsmen running more \\nthan the boundary allowance is not included as an exception The reason for this and details \\nof other boundary situations, including boundary overthrows, are dealt with under Law 19 \\n(Boundaries). \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Disallowance of runs. As discussed above, when runs are disallowed under the Laws, the \\nbatsmen are returned to their original ends. This does not apply to situations where \\nincomplete runs are not counted, such as accidental short runs, but to situations where all \\nruns by the batsman are to be disallowed. \\nDecision by batsman under Law 41.5.9. In this case of deliberate distraction, deception or \\nobstruction by the fielding side, the batsmen have the right to choose which of them will \\nface the next delivery. The umpire\'s only responsibility is therefore to see that the batsmen \\nknow this. A question \'Which ends do you want to go to?\', if necessary, will achieve this. \\n \\n2. Batsman dismissed \\nLaw 18.11 sets out the cases in which the Not out batsman will return to his/her original \\nend, while Law 18.12 sets out the cases in which he/she will go to his/her end as \\ndetermined by the batsmen having crossed or not. The following chart lists the various cases \\nfor each of the methods of dismissal. \\n \\nDismissal of a batsman \\n \\nThe not out batsman will \\nReturn to/stay at original end \\nGo on it crossed Go back it not \\nBowled \\n \\n \\nCaught \\nHit the ball twice \\n \\nHit wicket \\n \\nLBW \\n \\n \\nObstructing the field \\n \\nRun out (except next case) \\nStriker who has a runner himself/herself run \\nout \\n \\nStumped \\n \\nTimed out \\n \\nIf runs are to be disallowed the not out \\nbatsman always returns to his/her original end \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'It will be seen that, except for the striker who has a runner, those on the left are situations \\nwhere in normal circumstances there is no opportunity for runs to be taken. \\n \\nField technique \\nThe need for the umpires to be aware of and watch the completion of each run is clear. To \\ndo that, the bowler end umpire must move to one side (ideally in line with the popping \\ncrease) where he/she can have a view of the creases and of the wicket. The umpire will \\nnormally move to the side where the ball has been struck (though this is not always the case \\n- there is more on positioning techniques in Part 1, Law 2 and Law 38). As well as watching \\nfor the batsman grounding behind the crease, he/she must also know what is happening to \\nthe ball and what the fielders are doing. However, the umpire must not forget to watch the \\nbatsmen whilst following the progress of the ball. An umpire must be able to adjudicate on \\nany action at his/ her end, such as an attempted run out, and also assist the other umpire \\nwith information on other events. In addition, the umpires must know whether or not the \\nbatsmen have already crossed at the appropriate moment. \\nThis moment might be the completion of a catch, the breaking of the wicket for a successful \\nrun out, the fielders throw in the case of a boundary overthrow, or the ball crossing the \\nboundary, If from observing the run of play an umpire sees that an attempted run out may \\nbe imminent at the other end, then it will be for him/her to watch for the batsmen crossing \\nor not, while his/her colleague is judging the run out. With a little practice, a rhythm can be \\nbuilt up to increase awareness of what is happening in the field, to glance back at the crease \\nat the right moment for the touch-down and then look to see the batsmen cross. At the \\nsame time, of course, an umpire must position him/herself so as not to be in the way of the \\nfielder, in the path of the throw-in and always be aware of the location of the ball, watching \\nit as often as possible. \\n \\nScorers \\nThis Law and the next demand the maximum cooperation and communication between \\numpires and scorers. The scorers have to work out what is going on by what they can see \\nfrom a great distance, sometimes unable to hear any calls by the umpire. As well as the \\npoints where the Law instructs the umpires to inform the scorers - such as in short running - \\nthe umpires should assist in every situation where there could be doubt. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'LAW 19 BOUNDARIES \\n19.1 Determining the boundary of the field of play \\n9.1.1 \\nBefore the toss, the umpires shall determine the boundary of the field of \\nplay, which shall be fixed for the duration of the match. See Law 2.3.1.4 \\n(Consultation with captains). \\n9.1.2 \\nThe boundary shall be determined such that no part of any sight-screen will, \\nat any stage of the match be within the field of play. \\n \\n19.2 Identifying and marking the boundary \\n9.2.1 \\nWherever practicable, the boundary shall be marked by means of a \\ncontinuous white line or by an object in contact with the ground.  \\n9.2.2 \\nIf the boundary is marked by means of a white line, \\n19.2.2.1 \\nthe edge of the line nearest the pitch shall be the boundary. \\n19.2.2.2 \\nan object such as a flag post or board, used merely to highlight the \\nposition of a line marked on the ground, must be placed beyond the \\nboundary and is not itself to be regarded as being the boundary. \\n9.2.3 \\nIf the boundary is marked by means of an object that is in contact with the \\nground the boundary will be the edge of the grounded part of the object which is \\nnearest the pitch. \\n9.2.4 \\nWhere there is no white line or object providing continuous marking, objects \\nsuch as flags, posts or boards may be used to mark specific points on the boundary. \\nThe boundary shall be the imaginary straight line on the ground joining the two \\nnearest marked points. \\n9.2.5 \\nA boundary that cannot be identified as in 19.2.2. 19.23 or 19.2.4 shall be \\ndetermined by the umpires before the toss. \\n9.2.6 \\nAn obstacle within the field of play, other than as in 19.2.7, shall not be \\nregarded as a boundary unless so determined by the umpires before the toss. See \\nLaw 23.1.4 (Consultation with captains). \\n9.2.7 \\nA person or animal coming onto the field of play while the ball is in play shall \\nnot be regarded as a boundary unless the umpires determine otherwise at the time \\nthat contact between the ball and such a person or animal is made. The decision \\nshall be made for each separate occurrence. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'19.3 Restoring the boundary \\nIf a solid object used to mark the boundary is disturbed for any reason, then \\nthe boundary shall be considered to be in its original position. \\nthe object shall be returned to its original position as soon as is practicable; if play is taking \\nplace, this shall be as soon as the ball is dead. \\nif some part of a fence or other marker has come within the field of play, that part shall be \\nremoved from the field of play as soon as is practicable; if play is taking place, this shall be \\nas soon as the ball is dead. \\n19.4 Ball grounded beyond the boundary \\n19.4.1 \\nThe ball in play is grounded beyond the boundary if it touches  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the boundary or any part of an object used to mark the boundary; \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 he ground beyond the boundary; \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 any object that is grounded beyond the boundary \\n19.4.2 \\nThe ball in play is to be regarded as being grounded beyond the boundary if \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 a fielder, grounded beyond the boundary as in 19.5, touches the ball; \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 a fielder, after catching the ball within the boundary becomes grounded \\nbeyond the boundary while in contact with the ball, before completing the \\ncatch. \\n \\n19.5 Fielder grounded beyond the boundary \\n19.5.1  A fielder is grounded beyond the boundary if some part of his/her person is in \\ncontact with any of the following:  \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 the boundary or any part of an object used to mark the boundary \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 the ground beyond the boundary: \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 any object that is in contact with the ground beyond the boundary \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 another fielder who is grounded beyond the boundary, if the umpire considers that \\nit was the intention of either fielder that the contact should assist in the fielding of \\nthe ball. \\n \\n \\n \\n19.5. \\n'"b"b'19.5.2    A fielder who is not in contact with the ground is considered to be grounded \\nbeyond the boundary if his her final contact with the ground, before his/her first contact \\nwith the ball after it has been delivered by the bowler, was not entirely within the boundary \\n \\n19.6 Boundary allowances \\n19.6.1 \\nBefore the toss the umpires shall determine the runs to be allowed for \\nboundaries with both captains. In deciding the allowances the umpires and captains \\nshall be guided by the prevailing custom of the ground 19.6.2  \\n19.6.2 \\nUnless determined differently under 19.61, 6 runs shall be allowed for a \\nboundary 6; and 4 runs for a boundary 4. See also 19.7. \\n \\n19.7 Runs scored from boundaries \\n19.7.1 \\nA Boundary 6 will be scored if and only if the ball has been struck by the bat \\nand is first grounded beyond the boundary without having been in contact with the \\nground within the field of play. This shall apply even the ball has previously touched \\na fielder \\n19.7.2 \\nA Boundary 4 will be scored when a ball that is grounded beyond the \\nboundary \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 whether struck by the bat or not, was first grounded within the boundary, or \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 has not been struck by the bat. \\n19.7.3 \\nWhen a boundary is scored, the batting side, except in the circumstances of \\n19.8, shall be awarded whichever is the greater of  \\n19.7.3.1 \\nthe allowance for the boundary \\n19.7.3.2 \\nthe runs completed by the batsmen together with the run in progress \\nif they had already crossed at the instant the boundary is scored. \\n19.7.4 \\nWhen the runs in 19.7.3.2 exceed the boundary allowance they shall replace \\nthe boundary allowance for the purposes of Law 18.12.2 (Batsman returning to \\nwicket he/she has left). \\n19.7.5 \\nThe scoring of Penalty runs by either side is not affected by the scoring of a \\nboundary. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'19.8 Overthrow or wilful act of fielder \\nIf the boundary results from an overthrow or from the wilful act of a fielder, the runs scored \\nshall be  \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 any runs for penalties awarded to either side  \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 and the allowance for the boundary \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 and the runs completed by the batsmen, together with the run in progress if they \\nhad already crossed at the instant of the throw or act. \\nLaw 18.12.2 (Batsman returning to wicket he/she has left) shall apply as from the instant of \\nthe throw or act. \\nAgreeing the boundary \\nProbably the most crucial part of the application of this Law will take place before play \\nstarts, when the umpires first inspect the ground and determine what the boundary of the \\nfield of play is. On some grounds, the boundary will be clear and will be suitably marked by a \\nline or a rope. On others, it will be a patchwork of a stretch of wall, an area of long grass, or \\na piece of picket fence where only the main posts are in contact with the ground, and so on. \\nJust as the creases are edges, so the boundary is an edge. It is the dividing line between the \\nfield of play and the world outside. At every point round the field there must be a boundary \\nedge to be this dividing line, even if in some parts it is a line to be imagined on the ground. \\nLaw 19.2 sets out in great detail what that edge is to be for various possible types of \\nmarking. If in any section of the field none of those types of marking exists, it is essential \\nthat the boundary edge in that section is clearly determined by the umpires and \\ncommunicated to the captains. When the ball is trickling towards the long grass, there must \\nbe a clear definition of when it has reached the boundary. \\nUmpires should note the procedure if some part of a physical boundary collapses and \\nintrudes into the field of play. Similarly, if the boundary marking is disturbed during the \\nfielding of a ball it should be restored to its original position if possible as soon as the ball is \\ndead, but if not possible, or not done immediately for whatever reason, then the boundary \\nremains as the original line. So if the ball goes into the area between the original boundary \\nline and the disturbed markers, it will have been deemed to have been grounded beyond it. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b' \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nThe best type of boundary is a \\nclearly visible rope. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n        \\n                           \\nIf the boundary is a fence then it is the innermost point of the bottom of the fence. \\nHowever, in this example, it may be more sensible to make the boundary the point at which \\nthe grass ends as shown by the red line. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Since it is only the innermost \\nedge of the boundary marker \\nwhich marks the boundary, \\nthere is no problem with a rope \\nthat overlaps in places. \\n \\nSight-screens \\nThe sight-screens must be completely outside of the boundary at all stages of the match. On \\na small ground this may necessitate indenting the boundary in that area. If there is no white \\nline and no rope then it is helpful if at least boards or flags can be put across in front of the \\nscreen. If there is such an indentation, then it must be marked in such a way that it will \\naccommodate the screen in any possible position during the match (as shown below). The \\nboundary must not change when the screen is moved. \\n \\nObstacles \\nThere may be some grounds, in company with the famous one at Canterbury, where there is \\na tree entirely within the field of play. The umpires must decide before the toss whether or \\nnot fixed objects are to be regarded as a boundary. Local custom often dictates this. Having \\nmade a decision, they will inform the captains. If such an item is to be a boundary, then as \\nsoon as the ball in play touches it, a boundary is scored and the ball, of course, becomes \\ndead. If it is not a boundary, then the ball remains in play after contact with it. Note, \\nhowever, that trees growing outside the boundary with branches overhanging the field of \\nplay are covered by the Law, since the whole of the tree is an object in contact with the \\nground outside the boundary edge\'. As soon as the ball touches any part of the tree, a \\nboundary is scored. \\n"'b"b'If the bail strikes a \\ntree which is \\noverhanging the \\nboundary, like these \\nones are, it is \\ndeemed to have \\ncrossed the \\nboundary, since the \\ntree is grounded \\noutside the \\nboundary. \\n \\nLaw 19.2.7 states that obstacles such as an animal which has run onto the pitch should not \\nautomatically be counted as a boundary. This applies similarly, in grounds that are more \\npublic, to people unconnected to the game walking across the field, or spectators spilling \\nonto the outfield. If the ball makes contact with any such animal or spectator, the Law puts \\nthe responsibility for deciding whether a boundary has been scored with the umpires. They \\nshould make the decision at that time, based on whether they believe the ball would more \\nthan likely have reached the boundary without the intervention. In some cases it will be \\nobvious; in others, it will be less so. Still, a judgment is required. \\nAllowances \\nBefore the start of the match, the umpires will determine the allowances for boundaries. \\nThe traditional allowances of four and six runs will apply in most cases. The boundary six is \\nawarded only if the ball has been struck by the bat and only if it is first grounded beyond the \\nboundary. It is permitted to agree different allowances. A nearby glass building could be a \\ngood reason for discouraging hits over the fence, by having an allowance much lower than \\nsix. In making such judgments, local customs are to be observed. \\nAlthough all determinations about boundary allowances are by the umpires, they should \\nattempt to work with the captains, particularly where local customs exist. Most importantly, \\nonce agreement is made, the scorers must be Informed. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Scoring a boundary \\n \\nA boundary will be scored whenever the ball makes contact with the boundary or with the \\nground outside the field of play, beyond the boundary edge, or anything in contact with the \\nground outside the field of play including trees and fielders. Where there is a physical \\nmarking - be it white line, rope, fence, tufted grass or whatever - it is to be appreciated that \\nonly the innermost edge of the object is the boundary edge. Hence, every part of the \\nmarking other than this innermost edge is outside the field of play. A ball landing on the \\nboundary line or rope, or hitting a boundary fence has made contact with something \\noutside the boundary. \\nIf a fielder, who has any part of his/her person in contact with the ball, touches or goes \\nbeyond the boundary edge, it is to be considered that the ball itself has done so. A boundary \\nwill therefore have been scored - whether a boundary 4 or a boundary 6 will depend on if \\nthe ball has been grounded or not before coming into contact with the fielder. \\nA fielder is not within the field of play if he/she is either touching the boundary or grounded \\nbeyond the boundary Therefore, even if the fielder is standing with both feet Inside the \\nboundary line, he/she can be \'not within the field of play - for example, if the fielder is \\ntouching the boundary fence. Conversely, it is possible for a fielder to handle the ball, or \\neven take a catch, when he/she is wholly or partly outside the boundary as long as he/she is \\neither standing Inside the field of play, merely leaning over but not touching the boundary, \\nor, in some cases, even in the air beyond the boundary (further explanation of this later).  \\nOnce the ball is grounded over the boundary, on its own or in contact with a fielder, a \\nboundary is scored and the ball is dead - whatever happens after: \\nThe taking of a catch while going outside the boundary has become both more \\ncommonplace, and more complicated in recent years - but the Law has been Simplified and \\nrationalised - It is now wholly, and clearly, set out in this Law, rather than across Laws 19 \\nand 32, as was previously the case. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'The fielder has both his feet \\ninside the boundary, but his \\nknee is in contact with the \\nfence. The fielder and \\ntherefore the ball, are thus \\ngrounded outside the \\nboundary \\n \\nThe important moment, for any fielder, is the last time they were touching the ground \\nbefore they FIRST touch the ball. If that is within the field of play, and they never touch the \\nball whilst grounded outside the field of play, they may fairly go over the boundary, and \\ntouch the ball, in order to prevent it crossing the boundary, or to complete a catch. \\nThat applies not only to the first fielder to touch the ball, but to any fielder involved in the \\ntaking of a catch - the first time each fielder touches the ball they must be grounded wholly \\ninside the boundary, or, if they are in the air, they must have taken off from wholly within \\nthe boundary. Of course, for a catch to be completed the ball must never touch the ground, \\ninside or outside the boundary, and no fielder must ever be grounded outside the boundary \\nwhile in contact with the ball. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b" \\nThe ball is over the boundary, but \\nit is not grounded Because the \\nfielder is grounded fully inside the \\nrope, neither he nor the ball are \\ndeemed \\nto \\nbe \\noutside \\nthe \\nboundary. \\nWhile the fielder will catch the ball \\nbefore it crosses the rope, because his \\nfoot is touching the boundary, he and \\nthe ball will be deemed to be \\ngrounded outside. \\nUmpires must be clear about the meaning of all the phrases in this Law. The Law defines in \\ndetail what is meant by \'touching the boundary\' and \'grounded beyond the boundary. They \\nare crucial to the scoring of a boundary and the runs awarded for it. Note, in particular, that \\n\'pitching beyond the boundary\' does not necessarily imply landing on the ground, as long as \\nthere is contact with some object that is in contact with ground in the world outside the \\nfield of play. A ball landing on the pavilion roof has pitched beyond the boundary. In the \\nsame way, until the ball comes into contact with something beyond the boundary, a \\nboundary has not been scored. The ball is still in play. It can be fielded or even caught if \\nother conditions are right. \\n"'b"b' \\nThe ball has passed over the fence; it has not \\nyet touched anything grounded outside. A \\nboundary has not been scored. The ball is \\nstill in play. \\nThe same is true even though it has passed \\nthrough a fence. \\n \\n \\nIt does not \\nmatter \\nthat \\nthe ball has \\ncrossed \\nthe \\nbound \\nary. \\nNeither \\nthe \\nball nor the \\nfielder \\nis \\ngrounded \\nbeyond \\nthe \\nboundary; \\nthe ball is still \\nin play and \\ncan \\nbe \\nfielded \\nor \\ncaught. \\n \\nRuns scored \\nAlthough Law 19.6 is of principal interest to scorers, umpires must also note and understand \\nits provisions, since they have to satisfy themselves as to the correctness of the Scores. \\nIf the ball reaches the boundary when No ball or Wide has been called, then the boundary \\nallowance is added to the one run penalty. For a Wide, this will be scored as five Wides, \\nthough for a No ball, everything aside from the one run penalty will be scored as runs, Byes \\nor Leg byes.  \\nIf the batsmen run more than the number allowed for the boundary, before the ball reaches \\nit, then they will score as many as they have run. It should be noted that the run in progress \\n'"b'b"will count as one run if they had already crossed by the time the ball reached the boundary. \\nIf, for instance, they had run four and crossed on the fifth at the moment the ball crossed \\nthe line, not only would they have all five runs, but the score would no longer be regarded \\nas a boundary (although the ball would nevertheless become dead), and they would not \\nreturn to their original ends. Any penalties would not be affected. \\n \\nOverthrows \\nIn the normal situation, when a boundary is scored, the boundary allowance replaces any \\nruns that the batsmen may have completed. Exceptionally, if they have run and crossed on \\nmore runs than the boundary allowance, these runs replace the allowance, as discussed \\nabove. If, however, the ball crosses the boundary as the result of an overthrow, both the \\nbatsmen\'s runs and the allowance are scored. \\nAn overthrow is difficult to define, but easy to see on the field. Generally speaking, it is a \\nthrow by a fielder that gives the batsmen opportunity to score further runs. A ball thrown \\nby a fielder that goes over the boundary, whether thrown to hit the stumps and missing, or \\nslipping out of his/her hand and going over the boundary behind him/ her, is a boundary \\noverthrow. If there is an obstacle within the field of play that has been designated a \\nboundary, a fielder\'s throw hitting it will be a boundary overthrow. If there is a tree \\noverhanging the boundary, for example, and the fielder\'s throw just touches a branch on \\nthe way to the wicket-keeper, that will be a boundary overthrow. \\nOften the ball will travel a long way from the throw before it reaches the boundary. \\nMeanwhile the batsmen may have completed further runs. The runs that count and the \\nends to which the batsmen are to go are determined by where they were when the ball was \\nthrown - another important reason for the umpires to watch for the batsmen crossing on \\nevery run. There may be more than one throw. A fielder throws the ball and misses the \\nstumps. The batsmen run one. Another fielder picks up the ball on the far side and throws it. \\nThis time it goes to the boundary unhindered. The counting of runs and decision on going \\nback not is to be taken from the moment of the last throw - the second one in this example. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Wilful act of fielder \\nIt is rare for a fielder wilfully to kick the ball over the boundary in the mistaken belief that by \\ngiving away a boundary he/she can dictate which batsman will take next strike. \\nNevertheless, if he/she does so, then it counts as a boundary overthrow exactly as above. \\nThe words wilful and act are both significant. It will not count as a boundary overthrow if \\nthe fielder accidentally pushes the ball over, nor if he/she deliberately refrains from \\nstopping the ball to let it cross the boundary under its own impetus. \\nThere can occasionally be confusion if a fielder, believing he/she has taken a catch, throws \\nthe ball over the boundary in celebration. If the catch had indeed been completed, then it \\nwill stand. However, if, unnoticed by the fielder, the umpire had called No ball, then the ball \\nis still in play, and the throwing of it over the boundary is a wilful act. Thus, overthrows \\nwould be awarded - the only way that \'a boundary 6\' overthrows can be scored - as well as \\nthe runs completed, the run in progress if the batsmen had crossed before the throw, and \\nthe one run penalty for the No ball. \\nField technique  \\n1. Judging a boundary \\nIt will often be difficult for an umpire to know whether the ball actually reached and crossed \\nthe boundary edge or not. He/she will be a long way from it. His/her colleague may have \\nbeen nearer to that bit of boundary and could help - and on occasion, fielders may also be \\nhelpful, although it is not sensible to overly rely on their evidence. It is prudent, when \\ninspecting the ground before the match, to note any landmarks near the boundary, such as \\nadvertisement boards, seating, flagpoles etc, which help to pinpoint where the line actually \\nis. The umpire must do his/her best, using all evidence available. \\n2. Signalling to the scorers \\nWhen a boundary is scored, there may be other signals to be made to the scorers as well as \\nthe boundary. Perhaps a No ball has been called or there are other penalties. Although for a \\nsix or a Wide there will be no ambiguity, the scorers will certainly want to know in the case \\nof a No ball whether the ball came off the bat or not.  \\nIf a boundary results from a No ball then, after repeating the No ball signal, a further signal \\nof Bye, Leg bye, or no further signal at all, followed by the boundary signal, will clearly \\ncommunicate what has happened. \\nThe umpire should make each signal separately, in the order that events took place, so, if for \\nexample a No ball that hits the pad and goes to the boundary for four Leg byes, the umpire \\nwill first signal No ball, then Leg byes, and then the boundary. \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 20 DEAD BALL \\n20.1 Ball is dead \\n20.1.1 \\nThe ball becomes dead when \\n20.1.1.1 \\nit is finally settled in the hands of the wicket-keeper or of the \\nbowler.  \\n20.1.1.2 \\na boundary is scored. See Law 19.7 (Runs scored from \\nboundaries). \\n20.1.1.3 \\na batsman is dismissed. The ball will be deemed to be dead \\nfrom the instant of the incident causing the dismissal. \\n20.1.1.4 \\nwhether played or not it becomes trapped between the bat \\nand person of a batsman or between items of his/her clothing or \\nequipment. \\n20.1.1.5 \\nwhether played or not it lodges in the clothing or equipment \\nof a batsman or the clothing of an umpire, \\n20.1.1.6 \\nunder either of Laws 24.4 (Player returning without \\npermission) or 28.2 (Fielding the ball), there is an offence resulting in \\nan award of Penalty runs. The ball shall not count as one of the over. \\n20.1.1.7 \\nthere is contravention of Law 28.3 (Protective helmets \\nbelonging to the fielding side) \\n20.1.1.8 \\nthe match is concluded in any of the ways stated in Law 12.9 \\n(Conclusion of match).  \\n20.1.2 \\nThe ball shall be considered to be dead when it is clear to the bowler\'s \\nend umpire that the fielding side and both batsmen at the wicket have \\nceased to regard it as in play. \\n \\n20.2 Ball finally settled \\nWhether the ball is finally settled or not is a matter for the umpire alone to decide \\n \\n20.3 Call of Over or Time \\nNeither the call of Over (see Law 174), nor the call of Time (see Law 12.2) is to be made until \\nthe ball is dead either under 20.1 or under 20.4 \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"20.4 Umpire calling and signalling Dead ball \\n20.4.1 \\nWhen the ball has become dead under 20.1, the bowler\'s end umpire \\nmay call and signal Dead ball if it is necessary to inform the players. \\n20.4.2 \\nEither umpire shall call and signal Dead ball when  \\n \\n20.4.2.1 \\nintervening in a case of unfair play.  \\n20.4.2.2 \\na possibly serious injury to a player or umpire occurs. \\n20.4.2.3 \\nleaving his/her normal position for consultation. \\n20.4.2.4 \\none or both bails fall from the striker\'s wicket before the striker has \\nhad the opportunity of playing the ball. \\n20.4.2.5 \\nthe striker is not ready for the delivery of the ball and, if the ball is \\ndelivered, makes no attempt to play it. Provided the umpire is satisfied that \\nthe striker had adequate reason for not being ready, the ball shall not count \\nas one of the over. \\n20.4.2.6 \\nthe striker is distracted by any noise or movement or in any other way \\nwhile preparing to receive, or receiving a delivery. This shall apply whether \\nthe source of the distraction is within the match or outside it. Note also \\n20.4.2.7. The ball shall not count as one of the over. \\n20.4.2.7 \\nthere is an instance of a deliberate attempt to distract under either of \\nLaws 41.4 (Deliberate attempt to distract striker) or 41.5 (Deliberate \\ndistraction, deception or obstruction of batsman). The ball shall not count as \\none of the over.  \\n20.4.2.8 \\nthe bowler drops the ball accidentally before delivery . \\n20.4.2.9 \\nthe ball does not leave the bowler\'s hand for any reason other than \\nan attempt to run out the non-striker under Law 41.16 (Non-striker leaving \\nhis/her ground early). \\n20.4.2.10 satisfied that the ball in play cannot be recovered. \\n20.4.2.11 required to do so under any of the Laws not included above. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"20.5 Ball ceases to be dead \\nThe ball ceases to be dead - that is, it comes into play - when the bowler starts his/her run-\\nup or if there is no run-up, starts his/her bowling action. \\n \\n20.6 Dead ball; ball counting as one of over \\n20.6.1 \\nWhen a ball which has been delivered is called dead or is to be considered \\ndead then, other than as in 20.6.2  \\n20.6.1.1 \\nit will not count in the over if the striker has not had an opportunity to \\nplay it. \\n20.6.1.2 \\nunless No ball or Wide ball has been called it will be a valid ball if the \\nstriker has had an opportunity to play it, except in the circumstances of \\n20.4.2.6 and Laws 244 (Player returning without permission), 28.2 (Fielding \\nthe ball), 41.4 (Deliberate attempt to distract striker) and 41.5 (Deliberate \\ndistraction, deception or obstruction of batsman). \\n20.6.2 \\nIn 20.4.25, the ball will not count in the over only if both conditions of not \\nattempting to play the ball and having an adequate reason for not being ready are \\nmet. Otherwise the delivery will be a valid ball. \\nPlay is in progress for the whole period from a call of Play until the next call of Time, as \\nexplained in Law 12. The ball is not continuously in play - the opposite of being dead (the \\nword \'alive\', to mean the opposite of dead, never appears in the Laws of Cricket) - during \\nthis period. The ball is brought into play each time a bowler starts his/her run-up (or action, \\nin the absence of a run up) to deliver the ball. It remains in play until all activity from that \\ndelivery is halted (either automatically or by action from the umpires), and is then dead until \\nnext brought into play for the following delivery. When the ball is dead, even though play is \\nin progress, runs cannot be scored and batsmen cannot be dismissed. It is, however, not \\nquite a total absence of activity. Exceptions include that an appeal can be made while the \\nball is dead, and some breaches of Laws which give rise to 5Penalty runs or breaches under \\nLaw 42, which can take place whilst the ball is dead will still apply. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Dead ball \\nThere are three ways that the ball becomes dead. \\n1. Automatically \\nLaw 20.1.1 lists incidents in which the ball becomes dead without action by the umpire. \\nSometimes, however, the players, or the other umpire, will not be aware that the ball has \\nbecome dead and it is then sensible for the umpire to call and signal Dead ball. This is \\nspecifically permitted in Law 20.4.1. \\nUsually it will be entirely clear when the ball has become automatically dead - if the ball \\ncrosses the boundary (the moment at which the ball crosses the boundary is laid down in \\nLaw 19) or the batsman is out bowled. However, there are three instances in which it may \\nbe more complicated: \\n(i) The umpire will have to judge when the ball is finally settled in the hands of the wicket-\\nkeeper or the bowler.
