Obvious situations are: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the bowler receiving the ball from a fielder and starting to walk back to his/her mark  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the wicket-keeper taking the ball after it has passed the batsman and tossing it \\nwithout any urgency to a fielder. \\nNot all situations are as clear cut as these Normally, the urgency or otherwise with which \\nthe bowler or wicket-keeper acts will be a good guide However, it is the umpire\'s opinion, \\nnot the players, that matters here. If the umpire considers the ball is finally settled, then it is \\ndead, even if one or more of the players subsequently believe it to be in play (a key \\ndistinction with Law 20.1.2, discussed below). \\n \\n(ii) Law 20.1.2 explains when the ball may become dead automatically if all the players cease \\nto regard it as in play. Note that it is still the judgment of the bowler\'s end umpire that \\nmatters, but now that umpire is deciding not whether the ball has finally settled, but \\nwhether all the players believe the ball is dead. In that case, the ball is dead, even if the \\numpire him/ herself would not have deemed it finally settled. This can get complicated, but \\nthe principle remains that if a single player on the field believes the ball is still in play, then it \\ncannot be dead under this part of the Law (though, for clarity, it should be re-stated that it \\ncould be dead if finally settled under 20.1.1.1). This is particularly important at the end of a \\nmatch. If the fielding team believe they have won, but the batting side continue running, \\nthen Law 20.1.2 cannot be used to determine that the ball is dead. The fact that the \\nbatsmen have continued means not all the players regard it is as dead (though, again, it may \\nbe dead under 20.1.1.1 or 20.1.1.8). Often a batsman will seek clarification from the fielder \\nholding the ball that he/she considers the ball is dead - a nod of approval, for example - \\nbefore leaving his/her ground. Should this approval be given, it is reasonable for the \\n"'b'b\'batsman to consider the ball dead, the fielder with the ball having accounted for his/her \\nteam as a whole. \\n(iii)Law 31 defines the difference between being out and being dismissed. If the batsman is \\nout but not dismissed, the ball will remain in play. If the batsman is dismissed, the ball will \\nhave been dead from the moment he/she was out. For example, there is an appeal for LBW. \\nIf the appeal is upheld, but not otherwise, the ball will be dead from the moment the ball \\nmade contact with the striker\\\'s person, even though by the time the umpire has weighed up \\nthe evidence and made his/her decision a second or two has elapsed. Any run attempted \\nduring that second or two will not be allowed and the not out batsman should be returned \\nto his/her original end. \\nNB: Law 20.1.1.4 and Law 20.1.1.5 talk about the ball being "trapped", and the ball "lodging" \\nrespectively. There is a difference between becoming trapped and lodging. If the ball is \\ntrapped - say between bat and pad - it could possibly fall out. In order to decide that it is \\ntrapped, the umpire must be sure that there has been a period of time, even if it is only a \\nvery short one, during which the ball was held there. Lodging - say in the top of a batsman\\\'s \\npad - means that it will take positive action to free it. \\n \\n \\n\''b'b\'It should also be noted here that the ball lodging in the worn helmet or equipment of a \\nfielder or the wicket-keeper is not Dead. \\n2. To be called Dead by an umpire \\nLaw 20.4.2 lists incidents in which an umpire is to call and signal Dead ball, to make the ball \\ndead. In each case it is to be \\\'either umpire. It will be the umpire who first sees the incident. \\nThe reason for calling Dead ball is to prevent further action of any kind by either side. Such \\nfurther action might be, for example, a fielder attempting to dismiss a batsman. Again, most \\nof these are self-explanatory and some are dealt with elsewhere but as some need further \\nattention it is worth going through this section of the Law clause by clause. \\n20.4.2.1 \\nIntervening in a case of unfair play is dealt with in Law 41 and for \\nunacceptable conduct under Law 42. \\n20.4.2.2 \\nIf either umpire thinks that an injury might be serious, he/she must \\ncall and signal Dead ball straightaway. Umpires are not usually in a position to \\nknow for sure whether someone has sustained a serious injury or not. It is for \\nthis reason that this Law provides for a call and signal of Dead ball in the \\nevent of a "possibly" serious injury. \\n20.4.2.3 \\nIf there is the need for umpires to consult, and one umpire decides \\nthat signals between him/ herself and the other umpire are not enough to \\nresolve any doubt, he/she may go to meet his/her colleague. If the doubt \\nrelates to an appeal, that will not yet been answered, so it might mean that \\nthe ball is not yet dead. Therefore any potential unfair action must be pre-\\nempted; before leaving his/her position, the umpire must call and signal \\nDead ball.  \\n20.4.2.4 \\nIf one or both bails fall from the striker\\\'s wicket, it is not intact and \\nwill disadvantage the fielding side in attempting a dismissal.  \\n20.4.2.5 \\nLaw 20.4.2.5 relates only to the striker not being ready. If he/she is \\ndistracted at any point after the ball comes into play, 20.4.2.6 applies. These \\ntwo clauses have similarities, and are often confused, but must be treated \\nseparately. If, in spite of drawing back to indicate that he/she was not ready, \\nthe striker attempts to play the ball, he/she will lose the protection afforded \\nby this piece of Law. Despite this, the umpire should not wait to see if the \\nstriker does play at the ball - if the striker backs away, the umpire should call \\nand signal Dead ball as soon as possible. The umpire cannot undo this call, so \\nit is only if, mistakenly, the umpire has not made the call immediately, and \\nthen the batsman plays at it, that the proviso about a batsman playing the \\nball will come into force. The umpire will often not know why the striker was \\nnot ready but should call and signal Dead ball nevertheless, to prevent an \\nunfair dismissal, in case the withdrawal was justified. The umpires might, in \\nsome exceptional circumstances, form the opinion that it was a ruse by the \\n\''b'b"striker to waste time. This would be a serious act of unfair play to be dealt \\nwith as laid down in Law 41.10. \\n20.4.2.6 \\nThe striker is considered to be preparing to receive the ball as soon as \\nit comes into play and has not finished receiving it until he/ she has played or \\nplayed at it or has had the opportunity to play it. Anything that the umpire \\nconsiders has distracted the striker during this period requires a call and \\nsignal of Dead ball. The distraction may be innocent - a sudden yelp from a \\nclose fielder stung by a bee, or a bird flying across the pitch. It may even not \\nbe connected with the game, coming from outside the boundary or \\noverhead. On the other hand, it can be a deliberate attempt by a fielder to \\nunsettle the striker in which case it is covered by 20.4.2.7. Whatever the \\ncause, the first response is to call and signal Dead ball. Further action will \\nhave to follow if the distraction was a deliberate one. \\n20.4.2.7 \\nLaw 41.4 deals with deliberate attempts to distract the striker and \\nLaw 41.5 with deliberate attempt to distract, deceive or obstruct either \\nbatsman. They are discussed more fully under Law 41. The point to note here \\nis that they are two of the six exceptions to the general principle that a ball \\ncalled or becoming dead will count in the over if the striker had the \\nopportunity to play it. Law 20.6 lists all these six exceptions. \\n20.4.2.8 \\nThis is usually self-evident and normally it is clear when the ball has \\nbeen dropped and not delivered. However, sometimes if before the usual \\npoint of delivery the ball is released accidentally by the bowler, but still \\ntravels forward, it will be difficult for the umpire to decide if it has been \\ndropped - and is thus dead - or is a delivery. Often these \'deliveries\' are a long \\nway from the striker and would be a Wide or even a No ball - it is for the \\numpire to determine whether they consider the ball was actually delivered. If \\nthe ball is released at the right place and time but goes astray, it should be \\ntreated as being delivered. If something is clearly \'wrong\' about the release, \\nparticularly if it is done too early, the umpire should call and signal Dead ball.  \\n20.4.2.9 \\nThe ball is to be called Dead immediately if the bowler does not let go \\nof it during the delivery. However, in attempting to run out the non-striker, \\nthe bowler may try to put down the non-striker\'s wicket with the ball in hand, \\nrather than throwing it at the stumps. Dead ball is not to be called unless the \\nattempt fails as described in Law 41.16. Or perhaps the bowler may stop in \\nhis/her run up, or go through their whole action and then not release the \\nball. Then Dead ball is to be called. A particular case relating to the striker \\nputting his/her own wicket down and the bowler subsequently not releasing \\nthe ball, is dealt with under Law 35.2. \\n20.4.2.10 The ball in play will be called Dead when the umpire decides it cannot \\nbe recovered within the field of play. This is a rare event even in recreational \\ncricket, when the ball might fall down a football goalpost hole or an animal\'s \\n"'b'b"burrow, and is unlikely to occur on modern grounds and even less often the \\nhigher up the game you go. The inclusion of this Dead ball situation replaces \\nthe old \'Lost ball\' scenario.  \\n20.4.2.11 Other Laws in which an umpire is required to call and signal Dead ball \\nare listed below. In each case it is stated in the Law whether either umpire is \\nto make the call and signal or whether a particular umpire is to do so. \\nLaw 17.8   Bowler incapacitated or suspended during an over  \\nLaw 21.8   Ball coming to rest in front of striker\'s wicket \\nLaw 21.9   Fielder intercepting a delivery  \\nLaw 23.3   Leg byes not to be awarded  \\nLaw 25.7   Restriction on the striker\'s runner \\nLaw 27.4   Movement by wicket-keeper  \\nLaw 28.5   Restrictions on actions of wicket-keeper \\nLaw 28.6   Movement by any fielder other than the wicket-keeper  \\nLaw 37.7   Batsman leaving the wicket under a misapprehension  \\nLaw 34.4   Runs permitted from ball lawfully struck more than once  \\nLaw 35.1.2   Out Hit wicket \\nLaw 35.2   Not out Hit wicket \\nLaw 41.2   Unfair actions  \\nLaw 41.9   Time wasting by the fielding side \\nLaw 41.15   Striker in protected area  \\nLaw 41.16   Non-striker leaving his/her ground early \\nLaw 41.17   Batsmen stealing a run  \\nLaw 42.1   Unacceptable conduct \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Whether ball is to count or not \\nIt is important that the umpire knows when 6 valid balls have been bowled, so that he/she \\ndoes sot allow too many or too few deliveries in an over. The final section of this Law states \\nquite clearly when a ball that has become or been made dead is valid and when it is not. The \\nbasic determining factor is whether or not the striker has had the opportunity to play the \\nball There are, as noted earlier (under Law 20.4.2.7), six exceptions to this principle. They \\nare listed in Law 206. Each is also noted in its own Law, and Law 20.1 and Law 20.4. Umpires \\nmust know what these exceptions are. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"LAW 21 NO BALL \\n21.1 Mode of delivery \\n21.1.1 \\nThe umpire shall ascertain whether the bowler intends to bowl right handed \\nor left handed over or round the wicket, and shall so inform the striker. \\nIt is unfair if the bowler fails to notify the umpire of a change in his/her mode of \\ndelivery. In this case the umpire shall call and signal No ball. \\n21.1.2 \\nUnderarm bowling shall not be permitted except by special agreement \\nbefore the match \\n21.2 Fair delivery - the arm \\n  \\nFor a delivery to be fair in respect of the arm the ball must not be thrown. \\n  \\nA ball is fairly delivered in respect of the arm if, once the bowler\'s arm has reached \\nthe level of the shoulder in the delivery swing, the elbow joint is not straightened \\npartially or completely from that instant until the ball has left the hand. This \\ndefinition shall not debar a bowler from flexing or rotating the wrist in the delivery \\nswing. \\n  \\nAlthough it is the primary responsibility of the striker\'s end umpire to assess the \\nfairness of a delivery in this respect, there is nothing in this Law to debar the \\nbowler\'s end umpire from calling and signalling No ball if he/she considers that the \\nball has been thrown. \\n21.3 Ball thrown or delivered underarm - action by umpires  \\n21.3.1 \\nAfter the bowler has entered his/her delivery stride, if in the opinion of either \\numpire, the ball has been thrown or, unless permitted by agreement in 21.1.2. \\ndelivered underarm, that umpire shall call and signal No ball and when the ball is \\ndead, inform the other umpire of the reason for the call. \\nThe bowler\'s end umpire shall then \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 warn the bowler indicating that this is a first and final warning. This warning \\nshall apply to that bowler throughout the innings.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the captain of the fielding side of the reason for this action. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the batsmen at the wicket of what has occurred. \\n21.3.2 \\nIf either umpire considers that, in that innings, a further delivery by the same \\nbowler is thrown or, unless permitted by agreement in 21.1.2. delivered underarm, \\nhe/she shall call and signal No ball and when the ball is dead inform the other \\numpire of the reason for the call. The bowler\'s end umpire shall then \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 direct the captain of the fielding side to suspend the bowler immediately \\nfrom bowling. The over shall, if applicable, be completed by another bowler, \\nwho shall neither have bowled the previous over or part thereof nor be \\n"'b'b"allowed to bowl any part of the next over. The bowler thus suspended shall \\nnot bowl again in that innings. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the batsmen at the wicket and, as soon as practicable, the captain of \\nthe batting side of the reason for this action. \\n21.3.3 \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after \\nthe match to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body \\nresponsible for the match, who shall take such action as is considered appropriate \\nagainst the captain, any other individuals concerned and, if appropriate, the team. \\n \\n21.4 Bowler throwing towards striker\'s end before delivery \\nIf the bowler throws the ball towards the striker\'s end before entering the delivery stride, \\neither umpire shall call and signal No ball. See Law 41.17 (Batsmen stealing a run). However, \\nthe procedure stated in 21.3 of first and final warning, informing, action against the bowler \\nand reporting shall not apply. \\n21.5 Fair delivery - the feet \\nFor a delivery to be fair in respect of the feet, in the delivery stride \\n21.5.1 \\nthe bowler\'s back foot must land within and not touching the return crease \\nappertaining to his/her stated mode of delivery.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the bowler\'s front foot must land with some part of the foot, whether grounded or \\nraised \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 on the same side of the imaginary line joining the two middle stumps as the return \\ncrease described in 21.5.1. and \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 behind the popping crease \\nIf the bowler\'s end umpire is not satisfied that all of these three conditions have been met \\nhe/she shall call and signal No ball. See Law 418 (Bowling of deliberate front foot No ball). \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"21.6 Bowler breaking wicket in delivering ball  \\nIf the ball is delivered and if the non-striker is not dismissed under Law 4116 (Non-striker \\nleaving his/her ground early), either umpire shall call and signal No ball if the bowler breaks \\nthe wicket at any time after the ball comes into play and before completion of the stride \\nafter the delivery stride. This shall include any clothing or other object that falls from his/her \\nperson and breaks the wicket. See Appendix A.12 Laws 20428, 20.4.29 (Umpire calling and \\nsignalling Dead ball) and 21.12 will apply. \\n21.7 Ball bouncing more than once, rolling along the ground or pitching off the pitch  \\nThe umpire shall call and signal No ball if a ball which he/she considers have been delivered, \\nwithout having previously touched bat or person of the striker.  \\nbounces more than once or rolls along the ground before it reaches the popping crease \\nor \\npitches wholly or partially off the pitch as defined in Law 6.1 (Area of pitch) before it reaches \\nthe line of the striker\'s wicket. When a non-turf pitch is being used, this will apply to any ball \\nthat wholly or partially pitches off the artificial surface. \\n \\n21.8 Ball coming to rest in front of striker\'s wicket \\nIf a ball delivered by the bowler comes to rest in front of the line of the striker\'s wicket, \\nwithout having previously touched the bat or person of the striker the umpire shall call and \\nsignal No ball and immediately call and signal Dead ball. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"21.9 Fielder intercepting a delivery \\nIf except in the circumstances of Law 273 (Position of wicket-keeper), a ball delivered by the \\nbowler makes contact with any part of a fielder\'s person before it either makes contact with \\nthe striker\'s bat or person, or it passes the striker\'s wicket, the umpire shall call and signal \\nNo ball and immediately call and signal Dead ball. \\n \\n21.10 Ball bouncing over head height of striker \\nThe umpire shall call and signal No ball for any delivery which, after pitching, passes or \\nwould have passed over head height of the striker standing upright at the popping crease. \\n \\n21.11 Call of No ball for infringement of other Laws \\nIn addition to the instances above, No ball is to be called and signalled as required by the \\nfollowing Laws. \\nLaw 27.3 - Position of wicket-keeper \\nLaw 28.4 - Limitation of on side fielders \\nLaw 28.5 - Fielders not to encroach on pitch \\nLaw 41.6 - Bowling of dangerous and unfair short pitched deliveries \\nLaw 41.7 - Bowling of dangerous and unfair non-pitching deliveries  \\nLaw 41.8 - Bowling of deliberate front foot No ball \\n \\n21.12 Revoking a call of No ball \\nAn umpire shall revoke the call of No ball if Dead ball is called under any of Laws 20.4.2.4, \\n20.4.2.5. 20.4.2.6, 20.4.2.8 or 20.4.2.9 (Umpire calling and signalling Dead ball). \\n21.13 No ball to over-ride Wide \\nA call of No ball shall over-ride the call of Wide ball at any time. See Laws 22.1 (Judging a \\nWide) and 22.2 (Call and signal of Wide ball). \\n \\n21.14 Ball not dead \\nThe ball does not become dead on the call of No ball. \\n"'b"b'21.15 Penalty for a No ball \\nA penalty of one run shall be awarded instantly on the call of No ball. Unless the call is \\nrevoked, the penalty shall stand even if a batsman is dismissed. It shall be in addition to any \\nother runs scored, any boundary allowance and any other runs awarded for penalties. \\n \\n21.16 Runs resulting from a No ball - how scored \\nThe one run penalty shall be scored as a No ball extra and shall be debited against the \\nbowler. If other Penalty runs have been awarded to either side these shall be scored as \\nstated in Law 41.18 (Penalty runs). Any runs completed by the batsmen or any boundary \\nallowance shall be credited to the striker if the ball has been struck by the bat; otherwise \\nthey shall also be scored as Byes or Leg byes as appropriate. \\n \\n21.17 No ball not to count \\nA No ball shall not count as one of the over. See Law 17.3 (Validity of balls). \\n21.18 Out from a No ball \\nWhen No ball has been called, neither batsman shall be out under any of the Laws except 34 \\n(Hit the ball twice), 37 (Obstructing the field) or 38 (Run out). \\nA No ball is a delivery which places the batsman receiving the ball at some disadvantage or \\nis potentially dangerous. The offences which merit a call of No ball are all concerned with \\nthe delivery of the ball: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 unfairness by the bowler in the actual delivery  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 where the fielders are at the instant of delivery \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 how the ball behaves after it is delivered. \\n \\nUnfairness by the bowler in the actual delivery. \\n1. Mode of delivery \\nWhen a bowler first comes on to bowl, the umpire must find out what his/her mode of \\ndelivery is and tell the striker. The terms over the wicket and round the wicket are explained \\nin Appendix A.9.1. Some bowlers have most of their run up crossing diagonally behind the \\numpire from one side to the other. What counts is which side he/she is when passing the \\nwicket. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b" \\nWhen the bowler wishes to change his/her mode of delivery after commencing that spell, \\nhe/she is responsible for advising the umpire as to his/her mode of delivery. If a bowler \\nchanges their mode of action without such notification, the umpire is to call and signal No \\nball as soon as the bowler delivers the ball. \\n2. The bowler\'s feet \\nAlthough this is not next in the Law, it is appropriate to consider the bowler\'s feet alongside \\nthe mode of delivery This Law applies to the bowler\'s delivery stride- there is one restriction \\non the back foot and two restrictions on the front foot. It should be noted that the foot, in \\nthis case, means the bottom of the foot, which in reality is the underside of the shoe. It is \\nquite possible that, should the bottom of the front foot land in front of, and not touching, \\nthe popping crease, some of the ankle or a protrusion on the back of the boot might be \\nbehind the crease - this is not enough to make it a legal delivery. \\nThese restrictions are set out clearly in 21.5. They are Independent of each other. For the \\nback foot, its position in relation to the popping crease is immaterial. Equally it does not \\nmatter if the front foot crosses the return crease. A simple description is that, as each foot \\nlands, the back foot must not have any part grounded on or beyond the return crease; the \\nfront foot must have some part of the underside of the boot behind the popping crease, \\neven if that part of the foot is raised. The front foot must also have some part on the correct \\nside of an imaginary line joining the two middle stumps. Any movement of either foot after \\nit has landed is Irrelevant. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"The bowler is bowling \\nleft arm over the wicket. \\nThis is a No ball as the \\nbowler\'s front foot is \\nentirely the wrong side \\nof the middle stump. \\n \\nIn every case the umpire must be satisfied of the legality of where the bowler\'s feet land in \\nthe delivery stride. It is not an offence for a bowler to bowl from behind the umpire - this \\nwill not happen often, but does occur from time to time. In those situations the umpire \\ncannot see the bowler\'s back foot, so must make a judgment about whether he/she is \\nsatisfied that the bowler\'s back foot has landed inside the return crease. \\n \\nA fair delivery with respect to the \\nfeet. The back foot has landed \\nwithin the return crease: the \\nfront foot has landed with a \\n(raised) part behind the popping \\ncrease \\n \\n \\n \\nA No ball. The front foot has \\nlanded with heel on the crease \\nmarking. No part of it is behind \\nthe crease. \\n"'b'b" \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nthe back foot \\nhas landed \\nearly Although \\nOverhanging \\nthe realm \\nCrease, it is not \\ntouching it. \\n \\nAn umpire must be aware that the bowler is running up and be prepared to call and signal \\nNo ball immediately if he/ she sees one of the bowler\'s feet incorrectly placed. If the bowler \\nbowls a ball from behind the umpire such that the back foot is out of view and the umpire is \\nnot satisfied that it landed within the return crease, the umpire should call and signal No \\nball. \\nThere may be rare occasions when the umpire believes a bowler has deliberately bowled a \\nNo ball - perhaps to deny a batsman a milestone or even to intimidate the opposition. This \\nmeets the requirements of a No ball under Law 21.5 but is a more serious example of unfair \\nplay and thus dealt with under Law 41.8. \\n3. The bowler\'s arm \\nWhether a bowler bowls the ball, which is fair, or throws it, which is unfair, has been a \\nmatter of controversy for years. The difference is in the last part of the delivery swing. Limits \\nare set out for this last part of the swing. In simple terms, a bowler\'s delivery action involves \\ntransferring his/her arm from behind the body to a point in front and then releasing the ball. \\nThis transfer is usually, though not necessarily, with the arm close to his/her head. The ball \\nmust be above shoulder level before it is released, or the delivery will be deemed unfair, as \\nunderarm bowling, which will be dealt with in the same way as throwing. \\n"'b'b"The \'last part of the swing\' that is crucial to the fairness of the delivery starts when the arm \\nreaches shoulder level behind him/her, continues as he/she brings his/her arm over, and \\nlasts until the ball is released. During that time, the bowler\'s elbow joint must not straighten \\nor partially straighten. \\nMany bowlers start this part of the swing with a straight elbow and keep it so. Such bowlers \\npresent no problem. For other bowlers, however, the arm may be bent during this time. In \\norder for the delivery to be fair, it has to be judged that the arm does not straighten in the \\nbowling swing. It will usually be the striker\'s end umpire who is better placed to see this, but \\neither umpire may make the judgment. Should either of them consider that, during the final \\npart of the swing, the bowler\'s arm is bent at the elbow and, before release of the ball, it \\nbecomes straight, or even just somewhat straighter, or if the ball is delivered underarm, the \\nrelevant umpire will call and signal No ball as soon as possible. The umpire must take \\nappropriate actions including cautioning the bowler for a first offence and, if that bowler \\neither repeats such action, or bowls any further delivery underarm, will suspend the bowler \\nfor the rest of the innings. \\n \\n4. A special case \\nVery rarely, if the striker appears to be advancing well up the pitch, the bowler will try to \\nrun the striker out and is permitted to do so. I he/she throws the ball in the general \\ndirection of the striker\'s wicket, the umpire does not have to guess the bowler\'s intention. If \\nthe bowler makes such a throw after entering the delivery stride there is nothing to \\ndistinguish it from any other throw. No ball is to be called with all the consequences of that \\ncall. If however, the bowler throws before entering his/her delivery stride whilst the call of \\nNo ball is still to be made, with its one run penalty, the sanctions laid down in 21.3 are not \\nto be applied. \\nIf the batsman is not just advancing up the pitch, but the two batsmen are attempting to \\nsteal a run while the bowler is running up, Law 41.17 will apply. \\nWhere the fielders are at the instant of delivery  \\nThere are restrictions on the position of the wicket-keeper on the positions of the fielders \\non the on (leg) side of the wicket behind the popping crease and on the positions of fielders \\nclose to the pitch. These are dealt with in Laws 27 and 28. If any of these Laws are broken, \\nthere must be a call and signal of No ball, from the striker\'s end umpire in the first two cases \\nand the bowler\'s end umpire in the third. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Fielder intercepting a delivery \\nThis Law prevents any fielder, other than the wicket-keeper, from making contact with the \\nball before it has made contact with the striker\'s bat or person, or has passed the striker\'s \\nwicket. (It should not be confused with the restrictions on the wicket-keeper from taking the \\nball in front of the wicket, which is covered in Law 27.3) \\nThis might happen because a ball is bowled wide and hits a fielder who is fielding close to, \\nbut not encroaching on, the pitch. Had the ball not hit the fielder it would land off the pitch \\nand be a No ball any way. Clearly this Law also applies if the interception is deliberate, even \\nif the ball may not have reached the striker. \\nBreaking the wicket while delivering the ball  \\nLaw 21.6 is a long clause, but is actually very simple. If, in delivering the ball, the bowler\'s \\nperson, clothing or other object falling from the bowler, breaks the wicket at the bowler\'s \\nend in the run-up, delivery stride, or first foot placement of the follow-through, the umpire \\nshould call and signal No ball. This would not apply, however, if the bowler runs out, or \\nattempts to run out, a non-striker who has left his/her ground early under Law 41.16 or if \\nthe bowler drops or fails to release the ball thus making it Dead. \\n \\nHow the ball behaves after it is delivered \\nBalls which, after pitching, rear up so high as to be likely to inflict injury on the striker are \\noften known as \'bouncers. Those which, without pitching, reach the popping crease at a \\ndangerous height are usually referred to as \'beamers. They are dealt with in Laws 41.6 and \\n41.7 where they are defined, and the action to be taken if they are delivered is discussed in \\ndetail. \\nIn addition, although not dangerous, it is considered unfair if, \\n1.  before reaching the popping crease, the ball bounces more than once \\nor rolls along the ground \\nor pitches off the pitch or on or beyond the join of an artificial pitch  \\nor, 2. before reaching the line of the striker\'s wicket, the ball comes to a halt  \\nwithout in each case having touched the striker\'s bat or person. Umpires should note this \\nlast proviso - if the ball strikes the batsman before any of these events occur then these are \\nno longer relevant. \\n \\n"'b'b"3. A ball that has pitched and then passes, or would have passed above the head height of \\nthe striker, if he/she were standing upright on the popping crease, is considered unfair. \\nHowever, such deliveries may by their frequency become unreasonable and then would be \\nconsidered for further action under Law 41.6.2. \\nIn each of these three cases, the bowler\'s end umpire calls and signals No ball as the offence \\noccurs. In addition to that call, in the case of Law 21.8 (the ball coming to a halt), the \\nbowler\'s end umpire also immediately calls and signals Dead ball to prevent the striker from \\nplaying the ball once it has stopped. \\n \\nPenalties for a No ball \\nAs stated earlier, a No ball puts the striker at some disadvantage. The Law compensates the \\nbatsman in three ways. \\nA penalty of one run is awarded to the batting side in addition to any runs accruing in any \\nother way from that delivery. The one run penalty will always be scored as a No ball extra. \\nThe other runs will be recorded just as they would have been if No ball had not been called. \\nIt used to be that Byes and Leg byes scored in this scenario would be counted as part of the \\nNo ball penalty, but this is no longer the case - the scorers should note one No ball extra, \\nand as many Byes or Leg byes as would otherwise have been scored. The umpire should \\nrepeat the No ball signal to the scorers followed by the Bye or Leg bye signal as appropriate. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The striker is immune from dismissal in any of the ways for which the bowler gets \\ncredit. The three ways in which he/she can be out from a No ball are set out in 21.18. \\nUmpires and scorers must be familiar with which ones they are. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The ball does not count in the over, so (unless the umpire has mistakenly already \\nallowed the over to continue after 6 valid balls have been bowled (see Law 17.5.2)) \\nanother delivery must be made. Both umpires should adjust their counters to take \\naccount of this. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"No ball to over-ride Wide \\nIf a ball meets the conditions for both a No ball and a Wide, the call of No ball is to stand - it \\ncannot be both a Wide and a No ball. This is simply because a No ball offers a batsman \\ngreater protection, and allows the striker to hit the ball while still being protected from \\nmost forms of dismissal. So, for example, an extremely short-pitched delivery going over the \\nbatsman\'s head might attract an unorthodox, risky shot from the batsman - it is not \\nsufficiently within reach for him/her to be able to hit it with a normal cricket stroke, Even \\nthough it would otherwise meet the basic criteria for a Wide - being out of the batsman\'s \\nusual reach - the Law states that it is called a No ball so that, even if the striker makes \\ncontact, he/she is protected from being out from most forms of dismissal. Note that some \\nPlaying Conditions, most notably most international cricket, dictate that such deliveries are \\ndeemed to be Wides, rather than No balls - but this is not the case in Law. \\n \\nBall not dead \\nThe call for a No ball should be made as soon as an umpire notices that the offence has \\nbeen committed. In general the ball does not become dead on the call of No ball. However if \\nthe ball is subsequently called dead, either to protect the striker or because the ball is not \\nactually delivered, then the umpire must revoke that call. Note that if the ball is called dead \\nfor other reasons, the call of No ball will not be revoked. However, it would not be \\nappropriate to revoke a No ball call which resulted in a penalty to the batting side, or not to \\nwarn or suspend a bowler for a dangerous or unfair delivery. \\nNote that a delivery is No ball from the moment of the infringement, not the point at which \\nthe umpire calls it so. This is different from a Wide. \\n \\nField technique \\nBecause the ball does not become dead on the call of No ball, both umpires must therefore \\ncontinue to watch the course of play until the ball is dead. No signalling to the scorers is \\npossible during this time. Although the call and signal of No ball is made as soon as possible \\nafter the offence, it is to inform only the players and the other umpire. It must be loud \\nenough for everyone on the field to hear it. The signal without the call is repeated to the \\nscorers, with any others that may be needed, when the ball is dead. It of course requires \\nacknowledgement. \\n \\n"'b'b"Although much of what follows refers to action under other Laws, it is set out here as a \\ndescription of the umpires technique every time the ball comes into play, is delivered. \\nreceived and possibly played by the striker. \\n1. The striker\'s end umpire \\nBefore the ball comes into play, the striker\'s end umpire should check the positions of the \\non-side fielders, to see that there are no more than two behind the popping crease. If there \\nare, or if there is any possibility that one of the deep fielders on the on-side might stray over \\nthe line, he/she should move to the off-side. The striker\'s end umpire must not risk the \\nembarrassment of a contravention by a fielder that he/she cannot see. He/she will also \\ncheck the position of the wicket-keeper. \\nThe striker\'s end umpire will then watch the bowler\'s arm action. Whether he/she thinks it \\nis fair, or whether he/she calls and signals No ball because the ball is thrown, he/she must \\nimmediately switch attention to the striker, the creases and the stumps. If the wicket-\\nkeeper is standing close to the wicket, the striker\'s end umpire must watch him/her too. It is \\nunnecessary, and indeed can be disastrous, to watch the ball\'s travel through the air. Nor \\nshould he/she adjust his/her view to specifically watch to see if the wicket-keeper moves \\nfrom standing back to a close position. The striker\'s end umpire must fix his/her eyes ready \\nfor the arrival of the ball. Because he/she is watching that area closely, it will be obvious if \\nthe wicket-keeper has come close to the stumps from further back. He/she must be ready to \\ncall and signal No ball if there is a contravention by the fielders or the wicket-keeper. The \\nstriker\'s end umpire may need to adjudicate on a stumping or a rum out. He/she must be \\nready to assist the other umpire if necessary with information about how the wicket was \\nbroken, whether a catch carried and so on. Further stages of the striker\'s end umpire\'s role \\nin the ball is played into the outfield are discussed under Law 38. \\n \\n2. The bowlers end umpire \\n \\nAs the bowler begins his/her run-up, the umpire will check the positions of the fielders close \\nto the pitch. He/she must then be ready to watch the bowler\'s feet from when the landing \\nof the back foot starts the delivery stride until the landing of the front foot completes it. It is \\nessential that the umpire has a clear view of the landing of the bowler\'s feet, which may not \\nalways be in the same place. The umpire must control his/her breathing to be completely \\nready to call No ball at once if he/she sees either foot land illegally. He/she will signal as \\nhe/she calls. With the umpire focussing his/her eyes on the feet placement should a No ball \\nbe called here, it is completely understandable to have to revoke the call of No ball if for \\nsome reason, the bowler does not deliver the ball. The umpire should not feel embarrassed \\nin this situation as it is impossible to watch these two events simultaneously. \\n"'b'b"As soon as the umpire has seen the foot placements, whether fair or not, he/she must pick \\nup the flight of the ball as quickly as possible, in order to be able to see, and judge, events at \\nthe striker\'s end. If the umpire moves his/ her head to change view from the feet to the ball, \\nit will take a little longer for the eyes to focus on the next target. The change must be \\nachieved by merely swivelling the eyes without head movement. It is therefore usual to \\nstand some way back from the stumps. Each umpire must find the best position for \\nhim/herself.  \\nThe umpire must also be clearly aware of the line wicket to wicket, that is, the line joining \\nthe two middle stumps. To achieve this he/she should stand with the head directly in this \\nline and so that he/she can see both sets of stumps. To accommodate both these needs will \\nrequire an umpire of average height to be at least 4 feet behind the stumps, but a taller \\numpire will wish to be further back. Recently the rise in \'power-hitting\' means that many \\numpires will choose to stand further back to give themselves more time to react to, and \\nmove out of the way of, a powerful straight shot. Each umpire will choose the distance that \\nis comfortable for his/ her height, and is safe given their own reaction times. The once \\npopular method, of bending over in order to view both sets of stumps, places too much \\nstrain on the back to be sensible. Moreover it foreshortens the view of the pitch, which is \\ncertainly not desirable for judging distances. \\nAgain, the umpire\'s role in events after the potential call of No ball, until the ball is dead, is \\ndiscussed under more appropriate Laws. When the ball is dead, the relevant signals are to \\nbe made to the scorers - remembering the order for signals is, generally, the order in which \\nthe incidents happened. The responsibility for all such communication with the scorers lies \\nwith the bowler\'s end umpire. If his/her colleague at the striker\'s end has signalled a No ball, \\nthe bowler\'s end umpire is responsible for repeating that signal to the scorers when the ball \\nis Dead and may have to add signals, to indicate whether the runs are to be scored as Byes \\nor Leg byes, or to award penalties, and so on. Even if the bowler\'s end umpire has no signals \\n\'of his/her own\', he/she is still required to make the final communication to the scorers, \\nwhen the ball is dead, even for a Short run at the other end. There, the first signal is not \\nmade until the ball is dead and may be by the striker\'s end umpire, if that is the end where \\nthe short running occurred. The bowler\'s end umpire is still responsible for informing the \\nscorers as to the number of runs to be recorded, if more than one, and should repeat the \\nsignal. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 22 WIDE BALL \\n22.1 Judging a Wide \\n22.1.1 \\nIf the bowler bowls a ball, not being a No ball, the umpire shall adjudge it a \\nWide if, according to the definition in 22.1.2, the ball passes wide of where the \\nstriker is standing and which also would have passed wide of the striker standing in a \\nnormal guard position. \\n22.1.2 \\nThe ball will be considered as passing wide of the striker unless it is \\nsufficiently within reach for him/her to be able to hit it with the bat by means of a \\nnormal cricket stroke. \\n \\n22.2 Call and signal of Wide ball \\n  \\nIf the umpire adjudges a delivery to be a Wide he/she shall call and signal Wide ball \\nas soon as the ball passes the striker\'s wicket. It shall, however, be considered to \\nhave been a Wide from the instant that the bowler entered his/her delivery stride, \\neven though it cannot be called Wide until it passes the striker\'s wicket. \\n22.3 Revoking a call of Wide ball \\n22.3.1 \\nThe umpire shall revoke the call of Wide ball if there is then any contact \\nbetween the ball and the striker\'s bat or person before the ball comes into contact \\nwith any fielder. \\n22.3.2 \\nThe umpire shall revoke the call of Wide ball if a delivery is called a No ball. \\nSee Law 21.13 (No ball to over-ride Wide) \\n \\n22.4 Delivery not a Wide \\n22.4.1 \\nThe umpire shall not adjudge a delivery as being a Wide, if the striker, by \\nmoving. \\neither causes the ball to pass wide of him/her, as defined in 22.1.2 \\nor \\nbrings the ball sufficiently within reach to be able to hit it by means of a normal \\ncricket stroke. \\n22.4.2 \\nThe umpire shall not adjudge a delivery as being a Wide if the ball touches \\nthe striker\'s bat or person, but only as the ball passes the striker. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"22.5 Ball not dead \\n  \\nThe ball does not become dead on the call of Wide ball. \\n \\n22.6 Penalty for a Wide \\n  \\nA penalty of one run shall be awarded instantly on the call of Wide ball Unless the \\ncall is revoked, see 223, this penalty shall stand even if a batsman is dismissed and \\nshall be in addition to any other runs scored, any boundary allowance and any other \\nruns awarded for penalties. \\n \\n22.7 Runs resulting from a Wide - how scored \\n  \\nAll runs completed by the batsmen or a boundary allowance, together with the \\npenalty for the Wide, shall be scored as Wide balls. Apart from any award of 5 \\nPenalty runs, all runs resulting from a Wide shall be debited against the bowler. \\n \\n22.8 Wide not to count \\n  \\nWide shall not count as one of the over. A See Law 17.3 (Validity of balls). \\n \\n22.9 Out from a Wide \\n  \\nWhen Wide ball has been called, neither batsman shall be out under any of the Laws \\nexcept 35 (Hit wicket), 37 (Obstructing the field), 38 (Run out) or 39 (Stumped): \\nIn order to be fair to the striker, the bowler has to deliver the ball so that the striker can \\nreach it comfortably enough to hit it \'with a normal cricket stroke! It must be emphasised \\nthat this is all that the Wide ball Law demands. In limited overs matches of very many \\ncompetitions, there are other considerations of negative bowling and increasingly the \\naddition of marks on the bowling crease at a particular distance from the stumps. These do \\nnot apply in Law, but understanding of the Wide ball Law has been clouded because players, \\nhaving seen or played in so many limited overs matches, have had their view of Wide \\ninfluenced by those particular Playing Conditions. \\nWhat is important is consistency. Early in an innings, a batsman may be cautious about \\ntrying to reach a ball. Towards the end, when the side wants to score more quickly, the \\nsame batsman may be bolder and try to hit it. The umpires must maintain exactly the same \\njudgment of what is a Wide in all situations. They must therefore be conscious of what has \\n"'b'b"been considered to be within reach in the early stages, so as to make the same assessment \\nin the later stages of the match. The height and reach of the striker will change the umpire\'s \\nassessment, but the stage of the game should not. \\n \\nJudging a Wide \\nThe test is \'is it near enough to the striker for him/her to hit it?\' Merely being within reach is \\nnot sufficient. A ball which the striker could get the toe of the bat to with arms outstretched \\nat full length is within reach, but he/she could not hit it in any reasonable sort of way. A ball \\nhas to be nearer than that to escape being a Wide. The phrase in the Law \'a normal cricket \\nstroke\' does not mean a recognised text-book stroke. It simply requires that the striker \\ncould comfortably get the bat to it and hit it in a reasonable sort of way! \\n \\nAlthough the batsman \\ncould have reached the \\nball it would only have \\nbeen with the toe off \\nthe bat. This is a Wide \\nball. \\n \\nAs an example, a ball over head height could well be within comfortable reach, but hitting it \\nwould require a shot more suited to tennis than cricket. This would therefore qualify as a \\nWide, and would be one, were it not for the fact that any ball over head height is also a No \\nball - which over-rides a Wide. This is to protect the batsman from being out if he/ she \\nmishits the ball or knocks the stumps in the attempt. \\nIt must be remembered that, if the striker makes no move towards the ball, he/she will not \\nbe able to reach nearly as far on the leg side as on the off side. If, however, he/she opens up \\nthe shoulders by moving his/her front foot even a modest way towards the leg side, the \\nbatsman will have a very much increased reach on that side. A low ball must be nearer to be \\nhittable. \\n"'b'b"  \\n \\nThe batsman\'s reach is much less \\nfor a low ball. This is therefore a \\nWide ball. \\n \\n \\nIt should be obvious that a tall batsman will be within hittable distance of many more \\ndeliveries than a shorter one. Since, by definition, the batsman is not actually going to hit a \\nWide, the umpire must form a judgment about what he/she could reach comfortably, even \\nthough he/she does not put the bat in the right place. \\n \\nThe batsman has only failed \\nto hit the ball because his \\nbat was at the wrong height. \\nHe could have hit it \\nproperly. Therefore this is \\nnot a Wide ball. \\n \\nStriker\'s position \\nWhere the striker is as the ball passes by is crucial in determining a Wide. The umpire must \\nanswer two questions: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 First, \'Was the ball comfortably within reach when it passed the striker where he/she \\nactually was?\' The striker may have moved well across to the off, or to the leg side. \\nThey may have advanced some way down the pitch. Did the delivery pass the \\nbatsman in that position sufficiently near to hit in a reasonable sort of way? The \\nstriker may have changed his/her position completely - from left handed to right \\nhanded (or vice versa). This would mean that, for the purpose of this Law, the striker \\nnow has two \'off sides with a greater theoretical reach.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The second question is \'Would the ball have been comfortably within reach if the \\nstriker had been in a normal guard position?\'  \\n"'b'b"If the answer to either of these questions is \'yes\', then the delivery is not a Wide. \\nThe description \'where he/she actually was includes not only the piece of ground the \\nbatsman was on at that moment but the whole of the striker\'s body alignment. It must be \\nemphasised that a normal guard position is not necessarily this batsman\'s guard position. \\nThat may be unusual. Some batsmen take guard standing almost entirely outside the \\nstumps on the leg side. It is not unusual for a batsman to take guard some way down the \\npitch (although if this is within, or would mean likely incursion into the protected area, the \\nstriker may be warned or penalised under Law 41.15). The umpire has to judge the ball in \\nrelation to an average, conventional guard position, round about the popping crease and \\nstanding so that the bat and pads together largely cover the stumps. \\nIt should also be noted that a ball which passes very wide of the stumps is not necessarily a \\nWide. It may have passed the striker within comfortable reach and moved away sharply \\nafterwards. \\n \\nDelivery not to be a Wide \\nThree situations are specified in which a delivery is not to be a Wide. The first two relate to \\nmovement by the striker. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If a ball was within comfortable reach, and so not a Wide, the striker cannot make \\nthat delivery a Wide by moving away from it, to put it out of reach. \\n \\nThe ball is \\nout of the \\nbatsman\'s \\nreach only \\nbecause he \\nhas moved \\nback from it. \\nThis is not a \\nWide. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The striker can prevent a ball from being a Wide by moving sufficiently close to it. It \\nwill then be comfortably within his/her reach whether or not it would have been if \\nhe/she had been in a normal guard position. \\nIn addition, any contact between ball and striker, at any time before the ball comes into \\ncontact with a fielder, will mean that a delivery is not a Wide, whether it would otherwise \\nhave qualified or not. Any ball which the striker actually hits is not a Wide. \\nThe striker may reach out towards a ball but find it is still not near enough to \'hit in a \\nreasonable sort of way. However, he/she may just touch it with the toe of the bat. This will \\nmean it is not a Wide after all and if the umpire has already called it as one, he/she must \\nrevoke that call. \\n \\nCalling and signalling Wide ball \\nAlthough the umpire will be aware during the ball\'s flight that it is likely to go wide of the \\nstriker, they must not call it a Wide until it passes the striker\'s wicket - otherwise the striker \\nmay touch it, may move towards it and bring it within reach, or the ball may deviate on \\npitching and come in close to the striker. If the ball survives all these hazards, and is indeed \\nWide, the instruction that it is to be considered to have been a Wide since the bowler \\nentered his/her delivery stride is therefore logical and will also remove any doubt as to \\nwhether it happened before or after any subsequent events. This instruction is not to be \\ntaken as invalidating the statement that No ball is to over-ride Wide. \\nIt is worth re-stating this point. A ball that is a Wide is deemed always to have been a Wide \\nfrom the moment the bowler entered the delivery stride - that means, if, for example a \\nteam need one run to win, and lose a wicket from that Wide ball, the one run penalty has \\nhappened before any other action, and the game would be over before that wicket had \\nfallen. \\nIt is of course possible that a striker may hit the ball after it has passed the wicket, after it \\nhas been called Wide. In this case, as described above, the umpire must revoke the call. It \\nshould also be revoked if the other umpire calls and signals No ball. If the call is for \\nencroachment by the wicket-keeper only just before the ball reaches the striker, the striker \\nend umpire may well not articulate it until after the call of Wide ball. Nevertheless, the No \\nball is to take precedence. This fact is stated in both Laws. \\nJust as with No ball, the ball does not become dead on the call of Wide. The call and signal \\nas the ball passes the wicket are purely to inform the players and the other umpire, The \\numpire is to repeat the signal to the scorers when the ball is dead. \\n \\n"'b"b'Recompense for a Wide \\nby definition a Wide is not within comfortable hitting distance of the striker This does not \\nprevent him/her from hitting it (thereby annulling 10, but means a choice between moving \\nsome way from guard position to hit it and not attempting to score off it. The batsman is \\ntherefore given recompense similar to that for a No ball. It is, however, less generous. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 A penalty of one run is awarded to the batting side in addition to any runs accruing \\nin any other way from that delivery. All these runs will be recorded as extras, since \\nthe striker will not have hit the ball. They will, however, with the exception of any 5 \\nrun penalty, be debited against the bowler \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The striker is given some immunity from dismissal, but it is less immunity than for a \\nNo ball. In particular the striker can be out Stumped or Hit wicket and the bowler will \\nbe given credit. In addition, either batsman can be out Obstructing the field or Run \\nout. These ways in which a batsman can be out from a Wide are set out in Law 22.9. \\nUmpires and scorers must be familiar with these. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The ball does not count in the over, so (unless the umpire has mistakenly already \\nallowed the over to continue after 6 valid balls have been bowled) another delivery \\nmust be made. Both umpires should adjust their counters to take account of this. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"LAW 23 BYE AND LEG BYE \\n23.1 Byes \\nIf the ball, delivered by the bowler, not being a Wide passes the striker without touching \\nhis/her bat or person, any runs completed by the batsmen from that delivery, or a boundary \\nallowance, shall be credited as Byes to the batting side. Additionally, if the delivery is a No \\nball, the one run penalty for such a delivery shall be incurred. \\n \\n23.2 Leg byes \\n23.2.1 \\nIf a ball delivered by the bowler first strikes the person of the striker, runs \\nshall be scored only if the umpire is satisfied that the striker has \\neither   attempted to play the ball with the bat \\nor          tried to avoid being hit by the ball. \\n23.2.2 \\nIf the umpire is satisfied that either of these conditions has been met runs \\nshall be scored as follows. \\n22.2.2.1 \\nIf there is \\neither no subsequent contact with the striker\'s bat or person  \\nor        only inadvertent contact with the striker\'s bat or person.  \\nany runs completed by the batsmen or a boundary allowance shall be \\ncredited to the striker in the case of subsequent contact with his/her bat but \\notherwise to the batting side as in 23.2.3. \\n22.2.2.2 \\nIf the striker wilfully makes a lawful second strike, Laws 34.3 (Ball \\nlawfully struck more than once) and 34.4 (Runs permitted from ball lawfully \\nstruck more than once) shall apply. \\n23.2.3 \\nThe runs in 23.2.2.1, unless credited to the striker, shall be scored as Leg \\nbyes. \\nAdditionally, if the delivery is a No ball, the one run penalty for the No ball shall be \\nincurred. \\n \\n23.3 Leg byes not to be awarded \\n \\nIf in the circumstance of 23.2.1 the umpire considers that neither of the conditions therein \\nhas been met, then Leg byes shall not be awarded. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"If the ball does not become dead for any other reason, the umpire shall call and signal Dead \\nball as soon as the ball reaches the boundary or at the completion of the first run. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The umpire shall then \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 disallow all runs to the batting side, \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 return any not out batsman to his/her original end;  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 signal No ball to the scorers if applicable; \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award any 5-run Penalty that is applicable except for Penalty runs under Law 28.3 \\n(Protective helmets belonging to the fielding side). \\nAs explained in Law 18, there are ways a batsman can score runs without the striker having \\nhit the ball in the first place, namely Byes and Leg byes. These runs are scored as extras and \\nare added to the total score but neither credited to the batsman nor debited against the \\nbowler. The only exception to this is when further runs are accrued from a Wide - these runs \\nare debited, as Wides, against the bowler. \\nByes \\nA Bye is simple and straightforward. If there is no contact at all between the ball and the \\nstriker\'s bat or person, the batsmen are entitled to run without restriction, except of course \\nfor the Run out Law. Unless the runs are to be disallowed for other reasons, any runs \\ncompleted are Byes. No signal is to be made until the ball is dead, although it can be helpful \\nto give an unobtrusive pre-arranged indication (a pre-signal) to the scorers that a signal will \\nbe made when the ball is dead. The umpire then confirms this by giving the signal for Byes \\nafter the ball has become dead, to make sure the scorers know that the runs are to be \\nscored as Byes. \\n \\nLeg byes \\nRuns can be scored, as Leg byes, by the batsmen running after the ball has made contact \\nwith the striker\'s person but not the bat. Here, however, there are important restrictions. \\nNo runs, except penalty runs, are allowed to be scored, unless the striker has \\neither  attempted to play the ball with the bat  \\nor         tried to avoid being hit by the ball. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"If the ball first hits the striker\'s bat, whether intentionally or not, then no question of Leg \\nbyes arises and play will proceed normally. If the ball first hits the striker\'s person, then \\n(ignoring the chance that the striker may be out LBW), the umpire must consider whether, \\nwithin these restrictions, Leg byes could be allowed or not. Note that later contact with the \\nbat cannot alter a decision that runs are not to be allowed if no attempt to play or avoid the \\nball has been made. \\n \\nStriker attempting to play the ball \\nThere are two phrases which have arisen from earlier versions of the Law and which now \\nrepresent an entirely mistaken view of it. They are \'illegal deflection\' and \'deliberately \\npadding the ball away! \\nThere is nothing illegal about the ball being deflected off the striker\'s person. It is running \\nafter such a deflection which may be illegal. Whilst an unwise batsman may deliberately \\npush the ball away with his/her pad, he/ she is equally subject to the prohibition on scoring \\nif he/ she merely lets it hit his/her body, even if he/she did not intend that to happen. The \\nstriker may consider that the ball would pass off stump and lift the bat high to avoid it \\ntouching the ball. Then the ball turns sharply and unexpectedly hits the pad. There was \\nnothing deliberate about the ball-pad contact, but he/she is still not allowed to Score runs. \\n \\nClearly the batsman \\nhas not tried to hit \\nthe ball with the \\nbat. The batsman \\ncannot therefore \\nscore Leg byes. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'An attempt by the striker to avoid being hit by the ball is usually obvious. Whether or not \\nthe striker has attempted to hit the ball with the bat may be more difficult to decide. A \\nthird, long-outdated, incorrect description to add to the two above is playing a (genuine) \\nshot. Playing a shot is not an adequate description of trying to play the ball with the bat. A \\nbatsman could sweep the bat through the air in what looks like, say, an off drive, but with \\nevery intention of missing the ball. In general, the greater the skill of the batsman the more \\nhe/she will be able to pretend that they are trying to hit the ball while having no intention of \\ndoing so. Whilst not being an exhaustive list, the following pointers may be helpful. If the \\nstriker \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 plays forward defensively, with the bat clearly behind the pad, \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 makes a flourish of the bat well outside the line of the ball, \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 puts his/her leg forward into the line of the ball and then deliberately brings the bat \\nthrough outside his/her pad, \\nthen he/she is playing a shot but not attempting to hit the ball with the bat. It must not be \\nsupposed, however, that all batsmen are trying to deceive. The umpire must use best \\njudgment in every case \\n \\nThe batsman cannot be genuinely \\ntrying to hit the ball. It has hit the pad with \\nthe bat tucked behind it. Leg byes cannot be \\nscored, \\nWas the batsman trying to hit the ball with \\nthe bat? Unless the ball had turned sharply, \\nno. Leg byes cannot be scored. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Action for the umpire \\nIf the umpire is satisfied that the sticker was genuinely attempted to hit the ball with the bat \\non alternatively has tried to avoid being hit by the ball, then the batsmen are entitled to run \\nor benefit if the ball travels to the boundary and no action is required, other than signalling \\nLeg byes (and a boundary 4 if appropriate) to the scorers when the ball is dead and receiving \\nacknowledgement. \\nIf, however, the umpire decides otherwise and Leg byes are not to be allowed and the \\nbatsmen run, then the umpire must follow the procedure set out in Law 233. Dead ball is \\nnot called until either one run has been completed (both batsmen making good their \\nground), or the ball goes to the boundary. This \'delay\' is in order not to deny the fielding \\nside the opportunity of dismissing one of the batsman if they are running The calling of \\nDead ball and returning the batsmen to their original ends will inform the scorers that any \\ncompleted runs are not to be allowed. \\nThe one run Penalty for a No ball (which will have happened before the ball struck the \\nbatsman) is still awarded and if either team commits an offence which leads to the awarding \\nof 5 Penalty runs these will also be applied However, there cannot be 5 Penalty runs for the \\nball striking a protective helmet placed on the ground behind the wicket keeper- this is not a \\nfielding offence and thus they are not punished if Leg byes are disallowed. \\n \\nBatsman dismissed \\nIf the ball\'s first contact with the striker is on his/her person, and the umpire is not satisfied \\nthat there has been either an attempt to hit the ball with the bat or an attempt to avoid \\nbeing hit by the ball, the prohibition on running is quite independent of any dismissal. An \\nappeal for LBW may be valid. If the ball is deflected off the striker\'s person on to the stumps \\nthe striker will bowled. There is in fact no method, other than Timed out, by which the \\nstriker cannot be out. \\nIt is key to remember that if the ball strikes the bat after the person, although the batsman \\ncan be out Caught that doesn\'t mean he/she can then score runs - the embargo on scoring \\nruns remains if no attempt to hit, or avoid being hit by, the ball was made. \\nWhatever is appropriate in Law if a batsman is dismissed, about returning to original ends or \\nstaying at the end they have run to, will apply (Laws 18.11 and 18.12) but the embargo on \\nscoring any runs remains. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"How runs are to be recorded \\nThere can be runs after the ball has struck the batsman\'s person first only if the umpire is \\nsatisfied that the striker either tried to hit the ball with his/her bat or tried to avoid being hit \\nby the ball. If runs are allowed, they will not be Byes, since the ball has struck the person. \\nThey will be Leg byes, except in the one case of inadvertent contact with the striker\'s bat \\nafter hitting his/her person. Then the Law permits them to be credited to the striker. If, \\nhowever, there is later contact with the bat but it is a deliberate but legal second stroke, \\nthen still no runs will be allowed and the procedure above applies. This is set out more fully \\nunder Law 34. \\nRuns off a No ball \\nIf No ball is called, it will make no difference to the decision as to whether Leg Byes are to be \\nallowed or not. As already noted, the one run penalty will still be awarded even if runs are \\nnot allowed. They will be scored as one No ball extra, and then Leg Byes if allowed. \\nIf a No ball is bowled and Byes are scored, there is no question of disallowing the runs under \\nthis Law, though there may be under other Laws. The umpire must signal appropriately No \\nball alone, No ball and Byes or No ball and Leg byes to make the scorers aware what the ball \\nhas, or has not, hit. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 24 FIELDER\'S ABSENCE; SUBSTITUTES \\n24.1 Substitute fielders \\n24.1.1 \\nThe umpires shall allow a substitute fielder \\n24.1.1.1 \\nif they are satisfied that a fielder has been injured or become ill and \\nthat this occurred during the match, or \\n24.1.1.2 \\nfor any other wholly acceptable reason. \\nIn all other circumstances, a substitute is not allowed. \\n24.1.2 \\nA substitute shall not bowl or act as captain but may act as wicket-keeper \\nonly with the consent of the umpires. Note, however, Law 42.7.1 (Additional points \\nrelating to Level 3 and Level 4 offences). \\n24.1.3 \\nA nominated player may bowl or field even though a substitute has \\npreviously acted for him/her, subject to 24.2, 24.3 and Law 42.4 (Level 3 offences \\nand action by umpires). \\n \\n24.2 Fielder absent or leaving the field of play \\n \\n24.2.1 \\nA player going briefly outside the boundary while carrying out any \\nduties as a fielder is not absent from the field of play nor, for the purposes of \\nthis Law, is he she to be regarded as having left the field of play. \\n24.2.2 \\nIf a fielder fails to take the field at the start of play or at any later \\ntime, or leaves the field during play.  \\n24.2.2.1 an umpire shall be informed of the reason for this absence. \\n24.2.2.2 he/she shall not thereafter come on to the field of play during a \\nsession of play without the consent of the umpire. See 24.4. The \\numpire shall give such consent as soon as it is practicable. \\n24.2.2.3 he/she shall not be permitted to bowl, until having been back on the \\nfield of play for a period of time known as Penalty time as described in \\n24.2.3 to 24.2.7 and 24.3. 24.2.3 . \\n24.2.3 \\nA player\'s unserved Penalty time shall be limited to a maximum of 90 \\nminutes. \\n24.2.4 \\nIf the player leaves the field before having served all of his/her \\nPenalty time, the balance is carried forward as unserved Penalty time. \\n"'b"b'24.2.5 \\nThe player shall not bowl until all of his/her Penalty time has been \\nserved. On any occasion of absence, the amount of Playing time for which the \\nplayer is off the field shall be added to any Penalty time that remains \\nunserved subject to 24.2.3. \\n24.2.6 \\nIf there is an unscheduled break in play, the stoppage time shall count \\nas Penalty time served, provided that, \\n22.2.6.1 \\nthe fielder who was on the field of play at the start of the \\nbreak either takes the field on the resumption of play. or his/her side \\nis now batting. \\n22.2.6.2 \\nthe fielder who was already off the field at the start of the \\nbreak notifies an umpire in person as soon as he/she is able to \\nparticipate, and either takes the field on the resumption of play, or \\nhis/her side is now batting Stoppage time before an umpire has been \\nso notified shall not count towards unserved Penalty time. \\n24.2.7 \\nAny unserved Penalty time shall be carried forward into the next and \\nsubsequent days and innings of the match, as applicable. \\n24.2.8 \\nIf a Level 3 offence under Law 42.4 (Level 3 offences and action by \\numpires) is committed by a fielder, the period of time spent off the field of \\nplay as a result of the suspension shall not be added to any unserved Penalty \\ntime if he/she takes the field at the end of the period of suspension. \\n24.2.9 \\nIf a Level 3 offence under Law 42.4 (Level 3 offences and action by \\numpires) is committed by a substitute, the following shall apply. \\n24.2.9.1 \\nThe period of time spent off the field as a result of the \\nsuspension shall not be added to any Penalty time that remains \\nunserved if the nominated player takes the field at the end of the \\nperiod of suspension However, any time spent off the field prior to \\nthe offence causing the suspension will remain as unserved Penalty \\ntime, subject to 24.2.3. \\n24.2.9.2 \\nIf the nominated player does not take the field at the end of \\nthe period of suspension, then the entire period of his/ her absence \\nwill be treated as Penalty time up to a maximum of 90 minutes. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"24.3 Penalty time not incurred \\nA nominated player\'s absence will not incur Penalty time if, \\n24.3.1 \\nhe/she has suffered an external blow during the match and, as a \\nresult, has justifiably left the field or is unable to take the field. \\n24.3.2 \\nIn the opinion of the umpires, the player has been absent or has left \\nthe field for other wholly acceptable reasons, which shall not include illness \\nor internal injury. \\n \\n24.4 Player returning without permission \\nIf a player comes on to the field of play in contravention of 24.2.2 and comes into contact \\nwith the ball while it is in play, the ball shall immediately become dead. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The umpire shall award 5 Penalty runs to the batting side. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Runs completed by the batsmen shall be scored together with the run in progress if \\nthey had already crossed at the instant of the offence. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The ball shall not count as one of the over. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The umpire shall inform the other umpire, the captain of the fielding side, the \\nbatsmen and, as soon as practicable, the captain of the batting side of the reason for \\nthis action. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after the match \\nto the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body responsible for the \\nmatch, who shall take such action as is considered appropriate against the captain \\nany other individuals concerned and, if appropriate, the team. \\n \\nThis Law, along with Law 25, explains what happens if a player has to leave the field, or the \\ngame, for any period of time. The two used to be bound up together in one Law, but have \\nnow been separated. Law 24 deals with absences affecting the fielding side, while Law 25 is \\nfocussed on the side batting. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"During the match - substitute by right \\nAn injury or illness that occurs \'during the match\' means at any time after the toss until the \\nmatch is ended. This includes the times, between those moments, when there is no play in \\nprogress. A player who falls down the pavilion steps during the lunch interval, injuring \\nhis/her ankle, is allowed a substitute with the consent of the umpires, just the same as one \\nwho has been injured while fielding. \\nThe umpires must be satisfied that an injury has occurred to a player, or that he/she has \\nbecome ill, during that period. Of course, it must be remembered that the umpires are not \\nmedically qualified. They must judge as well as they can. \\nAn injury does not have to be completely new to qualify a player for a substitute. If a player \\ncomes into a match with an injury - but is fit enough to take part - and it gets worse during \\nthe match, the umpires might consider that they are entitled to a substitute. However, this \\nonly applies if something during the match causes it to get worse - a player who comes into \\nthe match injured should not be allowed a substitute (or, under Law 25, a runner) if nothing \\nduring the match has made their injury worse. Umpires are not medically trained, and \\nshould therefore usually assume that a player who is nominated is fit to play a full part. \\nHowever, a player who has had a fitness test before the game, and failed it, should not \\nexpect a substitute fielder, or a runner, if they are only fit enough to bat. \\n \\nOther wholly acceptable reasons \\nThe umpires should act together in deciding whether a player, who is not ill or injured, has a \\ngood reason to have a substitute fielder. A doctor called away to a case would be one \\nacceptable reason. If a player is injured and another player has to drive them to hospital, \\nboth players would be eligible for a substitute, even though only one is actually injured. A \\nplayer arriving late because of heavy traffic is unlikely to be deemed as a wholly acceptable \\nreason. \\nNo player who is suspended from the game under Law 42 is allowed a substitute for the \\ntime they are off the field. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Substitutes \\nIt is important to understand the difference between a substitute fielder and a replacement \\nfor a nominated player. After the teams have been nominated, the opposing captain may, as \\ndescribed in Law 1, allow a nominated player to be replaced by another person. Though \\nthey may have to remind the captains of the terms of the Law, the umpires have no say in \\nthis. They should change their lists of nominated players, to include the new player instead \\nof the replaced one. They will allow any player now on the list, and no others, to take a full \\npart in the game. Any player not on the current list is not allowed to take any part in the \\ngame other than simply fielding and, in some circumstances, keeping wicket - and then only \\nas a permitted substitute for another player. \\nIn contrast, it is the umpires alone who decide whether a player can have a substitute or \\nrunner for whatever reason. The captains have no say. Once a substitute fielder is allowed, \\nthere can be no objection as to who it is or where he/she can field. If a wicket-keeper is \\ninjured, a captain may ask for the umpire\'s consent for a substitute to keep wicket - this will \\nbe a decision for the umpires, and the umpires alone, to make. There is nothing to stop \\nthem saying yes, but they should not be swayed by the pleas, or complaints, of either \\ncaptain. \\nThere is, however, provision for the umpires to refuse consent. If, for example, a fielder - \\nnot the wicket-keeper - picks up an injury, and is replaced by a substitute, but that \\nsubstitute is a more competent wicket-keeper than the original wicket-keeper, the umpires \\nshould not give consent for the substitute to keep wicket. The wicket-keeper himself/herself \\nis not injured, and a substitute fielder is not a tactical tool. If, on the other hand, the wicket-\\nkeeper picks up an injury that is enough to prevent him/her from keeping, but does not \\nnecessarily force him/her off the field, a substitute who had already come on for a different \\nfielder should be allowed to take over the gloves. Once again, this is a decision for the \\numpires to make based on the facts, and is not always black and white, but in general, the \\nprinciple should be that consent must only be given if the original wicket-keeper has a \\ngenuine injury. It should be noted, however, that if the wicket-keeper has been suspended \\nunder Law 42 then only a nominated player can act as wicket-keeper even if there is \\nanother substitute on the field already. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b" The umpires have to make sure substitutes play within the Laws. If, for example, a captain \\ncalls on a substitute to bowl, the umpires will remind him/her that this is not allowed. The \\nsubstitute can do no more than field and cannot captain the side. \\nIf a nominated player has had a substitute, he/she can still act as a full player after returning \\nto the field. For example, if a fielder has a badly cut finger, a substitute may act for him/her \\nwhile it is treated and bandaged. He/ she is allowed to return and resume as a fielder \\nwithout restriction except that the umpire must give permission for the actual moment of \\nhis/her return. Restrictions on when a returning player may bowl or bat are covered below \\nand in Law 25. \\nIn light of the new Law 42, which can see players temporarily or permanently removed from \\nthe field for poor conduct, it is important that the umpires know which substitute is fielding \\nfor which nominated player. A breach of Law 42 by a substitute will see the nominated \\nplayer for whom they are acting punished and so, particularly when multiple substitutes are \\non the field, it is vital that the umpires are aware of which substitute is representing which \\nnominated player. \\nFielder absent from field of play \\nA player is counted as absent if, when his/her side comes on a the field, he/she is not among \\nthe players. This must not be taken too literally. If a fielder is simply a moment or two later \\nthan the rest of the team, that doesn\'t matter provided he/she arrives before Play is called. \\nA fielder will also be absent if he/she leaves the field during a session of play Law 24.2.1 \\nstates that going to retrieve the ball from beyond the boundary is not \\xe2\\x80\\x98leaving the field\\xe2\\x80\\x99. \\nIdeally, all the fielders should be within the boundary when the ball comes into play. \\nHowever, it would be unreasonable to prevent a fielder from starting one or two paces \\noutside the boundary to give himself/herself room to walk in towards the wicket. On the \\nother hand, the batsmen have a right to know where the fielders are when the ball comes \\ninto play, so that the striker can judge where to play his/her shots and both of them can \\nassess whether to attempt a run or not. The umpire should not allow a fielder to start \\noutside the boundary if it might deceive the batsmen but, as long as it is obvious that \\nhe/she is there and is taking part in the game, walking in from outside the boundary can be \\nallowed without penalty. Of course, actually fielding the ball outside the boundary will result \\nin a boundary being scored. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"To be leaving the field, the player must be completely taking themselves out of the game. \\nThe umpire has to be told, not necessarily by the player himself/herself, the reason for this \\nabsence but does not have to give permission for the absence. The umpire will immediately \\ntell his/ her colleague and both will note the time, so that if the fielder returns they will \\nknow how long he/she has been absent. They will together decide whether a substitute can \\nbe allowed or not as outlined above. If a fielder does not inform the umpires or the umpires \\ndo not accept the reason given as a valid reason for a substitute (i.e. injury, illness or any \\nother wholly acceptable reason), no substitute is to be allowed. Umpires should take a \\nstrong approach to such requests and must ensure that they are given a valid and genuine \\nreason before giving permission for a substitute. After a fielder leaves the field, the umpires \\nshould regularly check on an absent player\'s conditions at appropriate times - drinks, other \\nintervals or the end of a day\'s play. \\nWhen the fielder wishes to return, the bowler\'s end umpire has to give permission before \\nthat fielder can come on to the field again. Both will check the time that this happens. \\nIf a substitute has been fielding while the player was absent, he/she must go off when the \\noriginal player returns. The umpire should not allow a player to return unless the ball is \\ndead and this should be timed so that this fielding change-over does not delay play \\nsignificantly. On the other hand, umpires should not make the fielder wait unnecessarily to \\nreturn.
