Perhaps the end of an over might be best, but if the substitute is fielding near the \\nboundary, and can swap over with the returning fielder quickly, there is no reason why \\npermission shouldn\'t be given for the change mid-over, between deliveries. The batsmen \\nneed to be made aware of the change in the fielding side. \\nThe penalty if a player returns without permission, and makes contact with the ball, Is laid \\ndown in Law 24.4. It is quite severe but it must be applied if the situation arises. Once the \\nprocedure has been implemented, apart from the reporting to be made after the match, the \\numpire should then give permission for the fielder to return, if that is appropriate. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Penalty time \\nIf a player is absent while his/her team is fielding, he/she will, in most cases, suffer a \\npenalty. On returning, he/she is not permitted to bowl for the same length of time as \\nhe/she was absent from the field. This time is known as Penalty time. \\nUmpires must be clear about the exact time that a fielder Is to be counted as absent. Only \\nthat part of the fielder\'s absence which was playing time is significant. The umpires will have \\nnoted the time of his/her departure, or the start of the innings, if he/she does not come on \\nwith the rest of the team. They will also have noted the time of his/her return. From the \\ndifference between these two they must subtract the time for any intervals - not forgetting \\ndrinks intervals! - or interruptions in play, since neither of these is playing time. \\nFielders used to be given a 15 minute \'grace period\', during which their absence was not \\npenalised. This is no longer the case. The moment a fielder leaves the field during play (note \\nthe exceptions discussed below), their Penalty time begins, and, having returned, they may \\nnot bowl, or bat (see Law 25), until they have served the equivalent time. \\nNon-playing time cannot be used to offset any Penalty time, except for unscheduled breaks, \\nas outlined in 24.2.6 - however this is far from straightforward, and umpires will do well to \\nstudy that section, alongside the examples that follow on page 226. \\nIn principle two situations can arise: \\n1: If a fielder with Penalty time to serve is on the field immediately before and after the \\nunscheduled break, the whole of the time of that break counts as Penalty time served \\n2. If a fielder with Penalty time to serve is off the field at the start of the break, he/she must \\ntell the umpires as soon as they are fit to return. Providing the player then does return to \\nthe field with the rest of the team at the end of the break,the time from notifying the \\numpires will count towards Penalty time served. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"In both cases, if the innings has ended during the unscheduled break and the player\'s team \\nare now batting then Penalty time would be offset by all of the break, in the first case, or \\npart in the second case and continue to be served during the batting innings. \\nNote that suspensions under Law 42 do not count as Penalty time served - that is to say that \\nif a player who is serving Penalty time is suspended, their Penalty time will be the same \\nlength when they return from their suspension as when they left. However, time off the \\nfield due to a suspension does not count as an absence for the sake of Penalty time - once a \\nplayer who was not serving Penalty time returns from their suspension, they may bat and \\nbowl immediately. \\n If a substitute is on the field, then there will be a nominated player off the field, accruing \\nPenalty time. If that substitute is then suspended for a Level 3 offence under Law 42.4, the \\ntime he/she spends off the field will not be added to the nominated player\'s Penalty time if, \\nand only if, the nominated player is ready to return to the field immediately at the end of \\nthe suspension. If the nominated player remains off the field, that is to say the substitute \\nreturns after his/her suspension, then the entire period will be treated as Penalty time. \\nIt is important to note that, however long a player has been absent, the maximum Penalty \\ntime they will have to serve is 90 minutes. If they return, that time will begin to be reduced \\nimmediately from 90, until they are once again able to take full part in the game. However, \\nif they leave the field again then the Penalty time will increase back to the maximum of 90 \\nminutes. \\nExceptions to Penalty time  \\nThere are exceptions when Penalty time does not need to be served. If the injury is because \\nof an \'external\' blow, and suffered during the match, Penalty time is not incurred. The same \\nis true if the absence is due to \'other wholly acceptable reasons (discussed above). In these \\ncases no Penalty time is served, and as soon as the player is ready to return he/she may bat \\nand bowl as a full member of the team, as if they had never left the game. \\nWhat counts as an external blow should be fairly obvious - it means an injury caused by \\nimpact, whether with the ball, another person, or even the ground. It does not cover \\nmuscular injuries or illness. It must be remembered that the umpires are not (usually) \\nmedical professionals, and they must make a decision about what injuries are \'justifiable\' \\nbased on the best of their knowledge. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Limits to Penalty time \\nPenalty time was previously, limited within the fielding innings of an absence. This is no \\nlonger the case. A player who goes off the field when his/her side is fielding will serve \\nPenalty time during the rest of that innings, and, if there is still Penalty time remaining at \\nthe end of the innings, will not be permitted to bat until that Penalty time is over (or until \\nfive wickets have fallen -for more details see Law 25). \\nIn the same way, Penalty time used to be wiped clear at the end of the day. This is no longer \\nthe case. A player who is serving Penalty time at the end of a day\'s play keeps that Penalty \\ntime at the start of the next day, until he/she has served all of the required time up to a \\nmaximum of 90 minutes. \\nPenalty time calculations \\nIn dealing with calculations for time absent and delay on return, the points to note are: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Time spent off the field from the start of the first session is not to be treated any \\ndifferently from any other time. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If a player is absent on more than one occasion, the Penalty time for any subsequent \\nabsence is added to the remaining Penalty time from previous absences, up to a \\nrolling maximum of 90 minutes. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Only actual playing time counts in any part of the calculation, except in the special \\ncase of Law 24.2.6, already mentioned. \\nThe following example demonstrates these points. It is a slightly artificial scenario, designed \\nto show what might happen, rather than what is likely to happen in a single game to a single \\nplayer. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'TIME \\nEVENT \\nCURRENT an internal injury meaning \\nBradman is unable to bowl or bat \\n until he has been on the held for the \\nsame length of time (the  Penalty \\ntime) he is off it. \\n1300 \\nScheduled time for Lunch interval \\n75 mins \\n \\n1340 \\nThe next session of play \\ncommences \\n75 mins \\nLunch is scheduled interval- no \\nadditional Penalty time is added. \\n1355 \\nBradman remains off the field \\n90 mins \\nThis is the maximum Penalty time \\xe2\\x80\\x93 \\nno further time will be added at this \\nstage. \\n1415 \\nPlay is suspended due to rain \\n90 mins \\nAs Bradman has not told the umpire \\nhe is ready to resume the time of the \\nunscheduled interruption is not taken \\noff the Penalty time. \\n1445 \\nPlay recommences but Bradman \\nremains off the field \\n90 mins \\n \\nserved. \\n1640 \\nPlay recommences with Bradman \\non the field \\n20 mins \\nBecause Bradman was on the field \\nwhen the unscheduled interruption \\nbegan \\nand \\nrata \\nwhen \\nplay \\nPenalty time has reduced to 10 \\nminutes but now time off the field \\nwill be added to the outstanding \\nPenalty time up to a maximum of 90 \\nminutes. \\n1800 \\nPlay ends for the day \\n80 mins \\n \\n \\n'"b'b" \\nTIME \\nEVENT \\nCURRENT PENALTY \\nTIME \\nCOMMENTS \\n1100 \\nPlay commences on \\nday 2 with Bradman on \\nthe field \\n80 mins \\nOutstanding Penalty \\ntime is carried over to \\nDay 2 but will now \\nstart to reduce. \\n1130 \\nThe first innings ends \\n50 mins \\nThe following part of \\nthe example refers to \\napplication of Law 25 \\nbut is included for innings is a \\nscheduled interval \\nmeaning Penalty time \\nis not reduced. \\n1220 \\nThe 5th wicket tales \\nand Bradman comes \\ninto bat \\n10 mins \\nAlthough there is still \\n10 minutes of Penalty \\ntime to serve because \\n5 wickets have fallen \\nBradman is entitled to \\nbat immediately. \\n1225 \\nBradman is dismissed \\nand the captain \\ndeclares \\n5 mins \\nPenalty time is \\nreduced while \\nBradman is batting. \\n1235 \\n2nd innings \\ncommences. Bradman \\nTakes the field but is \\nnot allowed to bowl \\n5 mins \\nThe 10 minutes \\nbetween innings is a \\nscheduled interval \\nmeaning Penalty time \\nis not reduced.  \\n1240 \\nBradman is allowed to \\nbowl \\n0 mins \\nAll of his Penalty time \\nhas now been served. \\n \\nFurther points to note \\nOnly playing time absent as a fielder counts. If a player is injured when batting and is absent \\nfor an hour before resuming his/her innings, this will have no effect on his/ her entitlement \\nto bowl (if fit) when his/her side fields, provided he/she takes the field at the start of the \\ninnings. \\nIt is the umpires\' responsibility to see that the restrictions on bowling or batting after \\nabsence are observed. They must be meticulous in noting the times involved, and should \\nnot rely on others - even if the scorers do make a note of the timings, the umpires should \\nhave their own, independent record. \\n"'b'b"LAW 25 BATMAN\'S INNINGS; RUNNERS \\n25.1 Eligibility to act as a batsman or runner  \\nOnly a nominated player may bat or act as a runner and, subject to 25.3 and 25.5.2, may do \\nso even though a substitute fielder has previously acted for him/her  \\n \\n25.2 Commencement of a batsman\'s innings \\nThe innings of the first two batsmen, and that of any new batsman on the resumption of \\nplay after a call of Time, shall commence at the call of Play. At any other time, a batsman\'s \\ninnings shall be considered to have commenced when that batsman first steps onto the field \\nof play. \\n \\n25.3 Restriction on batsman commencing an innings \\n25.3.1 \\nIf a member of the batting side has unserved Penalty time, see Law \\n24.2.7 (Fielder absent or leaving the field of play), that player shall not be \\npermitted to bat or act as a runner until that Penalty time has been served \\nHowever, even if the unserved Penalty time has not expired, that player may \\nbat after his/her side has lost 5 wickets. \\n25.3.2 \\nmember of the batting side\'s Penalty time is served during Playing \\ntime, subject to the conditions in 25.3.2.1 and 25.3.2.2. \\n25.3.2.1 \\nThe period of time while serving a suspension for a Level 3 \\noffence under Law 42.4 (Level 3 offences and action by umpires) does \\nnot count as Penalty time served \\n25.3.2.2 \\nIn the event of an unscheduled stoppage, the stoppage time \\nafter the batsman notifies an umpire in person that he/she is able to \\nparticipate shall count as Penalty time served. \\n25.3.3 \\nIf any unserved Penalty time remains at the end of a team\'s innings, it \\nshall be carried forward to the next and subsequent innings of the match if \\nappropriate. \\n \\n25.4 Batsman retiring \\n25.4.1 \\nA batsman may retire at any time during his/her innings when the ball \\nis dead. The umpires, before allowing play to proceed, shall be informed of \\nthe reason for a batsman retiring. \\n \\n"'b'b"25.4.2 \\nIf a batsman retires because of illness, injury or any other unavoidable \\ncause, that batsman is entitled to resume his/her innings. If for any reason \\nthis does not happen, that batsman is to be recorded as \'Retired - not out\'. \\n25.4.3 \\nIf a batsman retires for any reason other than as in 25.4.2, the innings \\nof that batsman may be resumed only with the consent of the opposing \\ncaptain. If for any reason his/her innings is not resumed that batsman is to be \\nrecorded as \'Retired - out\'. \\n25.4.4 \\nIf after retiring a batsman resumes his/her innings, subject to the \\nrequirements of 25.4.2 and 25.4.3, it shall be only at the fall of a wicket or the \\nretirement of another batsman. \\n \\n25.5 Runners \\n25.5.1 \\nThe umpires shall allow a runner for a batsman if they are satisfied \\n \\n25.5.1.1 that the batsman has sustained an injury that affects his/her \\nability to run and \\n25.5.1.2 that this occurred during the match  \\nIn all other circumstances, a runner is not allowed. \\n25.5.2 \\nA runner shall \\n25.5.2.1 \\nbe a member of the batting side. \\n25.5.2.2 \\nif possible, have already batted in that innings, when this is not \\npossible, if circumstances change such that a dismissed batsman can \\nact as runner, then the runner must be changed immediately.  \\n25.5.2.3 \\nbe changed only with the consent of the umpires. \\n25.5.2.4 \\nwear external protective equipment equivalent to that worn \\nby the batsman for whom he/she runs and shall carry a bat.  \\n25.5.2.5 \\nnot have any unserved Penalty time as described in Law 24.2.7 \\n(Fielder absent or leaving the field of play). \\n25.5.3 \\nA batsman\'s runner is subject to the Laws and will be regarded as a \\nbatsman except where there are special provisions for his/her role as a \\nrunner See Law 302 (Which is a batsman\'s ground). \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"25.6 Dismissal and conduct of a batsman and his/her runner  \\n25.6.1.1 A batsman who has a runner will suffer the penalty for any infringement of \\nthe Laws by the runner as it he/she, the batsman, had been responsible for \\nthe infringement. In particular he/she will be out if the runner is out under \\neither of Laws 37 (Obstructing the field) or 38 (Run out). \\n25.6.1.2 When a batsman who has a runner is the striker he/she remains subject to \\nthe Laws and will be liable to the penalties that any infringement of them \\ndemands. In the case of Run out and Stumped, however, special provisions, \\nset out in 25.6.3, 25.6.4 and 25.6.5, apply to him/her as a striker who has a \\nrunner. \\n25.6.1.3 When a batsman who has a runner is the striker his/her ground is always at \\nthe wicket-keeper\'s end. \\n25.6.1.4 If the striker who has a runner is in his/her ground, and the runner is out of \\nhis/her ground at the wicket keeper\'s end when the wicket is fairly put \\ndown at that end, the conditions of Law 38 (Run out) shall apply. \\n25.6.1.5 If the striker who has a runner is out of his/her ground and the wicket at the \\nwicket-keeper\'s end is fairly put down, the striker is liable to be out under \\nLaws 38 (Run out) or 39 (Stumped). If the runner is also out of his/ her \\nground at the wicket-keeper\'s end, then only Law 38 (Run out) can apply. \\n25.6.1.6 if a striker who has a runner is dismissed as in 25.6.5, the umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 disallow all runs to the batting side  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 return any not out batsman to his/her original end \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award any 5-run Penalty that is applicable  \\n25.6.1.7 When a batsman who has a runner is not the striker, he/she \\n25.6.7.1 remains subject to Law 37 (Obstructing the field) but is \\notherwise out of the match.  \\n25.6.7.2 shall stand where directed by the striker\'s end umpire so as not \\nto interfere with play. \\n25.6.7.3 will be liable, even though out of the match as in 25.6.7.1. to \\nany penalty demanded by the Laws should he/she commit any \\nact of unfair play. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"25.7 Restriction on the striker\'s runner \\nOnce the ball comes into play, the runner for an injured batsman who is the striker shall \\nhave part of his/her person or bat grounded behind the popping crease until the ball \\nreaches the striker or passes the popping crease, whichever is the sooner. \\nif the striker\'s end umpire considers the runner for an injured striker to be in breach of this \\nrestriction then, if the ball does not become dead for any other reason, he/she shall call and \\nsignal Dead ball as soon as the ball reaches the boundary or at the completion of the first \\nrun. However, he/she shall delay the call of Dead ball to allow the opportunity for a catch to \\nbe completed. The bowler\'s end umpire shall  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 disallow all runs to the batting side \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 return any not out batsman to his/her original end  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award any 5-run Penalty that is applicable except for Penalty runs under Law 28.3 \\n(Protective helmets belonging to the fielding side). \\n \\nRunners \\nA batsman is allowed a runner only if the umpires are satisfied that an injury has been \\nsustained during the match which affects that batsman\'s ability to run. Note that unlike the \\ncase for allowance of a fielding substitute, no other reasons are allowed. However, having \\npreviously had a substitute acting for a player does not prevent them having a runner when \\nbatting. \\nThe umpires must make sure that any player acting as a runner is a nominated player of the \\nbatting side and that his/her dress and equipment comply with Law 25.5. It is specified in \\nLaw 25.5.2.4 that the runner must carry a bat and wear external protective equipment \\nequivalent to the batsman for whom he/she runs. As defined in Appendix A.2.2 this means: \\nFor a batsman, items permitted are a protective helmet, external leg guards (batting pads), \\nbatting gloves and, if visible, forearm guards. This, it should be remembered, does not apply \\nto internal protective equipment (such as a thigh pad or box) which is classified as clothing, \\nunder Appendix A.12.2. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Clearly the requirement that he/she should have already batted cannot be met if one of the \\nopening pair is injured. In that case another batsman is allowed to act as runner. Then, if the \\nother opener (who is not batting with a runner) is out that opener should take over as the \\nrunner, and the original runner should leave the field. \\nIf there is no batsman who has already batted able to act as runner, the runner should not \\nbe the next batsman to come in, so that he/she is not given an unfair advantage of seeing \\nthe bowling and getting used to the light. To prevent an advantage to the batting side of \\nhaving a tired runner replaced by a fresh one, he/she should not be changed for another \\none without the consent of the umpires, and only if there is no alternative (or, as above, if \\nthere is a batsman dismissed who can now do the job). This means that the runner should \\nbe from low in the order until a batsman is dismissed and then that dismissed batsman will \\nbecome the runner. \\nUmpires may have to explain these conditions to the captain of the batting side, particularly \\nif he/she believes that the runner can become the next batsman when a wicket falls. \\nIt is also important to note that no player should act as a runner if they are serving Penalty \\ntime, or while they are suspended under Law 42. \\n \\nInnings commencing \\nAt the start of each innings, it is not until Play is called that the innings of the opening \\nbatsmen start. The instant of stepping on to the field of play applies only to new batsmen \\ncoming in during a session of play. When Time is called in any session, the innings of those \\nbatsmen already at the wicket will continue in the next session, if their side is still batting. If, \\nhowever, a new batsman comes in as play resumes, his/her innings will not start until Play is \\ncalled to restart after the break, whatever the nature of the break. \\nIf a batsman steps onto the field of play but then does not begin his/her innings - because \\nthe previous batsman had left the field under a misapprehension, or the fielding captain \\nwithdraws an appeal and the decision is reversed (Laws 31.7 and 31.8) - then the innings of \\nthat new batsman will be deemed not to have started and is just removed from the record \\nat that time. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'Penalty time \\nPenalty time is only built up while a team is fielding. However, it is served, if necessary, \\nwhen a team is batting as well. Umpires will need to keep a note of any outstanding Penalty \\ntime from previous innings at the start of a new innings, and it is for them to prevent a \\nbatsman who is serving Penalty time from batting until he/she has either served that time, \\nor the batting side have lost their fifth wicket. Any unserved Penalty time from the fielding \\ninnings must therefore be clearly outlined to the captain of the batting side at the start of a \\nnew innings, to ensure the player does not come out to bat too early and create any \\nunnecessary delays. \\nThis Law prevents an opening batsman leaving the field towards the end of the innings \\nwhen their side is fielding, so they are rested when they come out to bat. Such a batsman \\nwould not be allowed to open the batting, and could not come in to bat until after the next \\nwicket, or a batsman retires, following their Penalty time having been served, or the fall of \\nthe 5th wicket, whichever is sooner. \\nAs discussed under Law 24, if there is an unscheduled break during the batting innings, the \\nbatsman with unserved Penalty time will benefit from all of this break providing he/she has \\nnotified the umpires that he/she is available to bat at the start of the break. If the batsman \\nis not available to bat at the start of the interruption, but is available at some point during \\nthe break, the time after he/ she notifies the umpires of his/her availability will count as \\nPenalty time served. In a two innings match, it might be that the batting innings ends during \\nan unscheduled break and the second innings starts. Provided the player with unserved time \\nis available to retake the field then he/she will benefit from that time. \\nAs in Law 24, suspension of a batsman under Level 3 of Law 42 will not count as Penalty \\ntime served.  \\nThere is a complete explanatory example of Penalty when batting or fielding, on pages 226-\\n7. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Batsman retiring \\nLaw 25.4 explains that a batsman may retire for any reason during his/her innings when the \\nball is Dead, but the umpires must be told the reason for retirement. This reason will \\ndetermine: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 first, whether he/she may resume his/her innings by right or only with the consent of \\nthe opposing captain. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Secondly, where he/she fails to resume that innings, how this is recorded by the \\nscorers.  \\nIf a batsman retires due to illness, injury or any other reasonable cause, he/she may resume \\nhis/her innings by right. If he/she does not do so, this is recorded as \'Retired- Not out. \\nIf the batsman retires for any other reason, he/she may only resume his/her innings with \\nthe consent of the opposition captain. If he/she does not do so, this is recorded as \'Retired - \\nOut\', \\n \\nBatsman who has a runner \\nFor the most part, an injured batsman and his/her runner are regarded as two parts of the \\nsame person. The batsman can be out, or penalties can be awarded against the batting side, \\nwhether the batsman or the runner is responsible There are times when this does not apply, \\nhowever. For example, if the injured batsman is out of his/her ground and not attempting a \\nrun, he/she can be Stumped. However, if the runner is also out of his/her ground, he/she is \\nalways considered to be attempting a run thus only Run out is possible, and not Stumped \\n(for more on this, see Law 39.) \\nIf the runner is out of his/her ground before the ball reaches the injured striker or passes \\nthe popping crease, whether or not he/she is attempting a run, this is deemed unfair and \\nthe batting team are punished under Law 25.7.2. Rather like a non-striker leaving his/her \\nground early (Law 41.16) a runner leaving his/her ground early when the injured batsman is \\non strike is putting himself/ herself at risk, and has only himself/herself to blame if the \\nmisdemeanour is spotted. However, in this case, the punishment is not only that the runner \\n(and thus the batsman for whom he/she is running) may be run out, but also that the \\nbatsmen are denied the chance to score any runs from that delivery. In this scenario, the \\nstriker\'s end umpire should call Dead ball as soon as the batsmen have completed one run \\nor the ball reaches the boundary - unless there is the opportunity for a catch to be taken, in \\nwhich case they must delay the call until the catch is either taken or dropped. In any case \\nthe runs will be disallowed, the batsmen returned to their original ends and any Penalty \\nruns (except under Law 28.3) awarded. \\n \\n"'b'b"When batting with a runner, the injured striker can never have the ground at the bowler\'s \\nend as his/her ground - if the batsman with a runner attempts to run despite the apparent \\ninjury, and reaches the bowler\'s end, he/she can still be Run out if the wicket is put down at \\nthe wicket keeper\'s end. When not on strike, the injured batsman has no ground and can \\nonly be out himself/herself Obstructing the Field. \\nIf the injured striker him/herself is Run out, no runs other than penalties will be scored, \\nwhereas normal scoring of runs applies if the runner is Run out. There is more detailed \\ndiscussion of this under Law 38 (Run out) and 39 (Stumped). \\nThere may be a situation where the striker has a runner, and the striker runs, and the \\nrunner leaves his/her ground early. In that situation, the striker\'s running is to be ignored. If \\nthe runner and the non-striker complete a run, or the ball reaches the boundary, the ball is \\nto be called dead, the runs disallowed and any Penalty runs due (except under Law 28.3) \\nawarded. If either batsman is Run out before the call of Dead ball, then they will be out. \\nHowever, if the fielding side attempts to Run out the striker - who may be out of his/her \\nground, after the call of Dead ball, this will not be out-the ball is already dead. \\n \\nField technique \\nWhen the injured striker has a runner, the umpire will have to judge whether or not the \\nrunner is within his/her ground, both during the delivery and for checking on short runs and \\nfor a possible run out. He/she must therefore position the runner so that the umpire can see \\nthe creases and the wicket as well as the runner. Additionally, the runner must not get in \\nthe way of the fielding side. The most suitable position is usually with the runner at square \\nleg and the umpire on the off side. If, however, there is good reason, such as low sun or \\nclearer markings on the ground to help judge the position of the runner, for the umpire to \\nbe on the leg side, then the positions can be reversed. \\nThe diagram below shows positioning for a right handed batsman. It demonstrates that, \\nregardless of which side the ball has gone, the bowler\'s end umpire must always go to the \\nside that his/her colleague is on, to avoid the embarrassment of having the runner running \\nbehind the umpire and therefore not visible. \\nWhen the injured batsman is not on strike then he/ she should stand as directed by the \\nstriker\'s end umpire so as not to interfere with play, usually near the umpire at the striker\'s \\nend. At this moment, the injured striker is not involved in the game in any way this is both \\nthe best position to avoid interference in play and makes it clear to the umpires of the \\ninjured striker were to bring him/herself Into play deliberately. However, the injured striker \\nshould neither be in front of the umpire (thus obscuring his/her view) nor behind (thus \\nunable to see the ball and potentially In a dangerous position). \\n"'b"b' The problems associated with appeals for Run out which there is a runner or even two \\nrunners on the field are discussed in more detail in Law 38. \\n \\n \\nU \\nUmpire \\nWK \\nWicket-keeper \\nR \\nRunner \\nIS \\nInjured striker for whom he/she runs \\nNS \\nNon-striker \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'LAW 26 PRACTICE ON THE FIELD  \\n26.1 Practice on the pitch or the rest of the square \\n26.1.1 There shall not be any practice on the pitch at any time on any day of the \\nmatch.  \\n26.1.2 There shall not be any practice on the rest of the square at any time on any day \\nof the match, except with the approval of the umpires. \\n \\n26.2 Practice on the outfield \\n26.2.1 On any day of the match, all forms of practice are permitted on the outfield  \\nbefore the start of play. \\nafter the close of play and \\nduring the lunch and tea intervals or between innings, providing the umpires are \\nsatisfied that such practice will not cause significant deterioration in the \\ncondition of the outfield. \\n26.2.2 Between the call of Play and the call of Time, practice shall be permitted on the \\noutfield, providing that all of the following conditions are met. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 only the fielders as defined in Appendix A.7 participate in such practice. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 no ball other than the match ball is used for this practice \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 no bowling practice takes place in the area between the square and the \\nboundary in a direction parallel to the match pitch. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the umpires are satisfied that it will not contravene either of Laws 41.3 \\n(The match ball -changing its condition) or 41.9 (Time wasting by the \\nfielding side). \\n \\n26.3 Trial run-up \\nA bowler is permitted to have a trial run-up provided the umpire is satisfied that it will not \\ncontravene either of Laws 41.9 (Time wasting by the fielding side) or 41.12 (Fielder \\ndamaging the pitch). \\n \\n26.4 Penalties for contravention \\nAll forms of practice are subject to the provisions of Laws 41.3 (The match ball - changing its \\ncondition), 41.9 (Time wasting by the fielding side) and 41.12 (Fielder damaging the pitch). \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"26.4.1 If there is a contravention of any of the prisoner 26.1 or 26.2, the umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 warn the player that the practice is not permitted. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other umpire and, as soon as practicable both captains of the \\nreason for this action. \\n26.4.1.1 If the contravention is by o batsman at the wicket, the umpire shall \\ninform the other batsman and each incoming batsman that the \\nwarning has been issued. The warning shall apply to the team of that \\nplayer throughout the match. \\n26.4.2 If during the match there is any further contravention by any player of that \\nteam, the umpire shall  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the opposing side; \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other umpire, the scorers and, as soon as practicable, both \\ncaptains, and, if the contravention is during play, the batsmen at the \\nwicket. \\n  \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after the match \\nto the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body responsible for the \\nmatch, who shall take such action as is considered appropriate against the captain \\nany other individuals concerned and, if appropriate, the team. \\n \\nPractice on the field \\nThis Law differentiates between the three areas the pitch, the rest of the square and the \\noutfield, namely the rest of the field of play. It sets out clearly when and where batting \\npractice or bowling practice, or other forms of practice, are or are not allowed in each area. \\nHowever, it is also less prescriptive than in previous Codes, giving the umpires a little more \\nflexibility - umpires should therefore be vigilant, but need not be too pedantic when \\nenforcing this Law. \\nPlayers will often practise throwing and catching the ball during a lull in play. At times, this \\ncan lead to waste of playing time. However, as long as they do this practice only with the \\nmatch ball and in a way that does not waste time or damage the ball, this is legitimate. This \\nmeans such practice will almost always be when there is some other necessary activity \\ntaking place, such as the captain re-setting the field for a new bowler, or the sight-screen \\nbeing moved. It is common for a bowler to bowl the ball to a fellow fielder at the beginning \\nof a new spell. If this is done while the striker is taking a new guard, or the sight-screen is \\nbeing moved, no time is being wasted. However the umpire should watch for signs of \\npotential damage to the ball, especially if bowled hard into the ground, even though the \\nintention was to loosen the bowler\'s shoulder, and intervene if necessary. \\n \\n"'b"b'The umpires must be ready to require the bowler to start the over as soon as such other \\nlegitimate activities are completed. A short comment to the bowler in good time will \\nprobably prevent the more severe action in Law 41.9 becoming necessary. \\nIf, however, fielders damage the ball or waste time, the umpire should implement the \\nprocedure set out in Law 41.3 or 41.9, initially giving a first and final warning to the captain \\nof the fielding side. \\n \\nPractice on the square \\nPractice on the pitch is never allowed, under any circumstances, at any time on the day of \\nthe match. \\nPractice on the square, excluding the pitch, at any time on any day of the match is only \\nallowed with the permission of the umpires. Any running, or even walking, on the square \\nwill cause some damage to the surface, but in good conditions this will not be significant. \\nUmpires must not allow practice on the square if they consider that the damage will be \\nsignificant. This may be because the surface is damp and soft. It may be because the surface \\nis very dry and easily scuffed up. \\n \\nPractice on the outfield \\nMuch pre-match practice takes place on the outfield. Players will also often practise before \\nplay starts, during the lunch and tea intervals, between innings, and between the close of \\nplay on one day and the start on the next in a multi-day game. This is permitted, provided \\nthe umpires believe it will not cause significant deterioration. The outfield usually has a \\nmore robust surface than the square. Nevertheless, in very damp conditions, significant \\ndamage could be caused. The umpires must not allow this. \\n \\nBetween the call of Play and the call of Time - that is while a session of play is in progress - \\ncertain practice is allowed, as long as it does not contravene Laws 41.3 or 41.9. It is also \\nimportant to note that only players already on the field of play may take part in this - a \\ncoach or substitute fielder (not on the field at that time) may not practise, or help a fielder \\npractise, from off the field, for example by throwing a ball for them to catch or having them \\nbowl a ball to them. However, as only the match ball can be used it should be easier for the \\numpires to control such practice, particularly at the fall of a wicket when the umpires will \\nhave the ball. \\n \\n'"b'b"The Law now specifically bans bowling practice in the area between the square and the \\nboundary in a direction parallel to the pitch. This might arise if the next bowler is fielding in \\nthe slips or gully and when the ball is passed back from the wicket-keeper instead of \\nthrowing it to say mid-off in order to get some practice he/she bowls the ball. The umpire \\nmust intervene and stop this, applying the sanctions if necessary. \\nIt should be remembered that batting practice, although unlikely, is also forbidden between \\nthe calls of Play and Time. \\n \\nPenalties for contravention \\nAll forms of practice, including a trial run-up, are subject to Laws 41.3, 41.9 and 41.12. If \\nplayers contravene Laws 26.1 or 26.2 by practising on the square or outfield when it is not \\npermitted, or by practising on the pitch at any time, the umpire will give a first and final \\nwarning to the players involved, advise his/her colleague and as soon as practical inform \\nboth captains. If the contravention is by a batsman at the wicket, the umpire informs the \\nother batsman and any incoming batsmen of the warning. If any member of the offending \\nside contravenes the Law during the rest of the match - not just the innings - the umpire will \\naward 5 Penalty runs, inform his/her colleague, the scorers and both captains and, if during \\nplay, the batsmen at the wicket. The umpires will report the captain and any other \\nindividuals involved (and it might be a coach or player not in the nominated team), as set \\nout in Law 26.4. \\n \\nTrial run-up \\nIt is inevitable that play will be held up if there is a change of bowler. The captain of the \\nfielding side is entitled to a reasonable time for re-arranging the field, though the umpire \\nshould intervene if he/she considers the captain is taking overlong to do this. The striker is \\nentitled to ask for the sight-screen to be moved, particularly if the new bowler\'s action is to \\nbe from the other side of the wicket While such a temporary hold up is occurring the bowler \\nis allowed to have a trial run-up if he/she wishes. However, the bowler is not allowed a trial \\nrun-up if there is no convenient and legitimate gap in play. Again, Law 41.9 is to be \\nimplemented if the umpire feels this is time wasting. If the bowler continues his/her trial \\nrun-up and follows through onto the pitch, they may be damaging the pitch, and should be \\nsanctioned under Law 41.12. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Damaging the ball \\nOften, as well as a trial run-up, a bowler may, at the beginning of his/her spell, want to bowl \\na \'loosener\' to a team-mate. This, as with the trial run-up, is fair so long as there is an \\nappropriate time to do so, and also if the bowler does not damage the ball (see Law 41.3). \\nThe bowler may, therefore, \'bowl\' the ball to a team-mate at mid-off. However, a practice \\nthat used to be common, of slamming the ball into the ground to loosen the shoulder, is \\nobviously more likely to damage the ball, and this should not be allowed. Umpires should \\nemploy reasonable judgment over whether the practice is likely to cause damage to the ball. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 27 THE WICKET-KEEPER  \\n27.1 Protective equipment \\nThe wicket-keeper is the only fielder permitted to wear gloves and external leg guards. If \\nthese are worn. they are to be regarded as part of his/her person for the purposes of Law \\n28.2 (Fielding the ball). If by the wicket-keeper\'s actions and positioning when the ball \\ncomes into play it is apparent to the empire that he she will not be able to carry out the \\nnormal duties of a wicket-keeper, he/she shall forfeit this right and also the right to be \\nrecognised as a wicket-keeper for the purposes of Laws 33.2 (A fair catch). 39 (Stumped). \\n28.1 (Protective equipment), 28.4 (Limitation of on-side fielders) and 28.5 (Fielders not to \\nencroach on pitch). \\n27.2 Gloves \\n27.2.1 If, as permitted under 271, the wicket-keeper wears gloves, they shall have no \\nwebbing between the fingers. except joining index finger and thumb, where \\nwebbing may be inserted as a means of support. \\n27.2.2 If used, the webbing shall be a single piece of non-stretch material which, although it \\nmay have facing material attached, shall have no reinforcements or tucks. \\n27.2.3 The top edge of the webbing shall not protrude beyond the straight line joining the \\ntop of the index finger to the top of the thumb and shall be taut when a hand \\nwearing the glove has the thumb fully extended. See Appendix E. \\n27.3 Position of wicket-keeper \\n27.3.1 The wicket-keeper shall remain wholly behind the wicket at the striker\'s end from \\nthe moment the ball comes into play until a ball delivered by the bowler  \\ntouches the bat or person of the striker \\nor             passes the wicket at the striker\'s end \\nor             the striker attempts a run. \\n27.3.2 In the event of the wicket-keeper contravening this Law, the striker\'s end umpire \\nshall call and signal No ball as soon as applicable after the delivery of the ball. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"27.4 Movement by wicket-keeper \\n27.4.1 After the ball comes into play and before it reaches the striker, it is unfair if the \\nwicket-keeper significantly alters his/her position in relation to the striker\'s wicket. \\nexcept for the following:  \\n27.4.1.1 movement of a few paces forward for a slower delivery unless in so doing it \\nbrings him/her within reach of the wicket.  \\n27.4.1.2 lateral movement in response to the direction in which the ball has been \\ndelivered. \\n27.4.1.3 movement in response to the stroke that the striker is playing or that \\nhis/her actions suggest he/she intends to play. However the provisions of \\nLaw 27.3 shall apply. \\n27.4.2 In the event of unfair movement by the wicket-keeper, either umpire shall call and \\nsignal Dead ball. \\n27.5 Restriction on actions of wicket-keeper \\n  \\nIt in the opinion of either umpire, the wicket keeper interferes with the striker\'s right \\nto play the ball and to guard his/her wicket. Law 20 4 26 (Umpire calling and \\nsignalling Dead ball) shall apply. \\n  \\nIt, however, either umpire considers that the interference by the wicket -keeper was \\nwilful, then Law 41.4 (Deliberate attempt to distract striker) shall also apply. \\n \\n27.6 Interference with wicket-keeper by striker \\n  \\nIf in playing at the ball or in the legitimate defence of his/her wicket, the striker \\ninterferes with the wicket: keeper, he/she shall not be out except as provided for in \\nLaw 37,3 (Obstructing a ball from being caught). \\n \\nExternal protective equipment \\nThe wicket-keeper is a fielder. Any reference to fielders in the Laws will include the wicket-\\nkeeper unless specifically stated otherwise. The wicket-keeper is, however, a special fielder. \\nHe/she alone is allowed to wear external pads and gloves. These count as part of his/her \\nperson where relevant. \\nAlthough not defined, the normal duties of the wicket keeper are well understood. He/she is \\nexpected to stand behind the wicket, in a line along which the ball is likely to travel if the \\nstriker misses it or delete it only slightly The wicket-keeper must be far enough back to have \\ntime to see the ball, but not so far as to miss the chance of a catch or a possible stumping in \\nthe umpires consider the wicket keeper is not in such a position perhaps behind the stumps \\n"'b'b"but virtually on the boundary perhaps in a slip or fine leg position then that player is not a \\nwicketkeeper and loses the privilege of wearing protective equipment tr that fielder is not in \\na valid wicket-keeping position but continues to wear pads and gloves, then contact \\nbetween the ball and any such item is illegal fielding, to be dealt with as in Law 28.2. Where \\nsuch a fielder is not in a valid wicket-keeping position then, regardless of whether the ball \\ntouches his/her gloves or pads, he/she forfeits the right to be regarded as a wicket-keeper \\nand a stumping is impossible (though a Run out would still be possible). He/ she becomes \\nsubject to Laws 28.4 (Limitation of on-side fielders) and 28.5 (Fielders not to encroach on \\nthe pitch). \\nThis does not mean that the wicket-keeper cannot leave the \'keeper\'s position\' to field the \\nball. Provided on every delivery he/she stands in a suitable \'keeper\'s position\' while the ball \\nis being delivered and the striker is receiving it, he/ she can thereafter move away freely to \\nfield the ball, without forfeiting the right to pads and gloves. \\nIt is also worth stating, as strongly implied by the above, that this means a team is not \\nrequired to have a wicket keeper. It is rare, but not unheard of, in modern cricket to \\nposition a player on the boundary behind the wicket, so as to prevent the batsman scooping \\nthe ball for four over where the wicket-keeper would be standing. That is entirely permitted, \\nso long as that fielder is not wearing any extra protective equipment that comes into \\ncontact with the ball. Umpires must use their judgment to decide what is too far back to be \\nconsidered as the wicket-keeper. The speed of the bowler will be relevant and a rule of \\nthumb is that the wicket-keeper will normally stand in a position where the ball will carry to \\nhim/her before bouncing - i.e. in a position to take a catch. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Gloves \\nNote that very particular requirements are laid down about the wicket-keeper\'s gloves, \\nstated in Law 27.2 and illustrated in Appendix E. Although these restrictions caused some \\nproblems when first introduced in 2000, they are now generally accepted by manufacturers, \\nand players are not likely to transgress. However, there may be occasions when gloves do \\nnot meet the requirements. In those cases, a wicket-keeper wearing illegal gloves must be \\nmade to change them. If, for some reason, no other gloves are available, the umpires must \\nuse their judgment. The matter must be reported, and every measure possible taken to \\nmake sure the wicket-keeper is not permitted to use the illegal equipment. However, it \\ncould be unsafe for a wicket keeper not to wear gloves at all. Umpires must be careful not \\nto encourage law-breaking, while ensuring player safety, and should work with both \\ncaptains to come to a solution. If a wicket-keeper does end up using illegal gloves, it is \\nimperative that the matter is reported to ensure he/she cannot do so again. \\n \\nPosition of wicket-keeper \\nApart from where the wicket-keeper must be, in general terms, to have the right to wear \\nextra equipment, Law 27.3 adds a specific restriction on his/her position. Behind the wicket \\nis defined in Appendix A.4. It is clear that the wicket-keeper must not have any part of \\nhis/her person or equipment - from the tip of a finger to the peak of a cap, etc. - further \\nforward than the back of the stumps. That means, and it is worth repeating, that if any part \\nof the wicket keeper\'s person or equipment is in line with the stumps, it is NOT behind, and \\nis thus not permitted. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nThe wicket-\\nkeeper is not \\nwholly \\nbehind the \\nstumps while \\nthe ball is \\nbeing \\ndelivered This \\nis a No ball. \\n \\n      \\n"'b'b"The wicket-keeper is confined from the moment the ball comes into play - that is the \\nmoment the bowler starts his her run up. The three events, any one of which will release \\nthe wicket-keeper from this prison are set out in this section of Law - they are that the ball \\ntouches the striker\'s bat or person, the ball passes the wicket, or the striker sets off for a \\nrun. A wicket-keeper seeing a chance of a stumping will often push his/her hands too far \\nforward, at the last minute, in the anxiety to take the ball. The striker\'s end umpire must be \\nwatching for this. The wicket-keeper is allowed to come in front for a catch, since the ball \\nwill have touched the striker\'s bat. Again the striker\'s end umpire must watch that the \\nwicket-keeper does not anticipate the catch and come in front before the ball has touched \\nthe bat. No ball is to be called and signalled by the striker\'s end umpire after delivery of the \\nball if the wicket-keeper moves too soon. \\n \\nThe wicket-keeper is taking \\nthe ball in front of the \\nstumps. This is permitted \\nONLY if the ball has hit the \\nstriker, the striker\'s bat, or \\na run has been attempted \\nbefore the keeper moved \\nfrom behind the stumps. \\nOther-wise it is a No ball. \\n \\nMovement by wicket-keeper \\nAs well as confining the wicket-keeper behind the stumps during the specified period the \\nlaw prevents him/her from making any significant movement between the ball coming into \\nplay and the ball reaching the striker If the wicket keeper realises that the delivery is a \\nslower one he/she can move forward a few paces. It on the other hand, the wicket keeper is \\nstanding back and creeps up close to a stumping position during the bowler\'s run-up this is \\nnot allowed and Dead ball should be called. Either umpire may see this movement. \\nWhichever one does must make the call and signal. There is no harm done if both umpires \\ndo! \\nWhile the wicket-keeper is not permitted to move from standing back to standing up after \\nthe ball comes into play, neither is he/she required to notify either the batsmen, or the \\numpires, if he/she is changing from one to the other between balls. So long as there is no \\nattempt to trick a batsman whilst the ball is live, a wicket-keeper may go from one to the \\nother as often as they please. Umpires should be aware of the wicket-keeper\'s position \\nimmediately before the bowler begins running in. \\n"'b'b"The wicket-keeper will be generally behind as described above, but may be slightly to the off \\nor leg side. This is not a problem, and he/she does not have to adopt exactly the same \\nposition every ball. However, the wicket keeper may only move from one side to the other \\nafter ball has come into play in response to the delivery, or a shot that the batsman shapes \\nto play. For example, the striker may change his/her position in an attempt to hit the ball \\nby means of a fine sweep or even changing from a right to left handed stance. If the striker \\ndoes this and the wicket keeper moves in response and, by doing so, is on the other side of \\nthe wicket then this is allowed and does not count as significant movement by the wicket-\\nkeeper\'. The provisions of Law 27.3 still apply.  \\nStriker\'s right to play the ball  \\nThe striker is entitled to play the ball, and guard his/her wicket, without interference from \\nthe fielding side. This enables the batsman to make a late cut, or to follow the ball round on \\nthe leg side and hit it after it has passed the line of the wicket. If the wicket-keeper \\ninterferes with this, it is likely to be accidental and the call of Dead ball and the ball not \\ncounting in the over is the only action required. The umpires must, however, judge whether \\nthe action was deliberate. If either umpire so decides then this is a case of obstruction or \\ndistraction of the striker. Action will be as set out in Law 41.4. \\n \\nStriker interfering with wicket-keeper \\n The striker has an absolute right to play the ball and to defend the wicket, even with a \\nsecond stroke, subject to the two provisos: \\n  \\nThe batsman must be either genuinely trying to play the ball or solely defending \\nhis/her wicket and     he/she must not obstruct a catch by any fielder  \\n  \\n                                   not only one by the wicket-keeper. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"As long as these two requirements are met, no action is to be taken if the striker interferes \\nwith the wicket-keeper\'s attempt to gather the ball. He/she may play a very late cut when \\nthe wicket-keeper is already in the process of taking the ball. Provided the batsman is \\ngenuinely playing the ball, he/she is allowed to do so without penalty, although he/she will \\nbe out Obstructing the field if the \'interference is deliberate and prevents a catch. The same \\nis true of a second strike, provided it is genuinely and solely in defence of the stumps. \\nIn the case of a second strike, however, should either umpire form the opinion that \\ndefending the wicket was not the striker\'s sole motive, if the action interferes with the \\nwicket-keeper then, rather than being out Hit the ball twice, the batsman will be out \\nObstructing the field. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 28 THE FIELDER \\n28.1 Protective equipment \\n  \\nNo fielder other than the wicket-keeper shall be permitted to wear gloves or \\nexternal leg guards. In action protection for the hand or fingers may be women only \\nwith the consent of the umpires. \\n \\n28.2 Fielding the ball \\n28.2.1 A  fielder may field the ball with any part of his/her person (see Appendix A12), \\nexcept as in 28.2.1.2. However, he/she will be deemed to have fielded the ball \\nillegally it, while the ball is in play he/she wilfully  \\nuses anything other than part of his/her person to field the ball.  \\nextends his/her clothing with his/her hands and uses this to field the ball. \\ndiscards a piece of clothing, equipment or any other object which subsequently \\nmakes contact with the ball. \\n28.2.2 It is not illegal fielding if the ball in play makes contact with a piece of clothing, \\nequipment or any other object which has accidentally fallen from the fielder\'s \\nperson. \\n28.2.3 If a fielder illegally fields the ball, the ball shall immediately become dead and \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the penalty for a No ball or a Wide shall stand. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 any runs completed by the batsmen shall be credited to the batting side, \\ntogether with the run in progress if the batsmen had already crossed at the \\ninstant of the offence. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the ball shall not count as one of the over.  \\nIn addition the umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the batting side. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other umpire and the captain of the fielding side of the reason for \\nthis action. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the batsmen and, as soon as practicable, the captain of the batting \\nside of what has occurred. \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after the \\nmatch to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body responsible \\nfor the match, who shall take such action as is considered appropriate against the \\ncaptain, any other individuals concerned and, if appropriate, the team. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"28.3 Protective helmets belonging to the fielding side  \\n28.3.1 Protective helmets, when not in use by fielders, may not be placed on the ground, \\nabove the surface except behind the wicket-keeper and in line with both sets of \\nstumps.  \\n28.3.2 If the ball while in play strikes a helmet, placed as described in 28.3.1 \\n28.3.2.1 the ball shall become dead and subject to 28.3.3. \\n28.3.2.2 an award of 5 Penalty runs shall be made to the batting side; \\n28.3.2.3 any runs completed by the batsmen before the ball strikes the protective \\nhelmet shall be scored, together with the run in progress if the batsmen \\nhad already crossed at the instant of the ball striking the protective \\nhelmet.  \\n28.3.3 If the ball while in play strikes a helmet, placed as described in 28.3.1, unless the \\ncircumstances of Law 23.3 (Leg byes not to be awarded) or Law 34 (Hit the ball \\ntwice) apply, the umpire shall  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 permit the batsmen\'s runs as in 28.3.2.3 to be scored \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 signal No ball or Wide ball to the scorers if applicable  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs as in 28.3.2.2 \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award any other Penalty runs applicable. \\n28.3.4 if the ball while in play strikes a helmet placed as described in 28 31 and the \\ncircumstances of Law 23.3 (Leg byes not to be awarded) or Law 34 (Hit the ball twice \\napply, the umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 disallow all runs to the batting side \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 return any not out batsman to his/her original end  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 signal No ball or Wide ball to the scorers if applicable \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award any 5-run Penalty that is applicable except for Penalty runs under \\n28.3.2. \\n \\n28.4 Limitation of on side fielders \\n  \\nAt the instant of the bowler\'s delivery there shall not be more than two fielders, \\nother than the wicket-keeper, behind the popping crease on the on side. A fielder \\nwill be considered to be behind the popping crease unless the whole of his/her \\nperson whether grounded or in the air is in front of this line. \\n  \\nIn the event of infringement of this Law by any fielder the striker\'s end umpire shall \\ncall and signal No ball. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"28.5 Fielders not to encroach on pitch \\n  \\nWhile the ball is in play and until the ball has made contact with the striker\'s bat or \\nperson, or has passed the striker\'s bat, no fielder, other than the bowler, may have \\nany part of his/her person grounded on or extended over the pitch. \\n  \\nIn the event of infringement of this Law by any fielder other than the wicket-keeper. \\nthe bowler\'s end umpire shall call and signal No ball as soon as possible after \\ndelivery of the ball. Note, however, Law 27.3 (Position of wicket-keeper). \\n  \\n \\n28.6 Movement by any fielder other than the wicket-keeper \\n28.6.1 Any movement by any fielder, excluding the wicket-keeper, after the ball comes into \\nplay and before the ball reaches the striker, is unfair except for the following: \\n28.6.1.1 minor adjustments to stance or position in relation to the striker\'s wicket.  \\n28.6.1.2 movement by any fielder, other than a close fielder, towards the striker or \\nthe striker\'s wicket that does not significantly alter the position of the \\nfielder. \\n28.6.1.3 movement by any fielder in response to the stroke that the striker is \\nplaying or that his/her actions suggest he/she intends to play. \\n28.6.2 In all circumstances Law 28.4 (Limitation of on side fielders) shall apply. \\n28.6.3 In the event of such unfair movement, either umpire shall call and signal Dead ball. \\n28.6.4 Note also the provisions of Law 41.4 (Deliberate attempt to distract striker). See also \\nLaw 27.4 (Movement by wicket-keeper). \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"External protective equipment \\n \\nThe only visible protective equipment that a fielder other than the wicket-keeper, is allowed \\nto wear is a protective helmet. Appendix A2 spells out that this term includes face guards If \\na fielder genuinely needs protection for a damaged hand or fingers permission to wear it \\nmust be obtained from the umpires who will ensure that it is neither excessive nor \\nunreasonable. No protection that may give any advantage to a bowler should be permitted \\nwhile he/she is bowling and umpires must be vigilant to ensure that any other protective \\nmeasures are genuinely needed, and not in order to gain an unfair advantage. While player \\nsafety is paramount, umpires and players should ensure the wearing or exchange of \\nexternal protective equipment does not unduly delay play. \\n \\nFielding the ball \\nFielding the ball is not necessarily stopping it and grasping it. It is contact with the ball in a \\ndeliberate attempt to stop it. A fielder is allowed to do this with any part of his/her person, \\nas defined in Appendix A.12. \\nWilfully fielding it otherwise\' is deliberately using something that is not a part of his/her \\nperson to field it. The classic (but extremely rare) example is a fielder using a cap he/she has \\ntaken off to field the ball. \\nIt could be contact with an illegal item of visible protective equipment or clothing, though \\nthe umpires should ensure such items are promptly removed. An example might be a shin \\nguard often worn by a close fielder being quickly put on during the innings, over the trouser \\nleg and hence visible. The umpire should ensure this is not visible before the ball comes into \\nplay - but if it is, and the ball makes contact with it, that is illegal fielding \\nIf the fielder uses some form of equipment, deliberately, to field the ball, that is \'wilful, and \\nthere have been cases of a fielder fielding the ball while holding a glove discarded by the \\nkeeper. \\nIf the ball makes contact with clothing equipment or other object deliberately discarded by \\na fielder, this constitutes illegal fielding. A fielder wilfully discarding an Item of clothing or \\nequipment e.g. his/her cap or helmet. whilst running to field a ball which is then hit by the \\nball. is an example of illegal fielding. The same applies to the wicket-keeper tossing a glove \\naside, and a thrown ball hitting that discarded glove. While the act of the ball hitting the \\nglove may not be deliberate, the ball comes into contact with the item of equipment \\nbecause of a deliberate act (the removal). It is thus deemed to be a wilful act.  \\n \\n"'b'b"It is not illegal fielding, however, if the item (be it a cap, a helmet, a glove or any other piece \\nof equipment or clothing) has fallen off accidentally and is then hit by the ball.  \\nCases have been known of the ball accidentally falling into a fielder\'s open-necked shirt, and \\neven into one of his/ her pockets. Such incidents are not unlawful, because they are not \\nwilful acts. It is however illegal to deliberately field the ball using worn clothing, such as \\nextending a sweater or loose shirt to catch the ball. \\nThere is a difference between illegal fielding, defined in this Law, and the transgression in \\nLaw 24.4 of a fielder coming onto the field without permission. There the contact with the \\nball does not have to be deliberate, though it almost always is. The offence is merely coming \\non to the field without permission and touching the ball, even accidentally. The penalty is \\nnevertheless the same in both Laws: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The ball immediately becomes dead and does not count in the over. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Five penalty runs are awarded to the batting side, together with runs completed or \\ncrossed on at the instant of the illegal contact and any penalty for a No ball or Wide. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The umpire informs his/her colleague, the fielding captain and the batsmen and, as \\nsoon as practical, the batting captain.  \\nIllegal fielding should always be reported in both cases. \\n \\nHelmets on the ground \\n \\nIt has long been accepted that, whilst umpires will hold a bowler\'s cap or sweater during the \\nover at their end, umpires do not carry helmets. A batsman\'s unwanted helmet needs to be \\nremoved from the field of play. Fielders are allowed to put them on the ground behind the \\nwicket keeper to save the time to take them off the field. Umpires should check that they \\nare in line with the stumps and far enough back. The ball becomes Dead, and the penalties \\nset out in Law 28.3 apply, only when the ball touches a helmet in this position - not if it falls \\noff accidentally. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b\' \\n \\n \\nHelmets not \\nbeing worn by \\nthe fielding side \\nshould be placed \\nas shown - \\ndirectly in line \\nwith the stumps \\nand behind the \\nwicket \\nkeeper \\n \\n \\nUnder Law 28.3 (except as indicated below where runs are "disallowed"), runs completed \\nand crossed before the ball touches the unworn helmet are scored and 5 Penalty runs are \\nawarded to the batting side.  \\nIt should be noted that there is no report to be made under Law 28.3 - the only instance in \\nthe Laws where the award of 5 penalty runs is not followed by a report after the match. This \\nis because putting the helmet on the ground is not in itself an unfair act, whereas trying to \\nfield the ball by manoeuvring it or, for example, by pulling out a sweater to form a pouch, is \\ndeliberately unfair. \\nIf a fielder\\\'s helmet is discarded deliberately, it is treated like any other item, in line with \\nLaw 28.2 and carries the additional sanction of being reported if the ball touches it. \\nThe penalty for a No ball or a Wide will always stand, and any other Penalty runs can be \\nawarded if necessary. However, as discussed in Law 23, the particular penalty for the ball \\nhitting the unworn fielder\\\'s helmet on the ground behind the wicket-keeper is not awarded \\nin those situations where runs are disallowed. \\n \\nLimitation on the position of fielders \\n1.Behind the popping crease \\nThe restriction in Law 28.4, to have no more than two fielders behind the popping crease on \\nthe on side, is absolute. A third fielder with as little as a hand overhanging the back edge of \\nthe popping crease marking contravenes the Law. Sometimes, however, the wicket-keeper \\nwill stand on the leg side to get a better view of the flight of the ball. If so, this does not \\ncount as one of the two.  \\n \\n\''b'b"The time when the embargo is in force is at the instant of delivery. Apart from having a \\nnumber of other things to watch, the main difficulty for the striker\'s end umpire is the \\nfielder walking in towards the wicket, who may start in a legitimate position before the ball \\nis delivered but, as he/she walks in, crosses the popping crease - which, of course, extends \\nto the boundary. A situation in which this could happen is when there are already two \\nfielders behind the popping crease as the bowler runs up, and a fielder at square leg in front \\nof it but close to it. It is essential that the umpire can observe that square leg fielder, which \\nis not possible if the fielder is positioned behind the umpire. The striker\'s end umpire must \\ntherefore cross to the off side so that he/she can see this fielder. For any contravention, the \\nstriker\'s end umpire must call and signal No ball. \\n \\n2. Encroaching on the pitch \\nThe other restriction on fielders\' positions is in Law 28.5. Here the embargo is in force, not \\njust at an instant in time, but over a longer period, set out in detail in the Law. The bowler \\nmay run on the pitch, within strict limits, in the follow-through. No other fielder is allowed \\non the pitch throughout the stated period. It should be noted that often the \'pitch\' will not \\nbe the same width as the cut strip - which is often slightly narrower. Again, just as for \\nfielders behind the popping crease, \'on the pitch includes not only feet grounded on the \\npitch, but any part of a fielder\'s person overhanging it. \\n \\nEven \\nthough the \\nfielder\'s \\nhand is in \\nthe air, the \\nfielder is \\nencroaching \\non the pitch \\nwhile the \\nball is being \\ndelivered - \\nthis is a No \\nball. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"The fielder may be encroaching on the pitch even if not on the cut strip, and umpires must \\nbe aware of the difference, and be prepared to make fielders aware of that difference to \\navoid them infringing. The penalty for contravention is again a call and signal of No ball but \\nin this case by the bowler\'s end umpire. Although it may appear that the wicket-keeper is \\nexcluded from the embargo, this is only because if he/she offends, as will be the case if, for \\nexample. he/she has any part of the wicket-keeping glove level with, or in front of the \\nstumps too soon, then action is to be taken as laid down in Law 27 (The wicket-keeper) \\nrather than under this Law. \\n \\nMovement by fielders \\nThe striker is entitled to know where the fielders are. Therefore, once the bowler starts \\nrunning up, when the striker will be concentrating on that, it is unfair if a fielder moves to a \\nsignificantly different position. Movement that a fielder is allowed to make is defined in Law \\n28.6. Close fielders are allowed to make minor adjustments to their stance, but should not \\nmove as the ball is bowled as this may distract the striker. For other fielders, walking in few \\npaces towards the striker\'s wicket is permitted, and expected, but that is usually the limit. \\nBoundary fielders are permitted to walk a few more paces than those fielding in the infield, \\nwhich is acceptable so long as their position is not materially changed. The exception is \\nwhen the movement is as a reaction to a shot the batsman is playing or looks to be about to \\nplay. The classic example of this is the fine, or paddle, sweep. As the striker makes clear his/ \\nher intention to play the shot, a slip might run towards leg slip - this is allowed, as long as it \\nis a reaction to the batsman\'s movement (subject to Law 28.4, discussed above). \\nWhen the movement is unfair, a call and signal of Dead ball must be made immediately by \\nwhichever umpire sees it. \\nField technique \\nAll the things that have been said previously, about where umpires must focus their \\nattention while the ball is being delivered by the bowler and received by the striker, will \\napply. An additional point for this Law is that it is quite possible that the players will \\ncontinue play, not realising that the ball has become dead automatically in a case of illegal \\nfielding, or even of the ball hitting a helmet on the ground. In such a situation, the bowler\'s \\nend umpire may need to take up the option offered in Law 20.4.1 of nevertheless calling \\nand signalling Dead ball. It will not be necessary to repeat this to the scorers, as it is an \\n\'extra\' signal to inform the players. It may well be helpful, however, to tell the scorers the \\nnumber of runs to be recorded. It is possible that they will not be able to tell whether the \\nbatsmen had crossed or not before the instant of the offence. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 29 THE WICKET IS DOWN \\n29.1 Wicket put down \\n29.1.1 The wicket is put down if a bail is completely removed from the top of the stumps, \\nor a stump is struck out of the ground, \\n29.1.1.1 by the ball, \\n29.1.1.2 by the striker\'s bat if held or by any part of the bat that he/she is holding, \\n29.1.1.3 for the purpose of this law only, by the striker\'s bat not in hand, or by any \\npart of the bat which has become detached, \\n29.1.1.4 by the striker\'s person or by any part of his/her clothing or equipment \\nbecoming detached from his/her person, \\n29.1.1.5 by a fielder with his/her hand or arm, providing that the ball is held in the \\nhand or hands so used, or in the hand of the arm so used. \\n29.1.1.6 The wicket is also put down if a fielder strikes or pulls a stump out of the \\nground as in 29.1.1.5. \\n29.1.2 The disturbance of a bail, whether temporary or not, shall not constitute its \\ncomplete removal from the top of the stumps, but if a bail in falling lodges between \\ntwo of the stumps this shall be regarded as complete removal. \\n \\n29.2 One bail off \\n  \\nIf one bail is off, it shall be sufficient for the purpose of putting the wicket down to \\nremove the remaining bail or to strike or pull any of the three stumps out of the \\nground, in any of the ways stated in 29.1. \\n \\n29.3 Remaking wicket \\nIf a wicket is broken or put down while the ball is in play, it shall not be remade by an \\numpire until the ball is dead. See Law 20 (Dead ball). Any fielder may, however, while the \\nball is in play, \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 replace a bail or bail on top of the stumps. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 put back one or more stumps into the ground where the wicket originally stood. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b\'29.4 Dispensing with bails \\nIf the umpires have agreed to dispense with bails in accordance with Law 8.5 (Dispensing \\nwith bails), it is for the umpire concerned to decide whether or not the wicket has been put \\ndown. \\n29.4.1 After a decision to play without bails, the wicket has been put down if the umpire \\nconcerned is satisfied that the wicket has been struck by the ball, by the striker\\\'s bat, \\nperson or items of his/her clothing or equipment as described in 29.11.2, 29.11.3 or \\n29.114. or by a fielder in the manner described in 29.1.1.5. \\n29.4.2 If the wicket has already been broken or put down, 29.41 shall apply to any stump or \\nstumps still in the ground. \\nAny fielder may replace a stump or stumps, in accordance with 29.3, in order to \\nhave an opportunity of putting the wicket down. \\nThere is a distinction between a wicket being down and being put down. A wicket is down if \\nit is not a complete wicket as defined in Law 8. The wicket is put down by the act of \\nremoving one or more of its stumps or bails. How the wicket is put down is crucial in \\ndetermining whether a batsman is out or not for Bowled, Hit wicket, Run out or Stumped. \\nLaw 29.1 sets out first what is to be achieved for the wicket to be down and then lists the \\nways which this stage is to be reached, in order for the wicket to have been fairly put down. \\nThese ways are clearly listed in Law 29.1.1. Umpires should study the list. The words \\\'break\\\' \\nand "broken\\\' are sometimes used in relation to removing bails, etc. They do not have the \\nsame significance as \\\'put down! A bowler may (accidentally) break the wicket by running too \\nclose to it while running up. He/she has not in the strict sense, put the wicket down and \\nalthough this would be a No ball, the wicket can be rebuilt by a member of the fielding side \\nbefore the ball is Dead to allow for a later Run out attempt. \\nLaw 29.2 then makes it clear that the starting point for putting the wicket down does not \\nhave to be a complete wicket. A bail may be off already; the other one (or a stump) can be \\nremoved. Both bails may be off; a stump can be removed. Putting the wicket down is \\nreducing whatever state it is in to one with fewer bails and/or stumps. Only when all three \\nstumps are out of the ground is it impossible to put a wicket down. Even then, or at any \\nearlier stage, the wicket can be partly re-assembled by a fielder replacing stumps or bails to \\ngive an opportunity to put the wicket down. It should be noted that \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n\''b"b'\\xef\\x82\\xa7 an umpire should not try to remake the wicket, in any way, until the ball is dead \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 if fielders replace stumps, as they are permitted to do, they must put them where \\nthe wicket originally was. \\n \\nIn talking of the cases where the striker may be responsible for putting down his/her own \\nwicket, it should be noted that this is one of only two instances in the Laws where items of \\nclothing or equipment, no longer attached to a player and therefore no longer part of either \\nhis/her equipment or person, are treated as though they were. This is important in \\nconsidering an appeal for Hit wicket. The other occasion is Law 21.6 when the bowler breaks \\nthe wicket delivering the ball. \\n \\nComplete removal \\n1. A bail \\nThere are instances where a bail has \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 jumped out of its groove and settled back into the groove  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 been jerked out of its groove and settled back on top of one stump \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 fallen between the stumps so that one end is pressed against one stump, the other \\nend sticking up above the neighbouring stump. \\nNone of these has been completely removed from the top of the stumps. Only when the \\nwhole bail, whether on the ground or jammed between two stumps, is below the level of \\nthe top of the stumps has it been completely removed: For the purposes of dismissal - \\ndeciding perhaps whether the batsmen had crossed or not - a bail has been removed at the \\nmoment that both ends of it leave their grooves. If, however, subsequently it is not \\ncompletely removed, as in the examples above, then the temporary removal will be \\nconsidered never to have happened. \\nThe umpire, however, is unlikely to be able to make such fine judgment with the naked eye \\n \\n \\n'"b'b" \\nBoth balls are on top of the \\nwicket - the wicket is not \\ndown. \\nPart of the displaced bail is \\nabove the top of the stumps \\n- the wicket is not down \\nOne bail is completely below \\nthe top of the stumps - the \\nwicket is down \\n \\n2. A stump \\nA stump is not \'knocked out of the ground\' until it is completely free from the ground Being \\nknocked so far as to be nearly horizontal is not enough. \\n \\nBall held in hand \\nAlthough a fielder can throw the ball on to the stumps to put the wicket down, they can also \\ndo it with the ball in hand. He/she can then use the ball in hand to put the wicket down, or \\ncan use his/her hand - or even that arm right up to the shoulder - but it must be the hand \\nwhich is holding the ball, or that same arm The fielder can use no other part of his/her body \\nto break the wicket even if the ball is in close proximity - eg the ball is trapped between the \\nknees or in the top of a wicket-keeper\'s pad which are then used to break the wicket. \\nPulling a stump out of the ground one-handed is virtually impossible with the ball held in the \\nsame hand, unless it is a very large hand. Usually it will have to be achieved by holding the \\nball in both hands and pulling up the stump with both hands Holding the ball in one hand \\nand pulling up the stump with the other is no use. Rather than pulling the Law permits a \\nfielder to strike a stump out. As noted above, he/she must knock it clear out of the ground \\ndifficult to do if the stumps are firmly set. In a mad rush for a run out, a fielder may throw \\ntheir whole body at the stumps, demolishing the wicket. So long as the ball is in at least one \\nhand, and that hand or arm is what knocks out the stumps, this is permissible - however, it \\n"'b'b"may be difficult to be sure it was not some other body part, so fielders are risking the \\numpire\'s uncertainty, and thus a Not out verdict. \\nThe fielder \\nhas struck the \\nwicket with his \\narm,  holding \\nthe ball in that \\nhand. The \\nwicket has been \\nfairly put down \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Artificial pitches \\nIf an artificial pitch is being used, it is often not possible to have normal stumps fixed in the \\nground. Various replacements, such as \'spring-back stumps\' are available. They can involve \\ndifficulties in judging the breaking of the wicket. Sometimes Playing Conditions for the \\nmatch will instruct umpires on the modifications needed. In any case, the two umpires must \\nagree any necessary details before the start of play: \\nDispensing with bails \\nThe decision whether to do so or not has been discussed in Law 8. If bails have been \\ndispensed with, the judgment of the wicket being fairly put down is considerably modified. \\nThe umpire at that end must judge not what might have happened if the bails had been \\nthere, but what has happened to the stumps. That means judging whether a stump or \\nstumps have been touched, however slightly, by \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the ball \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the striker\'s bat or person or items detached from him as set out in Law 29.1 \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 a fielder with the hand holding the ball, or with the arm of the hand holding the ball. \\n The umpire does not have to judge how hard the strike was, merely that it has happened. \\nHe/she does not have to consider that from some earlier incident the balls might already \\nhave been off, had they been there in the first place. The umpire looks, on each occasion, at \\nwhat happens to the stumps, even if they are askew, as long as they are in the ground, or at \\nleast the one that is struck is in the ground. It must be emphasised that all the above applies \\nonly if the umpires have decided not to have bails. It is not relevant to the situation of the \\nbails coming off accidentally in the course of play, or having been removed in an \\nunsuccessful attempt to secure a dismissal. \\nWhen bails have been dispensed with, fielders will not need to pull a stump fully out of the \\nground to put the wicket down - even if one or more of the stumps have already been \\nremoved. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Field technique \\nIt was emphasised in Law 18 how important it is for each umpire to have a view of the \\nwicket and the creases while the batsmen are running.
