The need to watch whether the \\nwicket is fairly put down, especially for a possible run out, underlines again the importance \\nof this. \\nThe essentials are that the umpire must be able to see with his/her head stationary, the \\nputting down of the wicket and the position of the batsman in relation to the popping \\ncrease at that time. This means he/she must move as quickly as possible without waiting to \\nassess the situation. He/she must not get in the way of the fielding of the ball, a particular \\nhazard if this is happening close to the wicket. In that situation, however, it will be obvious \\nwhere the umpire must go to avoid the fielders and the ball. If there is a runner, the umpire \\nmust not have the runner behind him/ her and must go to the same side as the striker\'s end \\numpire is on. He/she must not get in the way of the throw in, which would disadvantage the \\nfielding side, or get hit him/herself. \\nWith all these difficulties, the traditional advice is still to go to the side to which the ball has \\nbeen hit, avoiding the path of the throw in, unless the existence of a runner, or of the \\nfielding being close to the wicket, demand otherwise. The growing practice, particularly at \\nhigher levels of the game, of the fielder receiving the throw in standing between the wicket \\nand the fielder throwing the ball, can make it easier to move to the other side to have a \\nbetter and safer view of the putting down of the wicket. \\nWhat is important is not so much which side the umpire goes to - there are advantages and \\ndisadvantages for either side, and, as discussed elsewhere, it may depend on where the ball \\nhas been hit - but that he/she should do it very quickly and therefore as a habit without \\nhaving to think about it. At worst the umpire may have to have a diagonal view of events \\nrather than a side-on one - though this should be a last resort and should only happen if the \\nball is hit to a close fielder very quickly. \\nThe striker\'s end umpire is \'stuck\' on one side of the wicket but, as he/she is already at a \\ngood distance from the wicket, can usually move to one side in sufficient time if his/ her \\nview becomes blocked by a fielder. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b' \\nThe wicket-keeper takes a throw from a \\nfielder... \\n...but drops the ball before breaking the wicket. \\nIt is rarely, if ever, as obvious as this. \\n \\nThe two things that an umpire must watch for with the utmost vigilance are that \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the fielder actually had the ball in his/her hand. It is not uncommon for the fielder to \\ntake the ball but in the act of moving the hand quickly to put down the wicket, to let \\ngo of it. He/she may be moving his/her hands towards the stumps in anticipation of \\nreceiving the ball but break the wicket before it is actually in his/her hand. This is a \\nspecial risk for the wicket-keeper. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 it is the hand (or arm of the hand) holding the ball that breaks the wicket. It can \\nhappen that a fielder, concentrating on catching the throw in, knocks against the \\nwicket with some other part of his/her person. \\nIf the umpire is unable to see clearly that the wicket has been put down correctly, he/she \\nmay wish to consult with his/her colleague, who might have had a different view. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"LAW 30 BATSMAN OUT OF HIS/HER GROUND \\n30.1 When out of his/her ground  \\n30.1.1 A batsman shall be considered to be out of his/her ground unless some part of \\nhis/her person or bat is grounded behind the popping crease at that end. \\n30.1.2 However, a batsman shall not be considered to be out of his/her ground if, in \\nrunning or diving towards his/ her ground and beyond, and having grounded some \\npart of his/her person or bat beyond the popping crease there is subsequent loss of \\ncontact \\nbetween the ground and any part of his/her person or bat \\nor            between the bat and person. \\n \\n30.2 Which is a batsman\'s ground \\n \\nIf only one batsman is within a ground, it is his/her ground and will remain so even if he/she \\nis later joined there by the other batsman. \\nIf both batsmen are in the same ground and one of them subsequently leaves it, the ground \\nbelongs to the batsman who remains in it. \\nIf there is no batsman in either ground, then each ground belongs to whichever batsman is \\nnearer to it. or, if the batsmen are level, to whichever batsman was nearer to it immediately \\nprior to their drawing level. \\nIf a ground belongs to one batsman then, unless there is a striker who has a runner, the \\nother ground belongs to the other batsman, irrespective of his/her position. \\nWhen a batsman who has a runner is striker, his/her ground is always at the wicket-keeper\'s \\nend. However, 30.21, 30.2.2, 30.2.3 and 30.24 will still apply, but only to the runner and the \\nnon-striker, so that that ground will also belong to either the non-striker or the runner as \\nthe case may be. \\n \\n30.3 Position of non-striker \\nThe non-striker, when standing at the bowler\'s end should be positioned on the opposite \\nside of the wicket to that from which the ball is being delivered unless a request to do \\notherwise is granted by the umpire. This Law defines when a batsman is in or out of his/her \\nground, for use in conjunction mainly with Laws 18 (Scoring runs), 38 (Run out) and 39 \\n(Stumped). \\n"'b'b" \\nA batsman\'s ground \\nA batsman\'s ground at each end of the pitch is defined in Appendix A.6.3 as the whole area \\nof the field of play behind the popping crease, which of course extends all the way to the \\nboundary on either side of the marking. To be in his/her ground a batsman must have some \\npart of his/her person, or bat held in his/her hand, grounded behind the popping crease at \\nthat end - on the line of the marked crease clearly does not count. \\n \\nThe bat is over the \\npopping crease, but is \\nnot grounded. Unless it \\nhas previously been \\ngrounded over the \\npopping crease and has \\nbounced up while the \\nbats man is running or \\ndiving, the batsman has \\nnot made his/ her \\nground. \\nThe bat is on the crease \\nmarking. This is not \\nbehind the popping \\ncrease, and thus not in \\nhis/her ground. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"There is a difference between a batsman being in his/her ground, and making good his/her \\nground. To be in their ground, batsmen need some of their person or bat in hand grounded \\nbehind the popping crease. To have made good their ground, having left it, is a little more \\ncomplicated:  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 When a batsman is running to make his/her ground to avoid being Run out or \\nStumped, he/ she is unlikely to slow down on reaching the popping crease. Having \\nsuccessfully grounded one or more feet behind the popping crease the batsman may \\ncontinue running in the same direction. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 When running or diving in an attempt to make his/her ground, it is often the bat \\nwhich first makes contact with the batsman\'s ground. However, if it hits an uneven \\npatch or a bowler\'s footmark as the batsman is sliding it in, it could bounce up, losing \\ncontact with the ground. In an extreme case, the bat could be forced out of the \\nbatsman\'s hand altogether. \\nIf the wicket were to be put down at the precise moment when either both of the batsman\'s \\nfeet or the bat, held in the hand, were not in contact with the ground, or the bat is not in \\ncontact with the batsman\'s hand, then technically the batsman would not be in his/her \\nground. However, he/ she might have already made good their ground\' by placing a foot or \\na bat behind the popping crease. The batsman is therefore protected in this situation by Law \\n30.1.2. In order for this protection to apply the batsman must, in running or diving, already \\nhave made good their ground and must not be in the process of turning to start another \\nrun. If both of those apply, then the batsman is deemed to be in his/her ground. \\nThe Law requires that the batsman is \'running or diving to ensure that a batman who has \\nsimply raised his/her foot in attempting to play a shot is not protected from being Stumped. \\nWhose ground is it? \\nWhether one end is a particular batsman\'s ground or not is set out in Law 30.2. It is a simple \\nmatter if each batsman is in a different ground. Each is then in his/her ground. It is equally \\nsimple if neither is in a ground, while they are running (or even stationary) between the \\nwickets and no part of their persons or equipment overlap. Each ground then belongs to the \\nbatsman who is nearest to it. If one is in a ground and the other is not, the empty ground \\nbelongs to the one who is not in a ground.  \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"However, there are several situations which are not as clear: \\n1. The batsmen are level or in the process of crossing.  \\nIt is important for assigning runs, for deciding who might be dismissed run out, or to \\ndetermine who is on strike for the next ball - especially given the use of technology in the \\nprofessional game to know whether the batsmen have crossed. In the process of running, \\nassuming both batsmen leave their ground, there will be a period of the run when the \\nbodies and equipment of the batsmen overlap. For batsmen to be deemed to have crossed \\nthere must be no part of their persons overlapping. \\nHowever, for deciding \'ownership\' of the ground at each end for the purposes of Run out, it \\nwill be whoever is nearest to each ground and if there is doubt then the ground at each end \\nbelongs to the batsman who last left it. \\n \\n2. Two batsmen in the same ground \\nThe ground belongs to whoever reached the ground first. If one batsman never left his/her \\nground, then that ground will always belong to him/her. If, however, a batsman leaves \\nhis/her ground and then attempts to return to it, but the other batsman reaches that \\nground before he/she does, then it belongs to the one who reached it first - not necessarily \\nthe one to whom it belonged before the ball was delivered. \\n \\n3. Injured Striker with a runner \\nHere the complication is that there are three people to share two grounds. As far as the \\nnon-striker and the runner are concerned, they are treated as the two batsmen in the \\nprevious paragraphs. For them, everything is as stated there. As they change ends, so that \\nthe runner, for instance, starts off with his/her ground at the wicket-keeper\'s end, but after \\npassing the non-striker, his/her end is at the bowler\'s end, and so on. The ground at the \\nwicket-keeper\'s end belongs alternately to the runner and to the non-striker. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"All the time that the injured striker with a runner is on strike, however, the ground at the \\nwicket keeper\'s end belongs only to the injured batsman. That striker never has the ground \\nat the bowler\'s end even if, in spite of the injury, he/she actually runs to that end. When a \\nbatsman who has a runner is not the striker he/she has no end at all. \'Ownership\' of the two \\ngrounds is decided between the other two in the normal way. \\n \\nPosition of non-striker \\nLaw 30.3 is quite separate from the rest of the Law. It specifies the position of the non-\\nstriker as the ball comes into play. It should cause no difficulty. \\nIt is accepted practice that, as the Law requires, the non-striker stands on the opposite side \\nof the wicket to that on which the bowler is going to deliver the ball. It is very rare for a \\nplayer to ask to stand on the other side. If he/she did, the umpire would have to consider \\nvery carefully whether to allow it. To be clear of the bowler\'s action and run-off from the \\npitch, the non-striker would have to be some way out on that side. This could make it \\ndifficult for the umpire to see his/her movements, particularly as the bowler runs between \\nthem. However, there might be good reason for the change - the fielding positions might \\nmean the non-striker would have to stand even further out on the normal side, or there \\ncould be damp patches on one side making running difficult. The umpire must weigh up the \\nbenefits and risks carefully. Wherever the non-striker stands, he/she must not obstruct \\neither the bowler delivering the ball, or the striker\'s view of that delivery, nor impede close \\nfielders. On the other hand, he/she must not stand so far from the pitch that the bowler\'s \\nend umpire cannot see his/her movements. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b\'LAW 31 APPEALS \\n31.1 Umpire not to give batsman out without an appeal \\n  \\nNeither umpire shall give a batsman out, even though he/she may be out under the \\nLaws, unless appealed to by a fielder. This shall not debar a batsman who is out \\nunder any of the Laws from leaving the wicket without an appeal having been made. \\nNote, however, the provisions of 31.7. \\n31.2 Batsman dismissed \\n  \\nA batsman is dismissed if he/she is \\n  \\neither    given out by an umpire, on appeal \\n  \\nor          out under any of the Laws and leaves the wicket as in 31.1. \\n \\n31.3 Timing of appeals \\n  \\nFor an appeal to be valid, it must be made before the bowler begins his/her run-up \\nor, if there is no run-up. his/her bowling action to deliver the next ball, and before \\nTime has been called. \\n  \\nThe call of Over does not invalidate an appeal made prior to the start of the \\nfollowing over, provided Time has not been called. See Laws 12.2 (Call of Time) and \\n172 (Start of an over). \\n31.4 Appeal "How\\\'s That?" \\n  \\nAn appeal "How\\\'s That?" covers all ways of being out. \\n31.5 Answering appeals \\n  \\nThe striker\\\'s end umpire shall answer all appeals arising out of any of Laws 35 (Hit \\nwicket), 39 (Stumped) or 38 (Run out) when this occurs at the wicket-keeper\\\'s end, \\nThe bowler\\\'s end umpire shall answer all other appeals. When an appeal is made, \\neach umpire shall answer on any matter that falls within his/her jurisdiction. When a \\nbatsman has been given Not out, either umpire may answer an appeal, made in \\naccordance with 31.3. if it is on a further matter and is within his/her jurisdiction. \\n \\n31.6 Consultation by umpires \\n  \\nEach umpire shall answer appeals on matters within his/her own jurisdiction. If an \\numpire is doubtful about any point that the other umpire may have been in a better \\nposition to see. he/she shall consult the latter on this point of fact and shall then give \\n\''b'b\'the decision If after consultation, there is still doubt remaining the decision shall be \\nNot out. \\n \\n31.7 Batsman leaving the wicket under a misapprehension  \\n  \\nAn umpire shall intervene if satisfied that a batsman not having been given out, has \\nleft the wicket under a misapprehension of being out. The umpire intervening shall \\ncall and signal Dead ball to prevent any further action by the fielding side and shall \\nrecall the batsman A batsman may be recalled at any time up to the instant when \\nthe ball comes into play for the next delivery, unless it is the final wicket of the \\ninnings, in which case it should be up to the instant when the umpires leave the \\nfield. \\n \\n31.8 Withdrawal of an appeal \\n  \\nThe captain of the fielding side may withdraw an appeal only after obtaining the \\nconsent of the umpire within whose jurisdiction the appeal falls. If such consent is \\ngiven, the umpire concerned shall, it applicable, revoke the decision and recall the \\nbatsman The withdrawal of an appeal must be before the instant when the ball \\ncomes into play for the next delivery or if the innings has been completed, the \\ninstant when the umpires leave the field. \\nLaw 31.1 makes it clear that an umpire is not to give a batsman out without an appeal from \\nthe fielding side. For that matter, neither should the umpire announce that a batsman is Not \\nout without being asked. The batsman however, knowing he/she is out, can in effect dismiss \\nhim herself without an appeal - by leaving the wicket and going off the field. \\nAn appeal "How\\\'s That?" is recommended; it will cover all ways of being out. This does not \\nmean that it is the only way to appeal to the umpire. As long as it is clear beyond all doubt \\nthat a fielder is asking the umpire\\\'s opinion as to whether or not a batsman is out, small \\nvariations such as "How was that?", "How was he?" can be considered as appeals. A player \\nwith a hearing or speech impairment may have difficulty appealing vocally, but can make it \\nabsolutely clear that he/she is requesting the umpire\\\'s judgment on a dismissal. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n\''b'b\'When Law 31.4 says that "How\\\'s that?" covers all ways of being out that means that a team \\nnever need to appeal for a certain type of dismissal. For example, a team could appeal \\nthinking the batsman is out LBW. The umpire, however, has seen the ball brush the inside \\nedge of the bat - LBW is thus not possible. If the ball had been caught fairly, after hitting the \\nbat and the pad, by a fielder, the umpire would answer the appeal "out" - the batsman is \\nout Caught. It does not matter that the fielding side are appealing for LBW - an appeal is for \\nany method of dismissal. If there is any confusion, the umpire should inform the scorers of \\nthe method of dismissal (although this is not essential immediately and can usually wait \\nuntil the next break in play). \\n \\nBatsman out; batsman dismissed \\nThere is a very clear distinction between a batsman being out and that batsman being \\ndismissed. Being \\\'out\\\' means that the conditions of the appropriate Law apply. Being \\n\\xe2\\x80\\x98dismissed\\\' means that an umpire has given the batsman out on appeal, or the batsman has \\nwalked from the wicket, acknowledging that he/she is out. A batsman who just fails to make \\ngood his/her ground in running when the wicket is put down will be out. If the umpire, with \\nwhatever assistance is available, is in doubt and so gives that batsman Not out, then the \\nbatsman is not dismissed unless he/she chooses to walk - unlikely in this situation, in which \\nthe batsman will not be likely to know for sure if he/she had made his/her ground. \\nSimilarly, the striker may be hit on the pad in circumstances which mean he/she is out LBW. \\nIf uncharacteristically, no one appeals, that batsman is not dismissed. For this reason, none \\nof the Laws on dismissals mentions an appeal. Each Law merely states the conditions in \\nwhich the batsman is out. Whether the batsman is dismissed or not depends on an appeal \\nbeing made and upheld, or on the batsman walking. Batsmen will often walk for something \\nobvious like a clearly taken catch in the outfield. It is virtually certain that they will not do so \\nfor an LBW appeal. \\n \\n \\n\''b'b"The Laws that follow this (32-40), which outline each method of dismissal, do not mention \\nappeals that is because Law 31 states that an appeal is always required Law 31.1 will always \\napply even though it is not mentioned in these subsequent Laws. \\n \\nTiming \\nAppeals need not be made at the instant of the Incident There are just two restrictions on \\nwhen an appeal can be made: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 it must be before the ball next comes into play after the incident - when the bowler \\nstarts the run up for action to deliver the next ball. Even the call of Over does not \\ninvalidate this \\nand  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 it must be before Time has been called in that session. \\nAn appeal for a possible run out, or any other dismissal, on the last ball of an over, provided \\nit is not the last over before an interval or interruption, can be made at any time up to the \\nstart of the next over. However, appealing before the next over starts after a drinks interval \\nwould be too late Time would have been called at the start of the interval. \\nThis is one reason that it is so important for an umpire to call Time at the appropriate \\nmoment - no umpire wants to be answering an appeal, potentially giving a batsman out \\nhalfway off the field before the tea interval Fortunately, the vast majority of appeals occur \\ninstantly and so such examples are extremely rare. \\n \\nUmpire\'s jurisdiction \\nLaw 31.5 allocates each of the methods of dismissal to a particular umpire. The Law requires \\nthe appropriate umpire to answer an appeal on any matter allocated to him/ her. Every \\nappeal must be answered. Pointedly ignoring an appeal by some gesture such as turning \\nhis/her head away is not acceptable. He/she must either raise an index finger to give the \\nbatsman out, or say Not out. Some umpires may shake their head - and this is by no means a \\nbad thing to do, as it is a clear sign to those who cannot hear the call of Not out. However, it \\nshould be as well as a verbal response, not instead. \\nAn appeal is not made to an umpire, even though a player may appear to be asking a \\nparticular umpire. An appeal is to be assumed to be general. An umpire will answer it if the \\nincident involves or could involves, a dismissal within his / her jurisdiction. For example, the \\nstriker is hit on the pad: the ball goes through to the keeper, who whips off the bails. If there \\nis an appeal, the bowler\'s end umpire will consider whether or not the striker was out LBW; \\nthe striker\'s end umpire will consider whether or not the batsman was out Stumped. Two \\n"'b'b"answers will be given. The fact that the bowler\'s end umpire says Not out (for the LBW) will \\nnot negate any judgment of Out Stumped by the striker\'s end umpire. If both umpires give \\nthe striker out, the general rule is that the action that happened first will take precedence. \\nIn the example above, if both umpires signal Out, then LBW will over-ride Stumped, since \\nthe strike on the pad was before the removal of the bails. Where there are such multiple \\npossibilities, it is helpful for the umpire to indicate to the scorers which one is to be \\nrecorded. \\nThe Laws on Bowled and Caught modify the general rule by stating specifically which is to \\ntake precedence, with Bowled trumping all forms of dismissal and Caught taking precedence \\nover everything except Bowled. \\n \\nConsultation \\nLaw 31.6 makes it clear that an umpire is permitted to seek information from his/her \\ncolleague concerning the facts of an incident. For example, the bowler\'s end umpire may \\nsee clearly that a batsman has hit the ball, but not whether it was cleanly caught by a \\nfielder. The umpire at the other end may be in a better position to see whether it touched \\nthe ground or was caught fairly. Consultation may remove any doubt as to whether the \\nstriker was out Caught or not provided the other umpire is in a better position to see the \\nrelevant incident. Although both umpires are involved in making the decision, it is the \\nbowler\'s end umpire who must give it, since Caught is one of the Laws on which he/she is to \\nadjudicate. \\nConsultation on dismissals must be on questions of fact. on which the other umpire may \\nhave better information, as in the example. If, in a question of dismissal, the appropriate \\numpire is unsure of some of the facts and the other umpire cannot help with them, he/she \\nis required to give the batsman Not out. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Consultation need not require a conference. Ideally simply an enquiring look would \\nprobably be enough, particularly where relevant signals have been agreed between the \\numpires pre-match. If it does require person to-person conversation, then that is not a \\nproblem. An umpire who leaves his/her position for such consultation is to call and signal \\nDead ball. \\n \\nLeaving wicket under a misapprehension \\nThere may be an occasion when a batsman, mistakenly thinking he/she is out, walks from \\nthe wicket. For instance, if in attempting a run the batsman knows he/she was well short of \\nmaking his/her ground when the wicket was broken, the batsman will believe he/she is out \\nnot aware that the wicket was not fairly put down because the ball slipped out of the \\nwicket-keeper\'s hand just before the wicket was broken. Similarly, a batsman may believe \\nhe/ she is out if there appears to have been a fair catch, but the striker did not hear a call of \\nNo ball. In each of these situations no appeal has been made and no decision has been \\ngiven. Law 31.7 would therefore apply. It must be emphasised that this will apply only if the \\nbatsman has not been given out. It is not a way for one umpire to over-ride a decision by \\nthe other one. \\nAs soon as either umpire becomes aware that a batsman has left their wicket under a \\nmisapprehension, he/she should call and signal Dead ball, to prevent any action - especially \\nthe inappropriate running out of the further batsman. That umpire should then recall the \\nbatsman, consulting with his/her colleague if necessary, and ensure the scorers are aware of \\nany runs that have been scored. \\nThe recall of the batsman by the umpires can happen any time up the point of the ball next \\ncoming into play, or if this was the last wicket of the innings before the umpires leave the \\nfield. This means that should it be the end of a session of play, but not the end of an innings, \\nthe batsman who has left thinking he/she is out can be brought back after the interval as \\nlong as play has not recommenced, \\n \\nWithdrawal of an appeal \\nSometimes a captain may regret an appeal by one of his/ her side, feeling it is not within the \\nspirit of the game. For example, the bowler and a batsman may collide \\xe2\\x80\\x93 entirely \\naccidentally - leaving the batsman out of his/her ground. If the wicket is then put down, the \\numpire must give the batsman out on appeal. However, a captain may feel that this wicket, \\nwhich came about because of an accidental collision, is not deserved, and ask to withdraw \\nthat appeal. Whatever the reason, the captain is to follow the procedure set out in 31.8. The \\nkey points are that \\n"'b'b\'\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the captain must seek the permission of the appropriate umpire.  \\nIt is quite possible that the captain will ask the "wrong\\\' umpire. That umpire should \\nsimply redirect the question. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the captain must do so before the ball comes into play for the next delivery, or if this \\nwas the last wicket of the innings before the umpires have left the field of play. In \\nsome cases the request will come before any decision on the appeal has been taken \\nanyway. \\nAs always, the umpire must use judgment over whether to accept this withdrawal, \\nbut should not refuse without good reason. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 if permission is granted, it is the umpire, not the captain, who is to call the batsman \\nback. \\n \\nUmpire\\\'s decision is final \\nThis familiar phrase is enshrined as a principle in Law 2.12. However, an umpire may realise \\nthat a mistake has been made - in the case of this Law, specifically in reaction to a decision \\nfollowing an appeal - and want to reverse that decision. Perhaps the umpire gave a batsman \\nout Caught, not aware that the non-striker\\\'s end umpire had called No ball. Perhaps the \\nmistake is the umpire\\\'s own - a realisation, immediately after raising the finger for LBW, that \\nthere was an inside edge, for example. There may be embarrassment but there is no \\ndisgrace in admitting it and changing the decision - so long as it is done promptly and \\nwithout reacting or being influenced by any pressure from either team. It is much better \\nthan allowing a miscarriage of justice. An umpire will be the more respected for it than for \\nsticking to a decision he/she knows to be wrong. \\nThe reversal of decision must be made \\\'promptly\\\' and certainly before any progress at all \\nwith the game. Once an umpire has made a decision, and has not altered it, that decision \\nwill stand in all circumstances. \\nSpecial provisions at the highest levels, for requesting reviews of umpires\\\' decisions, do not \\napply in Law. \\n \\n \\n\''b'b"LAW 32 BOWLED \\n32.1 Out Bowled \\n32.2.1 The striker is out Bowled if his/her wicket is put down by a ball delivered by the \\nbowler not being a No ball, even if it first touches the striker\'s bat or person. \\n32.2.2 However the striker shall not be out Bowled if before striking the wicket the ball has \\nbeen in contact with any other player or an umpire. The striker will, however, be \\nsubject to Laws 37 (Obstructing the field), 38 (Run out) and 39 (Stumped). \\n \\n32.2 Bowled to take precedence \\n  \\nThe striker is out Bowled if his/her wicket is put down as in 32.1, even though a \\ndecision against him/her for any other method of dismissal would be justified. \\nBowled is the first of the nine methods of dismissal, listed in alphabetical order. It is in most \\ncases so obvious that the majority of batsmen will walk from the wicket without appeal, \\nperfectly understanding that they are out.  \\nThe crucial points for a batsman to be out Bowled are: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the wicket is put down by the ball \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the delivery is not a No ball  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 nobody, except sometimes the striker, is involved between the bowler delivering the \\nball and the ball breaking the wicket. \\nThe phrase \'clean bowled\' is not found in the Laws but it gives a good picture of the ball, \\nhaving left the bowler\'s hand, striking the stumps and breaking the wicket without touching \\nanything in between, except that usually it will have landed on the pitch. \\nThe striker can equally be bowled, however, if he/she and the ball make contact before the \\nball hits the stumps. The ball may touch the bat or be deflected off the striker\'s person. \\nHe/she may stop it with a defensive stroke, lose sight of it and accidentally kick it on to the \\nstumps. He/ she may attempt a second stroke to defend the wicket but succeed only in \\nhitting the ball on to the stumps. The question of completing a stroke\' is not relevant. Until \\nthe ball has been touched by another player or an umpire, the striker must suffer the \\nconsequences of his/her actions, voluntary or involuntary. If a delivery is not a No ball, and \\nthe ball hits the stumps, thereby putting down the wicket, the striker is out Bowled, \\nproviding the ball has not touched either another player or an umpire since it left the \\nbowler\'s hand. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Did the ball break the wicket?  \\nOccasionally there may be doubt about this. The wicket keeper may be standing very close \\nand might dislodge the bails, or the ball may rebound from the wicket-keeper onto the \\nstumps. Perhaps the ball fell off accidentally, due to a gust of wind. Perhaps there is \\nuncertainty as to whether the striker\'s person or the ball itself broke the wicket. In all these \\ncases the striker\'s end umpire should be in a position to help his/her colleague to determine \\nhow the wicket was put down (as discussed under Law 31). \\n \\nBowled to take precedence \\nThere may be other cause for appeal. The striker, having played a ball defensively, uses the \\nbat to tap the ball to a fielder without permission (see Laws 34.2 and 37.4) and accidentally \\nhits the ball on to the wicket. Alternatively, before going on to the wicket the ball may hit \\nthe pad in such a way that the striker is out LBW. Or, the ball might touch the edge of the \\nstriker\'s bat before hitting the wicket and then go on to be caught by the wicket-keeper. \\nNone of these other modes of dismissal are relevant if the batsman is Bowled. Law 32.2 \\nestablishes firmly that, providing it is not a No ball, if the ball breaks the wicket when no \\nfielder has been involved, then the striker is out Bowled, whatever other possibilities there \\nare. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'LAW 33 CAUGHT \\n33.1 Out Caught \\nThe striker is out Caught if a ball delivered by the bowler, not being a No ball, touches \\nhis/her bat without having previously been in contact with any fielder, and is subsequently \\nheld by a fielder as a fair catch, as described in 33.2 and 33.3, before it touches the ground. \\n \\n33.2 A fair catch \\n33.2.1 A catch will be fair only if in every case \\neither     the ball, at any time \\nOr          any fielder in contact with the ball, \\nis not grounded beyond the boundary before the catch is completed. Note Laws 19.4 \\n(Ball grounded beyond the boundary) and 19.5 (Fielder grounded beyond the \\nboundary). \\n33.2.2 Furthermore, a catch will be fair if any of the following conditions applies : \\n33.2.2.1 the ball is held in the hand or hands of a fielder, even if the hand holding \\nthe ball is touching the ground, or is hugged to the body, or lodges in the \\nexternal protective equipment worn by a fielder or lodges accidently in a \\nfielder\\xe2\\x80\\x99s clothing. \\n33.2.2.2 a fielder catches the ball after it has been lawfully struck more than once \\nby the striker, but only if it has not been grounded since it was first struck. \\nSee Law 34 (Hit the ball twice). \\n33.2.2.3 a fielder catches the ball after it has touched the wicket, an umpire, \\nanother fielder a runner or the other batsman. \\n33.2.2.4 a fielder catches the ball after it has crossed the boundary in the air, \\nprovided that the conditions in 33.21 are met. \\n33.2.2.5 the ball is caught off an obstruction within the boundary that is not \\ndesignated a boundary by the umpires. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"33.3 Making a catch \\n  \\nThe act of making a catch shall start from the time when the ball first comes into \\ncontact with a fielders person and shall end when a fielder obtains complete control \\nover both the ball and his/her own movement. \\n \\n33.4 No runs to be scored \\n  \\nif the striker is dismissed Caught, runs from that delivery completed by the batsmen \\nbefore the completion of the catch shall not be scored but any runs for penalties \\nawarded to either side shall stand. Law 18.12.1 (Batsman returning to wicket he/she \\nhas left) shall apply from the instant of the completion of the catch. \\n \\n33.5 Caught to take precedence \\n  \\nIf the criteria of 33.1 are met and the striker is not out Bowled, then he/she is out \\nCaught, even though a decision against either batsman for another method of \\ndismissal would be justified. \\nIn many cases it will be clear to the striker (and others on the field) that he/she is out \\nCaught and the batsman will often leave the wicket without an appeal. Equally, however, it \\nis sometimes difficult for the umpire to decide. The umpire must have observed all that \\nhappens to the ball from immediately after seeing the bowler\'s front foot land. By the time \\nan appeal is made, it is too late to gather the information needed. The requirements for the \\nstriker to be out Caught are simple. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The ball must have touched the striker\'s bat. Law 5.6 explains that the striker must \\nhave been holding/touching the bat and that the whole of the glove on a hand \\nholding the bat counts as part of the bat. In the very rare case of a batsman holding \\nthe bat with bare hands, the hand, or hands, will count instead of the worn glove.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 After contact with the bat, the ball must not touch the ground or any kind of \\nboundary, including fielders touching or grounded beyond the boundary. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The catch must be completed by a fielder within the field of play. \\nJudging these requirements may not always be so simple and each aspect is examined \\nbelow. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Did the ball touch the bat? \\n1. A fine edge \\nThe sooner the umpire picks up the flight of the ball from the bowler\'s hand, the better \\nchance he/she has of seeing whether there was very fine contact with the edge of the bat. \\nThe umpire must look to see if there was any deflection of the ball as it passed the bat. If \\nhe/she hears a sound, he/she must know whether it was at the moment the ball passed the \\nbat and whether the ball was then very close to the bat. The umpire must judge whether it \\nwas the sound of the ball striking the bat or the glove on the hand holding the bat and not, \\nfor instance, the bat hitting the striker\'s boot or pad. \\nWhatever difficulties there may be, the umpire must not guess. Instead, the umpire must \\njudge on what he/ she observes about the flight of the ball and the movement of the bat. \\nSometimes his/her colleague can help if the contact was on the leg side, hidden from the \\nbowler\'s end by the striker. As with any appeal, the umpire must not be influenced by the \\nstrength of an appeal - fielders will often claim a catch for a thin edge without being certain \\nwhether the ball has hit bat, pad, or nothing at all. If the bowler\'s end umpire is unsure and \\nthe other umpire is unable to help, he/she must give the striker Not out. \\n2. Other contacts \\nAll that the Law requires is that the ball touches the bat, or by definition the hand holding \\nthe bat. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 It does not matter if the ball touched the striker\'s person before or after it touched \\nthe bat. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 It does not matter whether the ball\'s contact with the bat was intentional or \\ninadvertent. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 It does not matter if it was part of a legitimate second strike, It does not matter, if it \\nwas a second strike, \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 whether it was the first or the second contact that was with the bat - although in the \\ncase of a double strike the ball must not have been grounded between the strikes. \\nIf the ball touched the bat and has not been grounded, the striker can be out Caught. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Was the ball grounded after contact with the bat?  \\nA point at which difficulty may arise is immediately after leaving the bat. If the striker hits \\nthe ball when it is near the ground, sharp observation is needed to see whether the ball hits \\nthe ground before going further. Such contact is often called a \'bump (or bumped) ball. The \\nangle of the bat as it strikes the ball, the lower speed of the ball than the strength of the \\nblow would suggest, the ball rising more than expected, are all pointers. The other umpire \\ncan often help from a side-on position. \\nThe ball that is grounded just before the fielder catches It is also difficult to discern. Again, \\nconcentration and the opinion of the other umpire, if available and necessary, can help - \\nand so can the honesty of the fielder - but ultimately it is a decision for the bowler\'s end \\numpire. \\nOccasionally, a fielder will have his/her fingers under the ball, but the ball may brush some \\ngrass between those fingers. This is unlikely to be visible to the naked eye. although may be \\npossible if the catch is taken extremely close to the umpire - but can be a bone of \\ncontention in televised cricket. If the fielder\'s fingers are underneath the ball, and the ball \\ndoes not strike the ground beneath his/ her hands, the fact that it may have brushed a blade \\nof grass should not be considered as the ball being grounded. \\nDid the ball come into contact with anything else?  \\nAnother problem is the catch near the boundary. The question of the fielder being within \\nthe field of play, including boundary catching, is discussed under Law 19. \\nAdditionally, there may occasionally be some object within the field of play, such as a tree, \\nwhich has been designated a boundary. If the ball hits it, no catch can follow. Where, \\nhowever, such an object has not been designated a boundary a catch can be taken off it. \\nApart from such rarities, the obstacles the ball may encounter on its path, and whether \\ncontact invalidates a catch, are set out in the chart below. In each case, the assumption is \\nthat the ball has come directly from the striker\'s bat and has not yet been grounded. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"CATCH POSSIBLE? \\nYES \\nNO \\nHeld in hand or hands of *fielder even hand \\nbut not ball is touching the ground \\n \\ntouches fielder\'s person or external \\nprotective equipment including the helmet \\nTouches fielder helmet on ground \\nLodge in external protective equipment \\nIncluding a helmet, of, accidentally, in \\nfielders clothing Lodges in *keeper\'s pads \\nFielder deliberately uses clothing to field ball \\nTouches either batsman or a runner \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nTouches either batsman\'s or runner\'s helmet \\nLodges in either batman\'s Dad Lodges in \\neither batsman\'s clothing Trapped among \\nitems of either batsman\'s clothing or \\nequipment \\n \\nLodges in either batsman\'s helmet \\nTouches stumps at bowler\'s end \\n \\nTouches stumps at keeper\'s end sometimes\\xe2\\x80\\xa0 \\nTouches stumps at keeper\'s end in normal \\ncircumstances\\xe2\\x80\\xa0 \\nTouches umpire \\nLodges in umpire clothing \\n \\n*\'Fielder\' always includes wicket-keeper; \'keeper\' being specified here means only the wicket-\\nkeeper. \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa0If the ball strikes the stumps after leaving the striker\'s bat, he/she would normally be out Bowled, \\nso that even if the ball were caught, Bowled would take precedence. However, the striker would not \\nbe out Bowled in the rare case of the ball failing to dislodge a bail. Then a catch would be possible. \\nNote also that the chart specifically excludes the ball touching anything else between leaving the bat \\nand striking the named object. However, if the ball were to touch a fielder between leaving the bat \\nand hitting the stumps at the wicket-keeper\'s end, the striker would not be out Bowled. Again, a \\ncatch would be possible and would, if relevant, over-ride Run out or Stumped. \\n"'b'b"It should be noted that if the ball lodges in a fielder\'s clothing, a catch can be valid. \\nNevertheless, the fielder cannot use his/her clothing to deliberately make a catch -as \\ncovered under Law 28. The lodging has to be accidental. \\nIt is also worth pointing out that, in the past, the ball coming into contact with a fielder and \\nwicket-keepers helmet used to prevent a catch from being taken. This is no longer the case. \\nPlayers fielding close to the wicket are to be encouraged to wear protective helmets, not \\ndiscouraged, and the ball rebounding off, or lodging in, a helmet can still be caught fairly. \\nHowever, for a catch to be completed after lodging in the helmet, the helmet must remain \\non the fielder\'s head. If the ball lodges in the helmet, and in the process knocks the helmet \\noff the fielder\'s head, onto the ground, it will be deemed to have made contact with the \\nground before the catch had been completed. \\nDid the fielder catch it fairly? \\nA fielder does not have to catch the ball in his/her hands He/she might try to catch the ball \\nhigh up but it slips through his/her hands. It does not fall to the ground because it lodges in \\nthe V made by the forearms and is held against his/her body. This is an instance of \'hugged \\nto his/her body. Instances have been known of a catch being held between a fielder\'s knees! \\nThere is more on the question of valid catches near the boundary under Law 19. If the ball \\nlodges in the fielder\'s helmet, he/she does not have to remove the ball to claim the catch, \\nbut must have control over it (see below). \\n \\nDid the fielder complete the catch? \\nThis is a vital issue. The fact that a fielder interrupts the flight of the ball and holds it in \\nhis/her hands does not necessarily mean that a catch had been taken. There is no catch until \\na fielder has completed it. The umpire has to be satisfied that the fielder has both \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 complete control over the ball, and \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 complete control over his/her own movement \\n \\n1. Control over the ball \\nThe fielder can catch the ball between his/her knees, but unless he/she can keep it there, \\neven if he/she overbalances, the fielder has not completed the catch. A diving catch which \\nsees the ball knocked from the fielder\'s hand as he/she lands on the ground has not been \\ncompleted. If the fielder can retain the ball it does not matter that the hand holding the ball \\nis on the ground, as long as the ball has been kept clear of the ground - this will often be the \\ncase with low catches, with the fielder\'s fingers almost flat on the ground, but the ball \\nlanding on top of them, and thus never coming into contact with the ground. \\n"'b'b" \\nIf the ball lodges in the fielder\'s helmet, then providing it is firmly wedged it is not necessary \\nto remove it to complete the catch. However, if the fielder\'s movements immediately \\nafterwards, for example throwing up his/her hands to appeal, cause the ball to fall from the \\ngrille of the helmet then there has not been control. It might also be that the force of the \\nball lodging in the grille of the helmet knocks it from the fielder\'s head, and as the helmet is \\nnow not worn, the catch would not be valid. In both these situations, the ball would not be \\nDead. \\n2. Control over his/her movement \\nA diving catch, taken when the fielder is in the air and nearly horizontal, is again an example. \\nUntil the fielder has recovered after hitting the ground, he/she is not in control of his/her \\nmovement. If a fielder takes a running catch, he/ she will not have completed the catch until \\nhe/she can stop running. Sometimes a fielder will continue running from sheer excitement \\nat having taken the catch. Provided the umpire is satisfied that the fielder has control over \\nhis/her running, so that he/she could stop within the field of play, rather than being unable \\nto the catch is completed. \\nSimilarly, if the ball lodging in the helmet causes the fielder to fall over and the helmet falls \\nfrom the head it would not be a catch, but in this situation the ball would not be Dead as the \\nhelmet was not deliberately removed by the fielder. The fielder falling over and the ball \\nremaining in the worn helmet would however still be a fair catch providing the ball did not \\ntouch the ground. \\n \\nWas the fielder within the field of play? \\nThis is a crucial question on the taking of a catch - and used to be covered under Caught. \\nHowever, it is now covered in detail in Law 19- Boundaries. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'No runs to be scored \\nIt may be some time before a catch is completed if it is bit high into the air or is juggled \\nbetween fielders The batsmen may run while that is happening. If the catch is not held the \\nruns will count. If the catch is held, the striker will be out and if dismissed the runs will not \\ncount. The end to which the non-striker must go is dealt with in Law 18.12. \\n \\nCaught to take precedence \\nIt has already been stated in Law 32 that Bowled takes precedence over any other form of \\ndismissal. Caught is next in priority, taking precedence over everything except Bowled. If for \\ninstance, the striker made an illegal second strike, this would mean that he/she was out Hit \\nthe ball twice. If however one of these strikes was with the bat and the ball was caught \\nwithout being grounded at any point the striker would be out Caught, even though the \\nsecond strike came before the catch. \\nThere is a slight complication here, however. The ball becomes dead, automatically, at the \\nfall of the wicket, as in Law 20.1.1.3. Therefore, if more than one dismissal is theoretically \\npossible, but an umpire gives a decision on one of those methods before the other is \\ncompleted, it is the first decision that counts. For example, a batsman may stand on his/her \\nstumps while playing a pull shot into the air. If the batsman is out Hit wicket while the ball is \\nin the air, the umpire should wait to see if the catch is taken, and if it is, he/she should give \\nthe batsman out Caught - should the catch not be taken, the umpire can then give the \\nbatsman out Hit Wicket. However, if the umpire does not wait, and instead gives the \\nbatsman out before the catch is taken then the dismissal is Hit wicket. \\nThe same principle would apply to a straight drive which deflected off the bowler onto the \\nstumps before carrying to a fielder on the full to be caught. If the non-striker was out of \\nhis/her ground when the wicket was put down then the non-striker would only be dismissed \\nRun out if the umpire had given him/her out before the catch was taken Otherwise, the \\ncatch would take precedence and it is the striker who would be out. The difference here is \\nmore marked than the first example, as it is a different batsman that will be dismissed, \\ndepending on when the Out decision was made. \\nThe onus here is on the umpire, who should wait to see if the catch is taken before making \\nthe decision. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"LAW 34 HIT THE BALL TWICE \\n34.1 Out Hit the ball twice \\n34.1.1 The striker is out Hit the ball twice if, while the ball is in play, it strikes any part of \\nhis/her person or is struck by his/her bat and, before the ball has been touched by a \\nfielder the striker wilfully strikes it again with his/her bat or person, other than a \\nhand not holding the bat except for the sole purpose of guarding his/her wicket See \\n34.3 and Law 37 (Obstructing the field). \\n34.1.2 For the purpose of this Law \'struck or strike shall include contact with the person of \\nthe striker. \\n \\n34.2 Not out Hit the ball twice \\n \\nThe striker will not be out under this Law if he/she  \\n34.2.1 strikes the ball a second or subsequent time in order to return the ball to any fielder. \\nNote, however, the provisions of Law 37.4 (Returning the ball to a fielder)  \\n34.2.2 wilfully strikes the ball after it has touched a fielder Note, however the provisions of \\nLaw 371 (Out Obstructing the field). \\n \\n34.3 Ball lawfully struck more than once \\n  \\nThe striker may, solely in order to guard his/her wicket and before the ball has been \\ntouched by a fielder, lawfully strike the ball a second or subsequent time with the \\nbat, or with any part of his/her person other than a hand not holding the bat. \\n  \\nThe striker may guard his/her wicket even if the delivery is a No ball. \\n  \\nHowever, the striker may not prevent the ball from being caught by striking the ball \\nmore than once in defence of his/her wicket. See Law 37.3 (Obstructing a ball from \\nbeing caught). \\n \\n34.4 Runs permitted from ball lawfully struck more than once \\nWhen the ball is lawfully struck more than once as permitted in 34.3, if the ball does not \\nbecome dead for any reason, the umpire shall call and signal Dead ball as soon as the ball \\nreaches the boundary or at the completion of the first run. However, he/she shall delay the \\ncall of Dead ball to allow the opportunity for a catch to be completed. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"The umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 disallow all runs to the batting side \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 return any not out batsman to his/her original end  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 signal No ball to the scorers if applicable. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award any 5-run Penalty that is applicable except for Penalty runs under Law 28.3 \\n(Protective helmets belonging to the fielding side). \\n \\n34.5 Bowler does not get credit \\n  \\nThe bowler does not get credit for the wicket. \\n \\n34.5 Bowler does not get credit \\n  \\nThe bowler does not get credit for the wicket \\nThe striker is not allowed to make a deliberate second attempt to hit the ball, once he/she \\nhas touched it, except in one circumstance - in order to defend his/her wicket. It is not \\nuncommon for a ball to be hit twice accidentally; bat-pad\' is a familiar phrase. There is no \\nharm in this. Unless a second strike is deliberate it can be ignored. For convenience the text \\nwill refer to a second strike but, providing each is to defend his/her wicket, the striker can \\nmake as many as he/she feels necessary. A second (or third...) strike, however, belongs \\nentirely to the time after the ball has been bowled and before a fielder has touched it. Once \\na fielder has touched the ball, the striker no longer has any right to hit it and, if he/she does, \\nwill be subject to Law 37 (Obstructing the field). \\nAlthough pushing the ball with the bat to return it to a fielder could be categorised as a \\nwilful second strike, if the ball is not dead and the fielding side have not given permission, \\nthe striker is liable to dismissal under Law 37- Obstructing the field. \\nThe umpire has to make two judgments in order to give the striker out Hit the ball twice: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 was the second strike wilful? \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 if it was, was it solely in defence of the striker\'s wicket, or not? \\n \\nNeither of these is usually difficult to determine, although both are a matter of judgment. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Was it a strike? \\nIn Appendix A.10.1 \'strike\' is defined as the ball being hit by the bat unless specifically \\ndefined otherwise. There is just such a specific definition in this Law. In this Law, the ball \\nmaking contact with the striker\'s person is to count as a strike, just as much as a bat-with-\\nball contact. Further, although the first strike is subject to the restrictions discussed under \\nLeg byes in Law 23, the striker can make a legal second strike, not necessarily with the bat, \\nbut with any part of his/her person except a hand not in contact with the bat. \\nAs an example of a lawful second strike, the striker mishits the ball, knocking it downwards \\nwith the toe of the bat. It fizzes backwards, spinning violently, towards the wicket The \\nstriker kicks the ball away from the stumps with his/ her back foot. \\nApart from not being allowed to hit the ball at all once a fielder has touched it, there are just \\nthree restrictions on the second strike. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 It must solely be an attempt to stop the ball from hitting the wicket. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 It must not be with a hand unless the hand is in contact with the bat. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 It must not interfere with a fielder trying to take a catch. \\nIf any of these restrictions is not observed, the striker will be out: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Hit the ball twice in the first case. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Obstructing the field in the second and third cases. \\n \\nWas it in defence of his/her wicket? \\nThe Law must be interpreted reasonably literally. \'Defending his/her wicket\' is literally trying \\nto prevent the ball hitting the wicket. The umpire is not required to make as fine as \\njudgment as he/she must for LBW, about whether the ball would certainly have hit the \\nwicket, but the ball must be going in that general direction. \\nHowever, the batsman is not protected from \'stopping himself/herself being out. The striker \\ncannot interfere with an attempted catch, even if he/she is trying to defend the wicket. That \\nwould be Obstructing the field, and the batsman would be out. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"On the other hand, should No ball be called, the striker is not at risk of being bowled, so in \\nthat sense the wicket does not need defending. Nevertheless he/she is still entitled to try to \\nstop the ball hitting the wicket. It would be unreasonable to expect the striker to work out \\nthe logic. of this in making an almost instinctive move to protect the stumps. However, the \\nnext paragraph, about the striker\'s motives, will still apply. Of course, the delivery will not \\nbe valid and the one run penalty for the No ball will apply and will be signalled in addition to \\nanything else relevant when the ball is dead. \\nA key phrase is for the sole purpose of. The sudden jab to push the ball away from the \\nstumps is clear enough. In contrast, a powerful hit which sends the ball deep into the \\noutfield may be defending the wicket but could not be regarded as solely for this purpose. \\nEven if the ball loops up so that the striker has a little more time for consideration, it is \\nusually clear whether the second (or third, or fourth etc.) stroke is \'for the sole purpose of \\nguarding his/her wicket. However, the umpire must be alert to the possibility that there may \\nbe an additional motive. If the umpire considers there is, then the striker will be out. \\n \\nNo runs allowed \\nRecompense to the fielding side for the striker being allowed to defend his/her wicket by a \\nsecond strike is that the batsmen are not allowed to score any runs from the delivery. This is \\nthe case, whatever else happens - a second strike allows the batsman to protect his/her \\nwicket, and that is it. If the batsmen attempt to run, or in the unlikely event that the ball \\ngoes to the boundary, the umpires should call and signal Dead ball as described in 34.4 and \\ndisallow any attempted runs. Note however that the Umpire should delay the call of Dead \\nball if there is the possibility of a catch. Any not out batsmen must be returned to their \\noriginal ends. The only runs allowed from the delivery will be a penalty for No ball if \\nappropriate and any 5 Penalty runs applicable (this does not include Penalty runs for the ball \\nhitting the unworn fielder\'s helmet on the ground behind the wicket-keeper under Law \\n28.3). \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Caught from a lawful second strike \\nIf any of multiple strikes is on the bat, then the striker can be out Caught if the ball is not \\ngrounded from the first strike until the fielder catches it. \\nPerson-bat-catch, bat-person-catch, bat-bat-catch are all valid catches. \\nPerson-ground-bat-catch is not valid for a catch. Nor is any other sequence if the ball is \\ngrounded at any point in the sequence, after the first strike and before the catch is taken \\nand completed. \\nIf a second strike is not lawful, and there is no Caught opportunity, then the striker is out Hit \\nthe ball twice. If, however, a valid catch is taken off this double strike, Caught will take \\nprecedence. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"LAW 35 HIT WICKET \\n35.1.1 Out Hit wicket 35.11 The striker is out Hit wicket if, after the bowler has entered the \\ndelivery stride and while the ball is in play his/her wicket is put down by either the \\nstriker\'s bat or person as described in Laws 29.11.2 to 2911 4 (Wicket put down) in \\nany of the following circumstances: \\n35.1.1.1 in the course of any action taken by him/ her in preparing to receive or in \\nreceiving a delivery. \\n35.1.1.2 In setting off for the first run immediately after playing or playing at the \\nball, \\n35.1.1.3 if no attempt is made to play the ball in setting off for the first run, \\nproviding that in the opinion of the umpire this is immediately after the \\nstriker has had the opportunity of playing the ball, \\n35.1.1.4 in lawfully making a second or further stroke for the purpose of guarding \\nhis/her wicket within the provisions of Law 343 (Ball lawfully struck more \\nthan once). \\n35.1.2 If the striker puts his/her wicket down in any of the ways described in Laws 29.11.2 \\nto 29.11.4 before the bowler has entered the delivery stride, either umpire shall call \\nand signal Dead ball. \\n \\n35.2 Not out Hit wicket \\nThe striker is not out under this Law should his/her wicket be put down in any of the ways \\nreferred to in 351 if any of the following applies: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 it occurs after the striker has completed any action in receiving the delivery other \\nthan in 35.1.1.2 to 35.1.1.4. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 it occurs when the striker is in the act of running, other than setting off immediately \\nfor the first run. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 it occurs when the striker is trying to avoid being run out or stumped. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 it occurs when the striker is trying to avoid a throw in at any time. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the bowler after entering the delivery stride dos not deliver the ball in this case \\neither umpire shall immediately call and signal Dead ball. See Law 204 (Umpire \\ncalling and signalling Dead ball). \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the delivery is a No ball. \\n \\nHit wicket is one of the three methods of dismissal for which the striker\'s end umpire is \\nresponsible. As pointed out under Law 21, it is vital that the striker\'s end umpire turns \\nhis/her attention to the striker, the crease and the stumps as soon as he/she has seen the \\nbowler\'s arm action He/she must see if the wicket is broken and if so how, and, crucially \\nwhen it was broken. Although not relevant to Hit wicket, the striker\'s end umpire must \\nknow whether the striker was in his/her ground or not at the time it was put down, to judge \\na possible stumping or run out. The striker\'s end umpire should also watch for the \\ncompletion of the shot and any immediate setting off for a run before turning their \\nattention or head to follow the ball into the field, to ensure a Hit wicket appeal can be \\nanswered, if necessary. \\nThe essential features for Hit wicket are:  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the bowler must deliver the ball \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the delivery must not be a No ball \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the wicket is put down by the striker (including the striker\'s clothing, equipment etc. \\nwhether worn or held or not), rather than the ball \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 when the striker does so, the bowler must have already entered his/her delivery \\nstride.  \\nThe end point of the period during which the striker may be out Hit wicket is set out in the \\ndetail below. \\n1. The bowler must deliver the ball. It must not be a No ball \\nIf because he/she sees the striker dislodge a bail from his/her wicket (or for any other \\nreason, the bowler stops in his/her run up, either before or after entering the delivery \\nstride, the umpire must call and signal Dead ball for the non-delivery. Any appeal must be \\nanswered Not out. That the delivery is not to be a No ball is straightforward. \\n2. How the striker puts down his/her wicket  \\nIt is unlikely (though not impossible) that the striker will knock a stump out of the ground \\nbut very possible that he/ she may dislodge a bail, with the \'bat or person (including any \\nclothing or equipment that fall off him/her). The striker putting down his/her own wicket is \\none of only two situations in the Laws in which items which are not part of his/her \'bat or \\nperson\' are included as though they were. Elsewhere in the Laws, apart from the specific \\nsituation in Law 30.1.2, mention of the bat implies it is being held. Here it counts even if the \\n"'b'b\'striker has let go of it - even if it breaks and the striker is holding one part and the other part \\nfalls onto the stumps. Either part will count. Similarly, in Appendix A.12, only items of \\nclothing or equipment that are attached to the striker are defined as part of the batsman\\\'s \\nperson. Nevertheless, even though they are not part of his/ her person, a cap, or glasses or \\nany other detachable items can be agents for putting down the wicket. It is important to \\nrealise that such items count only in the process of falling from the striker while in the act of \\nreceiving, playing at the ball or immediately setting off for their first run. \\n \\n3. When the striker puts down his/her wicket \\nThere is a very clear starting point for the period during which the striker is liable to be out \\nHit wicket. It is when the bowler enters the delivery stride. From that point onwards, if any \\nmovement the striker makes in preparing to receive the ball, or in receiving the ball causes \\nthe wicket to be put down the striker is out Hit wicket. The striker\\\'s end umpire will have \\nbeen watching the bowler\\\'s arm action. If when he/she turns his/her attention to the \\nstumps and creases at the striker\\\'s end, the wicket is already broken, the umpire may have \\nbeen aware of it happening or may have to rely on the bowler\\\'s end umpire to determine \\nwhether it happened before the bowler entered the delivery stride, or in that short period \\nbetween then and release of the ball. A bail may, of course, have come off accidentally, but \\nthe bowler\\\'s end umpire may have called Dead ball for that. Consultation may be necessary \\nif it is not clear \\n \\n"Preparing to receive the ball includes any movement of the bat - for example the striker\\\'s \\nback-lift- or any movement of his/her feet- perhaps to get into the line of the ball - before \\nthe ball reaches the striker: \\\'Receiving the ball\\\' is as much lifting the bat to let the ball \\nthrough without trying to play it as actually making a stroke to try to hit it. It includes any \\nfollow-through\\\' swing of the bat after hitting or trying to hit the ball. It also includes any \\nbody movement to recover balance after making the stroke - which may go on for some \\ntime, and is usually the hardest part of this Law to judge. During all this time, the striker can \\nbe out Hit wicket if he/she puts the wicket down. \\nFurthermore, the striker could still be out if he/she sets out immediately for a run and in \\ndoing so puts the wicket down. The striker\\\'s end umpire will have to judge what \\n"immediately\\\' is, especially if the striker has not actually played the ball. After that, Hit \\nwicket is no longer a risk unless, after originally receiving the ball, the striker makes a \\nsecond stroke to defend his/her wicket. This will usually be very soon after the first strike, \\nwhen the striker sees his/her wicket is in danger. \\n \\n\''b"b'Striker may not be out \\nLaw 35.2 lists the situations in which the striker, even though he/she has put down his/her \\nwicket, will nevertheless not be out Hit wicket. The majority of these have been addressed \\nabove. The other points (avoiding a stumping, a run-out or a throw from a fielder) are self-\\nexplanatory. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"LAW 36 LEG BEFORE WICKET \\n36.1 Out LBW \\nThe striker is out LBW if all the circumstances set out in 36.1.1 to 36.1.5 apply. \\n36.1.1 \\nThe bowler delivers a ball, not being a No ball  \\n36.1.2 \\nthe ball, if it is not intercepted full-pitch, pitches in line between wicket and \\nwicket or on the off side of the striker\'s wicket \\n36.1.3 \\nthe ball not having previously touched his/her bat, the striker intercepts the \\nball, either full-pitch or after pitching, with any part of his/her person \\n36.1.4 \\nthe point of impact, even if above the level of the bails, \\neither          is between wicket and wicket  \\nor                 if the striker has made no genuine attempt to play the ball with the bat, \\nis between wicket and wicket or outside the line of the off stump. \\n36.1.5 \\nbut for the interception, the ball would have hit the wicket. \\n \\n36.2 Interception of the ball \\n36.2.1 \\nIn assessing points in 36.1.3, 36.1.4 and 36.1.5, only the first interception is to \\nbe considered. \\n36.2.2 \\nIn assessing point 36.1.3, if the ball makes contact with the striker\'s person \\nand bat simultaneously, this shall be considered as the ball having first touched the \\nbat. \\n36.2.3 \\nIn assessing point 36.1.5, it is to be assumed that the path of the ball before \\ninterception would have continued after interception, irrespective of whether the \\nball might have pitched subsequently or not. \\n \\n36.3 Off side of wicket \\n  \\nThe off side of the striker\'s wicket shall be determined by the striker\'s stance at the \\nmoment the ball comes into play for that delivery. See Appendix A.13. \\nLBW could be described as a high profile Law. Most umpires tend to be judged, by players \\nand followers of the game, by their ability to get LBW decisions right, particularly in the \\nprofessional game, where they are examined closely. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"The requirements to be met for an umpire to uphold an appeal for LBW are clear, although \\njudging them is not always so easy. They are set out in a logical sequence in Law 36.1. It \\nmust be stressed, as in the first sentence of Law 36.1 that all the circumstances must apply. \\nThe only real variation is provided by Law 36.1.4, with its either or option. \\nThe other point at which there are apparent alternatives, is in Law 36.1.3, stating that the \\nball may pitch first or may hit the striker\'s person without having pitched. There is no real \\npractical variation here. The requirements about where the ball must pitch simply become \\nirrelevant if it doesn\'t pitch. \\n \\nBeing prepared for an appeal \\nSince all these requirements belong to the behaviour of the ball before it hits the striker, it is \\nobvious that they will have occurred without the umpire knowing beforehand whether \\nthere is going to be an appeal. The umpire must therefore be aware on every delivery, in \\naddition to all the other things he/she must be mindful of: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 whether or not the ball is a No ball \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 where the ball pitches - if it pitches at all  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 what the path of the ball is after it pitched \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 whether it misses the striker completely, or first hits the striker\'s person, or first hits \\nthe bat  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 if it hits the person first, either after pitching, or full pitch, \\n        \\xe2\\x80\\xa2 where the striker is in relation to the stumps at that time \\n        \\xe2\\x80\\xa2 and whether the striker is attempting to play the ball with his/her bat and \\nfinally \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 where the ball\'s path was taking it at that time. \\nWith experience, it will become a matter of almost unconscious habit to observe all these \\nthings, and more, every time the ball is bowled. Much of the information will be required for \\nadjudicating on other situations, anyway. \\nClearly the most difficult part of the decision is the last item in the list above. Others can, \\nhowever, sometimes cause problems. It can be difficult to see if the ball hit the bat or not a \\nvery fine edge - and if it did, whether it was before or after it hit the striker\'s person. It can \\nalso be difficult at times to see if the part of the striker that was hit was in line between \\nwicket and wicket. The striker will often move away quickly. The umpire must judge on \\nwhere the striker was at the moment of impact. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Where did the ball pitch? \\nBeing a round object, the ball pitches on its lowest point, which is its middle. It is this point \\nthat the umpire must judge. Anywhere, except outside leg stump will keep alive the \\npossibility of LBW. On the line of leg stump will do. Beyond it will not. \\nIt is crucial to note that if the ball does not pitch, and hits the batsman on the full outside \\nthe line of leg stump this also means that the batsman cannot, under any circumstances, be \\ngiven out LBW. \\n \\nThe ball, delivered by a left-\\narm over the wicket bowler, \\nhas pitched outside leg stump \\nwhatever else happens, this \\ncannot be out LBW. \\n \\nDid the ball hit the bat before hitting the person?  \\nWhether the ball did or did not get a fine edge on the bat before hitting the striker is no less \\ndifficult than the same judgment for a catch behind. A batsman playing defensively will \\nalmost certainly have the bat close to his/her pad - often behind it, as already discussed in \\nLaw 23. Sharp observation is needed to be sure, since a touch on the bat before hitting the \\nperson means that an appeal for LBW must fail. If the ball struck the pad first, the possibility \\nof LBW remains alive. An umpire who is not sure must not guess, but it is not fair to the \\nbowler and his/her side if the umpire persistently takes refuge in being unsure. An umpire \\nmust watch with the utmost concentration, to be sure, in as many cases as possible, which \\nwas hit first. \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"The Law gives clear guidance that if the impact on bat and person is simultaneous then it is \\nto be judged to have hit the bat first. This is most often useful for international matches \\nwith slow-motion replays. Umpires not able to use such technology will watch as closely as \\npossible, but if they are unsure which was hit first, or believe it to be simultaneous, must \\ngive the batsman the benefit of the doubt. \\nUmpires must also remember that it is not only a case of the first impact being on the \\nstriker\'s person rather than the bat, it is only the first impact on the striker which is to be \\nconsidered at all. \\nIf the ball hits the front pad and then goes on to hit the other pad, this second impact is \\nirrelevant. \\n \\nWas the point at which the striker was hit in line between wicket and wicket? \\nIf the striker is only partly in front of the wicket, this judgment is not too difficult. More \\noften, however, he/she will be completely hiding the stumps. It is helpful when first going \\nout to the pitch, before there is a batsman in the way, for the umpire to study carefully how \\nthe pitch looks and how the 9-inch (22.86cm) strip of grass between the wickets looks. The \\ninexperienced umpire is often surprised to see what a narrow strip it is. There may well be \\nsigns, such as a lighter patch of grass, a worn patch on a length, that will help the umpire to \\nbuild a mental picture of the stumps behind the striker\'s body. \\nIt can be useful to \\nstudy the pitch \\nwithout the players \\nbefore play begins, \\nto examine what \\nthe 9-inch strip of \\ngrass between the \\nwickets looks like on \\nthat particular pitch. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"As the game progresses, the bowler\'s footmarks at the er end may provide more points of \\nreference. \\n \\nWhere was the ball\'s path taking it when it hit the striker? \\nTo answer this question, the umpire must first have a good picture of where the ball was \\ngoing before it hit the striker. cannot be stated too often how important it is for the umpire \\nto switch attention from the bowler\'s feet to the might of the ball at the earliest possible \\nmoment. \\nThe umpire\'s first point of information is the bowler\'s delivery stride. Was it close to the \\nstumps, as wide out on the return crease as a fair delivery would allow, or somewhere in \\nbetween? \\nThis will give valuable information about the angle of flight from delivery point towards the \\nopposite end. It is worth noting that a bowler bowling round the wicket inevitably bowls \\nfrom a wider angle than one bowling over the wicket with his/her feet similarly placed.  \\nThe actual flight is also informative. Was the ball swinging? How well flighted was it? How \\nfast was it? Although the umpire will know that in general the bowler is quick, medium or \\nslow, every delivery is likely to have some variation of speed. The umpire needs to gauge \\nthe pace and flight. Both will affect how the ball will come off the pitch when it hits the \\nground. If it doesn\'t pitch, all this information is exactly what the umpire needs to know \\nabout where the ball was going when it hits the striker. \\nWhen the ball pitches, if it does, it will alter its path, even if only from descending to \\nascending. First, how quickly did the ball rise? If it rises sharply and at a good pace, it is \\npossible that it would have gone over the top of the stumps. A slower ball on the other \\nhand, might not. Very often, however, the ball will also deviate after pitching. The path of \\nthe ball as it hits the striker is what will determine whether or not it would have hit the \\nwicket. The umpire must therefore have seen enough of its path after pitching to know \\nwhat that path is. The fact that the umpire has good Information from before pitching \\nmeans that the way it changes can inform what he/she needs to know. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b\'If the umpire waited until the ball pitched before taking an interest he/she would have little \\nhope of being sure what it did thereafter. The greater the distance between pitching, and \\nimpact, the more information and evidence the umpire has, in order to judge the ongoing \\npath of the ball. \\nHow certain the umpire can be about the ball\\\'s flight will depend on the pace of the ball, its \\nprevious flight and on the alertness of observation by the umpire. In general, the faster the \\ndelivery, the less time it will take between pitching and impact and therefore the greater \\nthe distance required to assess its path. It will become easier with experience. The practised \\numpire will have accumulated knowledge of the way that moving balls behave, just as a \\ngood fielder knows where the ball will be by the time it arrives. The basic requirement to \\ngive a decision is \\\'Did the umpire see enough of the ball\\\'s travel after pitching to know what \\nits final path was?\\\' \\nIf the ball strikes the batsman on the full, the umpire must ignore whether the ball might \\ndeviate if it is likely to pitch before it reaches the wicket. The ball may hit the striker low \\ndown, or be dropping rapidly as it hits the pad. In this case, the umpire is to assume that \\nthere would have been no lateral deviation of the path if, being low, the ball would have \\nsubsequently hit the ground before reaching the stumps. If a ball is swinging in the air \\nbefore impact it should be assumed to be continuing to swing after impact - but a ball that \\nwas straight should be assumed to continue to go straight, whether or not previous \\ndeliveries have spun or seamed off the pitch. \\n \\nWould the ball have hit the wicket? \\nIt is a temptation for the umpire to read into the behaviour of the ball things which have not \\nbeen observed, by relying on the way previous balls have behaved. For instance, the umpire \\nmay, consciously or subconsciously, think "This is an off spin bowler. All the previous balls \\nhave turned so this one turned! Previous balls however did not strike the pad - so this one \\ncould well be different! The umpire must resolutely ignore previous deliveries and judge this \\nball on its own merits, strictly on what has been observed about it. \\nHaving decided what its final path before impact is, deciding whether the ball would have \\nhit the wicket is simply a matter of sufficient experience in extending that path in the \\numpire\\\'s mind, onward to the stumps.  \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n\''b'b"One very important point for consideration is how far does the ball still have to travel to \\nreach the stumps?  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If the striker\'s pad is only two feet (it is often more) in front of the popping crease \\nwhen hit, the ball would have had to travel another six feet before reaching the \\nstumps.  \\nUmpires must remember that, except in the case of the striker not having tried to hit the \\nball with his bat: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the point of impact on the striker and  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the point where it would have reached the wicket.  \\nmust both be within the 9-inch strip between wicket and wicket. \\nThis means that the last section of travel must be fairly straight, (parallel to the edges of the \\n9-inch strip).  \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 If the striker has played forward, the front leg may be 4 feet in front of the popping crease \\nand so 8 feet in front of the stumps. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If the impact is in line with off stump, a ball coming in from the off can move \\nsideways only 1 inch per foot of travel if it is not to miss leg stump. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If the striker is hit in front of middle stump, this amount of onward movement is \\nreduced to half that. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The path of a ball which has turned considerably, or that of a swinging ball, will \\nalmost certainly not meet this requirement.
