The continuation of such paths will be \\noutside leg, probably by a good margin. \\nIt follows that the further forward the striker is when hit the further the ball would still have \\nhad to travel, the straighter the path would have to be if it is to remain within the 9-inch \\nstrip until reaching the level of the stumps. \\nThe umpire must not assume that every batsman playing forward is immune. It is perfectly \\npossible for a batsman to be out LBW, but the margin for error is small and the umpire must \\nbe entirely sure of his/her observations. The balls with the best chance of remaining within \\nthe 9-inch strip are those whose deflection on pitching \'straightens\' them. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Striker not trying to play the ball with the bat  \\nAs indicated in the question on whether the striker was in line between wicket and wicket, \\nthere is a further scenario to consider, although very little changes in this situation. Only the \\nsecond part of Law 36.1.4 applies. If the striker is hit in line between wicket and wicket, that \\nis straightforward, but in addition LBW is still possible even if the impact is outside off \\nstump. The effect of this is that a turning ball will have more chance than otherwise of \\nsecuring an LBW dismissal. The last part of the travel can be at a greater angle to the line \\nbetween wicket and wicket and still satisfy the conditions. The difference is that if the ball \\nimpacts the striker outside the line of the off stump then he/she is only protected from \\nbeing considered for LBW if making a genuine attempt to hit the ball with the bat. The \\nproblems facing the umpire in deciding whether the striker has tried to play the ball with \\nthe bat are exactly the same as those considered when allowing or disallowing leg-byes \\nunder Law 23. \\n \\n  \\nThe batsman has been hit by the  \\n  \\nball \'plumb\' in front of middle stump - but  \\n  \\nthere is no guarantee of it being  \\n  \\nOut LBW. \\n"'b'b" \\n \\nThe batsman has been hit by the ball \\nplumb\' in front of middle stump - but the \\nball pitched outside the leg stump. Not \\nout \\nThe batsman has been hit by the ball \'plumb\' in \\nfront of middle stump but the ball would have \\ngone over the top of the stumps. Not out. \\n \\nThe batsman has been hit by the ball \\n\'plumb\' in front of middle stump - but the \\nball had hit the bat first. Not out. \\nThe batsman has been hit \'plumb\' in front of \\nmiddle stump - but the ball is swinging and \\nwould have missed leg stump. Not out. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b" \\n \\nHere, the batsman has been hit in front of leg \\nstump by four different deliveries. \\n \\nBall 1 - The ball is going towards leg. The \\ndistance left to travel means it would miss leg \\nstump. Not out. \\nBall 2 - The movement towards leg is slight. The \\nball will probably clip leg stump.  \\nBall 3 - The ball is going straight towards the leg \\nstump. This would be given out LBW.  \\nBall 4 - This ball has pitched outside leg stump \\nand cannot be out LBW. \\n \\nThe batsman is hit outside off stump. but \\nwith the bat \'hidden\' behind the pad, he \\nis not likely to be trying to hit the ball. \\nLBW is still a possibility \\n \\nThe batsman is hit outside off stump and \\nis not trying to hit the ball. \\n \\nBall 1 - The swing means this may be \\nmissing the stumps.  \\nBall 2 - This is going to hit the stumps. \\nOut LBW.  \\nBall 3 - This is going straight on, and is \\ntherefore missing the off stump. Not out. \\n"'b'b" \\n  \\n \\n  \\nThe batsman is clearly not \\ntrying to hit the ball with the \\nbat. The ball is headed for \\nthe stumps, so that is Out \\nLBW. \\n \\nThe off side of the wicket \\nWhatever is the off side of the field when the ball comes in to play, remains the off side until \\nthe ball is dead, even though the striker may change stance from right handed to left \\nhanded (or vice versa). This clarifies the position for the \'reverse sweep\' and the \'switch-hit. \\nIn the latter case, fewer deliveries will be Wides - so fewer one run penalties will accrue to \\nthe batting side. A batsman\'s reach is naturally greater on the off side than on the leg side. \\nRemember that, as covered in Law 22, a ball is not a Wide unless it is out of reach from \\nwhere the striker is and where he/she would be in a normal guard position. Although from \\nthe umpire\'s point of view in making any LBW decisions the off side stays the same \\nthroughout, from the point of view of the batsman\'s reach, he/she now has two off sides - \\none a normal guard position, the other the new stance in the other direction. The striker has \\ntwo sides with a greater theoretical reach. Apart from LBW, the fact that the off side of the \\nfield does not change when the batsman switches stance is important for consideration of \\nleg side fielders behind the popping crease. \\nBecause the off side stays the same, any appeal for LBW that would have been upheld in his \\noriginal stance would still be upheld. Even though the batsman may feel that balls are \\ncoming from the leg side, it is in fact still officially the off side. \\nThis is an appropriate point to remind umpires that if a ball pitches outside leg, or if the \\nstriker is first hit outside leg, whether the ball pitches or not, no appeal for LBW can be \\nconsidered. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'LAW 37 OBSTRUCTING THE FIELD \\n37.1 Out Obstructing the field \\n37.1.1 \\nEither batsman is out Obstructing the field if except in the circumstances of \\n37.2, and while the ball is in play. he/she wilfully attempts to obstruct or distract the \\nfielding side by word or action. See also Law 34 (Hit the ball twice). \\n37.1.2 \\nThe striker is out Obstructing the field if, except in the circumstances of 37.2. \\nin the act of receiving a ball delivered by the bowler he/she wilfully strikes the ball \\nwith a hand not holding the bat. This will apply whether it is the first strike or a \\nsecond or subsequent strike. The act of receiving the ball shall extend both to \\nplaying at the ball and to striking the ball more than once in defence of his/her \\nwicket. \\n37.1.3 \\nThis Law will apply whether or not No ball is called. \\n \\n37.2 Not out Obstructing the field \\n  \\nA batsman shall not be out Obstructing the field if \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 obstruction or distraction is accidental. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 or obstruction is in order to avoid injury. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 or in the case of the striker, he/she makes a second or subsequent strike to guard \\nhis/her wicket lawfully as in Law 34.3 (Ball lawfully struck more than once). \\nHowever, see 37.3 \\n \\n37.3 Obstructing a ball from being caught \\n37.3.1 \\nIf the delivery is not a No ball, the striker is out Obstructing the field if wilful \\nobstruction or distraction by either batsman prevents the striker being out Caught. \\n37.3.2 \\n37.3.1 shall apply even if an obstruction is caused by the striker in lawfully \\nguarding his/her wicket under the provision of Law 34.3 (Ball lawfully struck more \\nthan once). \\n37.3.3 \\nIf an obstruction or distraction takes place from a No ball then the batsman \\nwho caused the obstruction or distraction will be out Obstructing the field. \\n37.3.4 \\n37.3.3 shall not apply if the striker obstructs while instinctively defending \\nhis/her wicket with a lawful second strike. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'37.4 Returning the ball to a fielder \\n  \\nEither batsman is out Obstructing the field if at any time while the ball is in play and \\nwithout the consent of a fielder. he/she uses the bat or any part of his/her person to \\nreturn the ball to any fielder. \\n \\n37.5 Runs scored \\nWhen either batsman is dismissed Obstructing the field, \\n37.5.1 \\nunless the obstruction prevents a catch from being made, any runs \\ncompleted by the batsmen before the offence shall be scored, together with any \\nruns awarded for penalties to either side. See Laws 18.6 (Runs awarded for \\npenalties) and 18.8 (Runs scored when a batsman is dismissed). \\n37.5.2 \\nif the obstruction prevents a catch from being made, any runs completed by \\nthe batsmen shall not be scored but any penalties awarded to either side shall stand. \\n \\n37.6 Bowler does not get credit \\nThe bowler does not get credit for the wicket. \\n \\nThe general rule is that either batsman is Out Obstructing the field if, while the ball is in \\nplay, he/she wilfully attempts to obstruct or distract the fielding side by word or action. All \\nforms of Obstructing the field, including the special cases described below, can occur off a \\nNo ball and the bowler does not get credit for any of them. \\nThere are three exceptions to the general rule: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the obstruction or distraction was accidental; \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the obstruction was in order to avoid injury: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the striker made a second (or subsequent) strike to guard his wicket lawfully without \\npreventing a catch. \\n \\nOut Obstructing the Field \\nWhilst this Law covers any wilful obstruction or distraction of the fielding side, by word or \\ndeed, it describes a number of specific cases: \\n \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The first in Law 37.1.2 is when the striker touches the ball in play with his/her hand \\nnot holding the bat, during the act of playing at the ball, whether it is the first or a \\nsubsequent strike and includes any second strike with the non-bat hand in defence \\nof his/her wicket. \\n'"b'b" \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The second specific case is when either batsman wilfully prevents a ball from being \\ncaught by a fielder by obstructing that fielder by word or action. This could be: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the striker attempting to hit the ball a second time, with bat or person, even if \\nthis second strike was in lawful defence of his/her wicket. The right of the \\nfielder to take the catch takes precedence over the right of the striker to \\ndefend their wicket. \\n \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 either batsmen wilfully obstructing the taking of a catch where the \\nobstruction can take a number of different forms including shouting to \\ndistract a fielder. or running into the fielder\'s path. This is discussed further \\nbelow. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The situation where either batsman touches the ball with a hand not holding the bat \\nafter the ball has been played by the striker, is covered by the general statement in \\nLaw 37.1.1 or as part of the third specific case. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The third specific case is in Law 37.4 which describes the intervention of either \\nbatsman to return the ball to a fielder without permission, while the ball is in play. \\nThis might be either tapping the ball back with the bat, kicking it with a foot or \\npicking it up and throwing it back with a hand not holding the bat. \\nHowever, any act by a batsman, designed to reduce the capacity of the fielding side to field \\nthe ball or secure a dismissal, is Obstructing the field. The word \'designed\' is important. It is \\nonly when such acts are wilful that they become obstruction. It is important for the umpire \\nto distinguish wilful behaviour from attempts by the batsman to avoid injury. On the other \\nhand, the umpire should be aware of more subtle ways a batsman may act wilfully, such as a \\nbatsman deliberately changing course in running, to try to block the path of the ball being \\nthrown in for a run out attempt. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"This last example is being seen more commonly. Although it is hard to know if the intention \\nis to block the ball, a batsman who changes direction while running, particularly one who \\nchanges direction to run on the pitch, or takes any other route that would not be the \\nquickest way to the other end, is making a wilful act detours around the bowler are \\nacceptable, of course. Sometimes a batsman will not even change direction, but will \\ndeliberately run down the protected area in order to block a potential throw by the wicket-\\nkeeper to the bowler\'s end. This too could result in a batsman being out Obstructing the \\nField. \\nIn elite cricket where camera replays are available, it might be possible to a see what the \\nbatsman\'s intention is, particularly if the batsman has taken a look to see where the ball is \\ngoing! If the batsman is hit under these circumstances, it is more than likely that he/she will \\nbe out Obstructing the field, though not always. For example, if the direction change is an \\nattempt to avoid injury then the batsman should never be adjudged out Obstructing the \\nfield. However, if the umpire is satisfied that the action was wilful and of course if the ball is \\nin play and if there is an appeal, the umpire should be prepared to dismiss a batsman. The \\nfact that the particular throw would not have hit the stumps or Run out the batsman is \\nirrelevant. \\nWhich batsman is out and what happens subsequently will depend on the specific act of \\nObstruction. \\nThere was a case some while ago when a running batsman, realising he would not make his \\nground in time, threw his bat at the stumps to break the wicket. As he was in the act of \\nrunning he could not be out Hit wicket. He was, however, out Obstructing the field, as the \\nfielding side were prevented from breaking the wicket directly with the ball to run him out \\nand would take time to replace at least one bail, or pull a stump out with ball in hand to do \\nso. \\n \\nWas the obstruction wilful? \\nA batsman when running is intent on making his/her ground and may possibly collide with a \\nfielder, especially if the fielder is also moving quickly to field the ball. If, following such a \\ncollision, there is an appeal, the decision will hinge on whether or not the collision was \\ndeliberate or accidental. The bowler\'s end umpire is tasked with making this decision, and \\nthe Law does not require him/her to consult the other umpire, although it is often useful to \\ndo so. The bowler\'s end umpire may have had a clear view of what happened and give a \\ndecision. If not, however, his/her colleague at the striker\'s end may have had a better view \\nfrom his/her side-on position, and should be consulted. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Obstructing a catch  \\nA batsman is certainly guilty of obstructing the field if at any time he/she deliberately \\ninterferes with a fielder trying to take a catch. This may be by a carefully timed shout or in \\nany other way. It is not restricted to the special case of a second strike to defend his/her \\nwicket. If a catch is obstructed it does not matter which batsman is guilty, it is the striker \\nwho is Out - after all, it is the striker who would have been out if the catch had been taken. \\nFor any other obstruction, it is the batsman causing it who is out. If No ball had been called, \\nthen although a fielder might be trying to catch the ball, it will not be a catch. Distraction by \\na batsman will still be obstruction, however. In this case, it will be the batsman who caused \\nthe distraction who is Out, as a catch was not involved. \\n \\nRuns scored \\nIf a batsman is dismissed under this Law, runs scored follow the general principle that the \\nrun in progress does not count, because the batsman is dismissed. All completed runs \\nbefore the offence will count and a one run penalty for Wide or No ball will stand as usual, \\ntogether with any other appropriate Penalty runs awarded. Which end the new batsman \\nwill go to will depend on whether the batsmen had crossed at the moment of the offence. \\nHowever, on the same principle that no runs are scored if the striker is out Caught, if the \\nobstruction prevents a catch, not only will it always be the striker who is out, but no runs \\nexcept penalties will be scored. \\nThis is a case where the scorers may well be in doubt as to the method of dismissal and even \\nas to the number of runs to be recorded. The umpire should make sure they have this \\ninformation, at the earliest opportunity. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"LAW 38 RUN OUT  \\n38.1 Out Run out \\nEither batsman is out Run out, except as in 38.2. if. at any time while the ball is in play. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 he/she is out of his/her ground  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 and his/her wicket is fairly put down by the action of a fielder \\n \\neven though No ball has been called, except in the circumstances of 38.2.2.2, and whether \\nor not a run is being attempted. \\n \\n38.2 Batsman not out Run out \\n38.2.1 \\nA batsman is not out Run out in the circumstances of 38.2.1.1 or 38.2.1.2. \\n38.2.1.1 \\nHe/She has been within his/her ground and has subsequently left it to \\navoid injury. when the wicket is put down. Note also the provisions of Law \\n30.1.2 (When out of his/ her ground). \\n38.2.1.2 \\nThe ball, delivered by the bowler, has not made contact with a fielder \\nbefore the  wicket is put down. \\n38.2.2 \\nThe striker is not out Run out in any of the circumstances in 38.2.2.1 and \\n38.2.2.2. \\n38.2.2.1 \\nHe/She is out Stumped. See Laws 25.6.5 (Dismissal and conduct of a \\nbatsman and his/her runner) and 39.1.2 (Out Stumped). \\n38.2.2.2 \\nNo ball has been called \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 and he/she is out of his/her ground not attempting a run \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 and the wicket is fairly put down by the wicket-keeper without the \\nintervention of another fielder. \\nHowever, if the striker has a runner and the runner is out of his/her \\nground, then only 38.1 applies. \\n \\n38.3 Which batsman is out \\n  \\nThe batsman out in the circumstances of 38.1 is the one whose ground is at the end \\nwhere the wicket is put down. \\n  \\nSee Laws 25.6 (Dismissal and conduct of a batsman and his/her runner) and 30.2 \\n(Which is a batsman\'s ground). \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"38.4 Runs scored \\nIf either batsman is dismissed Run out, the run in progress when the wicket is put down \\nshall not be scored, but any runs completed by the batsmen shall stand, together with any \\nruns for penalties awarded to either side. See Laws 18.6 (Runs awarded for penalties) and \\n18.8 (Runs scored when a batsman is dismissed). If, however, a striker who has a runner is \\nhimself/herself dismissed Run out and runs are completed by the runner and the other \\nbatsman before the wicket is put down, \\n \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 all runs to the batting side from that delivery shall be disallowed. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the one run penalty for No ball shall stand and 5 run penalties shall be allowed. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the umpire shall return the non-striker to his/her original end. \\n  \\nSee Law 25.6 (Dismissal and conduct of a batsman and his/her runner). \\n \\n38.5 Bowler does not get credit \\n  \\nThe bowler does not get credit for the wicket. \\nThe basic application of this Law is simple. The ball is in play. A batsman is out of his/her \\nground. The wicket at his/ her end is fairly put down by the fielding side. The batsman is out \\nRun out. \\n \\nHis/her ground \\nWhich ground belongs to which batsman has been fully discussed under Law 30, including \\nall the complications of two batsmen in the same ground, and of an injured batsman who \\nhas a runner. When the wicket is put down, which batsman is out rests on an understanding \\nof which batsman\'s ground is at that end. \\nIf there is a runner, it is not as complicated as it might seem. It will always be a normal \\ndecision based on the positions of the runner and the non-striker only, except in one \\nsituation. If the injured striker with a runner is out of his/her ground (which is always at the \\nwicket-keeper\'s end) and the wicket is put down at that end, the injured striker will be out \\nregardless of the positions of the other two. Law 25.6 defines whether the striker is out \\nStumped or Run out. This situation is discussed in more detail later. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Exceptions - when a batsman will not be run out \\n1. Avoiding injury \\nA batsman in his/her ground may fear that a ball being thrown in to the wicket may hit \\nhis/her ankle, and jump in the air to avoid being hurt. If the wicket is put down while he/she \\nis in the air the batsman will not be run out. Similarly, perhaps a batsman moves out of \\nhis/her ground to avoid a potential collision with a fielder or umpire. Again, the batsman \\nwould not be out Run out. The two points are: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 he/she must have been within his/her ground.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 he/she must have left it only to avoid injury. \\n \\nThe first is a matter of fact, the second a matter of judgment. It will usually not be difficult \\nfor the umpire to make this judgment. \\n \\n2. In continuing his/her running \\nLaw 30.1 defines when a batsman is in or out of his/her ground and Law 30.1.2 makes an \\nexception, so that when a batsman is running, or diving, he/she may not need to maintain \\ncontact with the ground at all times. \\nThe two points needed to give the batsman this immunity are that he/she: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 is running or diving to gain or regain his/her ground. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 has grounded any part of his/her person, or the bat (in hand), behind the popping \\ncrease before the wicket is put down. \\nThis is explained in more detail under Law 30. In a game without the benefit of cameras, it is \\nunlikely but not impossible that an umpire could discern that both batsman and bat were in \\nthe air at the precise moment that the wicket was put down. It does mean, however, that \\nonce the umpire has seen some part of the batsman\'s person or bat grounded behind the \\npopping crease, he/she need not worry about what happens if the batsman continues \\nrunning or diving in the same direction. \\nHowever, the ball will not be Dead so further action might take place. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"3. Ball not touched by a fielder  \\nThe striker is not Run out if the ball delivered by the bowler has not made contact with any \\nfielder before the wicket put down. \\nThis covers three situations. \\nThe bowler is entitled, under Law 41.16, to run out the non-striker who has left his/her \\nground too soon, as long as the attempt is made before the point at which the bowler \\nwould normally release the ball. Once the bowler delivers the ball, it must then be touched \\nby another fielder before a run out can be attempted. \\nWhen the striker hits the ball, it cannot yet have been touched by a fielder (if it has, which is \\nvery rare, No ball will have been called). If the ball then breaks the wicket at the bowler\'s \\nend, even though the non-striker may be out of his/her ground, the non-striker will not be \\nrun out if a fielder (which obviously includes the bowler) has still not touched the ball since \\nit left the striker\'s bat. \\nA striker cannot be out Bowled if No ball has been called. If the ball breaks his/her wicket, \\ntechnically it has been put down by the opposing side. This provision of Law ensures that \\nthe failed Bowled will not simply turn into Run out, if the striker is out of his/her ground, \\nunless a fielder has touched the ball since it left the bowler\'s hand. The fielder is, of course, \\nnot entitled to do so until after the striker has had an opportunity to play the ball. However, \\nif the bowler throws the ball towards the striker\'s wicket before entering his/her delivery \\nstride (because, for example, the striker is advancing down the pitch) then, although No ball \\nwill be called, the striker can be Run out if the throw is successful. See Law 21.4 \\n \\n4. Out Stumped \\nStumped is a special form of Run out. The interlocking of Run out and Stumped is discussed \\nin the next Law. The proviso in this Law affirms that if the method of dismissal is Stumped, \\nthis will over-ride Run out. \\n \\n5. A special case of not out Stumped \\nAs the paragraph above notes, if a batsman is out Stumped, they would also meet all the \\nconditions of a Run out. \\nHowever, if the delivery is a No ball, the batsman cannot be out Stumped. Because a \\nbatsman can be Run out off a No ball, that would mean that any time a batsman is \\n\'Stumped\' off a No ball, he/she would simply be given out Run out. To ensure this is not the \\ncase, Law 38.2.2.2 stipulates that, even though the requirements for Run out have been \\nmet, if the batsman is not attempting a run, and only the wicket-keeper has touched the \\nball, then Run out is not an option (the only exception to this is covered below under Striker \\nwho has a runner). \\n"'b'b" \\nBall rebounding off a helmet \\nIf either wicket is put down by a ball rebounding from any fielder\'s helmet which is being \\nworn, then the batsman at that end is still liable to be Run out. The helmet is considered as \\na part of the fielder\'s person as it is legitimate external protective equipment. \\nIf the ball rebounds off a fielder\'s unworn helmet on the ground, it immediately becomes \\nDead on contact, so there is no question of a dismissal. \\nIf the ball rebounds from either batsmen\'s helmet, it is still in play and any normal dismissal \\nis possible, including a run out, as long as a fielder has touched the ball since it left the \\nbowler\'s hand. \\n \\nStriker who has a runner \\nIf the injured batsman who has a runner is not the striker, the question of being Run out \\nrests entirely on whether the runner is Run out. If, however the injured batsman is the \\nstriker, then any of the injured striker, his/her runner or the non-striker may be run out.  \\nLaw 30 makes it clear that the injured striker\'s end is always at the wicket-keeper\'s end, \\nnever at the bowler\'s end. The first consequence of this is that if the wicket is put down at \\nthe bowler\'s end, the injured striker cannot be run out. The umpire is to decide which of the \\nrunner or non striker, if either, is out, on the normal basis for two batsmen. \\nIf, however, the wicket is put down at the wicket-keeper\'s end, with both the injured striker \\nand the non-striker out of their ground, the question could arise as to whether it is the \\n\'injured striker\' who is out, or the other batsman. Law 25 and Law 30 together make it clear \\nthat if the injured striker is out of his/her ground, he/she will be the one who is out, \\nregardless of where the runner and the non-striker are. He/ she will be Stumped if the \\nconditions in Law 25.6.5 and Law 39 (Stumped) apply. Otherwise the injured striker is Run \\nout, even if No ball has been called. If the injured striker is in his/her ground, the decision is \\nagain on the normal basis for the other two batsmen. If it is the runner who is out, then \\nautomatically, the injured striker is out. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Runs scored \\nLaw 38.4 sets out the runs scored. Normally, when a batsman is out Run out, completed \\nruns only will be scored in addition to penalties. The run in progress is not scored, though of \\ncourse it is relevant in determining which batsman is nearer to the end at which the wicket \\nis put down, and thus who is out. \\n \\nWhen the striker who has a runner is him/herself Run out, which will always be at the \\nwicket-keeper\'s end, any runs taken are to be disallowed and the non-striker returned to \\nthe original end as set out in Law 25.6.6. Only penalties can be scored but not those for the \\nball hitting the fielder\'s helmet on the ground behind the wicket keeper. \\nThe chart summarises the above facts about Run out dismissal and runs scored. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b" \\nSTRIKER WHO HAS A RUNNER RUN OUT? \\nWicket put down at bowler\'s end \\nIgnore injured striker\'; consider positions of other two Normal Run out applies \\nCompleted runs and Penalties OK \\nNew batsman to end where wicket fell \\nWicket put down at keeper\'s end \\n \\n\\xe2\\x80\\x98injured striker\\xe2\\x80\\x99 in his/her ground \\n \\n\\xe2\\x80\\x98injured striker\\xe2\\x80\\x99 out of his/her ground \\n \\n \\nIgnore injured striker Consider positions of other \\ntwo Normal Run out applies \\n \\nCompleted runs + penalties Not out batsman \\ngoes to opposite end to where wicket fell \\n \\n \\nOnly Injured striker can be out Ignore other two \\nNo runs but Penalties OK except ball hitting \\nhelmet \\n \\nNew batsman goes to the end where the Injured \\nstriker had previously been \\n \\nOf course, if the runner is out, the injured batsman is automatically out by definition, but \\nruns will be scored on the normal basis. If there are two runners, one of them is always to \\nbe regarded as an ordinary non-striker. \\nIn order to record the correct number of runs, the scorers will certainly need to know if it \\nwas the striker who was Run out, or whether he/she was out because the runner was. The \\numpire should inform the scorers, especially if runs are disallowed. \\n \\nField technique \\nIt cannot be said too often that, whenever the batsmen are running, the umpires must be in \\na position to see the wickets and the creases at their respective ends, and to see where the \\nbatsmen are. They need: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 to see that, if the wicket is put down, it is fairly put down \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 to know whether or not the batsman was in his/ her ground at that time \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 to be aware whether and when the batsmen cross during the run \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 to watch for short running. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"The striker\'s end umpire is already in a good position for this, though he/she may have to \\ndodge quickly to one side if a fielder moves across his/her line of vision. If there is a runner, \\nwhere to stand him/herself and where to place the runner has been set out under Law 25. It \\nwill take time for the bowler\'s end umpire to reach the ideal position for adjudicating on \\nevents at his/her wicket. He/she must remain at the wicket until the ball has been hit, in \\ncase there are any events at the striker\'s end on which he/she must exercise judgment. \\nThen the umpire must move very quickly. He/she must develop a sense of where the players \\nare, and where they may be running to, to avoid getting in their way while moving. He/she \\nalso needs to avoid getting in the path of the throw in. This has already been discussed in \\nmore detail in Law 29. \\nIf the ball is returned to the stumps very quickly, the bowler\'s end umpire may not have \\ntime to get as far as he/she would wish. He/she must get to the best position achievable in \\nthe time. That means being able to see the stumps, the crease and the running batsman, \\nand the umpire must also have his/her head stationary at the moment when a fine \\njudgment has to be made about the grounding of the bat and the breaking of the wicket. \\nA particular difficulty is the ball hit hard by the striker back to the bowler\'s end stumps. \\nGetting any distance from the stumps in this case is impossible, but, if safe to do so, he/she \\nmay be able to get a sideways, or at least a diagonal, view of the action. The umpire must \\nsee where the non striker is when the wicket is put down. He/she must also see whether a \\nfielder touched the ball before the wicket was put down. \\nWhen there is a run out attempt at one end, the umpire at the other end will realise this \\npossibility and will watch to see whether or not the batsmen had crossed before the wicket \\nis put down. If the Run out decision is a close one, the problem of crossing is likely to be an \\neasier task. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 39 STUMPED \\n39.1 Out Stumped \\n39.1.1 \\nThe striker is out Stumped, except as in 39.3, If a ball which is delivered is not \\ncalled No ball \\nand            he/she is out of his/her ground, other than as in 39.3.1 \\nand            he/she has not attempted a run \\nwhen        his/her wicket is fairly put down by the wicket-keeper without the \\nintervention of another fielder. Note, however Laws 25.6.2 and 25.6.5 (Dismissal and \\nconduct of a batsman and his/her runner) and 27.3 (Position of wicket-keeper). \\n39.1.2 \\nThe striker is out Stumped if all the conditions of 39.11 are satisfied, even \\nthough a decision of Run out would be justified. \\n \\n39.2 Ball rebounding from wicket-keeper\'s person \\nIf the wicket is put down by the ball, it shall be regarded as having been put down by the \\nwicket-keeper if the ball: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 rebounds on to the stumps from any part of the wicket-keeper\'s person or \\nequipment \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 has been kicked or thrown on to the stumps by the wicket-keeper. \\n \\n39.3 Not out Stumped \\n \\n39.3.1 \\nThe striker will not be out Stumped if after having received the delivery, \\nhe/she has left his/her ground in order to avoid injury. \\n39.3.2 \\nIf the striker is not out Stumped he/she may, except in the circumstances of \\nLaw 38.2.22 (Batsman not out Run out), be out Run out if the conditions of Law 38.1 \\n(Out Run out) apply. \\n \\nStumped is a special case of Run out. Only the striker can be Stumped; either batsman can \\nbe Run out. The bowler will get credit for a stumping but not for a Run out. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"The basic conditions for a Run out are: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the batsman must be out of his/her ground  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the wicket at his/her end must be put down fairly by the fielding side while he/she is \\nout of the ground. \\n \\nThese are also the basic requirements for the striker to be out Stumped, but three more \\nconditions are added to these: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the delivery must not be a No ball \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the striker must not be attempting a run \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the putting down of the wicket must be by the wicket-keeper alone, without any \\nother fielder being involved. \\n \\nIf the first two conditions of the total of five are met, but any of the last three are not, then \\nthe decision can revert back, as it were, to Run out. Law 39.3.2 spells this out but also sets \\nout two specific situations where Stumped will not apply. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If all of the above apply but the injured striker\'s runner is also out of his/her ground, \\nthen the striker is not Stumped but is Run out. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 It is the same principle as stated in clause 38.2.2.2 of Run out. If all the conditions for \\nStumped are met except that No ball is called, then not only will the striker not be \\nout Stumped, he/she will not be out Run out either. However, if the injured striker\'s \\nrunner is out of his/her ground then the runner, and hence the injured striker, will \\nbe out Run out, even if it is a No ball. This is because a runner - whose only role is to \\nrun - will always be considered to be attempting a run if he/she is out of his/her \\nground.  \\nThis is one of the methods of dismissal within the jurisdiction of the striker\'s end umpire. \\nWicket put down by the wicket-keeper alone  \\nLaw 39.2 defines what is meant by being \'put down by the wicket-keeper\\xe2\\x80\\x99. For a stumping to \\nbe possible, \'put down by the wicket-keeper\' is not enough. The wicket-keeper must be the \\nonly fielder in contact with the ball before the breaking of the wicket. Intervention of \\nanother fielder need not be intentional. If the ball rebounds off the slip fielder\'s boot, then \\nslip has \'intervened\' and stumping is not possible - though Run out might be. Usually the \\nwicket-keeper will put the wicket down with the ball in hand. He/she may equally do so by \\nthrowing, or even kicking the ball onto the stumps. Further, even if the ball rebounds from \\nthe wicket keeper\'s person or worn protective equipment, whether accidentally or not, the \\nstriker is liable to be out either Stumped or Run out, depending on whether the ball makes \\ncontact with another fielder \\n \\n \\n"'b"b' \\nBatsman not attempting a run \\nIt is precisely because he/she has moved forward to play the ball that a striker may be at risk \\nof being Stumped. If the striker misses the ball and it goes through towards the wicket-\\nkeeper standing up, he/she will likely be aware of the danger and be only too anxious to \\nreturn to his/her ground immediately. It will therefore not be difficult to distinguish \\nbetween movement made to play the ball and an attempt to run. It should be noted that if \\nthe striker has left his/her ground to avoid injury, that will protect the batsman from being \\nStumped. Since only the wicket-keeper can be involved, it will be rare for the ball to come \\nfrom a direction where the striker might be injured, but it can happen. (It should be noted \\nthat if a batsman is trying to avoid injury from the original delivery - ducking a bouncer, for \\ninstance - and hence leaves his/her ground, that would not earn him/her protection under \\nthis Law). \\nAs already explained under Law 30, a striker who has moved forward to play a ball, missed \\nit, is not attempting a run and is running or diving to get back into his/her ground is \\nprotected from being out Stumped if he/she loses contact with the ground having already \\nmade good his/her ground. Note that the striker is protected under Law 30.1.2 only if \\nhe/she is diving or running towards the wicket at that end the back foot stretching back into \\nthe ground and then being raised is not sufficient. \\nIf the delivery is a No ball then, even if the striker is out of his/her ground when the wicket is \\nput down by the wicket-keeper alone, providing a run is not being attempted then the \\nstriker is protected from being Stumped or Run out. However, if the ball does not become \\ndead after this attempt, and the batsmen then choose to run, a decision for Run out can still \\nbe made. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Injured striker with a runner \\nWhere an injured batsman with a runner is on strike there are special provisions governing \\nRun out and Stumped.  \\nThe position can be summarized as follows: \\n \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The injured striker\'s ground is always at the wicket-keeper\'s end. It is not uncommon \\nfor an \'injured striker\' to set off instinctively as if for a run. Even if he/she crosses \\nwith the non striker, however, and reaches the other end, a run will not be scored. \\nThe injured striker will not have made good his/her ground from end to end\' - the \\nbowler\'s end cannot be his/her (new) end. Consequently, even though, as frequently \\nhappens, an \'injured striker sets off and leaves the crease, the question of \\nattempting a run cannot arise. He/she has handed over to his/her runner the right to \\nscore runs and cannot do so him/herself. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If the injured striker is in his/her ground and the runner is out of that ground when \\nthe wicket is fairly put down at that end, the runner will be Run out unless one of the \\nexceptions in Law 38.2.1 (avoiding injury or not touched by a fielder) applies. This \\nresult applies whether or not it occurs off a fair delivery. A runner can only be Run \\nout, never Stumped - only the injured striker him/herself can be Stumped. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 An injured striker can only be Stumped off a fair delivery where he/she is out of \\nhis/her ground and his/her runner is in his/her ground. In the same circumstances \\noff a No ball, the injured striker will be neither Stumped (no stumping off a No ball) \\nnor Run out (for the same reason as a batsman with no runner is not Run out in \\nthese circumstances). \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If the injured striker is out of his/her ground, his/her runner is out of his/her ground \\nand the wicket is put down by the wicket-keeper alone, the injured striker is out Run \\nout, not Stumped. This is because if the runner is out of his/her ground, it can only \\never be Run out (see Law 25.6.5). This result applies whether or not it occurs off a \\nfair delivery. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Field technique \\nIn judging a stumping, the umpire must watch for the wicket being fairly put down and \\nwhether or not the striker was at that time in his/her ground. As noted under Law 29, the \\nwicket-keeper may break the wicket just before he/ she actually has the ball in his/her \\nhands, or may drop the ball before his/her hands reach the stumps. The striker\'s end umpire \\nmust be sure the wicket was fairly put down and that a bail came off before the striker was \\ngrounded behind the popping crease. At times, the two actions will be very close, making it \\ndifficult to be sure which came first. Complete concentration is essential. However, until the \\nbatsmen start to run, the umpire does not have to worry about the batsmen crossing, nor \\nabout short running. Instead he/she must see that only the wicket keeper was involved in \\nputting down the wicket and judge whether or not the striker was attempting a run. \\nMost of the four pieces of information will come from the striker\'s end umpire\'s normal \\naction for every delivery - the close scrutiny of the creases, the stumps and the positions of \\nthe wicket-keeper, striker and nearby fielders. As stated before, as soon as he/she has seen \\nthe ball leave the bowler\'s hand, the striker\'s end umpire must switch attention to the \\ncreases and stumps at his/her own end. He/she must be aware on every delivery that there \\nmay be an attempted stumping. Even if the wicket-keeper is standing back, he/she may kick \\nor throw the ball at the stumps. If the wicket-keeper is standing close, the umpire must \\nwatch to see that he/she does not come in front of the wicket too soon - snatching the ball \\ntoo early, in the hopes of a stumping - and be ready to call and signal No ball if he/ she does \\n(for more on this see Law 27.3). Remember that even a small portion of the gloves being in \\nline with the wicket means that the wicket-keeper is not wholly behind the stumps, and No \\nball should be called. \\n \\nAnother of the striker\'s end umpire\'s duties, watching for too many fielders behind the \\npopping crease on the leg side at the instant of delivery, will also contribute, since a \\ncontravention will mean a call of No ball and the removal of a stumping chance. \\n"'b'b"LAW 40 TIMED OUT \\n40.1 Out Timed out \\n40.1.1 \\nAfter the fall of a wicket or the retirement of a batsman, the incoming \\nbatsman must. unless Time has been called, be in position to take guard or for the \\nother batsman to be ready to receive the next ball within 3 minutes of the dismissal \\nor retirement. If this requirement is not met, the incoming batsman will be out, \\nTimed out. \\n40.1.2 \\nIn the event of an extended delay in which no batsman comes to the wicket, \\nthe umpires shall adopt the procedure of Law 16.3 (Umpires awarding a match). For \\nthe purposes of that Law the start of the action shall be taken as the expiry of the 3 \\nminutes referred to above. \\n \\n40.2 Bowler does not get credit \\n  \\nThe bowler does not get credit for the wicket. \\n \\nThis Law is designed to minimise the time taken for a new batsman to come in when a \\nwicket has fallen. The first paragraph specifies the state of readiness he/she must achieve \\nwithin 3 minutes. This time should be ample. If a batsman does not meet the deadline, \\nhe/she is out and could be dismissed on appeal. \\nAction by umpires  \\nFor a batsman to be dismissed there must be an appeal. The umpires cannot properly \\nanswer the appeal unless they know how long the arrival process had taken. Both should \\nalways note the time at which any wicket falls, in case the next batsman is late in arriving. If \\nthere is an appeal, the umpires should check times with each other to be sure whether the 3 \\nminute limit has been breached or not. The bowler\'s end umpire will give the decision \\naccordingly. If that decision is Out, this is the fall of the next wicket, starting a new 3 minute \\nspan; the time must again be noted. Except in the extreme case of protracted delay, \\ndescribed below, a batsman cannot be given out until his/her innings has begun. The umpire \\nmust therefore ensure that the next batsman is on the field of play when giving the decision \\nOut. The umpire must wait if necessary until the batsman steps on to the field of play. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b\'Protracted delay - awarding the match \\n \\nIf no batsman appears at all, umpires must not allow play to be held up indefinitely. They \\nshould consider the possibility that the batting side may be refusing to play. As in Law 16.3, \\nthey must investigate the cause of the delay. The ball will have become dead at the fall of \\nthe wicket and the umpires will have taken possession of the ball. Now, before leaving the \\nfield and going to the pavilion, Time must be called, as the prolonged delay is to be \\nconsidered as an interruption. This interruption, for the purposes of any possible \\ncalculations of time lost is, however, to be taken as starting at the expiry of the 3 minutes. \\nIf no appeal is made prior to the call of Time, it cannot be made after. Although a batsman is \\nout under the Laws, he/ she cannot be dismissed. The umpires\\\' sole concern will now be \\nwhether there has been a refusal to play or not. The procedure for this has been discussed \\nin Law 16. \\nThe steps are set out below, as a reminder. \\n \\n1. Discover the cause of delay \\nIf the action is considered a refusal to play: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 warn the captain \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 if action persists award match to fielding side \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 otherwise play continues. \\nIf the action is not considered a refusal \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 set a time for play to resume \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 " jointly calculate time lost, from expiry of the 3 minutes until resumption \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 close of play on that day to be put back by this length of time \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 "if applicable, no overs shall be deducted during the last hour on account of this \\ntime. \\n \\n2. Consider the appeal \\nIf no appeal has been made and the action is not a refusal: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 return to the field of play \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 call Play. \\nIf an appeal has already been made: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 it must be answered. In addition to a possible refusal to play (addressed above), a \\nbatsman is out and will therefore be dismissed, whether play continues or not. \\n \\n \\n\''b'b"\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The umpires will so inform the captain of the batting side. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The captain of the batting side will decide which batsman is out. There may have \\nbeen a \'batting order\' provided before the start of play, but this is not relevant - it is \\nfor the batting side to decide who is the \'next\' batsman, who is thus given out. \\nIn all cases, all this information must also be reported to the scorers \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 41 UNFAIR PLAY \\n41.1 Fair and unfair play-responsibility of captains  \\nThe captains are responsible for ensuring that play is conducted within The Spirit of Cricket, \\nas described in The Preamble - The Spirit of Cricket, as well as within the Laws. \\n \\n41.2 Unfair actions \\n41.2.1 \\nThe umpires shall be the sole judges of fair and unfair play. If an umpire \\nconsiders that any action by a player, not covered in the Laws, is unfair, he/she shall \\ncall and signal Dead ball, if appropriate, as soon as it becomes clear that the call will \\nnot disadvantage the non-offending side, and report the matter to the other umpire. \\n41.2.1.1 \\nIf this is a first offence by that side, the bowler\'s end umpire shall then  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 summon the offending player\'s captain and issue a first and final \\nwarning which shall apply to all members of the team for the \\nremainder of the match. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 warn the offending player\'s captain that any further such offence by \\nany member of his/her team shall result in the award of 5 Penalty \\nruns to the opposing team.  \\n41.2.1.2 \\nIf this is a second or subsequent offence by that side, the bowler\'s end \\numpire shall then \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 summon the offending player\'s captain and inform him/her that there \\nhas been a further such offence. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the opposing side. \\n41.2.1.3 \\nThe umpires together shall report the incident as soon as possible \\nafter the match to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing \\nBody responsible for the match, who shall take such action as is considered \\nappropriate against the captain, any other individuals concerned and, if \\nappropriate, the team. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"41.3 The match ball - changing its condition  \\n41.3.1 \\nThe umpires shall make frequent and irregular inspections of the ball. \\nIn addition, they shall immediately inspect the ball if they suspect anyone of \\nattempting to change the condition of the ball except as permitted in 41.3.2. \\n41.3.2 \\nIt is an offence for any player to take any action which changes the \\ncondition of the ball \\nExcept in carrying out his/her normal duties, a batsman is not allowed to \\nwilfully damage the ball. See also Law 5.5 (Damage to the ball). A fielder may, \\nhowever \\n41.3.2.1 \\npolish the ball on his/her clothing provided that no artificial \\nsubstance is used and that such polishing wastes no time.  \\n41.3.2.2 \\nremove mud from the ball under the supervision of an umpire. \\n41.3.2.3 \\ndry a wet ball on a piece of cloth that has been approved by \\nthe umpires.  \\n41.3.3 \\nThe umpires shall consider the condition of the ball to have been \\nunfairly changed if any action by any player does not comply with the \\nconditions in 41.3.2.  \\n41.3.4 \\nIf the umpires consider that the condition of the ball has been unfairly \\nchanged by a member or members of either side, they shall ask the captain of \\nthe opposing side if he/she would like the ball to be replaced. If necessary, in \\nthe case of the batting side, the batsmen at the wicket may deputise for their \\ncaptain. \\n41.3.4.1 \\nIf a replacement ball is requested, the umpires shall select and \\nbring into use immediately, a ball which shall have wear comparable \\nto that of the previous ball immediately prior to the contravention.  \\n41.3.4.2 \\nRegardless of whether a replacement ball has been chosen to \\nbe used, the bowler\'s end umpire shall  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the opposing side. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 if appropriate, inform the batsmen at the wicket and the captain of \\nthe fielding side that the ball has been changed and the reason for \\ntheir action. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the captain of the batting side as soon as practicable of what \\nhas occurred. \\n  \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible \\nafter the match to the Executive of the offending side and to any \\nGoverning Body responsible for the match, who shall take such action \\nas is considered appropriate against the captain any other individuals \\nconcerned and, if appropriate, the team.  \\n41.3.5 \\nIf the umpires agree that in the match there has been any further \\ninstance by that team of unfairly changing the condition of the ball, they shall \\n41.3.5.1 \\nrepeat the procedure in 41.3.4.1 and 413.4.2. If the further \\noffence is committed by the fielding side, additionally the bowler\'s \\nend umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 direct the captain of the fielding side to suspend immediately from \\nbowling the bowler who delivered the preceding ball; he/she shall not \\nbe allowed to bowl again in the match. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the batsmen at the wicket and, as soon as practicable, the \\ncaptain of the batting side of the reason for the action. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 if necessary, the over shall be completed by another bowler, who \\nshall neither have bowled any part of the previous over, nor be \\nallowed to bowl any part of the next over. \\n \\n41.4 Deliberate attempt to distract striker \\n41.4.1 \\nIt is unfair for any fielder deliberately to attempt to distract the striker \\nwhile he/she is preparing to receive or receiving a delivery, \\n41.4.2 \\nif either umpire considers that any action by a fielder is such an \\nattempt, he/she shall immediately call and signal Dead ball and inform the \\nother umpire of the reason for the call. The bowler\'s end umpire shall: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the batting side \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the captain of the fielding side, the batsmen and, as soon \\nas practicable, the captain of the batting side of the reason for the \\naction. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"  \\nNeither batsman shall be dismissed from that delivery and the ball shall not count as \\none of the over Together with the other umpire report the occurrence as soon as \\npossible after the match to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing \\nBody responsible for the match, who shall take such action as is considered \\nappropriate against the captain, any other individuals concerned and, if appropriate, \\nthe team \\n \\n41.5 Deliberate distraction, deception or obstruction of batsman \\n41.5.1 \\nIn addition to 41.4. it is unfair for any fielder wilfully to attempt, by \\nword or action to distract, deceive or obstruct either batsman after the \\nstriker has received the ball. \\n41.5.2 \\nIt is for either one of the umpires to decide whether any distraction, \\ndeception or obstruction is wilful or not. \\n41.5.3 \\nIf either umpire considers that a fielder has caused or attempted to \\ncause such a distraction, deception or obstruction, he/she shall immediately \\ncall and signal Dead ball and inform the other umpire of the reason for the \\ncall. \\n41.5.4 \\nNeither batsman shall be dismissed from that delivery. \\n41.5.5 \\nIf an obstruction involves physical contact the umpires together shall \\ndecide whether or not an offence under Law 42 (Players\' conduct) has been \\ncommitted. \\n41.5.5.1 \\nIf an offence under Law 42 (Players conduct) has been \\ncommitted, they shall apply the relevant procedures in Law 42 and \\nshall also apply each of 41.5.7 to 41.5.9. \\n41.5.5.2 \\nIf they consider that there has been no offence under Law 42 \\n(Players\' conduct) they shall apply each of 41.5.6 to 41.5.10. \\n41.5.6 \\nThe bowler\'s end umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the batting side.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the captain of the fielding side of the reason for this action and \\nas soon as practicable inform the captain of the batting side. \\n"'b'b"41.5.7 \\nThe ball shall not count as one of the over.  \\n41.5.8 \\nAny runs completed by the batsmen before the offence shall be \\nscored together with any runs for penalties awarded to either side. \\nAdditionally, the run in progress shall be scored whether or not the batsmen \\nhad already crossed at the instant of the offence.  \\n41.5.9 \\nThe batsmen at the wicket shall decide which of them is to face the \\nnext delivery. \\n41.5.10 \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible \\nafter the match to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing \\nBody responsible for the match, who shall take such action as is considered \\nappropriate against the captain, any other Individuals concerned and, if \\nappropriate, the team. \\n \\n41.6 Bowling of dangerous and unfair short pitched deliveries \\n41.6.1 \\nThe bowling of short pitched deliveries is dangerous if the bowler\'s \\nend umpire considers that, taking into consideration the skill of the striker, by \\ntheir speed, length, height and direction they are likely to inflict physical injury \\non him/her. The fact that the striker is wearing protective equipment shall be \\ndisregarded. \\n41.6.2 \\nThe bowler\'s end umpire may consider that the bowling of short \\npitched deliveries, although not dangerous under 416.1, is unfair if they \\nrepeatedly pass above head height of the striker standing upright at the \\ncrease. See also Law 21.10 (Ball bouncing over head height of striker) \\n41.6.3 \\nAs soon as the umpire decides that the bowling of short pitched \\ndeliveries has become dangerous under 41.61, or unfair under 41.6.2, he/she \\nshall call and signal No ball. When the ball is dead, the umpire shall caution \\nthe bowler, indicating that this is a first and final warning, and inform the \\nother umpire, the captain of the fielding side and the batsmen of what has \\noccurred. This caution shall apply to that bowler throughout the innings. \\n41.6.4 \\nShould there be any further such delivery by the same bowler in that \\ninnings, the umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 call and signal No ball \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 when the ball is dead, direct the captain of the fielding side to suspend \\nthe bowler immediately from bowling \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other umpire of the reason for this action The bowler thus \\nsuspended shall not be allowed to bowl again in that innings \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"If applicable, the over shall be completed by another bowler, who shall \\nneither have bowled any part of the previous over nor be allowed to bowl any \\npart of the next over. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The umpire shall report the occurrence to the batsmen and, as soon as \\npracticable, to the captain of the batting side. \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after the \\nmatch to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body \\nresponsible for the match, who shall take such action as is considered \\nappropriate against the captain, any other Individuals concerned and, if \\nappropriate the team. \\n \\n41.6.5 \\nThe warning sequence in 41.6.3 and 41.6.4 is independent of the \\nwarning and action sequence in 41.7. \\n \\n41.7 Bowling of dangerous and unfair non-pitching deliveries  \\n41.7.1 \\nAny delivery, which passes or would have passed without pitching \\nabove waist height of the striker standing upright at the popping crease, is \\nunfair. Whenever such a delivery is bowled, the umpire shall call and signal No \\nball. \\n41.7.2 \\nThe bowling of a delivery as defined in 41.7.1 is also dangerous if the \\nbowler\'s end umpire considers that there is a risk of injury to the striker in \\nmaking that judgment the umpire shall: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 disregard any protective equipment worn by the striker  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 be mindful of: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the speed, height and direction of the delivery \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the skill of the striker \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the repeated nature of such deliveries. \\n41.7.3 \\nIf the umpire considers a non-pitching delivery, or a series of non-\\npitching deliveries, to be dangerous under 41.7.2, when the ball is dead, the \\numpire shall repeat the No ball signal to the scorers and then caution the \\nbowler indicating that this is a first and final warning, The umpire shall also \\ninform the other umpire, the captain of the fielding side and the batsmen of \\nwhat has occurred. This caution shall apply to that bowler throughout the \\ninnings. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'41.7.4 \\nShould there be any further dangerous such delivery by the same \\nbowler in that innings, the umpire shall  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 call and signal No ball \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 when the ball is dead, direct the captain of the fielding side to suspend \\nthe bowler immediately from bowling  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other umpire of the reason for this action. \\nThe bowler thus suspended shall not be allowed to bowl again in that innings \\nIf applicable, the over shall be completed by another bowler who shall neither \\nhave bowled any part of the previous over, nor be allowed to bowl any part of \\nthe next over. \\nAdditionally, the umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 report the occurrence to the batsmen and, as soon as practicable, to \\nthe captain of the batting side. The umpires together shall report the \\noccurrence as soon as possible after the match to the Executive of the \\noffending side and to any Governing Body responsible for the match, \\nwho shall take such action as is considered appropriate against the \\ncaptain, any other individuals concerned and, if appropriate, the team. \\n41.7.5 \\nThe warning and action sequences in 41.7.3 and 41.74 are \\nindependent of those in 41.6. \\n41.7.6 \\nIf the umpire considers that a bowler deliberately bowled a non-\\npitching delivery, deemed to be unfair as defined in 41.7.1, then the caution \\nand warning in 41.7.3 shall be dispensed with. The umpire shall  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 immediately call and signal No ball \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 when the ball is dead, direct the captain of the fielding side to suspend \\nthe bowler immediately from bowling and inform the other umpire for \\nthe reason for this action. \\nThe bowler thus suspended shall not be allowed to bowl again in that innings. \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'If applicable, the over shall be completed by another bowler, who shall \\nneither have bowled any part of the previous over nor be allowed to bowl any \\npart of the net over. \\nAdditionally the umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 report the occurrence to the batsmen and, as soon as practicable, to \\nthe captain of the batting side. \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after the \\nmatch to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body \\nresponsible for the match, who shall take such action as S considered \\nappropriate against the captain, any other individuals concerned and \\nappropriate the team. \\n \\n41.8 Bowling of deliberate front-foot No ball \\n \\nIf the umpire considers that the bowler has delivered a deliberate front-foot No ball, he/she \\nshall  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 immediately call and signal No ball. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 when the ball is dead, direct the captain of the fielding side to suspend the bowler \\nimmediately from bowling  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other umpire of the reason for this action The bowler thus suspended \\nshall not be allowed to bowl again in that innings. \\nif applicable, the over shall be completed by another bowler, who shall neither have bowled \\nany part of the previous over, nor be allowed to bowl any part of the next over. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 report the occurrence to the batsmen and, as soon as practicable, to the captain of \\nthe batting side.  \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after the match to the \\nExecutive of the offending side and to any Governing Body responsible for the match, who \\nshall take such action as is considered appropriate against the captain, any other individuals \\nconcerned and, if appropriate, the team. \\n \\n41.9 Time wasting by the fielding side \\n41.9.1 It is unfair for any fielder to waste time. \\n41.9.2 If either umpire considers that the progress of an over is unnecessarily slow, \\nor time is being wasted in any other way, by the captain of the fielding side or by \\nany other fielder, at the first instance the umpire concerned shall  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 if the ball is in play, call and signal Dead ball  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other umpire of what has occurred. \\n \\n'"b'b"The bowler\'s end umpire shall then  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 warn the captain of the fielding side, indicating that this is a first and final \\nwarning. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the batsmen of what has occurred. \\n41.9.3 If either umpire considers that there is any further waste of time in that \\ninnings by any fielder, the umpire concerned shall \\n41.9.4 if the ball is in play, call and signal Dead ball. \\n41.9.5 inform the other umpire of what has occurred. The bowler\'s end umpire shall \\nthen  \\neither,    if the waste of time is not during an over, award 5 Penalty runs to the \\nbatting side and inform the captain of the fielding side of the reason for this \\naction  \\nor             if the waste of time is during the course of an over, direct the captain of \\nthe fielding side to suspend the bowler immediately from bowling. \\n \\nThe bowler thus suspended shall not be allowed to bowl again in that innings.  \\nIf applicable, the over shall be completed by another bowler, who shall neither \\nhave bowled any part of the previous over, nor be allowed to bowl any part of \\nthe next over. \\nAdditionally the umpire shall inform the batsmen and, as soon as is practicable, \\nthe captain of the batting side of what has occurred. \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after the \\nmatch to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body \\nresponsible for the match, who shall take such action as is considered \\nappropriate against the captain, any other individuals concerned and, if \\nappropriate, the team. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'41.10 Batsman wasting time \\n41.10.1 It is unfair for a batsman to waste time. In normal circumstances, the striker \\nshould always be ready to take strike when the bowler is ready to start his/her \\nrun-up. \\n41.10.2 Should either batsman waste time by failing to meet this requirement, or in \\nany other way, the following procedure shall be adopted. At the first instance, \\neither before the bowler starts his/her run-up or when the ball becomes dead, as \\nappropriate, the umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 warn both batsmen and indicate that this is a first and final warning. This \\nwarning shall apply throughout the innings. The umpire shall so inform \\neach incoming batsman. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other umpire of what has occurred.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the captain of the fielding side and, as soon as practicable, the \\ncaptain of the batting side of what has occurred. \\n41.10.3 If there is any further time wasting by any batsman in that innings, the \\numpire shall, at the appropriate time while the ball is dead \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the fielding side.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other umpire of the reason for this action. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other batsman, the captain of the fielding side and, as soon as \\npracticable, the captain of the batting side of what has occurred. \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after the \\nmatch to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body \\nresponsible for the match, who shall take such action as is considered \\nappropriate against the captain any other Individuals concerned and, if \\nappropriate, the team. \\n \\n41.11 The protected area \\n \\nThe protected area is defined as that area of the pitch contained within a rectangle bounded \\nat each end by imaginary lines parallel to the popping creases and 5 ft/ 1.52 m in front of \\neach, and on the sides by imaginary lines, one each side of the imaginary line joining the \\ncentres of the two middle stumps, each parallel to it and 1 ft/30.48 cm from it. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"41.12 Fielder damaging the pitch \\n41.12.1 \\nIt is unfair to cause deliberate or avoidable damage to the pitch A \\nfielder will be deemed to be causing avoidable damage if either umpire \\nconsiders that his/her presence on the pitch is without reasonable cause. \\n41.12.2 \\nif a fielder causes avoidable damage to the pitch other than as in \\n41131, at the first instance the umpire seeing the contravention shall. when \\nthe ball is dead, inform the other umpire. The bowler\'s end umpire shall then \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 caution the captain of the fielding side and indicate that this is a first and \\nfinal warning. This warning shall apply throughout the innings. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the batsmen of what has occurred.  \\n41.12.3 \\n If, in that innings, there is any further instance of avoidable damage \\nto the pitch, by any fielder, the umpire seeing the contravention shall, when \\nthe ball is dead, inform the other umpire. The bowler\'s end umpire shall then \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 signal No ball or Wide to the scorers, if applicable  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the batting side. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award any other 5 run Penalty that is applicable  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the fielding captain of the reason for this action. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the batsmen and, as soon as practicable, the captain of the batting \\nside of what has occurred. \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after the \\nmatch to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body \\nresponsible for the match, who shall take such action as is considered \\nappropriate against the captain any other individuals concerned and, if \\nappropriate, the team. \\n \\n41.13 Bowler running on protected area \\n41.13.1 \\nIt is unfair for a bowler to enter the protected area in his/her follow-\\nthrough without reasonable cause, whether or not the ball is delivered. \\n41.13.2 \\nIf a bowler contravenes this Law, at the first instance and when the \\nball is dead, the umpire shall \\n"'b'b"\\xef\\x82\\xa7 caution the bowler and inform the other umpire of what has occurred. \\nThis caution shall apply to that bowler throughout the innings. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the captain of the fielding side and the batsmen of what has \\noccurred. \\n \\n41.13.3 \\n If in that innings, the same bowler again contravenes this Law, the \\numpire shall repeat the above procedure indicating that this is a final \\nwarning. This warning shall also apply throughout the innings.  \\n41.13.4 \\n If, in that innings, the same bowler contravenes this Law a third time, \\nwhen the ball is dead, the umpire shall, \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 direct the captain of the fielding side to suspend the bowler immediately \\nfrom bowling. If applicable, the over shall be completed by another \\nbowler, who shall neither have bowled any part of the previous over. nor \\nbe allowed to bowl any part of the next over. The bowler thus suspended \\nshall not be allowed to bowl again in that innings.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other umpire of the reason for this action. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the batsmen and, as soon as practicable, the captain of the batting \\nside of what has occurred. The umpires together shall report the \\noccurrence as soon as possible after the match to the Executive of the \\noffending side and to any Governing Body responsible for the match, who \\nshall take such action as is considered appropriate against the captain, \\nany other individuals concerned and, if appropriate, the team. \\n \\n41.14 Batsman damaging the pitch \\n41.14.1 \\nIt is unfair to cause deliberate or avoidable damage to the pitch. If the \\nstriker enters the protected area in playing or playing at the ball, he/she \\nmust move from it immediately thereafter. A batsman will be deemed to be \\ncausing avoidable damage if either umpire considers that his/her presence \\non the pitch is without reasonable cause. \\n41.14.2 \\nIf either batsman causes deliberate or avoidable damage to the pitch, \\nother than as in 4115, at the first instance the umpire seeing the \\ncontravention shall, when the ball is dead, inform the other umpire of the \\noccurrence. The bowler\'s end umpire shall then \\n"'b'b"\\xef\\x82\\xa7 warn both batsmen that the practice is unfair and indicate that this is a \\nfirst and final warning. This warning shall apply throughout the innings. \\nThe umpire shall so inform each incoming batsman.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the captain of the fielding side and, as soon as practicable, the \\ncaptain of the batting side of what has occurred. \\n41.14.3 \\n If there is any further instance of avoidable damage to the pitch by \\nany batsman in that innings, the umpire seeing the contravention shall, \\nwhen the ball is dead. Inform the other umpire of the occurrence. \\nThe bowler\'s end umpire shall  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 disallow all runs to the batting side \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 return any not out batsman to his/her original end  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 signal No ball or Wide to the scorers if applicable.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the fielding side. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award any other 5-run Penalty that is applicable except for Penalty runs \\nunder Law 28.3 (Protective helmets belonging to the fielding side). \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Inform the captain of the fielding side and, as soon as practicable, the \\ncaptain of the batting side of the reason for this action. \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after \\nthe match to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body \\nresponsible for the match, who shall take such action as is considered \\nappropriate against the captain, any other individuals concerned and, if \\nappropriate, the team. \\n \\n41.15 Striker in protected area \\n41.15.1 \\nThe striker shall not adopt a stance in the protected area or so close \\nto it that frequent encroachment is inevitable. The striker may mark a guard \\non the pitch provided that no mark is unreasonably close to the protected \\narea. \\n41.15.2 \\nIf there is breach of any of the conditions in 4115.1 by the striker, the \\numpire seeing the contravention shall, if the bowler has not entered his/her \\ndelivery stride, immediately call and signal Dead ball, otherwise, he/ she \\nshall wait until the ball is dead and then inform the other umpire of the \\noccurrence. \\n"'b'b"The bowler\'s end umpire shall then \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 warn the striker that the practice is unfair and indicate that this is a first \\nand final warning. This warning shall apply throughout the innings. The \\numpire shall so inform the non-striker and each incoming batsman.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Inform the captain of the fielding side and, as soon as practicable, the \\ncaptain of the batting side of what has occurred. \\n41.15.3 \\nIf there is any further breach of any of the conditions in 41.15.1 by \\nany batsman in that innings, the umpire seeing the contravention shall, if the \\nbowler has not entered his/her delivery stride, immediately call and signal \\nDead ball, otherwise, he/she shall wait until the ball is dead and then inform \\nthe other umpire of the occurrence.  \\nThe bowler\'s end umpire shall then  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 disallow all runs to the batting side  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 return any not out batsman to his/her original end  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 signal No ball or Wide to the scorers if applicable.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the fielding side.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award any other 5-run Penalty that is applicable except for Penalty \\nruns under Law 28.3 (Protective helmets belonging to the fielding \\nside). \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the captain of the fielding side and, as soon as practicable, the \\ncaptain of the batting side of the reason for this action. \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after \\nthe match to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body \\nresponsible for the match, who shall take such action as is considered \\nappropriate against the captain, any other individuals concerned and, if \\nappropriate the team. \\n \\n41.16 Non-striker leaving his/her ground early \\n41.16.1 \\nIf the non-striker is out of his/her ground at any time from the \\nmoment the bail comes into play until the instant when the bowler would \\nnormally have been expected to release the ball, the non-striker is liable to \\nbe Run out. In these circumstances, the non-striker will be out Run out if \\nhe/she is out of his/her ground when his/her wicket is put down by the \\nbowler throwing the ball at the stumps or by the bowler\'s hand holding the \\nball, whether or not the ball is subsequently delivered.  \\n41.16.2 \\nIf the ball is not delivered and there is an appeal,  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the umpire shall make his/her decision on the Run out. \\nIf it is not out, he/she shall call and signal Dead ball as soon as \\npossible. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the ball shall not count as one in the over. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"41.16.3 \\nIf the ball is delivered and there is an appeal, \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the umpire shall make his/her decision on the Run out.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If the non-striker is not dismissed, the ball remains in play and Law \\n21.6 (Bowler breaking wicket in delivering ball) shall apply. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If the non-striker is dismissed, the ball shall not count as one in the \\nover. \\n \\n41.17 Batsmen stealing a run \\n41.17.1 \\nIt is unfair for the batsmen to attempt to steal a run during the \\nbowler\'s run-up. Unless the bowler attempts to run out either batsman - see \\n41.16 and Law 214 (Bowler throwing towards striker\'s end before delivery) - \\nthe umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 call and signal Dead ball as soon as the batsmen cross in such an \\nattempt.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the other umpire of the reason for this action. \\nThe bowler\'s end umpire shall then  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 return the batsmen to their original ends. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the fielding side.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award any other 5 run Penalty that is applicable, except for Penalty \\nruns under Law 28.3 (Protective helmets belonging to the fielding \\nside). \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 inform the batsmen, the captain of the fielding side and, as soon as \\npracticable, the captain of the batting side, of the reason for this \\naction. \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible after \\nthe match to the Executive of the offending side and to any Governing Body \\nresponsible for the match, who shall take such action as is considered \\nappropriate against the captain, any other individuals concerned and, if \\nappropriate the team. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"41.18 Penalty runs \\n41.18.1 \\nWhen Penalty runs are awarded to either side, when the ball is dead \\nthe umpire shall signal the Penalty runs to the scorers. See Law 2.13 \\n(Signals). \\n41.18.2 \\nPenalty runs shall be awarded in each case where the Laws require \\nthe award, even it a result has already been achieved. See Law 16.6 (Winning \\nhit or extras) Note, however, that the restrictions on awarding Penalty runs, \\nin Laws 23 3 (Leg byes not to be awarded), 25.7 (Restriction on the striker\'s \\nrunner), 28.3 (Protective helmets belonging to the fielding side) and 34.4 \\n(Runs permitted from ball lawfully struck more than once) will apply. \\n41.18.3 \\nWhen 5 Penalty runs are awarded to the batting side,  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 they shall be scored as Penalty extras and shall be in addition to any \\nother penalties. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 they are awarded when the ball is dead and shall not be regarded as \\nruns scored from either the immediately preceding delivery or the \\nimmediately following delivery, and shall be in addition to any runs \\nfrom those deliveries. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the batsmen shall not change ends solely by reason of the 5-run \\npenalty. \\n41.18.4 \\nWhen 5 Penalty runs are awarded to the fielding side, they shall be \\nadded as Penalty extras to that side\'s total of runs in its most recently \\ncompleted innings If the fielding side has not completed an innings, the 5 \\nPenalty runs shall be added to the score in its next innings. \\n \\nIntroduction \\nLaw 41 is a wide-ranging set of clauses covering all manner of unfair play - in many ways it is \\nactually about half a dozen different Laws brought together in one place. \\nLaws 41.1 and 41.2 make clear the responsibility of the captains and the umpires. In \\nparticular, Law 41.2 instructs the umpires to intervene if they consider any action, not \\ncovered by the Laws, is unfair.  \\nThere are several other instances of \'unfairness\' specifically covered elsewhere in the Laws. \\nFor example, the batsmen attempting Leg byes when the striker has not attempted to play \\nthe ball, or avoid being hit by it, is acting unfairly, as is the wicket-keeper moving \\nsignificantly during the bowler\'s run up (unless the batsman has changed his/ her stance). \\nThese, however, are dealt with by \'on the spot penalties! There are just five cases in Laws 1 \\nto 40 in which more severe sanctions, including a report to the Governing Body, are to be \\nimposed.
