These are in Laws 18.5 (Deliberate short runs), 21.3.3 (Ball thrown or delivered \\nunderarm), 244 (Player returning without permission) - where the actual offence is touching \\nthe ball after coming on 26.4 (Practice on the Field Penalties for contravention) and 28.2 \\n(Fielding the ball). \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Law 41 covers 14 other unfair actions and additionally, in 41.2, makes provision for those \\nnot specifically covered in any Law. At first sight these 14, indeed all 18, may seem \\nconfusing, even overwhelming. However, they all conform to the same basic pattern, which \\nshould enable umpires to understand the detail more easily. \\nThere are four further instances of sanctions for unfair play in the Laws - those are to do \\nwith Player behaviour, and are covered under Law 42. Those will not be discussed here, but \\nwill be covered in detail under that Law. \\n \\nThe procedures for applying penalties \\nIn the Laws, there are some individual variations - whether there is an initial warning, \\nwhether 5 Penalty runs are to be awarded or the bowler is to be suspended from bowling \\nand so on- but the general pattern is always as follows:  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If the first step is to call & signal either No ball or Dead ball, the umpire to do this will \\nbe named and will be the one best placed to see the action, although in some cases \\nboth umpires will be equally qualified to make the call. Remember that \\xe2\\x80\\x98the umpire\\xe2\\x80\\x99 \\nalways means the bowler\'s end umpire.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 whichever umpire makes the call will inform the other umpire. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the appropriate action is then taken by the bowler\'s end umpire. The only exceptions \\nare unfairly changing the condition of the ball (Law 41.3), and any sanction under \\nLaw 42, where part of the action is to be by both umpires together. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 in addition to informing the other umpire immediately, the following are always to \\nbe informed: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the fielding captain; \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the batsmen at the wicket; \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the batting captain (at an appropriate time) \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the umpires together report the incident as soon as possible after the match to the \\nExecutive of the offending side and the Governing Body responsible for the match. \\nThe first of these will be the club committee or equivalent; the second will be the \\nLeague or other body that has set up the competition of which the match is part.  \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Notice that if the players are so misguided as to repeat the offence after warnings have \\nbeen given and final action taken, that final action will be repeated where appropriate for \\nevery further contravention. \\nLaw 41.18 sets out general points about the signalling awarding and recording of Penalty \\nruns. They apply in all cases, including the \'not unfair case of the ball hitting the helmet \\nplaced on the ground behind the wicket-keeper (but see restrictions under Law 18.6). Law \\n41.18 also allows for Penalty runs to be awarded when the final ball of the match has \\nbecome dead - the basis on which a win by Penalty runs (Law 16.6 and 16.7) can occur. The \\noffence prompting Penalty runs must, however, have occurred on the final delivery. If the \\numpires, walking off when the match is concluded, realise that the ball has sustained unfair \\ndamage. they cannot then award 5 Penalty runs retrospectively. If, however, on the last \\ndelivery of an innings a batsman, one warning having already been issued to that team, \\ncaused avoidable damage to the pitch, it would be acceptable to award the 5 Penalty runs \\nwhen the ball is dead (and disallow the runs scored), since that is when such awards are to \\nbe made in any case. \\nNote also from Law 41.18 that, although the penalty is an odd number of runs, the batsman \\ndo not change ends merely on that account. For example, suppose that the batsmen have \\nnearly completed two runs when the ball is thrown in and hits a fielder\'s helmet placed on \\nthe ground behind the wicket-keeper. The ball becomes dead. The batsmen having crossed \\non the second run count as having run 2. The penalty makes the score 7 in total, an odd \\nnumber. Nevertheless because of the 2 runs, they will be at the same ends as when the ball \\nwas delivered and will stay there. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Umpires must study the procedures to become familiar with the particular points of each \\none. However, MCC has also produced a \'Penalty runs card\' (see page 350), splitting the \\noffences between those with a warning and those with instant penalties. This card can \\nprove useful, and is small enough to be taken onto the field. Umpires should aim to know \\nthe information on this card thoroughly, using it only as an aid to memory. \\n \\nInforming players \\nWhen an incident in breach of Law 41, or the other Laws mentioned, has occurred, any of \\nthe players who have already been involved by being given a warning will not need telling \\nseparately but the fielding captain and batsmen are to be told at every occurrence of an \\noffence. \\nThis is not true of the batting captain. For an offence by a fielder, there is no need to inform \\nthe captain of the batting side until warnings have expired and final action is to be taken. \\nThe batting captain is to be told at every occurrence only if the offence is by a batsman. \\nHowever, play must never be held up in order to inform him/her (apart from for breaches \\nunder Law 42). The captain may, of course, be one of the batsmen currently at the wicket \\nand can be told immediately. Otherwise the umpire must take the first opportunity of a \\nbreak in play to inform the captain. In the case of a batsman transgressing, there may \\ntherefore be a gap in time before the captain can be told that a warning has been issued to \\nthe batting side. \\nWhen a batting offence which applies to the whole team (for example batsman damaging \\nthe pitch) has occurred and the batting captain is to be informed, the umpire is to also \\ninform each incoming batsman, even after the warning has been issued to the captain, to \\nensure that all understand that a warning is in force. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b' \\nLAWS OF CRICKET (2017 CODE 2ND EDITION - 2019) - PENALTY RUNS \\nLAW \\nNO WARNING \\nACTION \\n18.5^ \\nEither batsman deliberately \\nrunning short \\nNo runs to count + 5 Penalty runs + return batsmen to original ends + report* \\n24.4 \\nPlayer returning without \\npermission and coming into contact \\nwith the ball in play \\nBall becomes dead +5 Penalty runs + ball not to count as one of the over + runs \\ncompleted/run in progress if crossed to count + report* \\n28.2 \\nFielding the ball illegally \\nBall becomes dead + 5 Penalty runs + ball not to count as one of the over + runs \\ncompleted in progress if crossed to count +report* \\n28.3 \\nBall in play hitting helmet not being \\nworn \\nBall becomes dead + if allowable 5 Penalty runs + if allowable runs completed/in \\nprogress if crossed to count (no report) \\n \\n41.3^ \\nMatch ball - changing its condition \\nunfairly \\nUmpires consult + change ball if opposing captain wishes + 5 Penalty runs + report* \\nAny repetition in match by team \\nUmpires consult + change ball if opposing captain wishes +5 Penalty runs + if fielding \\noffence suspend bowler who bowled previous ball + report* \\n41.4^ \\nDeliberate attempt to distract \\nstriker preparing to receive or \\nreceiving a delivery \\nNo dismissal + 5 Penalty runs + ball not to count as one of the over + report* \\n41.5^ \\nDeliberate distraction, deception or \\nobstruction of batsman after striker \\nreceived delivery \\nNo dismissal + 5 Penalty runs + runs completed/in progress to count + batsman at \\nwicket decide which of them to face next delivery + ball not to count as one of the \\nover + report* \\n41.17 \\nBatsmen stealing a run \\n5 Penalty runs + no runs count + return batsmen to original ends + report* \\n42^ \\nLevel 2, 3 or 4 offence \\nAgree Level of offence. Call Time + summon and inform captain + 5 Penalty runs (+ \\ndirect captain to remove offending player if necessary) + call Play  + report* \\n*New Law or Law change in 2017 Code  \\n*Report to Executive and any Governing Body responsible for the match. N.B. 1 run penalty for a No ball or Wide always counts \\n \\nLAW \\nONE WARNING \\nACTION \\n26.4^ \\nIllegal practice on the field \\nWarn player\\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa0 + inform other umpire and both captains*** \\nRepetition by any player on the \\nsame team during the match \\n5 penalty runs + report* \\n41.2^ \\nUnfair actions not covered in Law \\nWarn the captain*** \\nRepetition by any member of side \\n5 penalty runs + report* \\n41.9 \\nTime wasting by fielding side \\nWarn captain** \\nAny repetition during that innings \\na. If not during an over - 5 Penalty runs + report* \\nb. If during an over- suspend bowler + report* \\n41.10 \\nBatsmen wasting time \\nWarn batsmen* \\nRepetition by any batsmen in that \\ninnings \\n5 Penalty runs + report* \\n41.12 \\nFielder causing avoidable damage \\nto pitch \\nWarn captain** \\nAny repetition during that innings \\n5 Penalty runs + report* \\n41.14 \\nBatsman causing avoidable damage \\nto pitch \\nWarn the batsman\\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa0 \\nRepetition by any batsman in that \\ninnings \\nNo runs to count + 5 Penalty runs + return batsmen to original ends + report* \\n41.15^ \\nStriker in protected area \\nWarn the batsman\\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa0 \\nRepetition by any batsman in that \\ninnings \\nNo runs to count + 5 Penalty runs + return batsmen to original ends + report* \\n42.2^ \\nLevel 1 offence \\nAgree offence. Call Time + summon and inform captain + first and final warning + call \\nPlay + report* \\nLevel 1 offence following any other \\noffence by that team \\nAgree offence. Call Time + summon and inform captain +  5 Penalty runs + call Play + \\nreport* \\n \\n*Report to Executive and any Governing Body responsible for the match . \\n**Warning applies to whole innings  \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa0Applies to the whole side for the rest of that innings. Inform each incoming batsman. \\n***Warning applies to whole team for rest of match.\\n'"b'b"Warnings \\nIf a batsman contravenes the Law, it is that batsman who is the one to be warned. If a \\nbowler commits a bowling offence, it is similarly, the bowler who is warned. If a fielder \\nother than the bowler commits an offence, or the bowler commits a fielding offence, it is \\nthe fielding side\'s captain who is to be warned. \\nThe only offence for which two warnings are issued is for a bowler running on the protected \\narea in the follow through, and is thus always the bowler\'s responsibility. The final penalty is \\nsuspension of that bowler. Umpires must ensure that they know which of the other offences \\nby the fielding side carry a 5-run Penalty and which lead to the suspension of the bowler. \\nThe offences committed by batsmen that can lead to an initial warning are illegal practice \\non the field, time wasting, causing avoidable damage to the pitch and taking guard in or too \\nclose to the protected area. Repetition can lead to cancellation of runs where appropriate, \\nand to the award of 5 Penalty runs to the fielding side. Note that when runs are cancelled, \\ni.e. disallowed, there is no award of the 5 Penalty runs if the ball hits the helmet on the \\nground behind the wicket keeper (see Law 18.2).  \\nIn every case, once a warning is issued it will remain in force for the remainder of the \\ninnings. \\n \\nBall not counting in the over \\nNot all offences relate to a particular delivery of the ball. For these, the question of the \\nvalidity of a delivery does not arise. In some of the others, No ball is to be called, so the \\ndelivery is not valid. For the rest, there is a general principle, based on the logic of the \\nsituation. If the fielding side offends after the ball is delivered and while the ball is in play, \\nthus possibly restricting the number of runs that the batsmen might have scored, then the \\ndelivery will not be a valid ball. This does not apply to the ball hitting a helmet on the \\nground behind the wicket-keeper, since this is not a direct unfair action by the fielding side. \\nThere the general principle enunciated in Law 20.6 applies; the delivery is a valid ball. Note, \\nhowever, that although a fielder causing unnecessary damage to the pitch could possibly be \\nput into this category, unless it also distracts or obstructs the batsmen under 41.5, it will not \\nrestrict the runs that can be scored from that delivery and the ball will count in the over.  \\nExcept for time wasting, where a particular delivery is not pertinent, when it is a batsman \\nwho has breached the Law, it would be unfair to reward him/her with a further chance to \\nscore. Thus, it will be a valid ball, counting in the over in all cases. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'The chart summarises the cases for fielders: \\nLAW \\nOFFENCE \\nBALL VALID \\n24.4 \\nReturning without permission* \\n \\nNO \\n21.3 \\nThrowing/Bowling underarm \\nNo ball called \\n26.4 \\nIllegal practice \\nNot applicable \\n28.2 \\nlegal fielding \\n \\nNO \\n41.3 \\nBall tampering \\nNot applicable \\n41.4 \\nDistracting striker \\nNO \\n41.5 \\nDistraction obstruction or deception of batsman \\nNO \\n41.9 \\nTime wasting \\nNot applicable \\n41.12 \\nPitch damage \\nYes. See note above \\n*Remember, it is not returning illegally that is the offence. \\nIt is then coming into contact with the ball. \\n'"b"b'Details of the offences \\nBefore looking at these, it should be noted that the statement in 41.1 is extremely \\nimportant. If there is any difficulty with the conduct of the game, as will also be seen under \\nall the breaches of Law 42, the umpires will act through the captain of the side, rather than \\nintervene to rebuke or discipline a player directly. The only direct contact with an individual \\nplayer is warning a batsman for an offence, or warning a bowler for one of the bowler only \\noffences. \\nThe offences in Law 41 fall under the headings: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 changing the condition of the ball \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 deliberate distraction, deception or obstruction of a batsman \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 dangerous and/or unfair bowling \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 time wasting \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 damaging the pitch \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 unfair running by the batsmen \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 unfair actions not thought to be within the Spirit of Cricket and not covered \\nelsewhere in the Laws \\n \\nChanging the condition of the ball \\nWear and tear on the ball is inevitable as it continually strikes the ground, is hit by the bat \\nand runs across the outfield. This wear must not be artificially assisted by any of the players. \\nThe Law makes it very clear what they are allowed to do, and what they are not allowed to \\ndo it is more often applied to fielders, but is equally important for batsmen. \\nThis Law used to stipulate specific actions that a player should not take. It now simply says \\nthat any action that is not allowed under 41.3.2 is deemed unfair. Umpires must therefore \\nbe vigilant of any action taken by either side in respect to the ball. \\nPolishing the ball by rubbing on the shirt, etc. is a time honoured ritual. It must be done \\nwithout the assistance of any artificial substance and must not waste time. The fielders can \\ndry and polish the ball on their own clothing - which can include shirt and trousers, etc. - but \\nthe umpire must watch that no artificial substances (including hair cream, lip salve, suntan \\nlotion or other such substances) are used. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Cleaning and drying the ball in wet or muddy conditions is permitted, but the umpire is to \\nwatch the cleaning process to ensure that nothing but cleaning is done. Removal of mud \\nfrom the ball by a fielder must also be done under the supervision of the umpire, whilst the \\nuse of sawdust on the ball, or rubbing the ball on the ground, even wiping it gently on wet \\ngrass, is not allowed. Any cloth provided by the fielders must have been approved by the \\numpires before being used. The well-equipped umpire will always have a suitable cloth of \\nhis/her own available on the field for cleaning and drying the ball. \\nOccasionally, in wet conditions, a fielder may think to pick the ball up, while it is in play, \\nusing a piece of cloth. This is not permitted - though their only intention may be to keep the \\nball dry, this is illegal fielding, as covered in Law 28.2. \\nThere are many actions a fielding side may take that an umpire might deem unfair - players \\noften come up with new ways to alter the condition of the ball. If these are deliberate, the \\numpires must intervene. A good, and common, example of this is throwing the ball in on the \\nbounce, particularly on the square or immediately surrounding area, which is likely to be \\nmore abrasive. Fielders are permitted to throw the ball to the wicket-keeper so that it \\nbounces first, but should not be allowed to deliberately scuff up the ball by persistently, and \\ndeliberately, throwing it in on the bounce. Umpires must use their best judgment to \\ndetermine when bouncing throws are fair, and part of the game, and when they are an \\nattempt to alter the condition of the ball. The distance of the throw, and the ability of the \\nfielders, will be relevant. \\nThere is also a growing practice known as \'loading\' the ball, which is not permitted. While \\nfielders are permitted to polish the ball using saliva and sweat, \'loading\' means applying \\nthese to one side of the ball and then not polishing it. This is unfair, and will change the \\ncondition of the ball, deliberately making it heavier on one side. It is not always easy for an \\numpire to know whether fielders are polishing or loading, but careful attention should be \\npaid to make sure that the condition of the ball is not being unfairly altered. \\nBatsmen are not allowed to wilfully damage the ball - perhaps by standing on it - but \\ndamage which might be done in the course of the ball being played, either going out of \\nshape or hitting a wall or path, is not considered wilful in this context. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"The umpires are required to make frequent and Irregular inspections. Both frequency and \\nirregularity are important, so that the players feel that the ball is under constant \\nsurveillance. That means umpires should not simply look at the ball at the end of every over, \\nbut instead make sure to take a look during overs, throughout both teams\' innings, to \\nensure fairness and visibility of inspections. Further opportunities for inspection are \\nprovided by the umpire taking the ball at the fall of each wicket and at each break in play as \\nrequired by Law 4. Some tell-tale signs to look for are: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the stitching of the seam still prominent after considerable use \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 disturbance of the corners where the quarters meet the seam \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 one side of the ball much rougher than the other, even allowing for the players \\nhaving polished only one side \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the bowler appearing suspiciously different, holding the ball out of sight in front of \\nhim/her, apparently in both hands, and looking down at it as he/she walks back to \\nhis/her mark. The striker\'s end umpire will be the one to see this.  \\nThe above list is far from exhaustive. Players continue to find new methods to alter the \\ncondition of the ball, and umpires must be vigilant. \\nIf either umpire has any suspicion that the ball is being mistreated, he/she should \\nimmediately inspect the ball together with the other umpire and see what change there has \\nbeen since the last inspection. If they agree that this is more than is consistent with the use \\nthe ball has had in that time, they will conclude that the relevant side has made unfair \\nchanges and will institute the procedure laid down in the rest of Law 41.3. It is particularly \\nworth noting that once the umpires have decided that the ball has been illegally tampered \\nwith, the penalties will apply but the ball is not necessarily changed. It is up to the captain of \\nthe side who did not offend to decide whether the ball should be changed and the Law \\nallows the batsmen at the wicket to act for their captain. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'The accusation of changing the condition of the match ball is a particularly emotive one in \\ncricket - it is seen as one of the most unsporting acts of cheating. Players, as noted above, \\nare permitted to attempt to keep all, or part of, the ball in a good condition, so there is \\nalways some grey area. Umpires should not be afraid to punish transgressions of this Law \\nwith 5 Penalty runs and reporting, as mandated in 41.3.4.2, and if the offence is repeated, \\nwith suspension of the bowler, as required by 41.3.5. Note that such suspension is for the \\nrest of the match, not just the innings. These sanctions reflect the severity of the offence, so \\numpires should not rush into punishment. By constantly checking the ball, and \\ncommunicating with the players, the umpires can make sure that cheating is punished, but \\nthat inadvertent degradation of the ball is not. (If the ball degrades without any unfair \\npractice, the umpires must still change it under Law 4.5, but the fielding side are not \\nsanctioned.) \\n \\nDeliberate distraction, deception or obstruction of a batsman \\nThe regrettable practice of fielders talking (or more aggressive utterances), the continual \\nlast minute movement of fielders and other such distractions could be designed to unsettle \\nthe striker. Any distraction, from within or outside the ground, while the striker is preparing \\nto receive or receiving a delivery is to bring an instant call and signal of Dead ball, as set out \\nin Law 20.4.2. If the umpire considers that this distraction is a result of behaviour by a \\nfielder that is a deliberate attempt to distract the striker, 41.4.2 sets out the procedure that \\nmust be followed. Remember because this is a deliberate attempt to distract, there are no \\nwarnings for the fielding side. \\nThere may be attempts at distraction, deception or obstruction of either batsman after the \\nstriker has received the ball. As discussed under Law 37, the umpire must decide whether or \\nnot a physical collision between a batsman and a fielder is accidental. If the umpire \\nconsiders, with his/her colleague if necessary, that it was wilful, it is not usually difficult to \\nidentify the perpetrator. If an attempt at obstruction or distraction is not physical but, \\nperhaps, by a sudden shout, it is more obvious that it was wilful and it will usually be clear \\nwho was responsible. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"If it is a deception, it can be difficult to be certain - this section of the Law was new in 2017, \\nand both players and umpires have had to adjust to it. The practice known as \'mock fielding\\xe2\\x80\\x99 \\nis one way (but not the only one) that a fielder might try to deceive a batsman. If a fielder \\nslides along the ground after the ball has passed them and pretends to have the ball in their \\nhand to prevent the batsmen running, when really they have missed it, that is a deception, \\nand is punished under this Law. If, however, a fielder running after the ball slides to the floor \\nto allow a team-mate to pick the ball up and throw it in over their head, that is acceptable. It \\nwill not always be easy for umpires to know which is which - but the act of pretending to \\nthrow the ball, after a slide and without the ball in hand, will be an indication and umpires \\nonly need to believe that an attempt to deceive the batsman has taken place to apply the \\nLaw. \\nFurther, some wicket-keepers have taken to pretending the ball is closer to them than it is, \\nin order to convince the batsman to dive to make their ground, when in fact the throw is to \\nthe other end or the ball is some distance away. This, too is deception, even though it is not \\nlikely to prevent a batsman scoring an extra run- it could lead to an unnecessary dive that \\ncauses injury. The wicket-keeper pretending to have taken the ball, which the striker has \\nmissed, and stopping the batsmen from running, when it is in fact half way to the boundary \\nis similarly deception and perhaps clearer to identify. However, if it is unclear for an umpire \\nto see, the umpires should consult and decide if the action appeared wilful and was an \\nattempt to deceive. \\nFor a deliberate attempt by a fielder to deceive, obstruct or distract a batsman, after the \\nstriker has completed receiving the ball, the penalties set out in 41.5 are six-fold: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Neither batsman can be dismissed. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 This Law is unique in that the run in progress will count, even if the batsmen have \\nnot crossed.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The delivery is not a valid ball, so the batting side get a further chance to score runs. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Five Penalty runs are awarded against the fielding side. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 The two batsmen also choose which of them will face the next delivery, this is \\nsomething the umpire should check that they know. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"\\xef\\x82\\xa7 There will be a report after the match. Since the umpire is to call and signal Dead ball \\nas soon as the attempt is made, it is not necessary to gauge how successful the \\nattempt might have been. It is making the attempt that breaches the Law.  \\n \\nDangerous and unfair bowling \\nThere are three types of delivery considered under this Law:  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the fast short pitched ball that rises sharply off the pitch (Law 41.6) \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the ball that without pitching reaches the striker at a dangerous height, above the \\nwaist (Law 41.7)  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 a deliberate front foot No ball (Law 41.8). \\nThey are collected here together under the one heading of dangerous and unfair bowling. \\nThe first two of these are firmly established in cricket\'s jargon as a \'bouncer\' and a \'beamer\' \\nrespectively. These terms are not used in the Law but are useful descriptions. \\nIn addition, included here is the short pitched ball, or any delivery that pitches and bounces \\nso that it passes or would have passed over the head of the striker, standing upright at the \\npopping crease. Since 2017 such deliveries have not been considered immediately \\ndangerous, but are unfair and are always called a No ball under Law 21.10. However, when \\nbowled too often, such deliveries can be considered and sanctioned as dangerous under \\n41.6. See below for further guidance. \\nIt must be emphasised that Playing Conditions in some high level matches, allowing a \\ncertain number of bouncers in an over, do not apply under Law, but only in those particular \\nmatches. \\n1. Bouncers (dangerous short-pitching deliveries)  \\nThe umpire does not have to read the bowler\'s intention What an umpire has to decide is \\nthe likelihood that the delivery could inflict injury on the striker ignoring the effect of \\nprotective equipment. A delivery at the striker\'s head is not to be considered harmless \\nbecause the striker is wearing a helmet. The umpire must decide that is a dangerous \\ndelivery, not \'how well protected the striker is\'. In making his/her assessment, the umpire \\nmust take into account: \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"\\xef\\x82\\xa7 length - was it short pitched? This fact can easily be observed.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 height - did it pass the striker knee high waist high, head high? The dangerous levels \\napply only to the striker standing upright at the crease. The striker may not be in this \\nposition. The umpire will have to judge from the flight of the ball what the situation \\nwould have been, had the striker been so positioned. If the ball would have hit the \\nstriker, upright at the crease, at upper rib level, at neck/shoulder level or on the \\nhead, it is \'high\' in this context. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 direction - was it travelling towards the striker\'s body? This is also an observable fact \\n- a ball at head height, but well wide of off stump, is not dangerous. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the skill of the striker - a really skilled batsman can score freely off such balls and will \\nenjoy doing so. A capable but less skilful batsman will be able to avoid injury but be \\nunable to score off them. Increasing frustration may eventually put that competent \\nplayer at risk. An inexperienced batsman will be in real danger: The umpire will have \\nto categorise the batsmen, whom he/she may not have seen before, by the way they \\nreact. A batsman\'s position in the batting order does not necessarily reflect their \\ncompetence. That is for the umpire to decide. \\nNote that the Law changed in 2017 so that repetition is not a necessary requirement for a \\nbouncer to be dangerous. One dangerous ball to a tail-ender is enough to be called No ball \\nand warned. However, although a competent batsman will probably be able to deal with \\nsome bouncers, receiving them continually is likely to unsettle even a very good player and \\nwear down the striker\'s resistance through frustration. \\nAs outlined above, there is a special point about the bouncer which passes over the striker\'s \\nhead. Clearly it is not likely to injure a batsman, because it cannot hit him/ her. \\nNevertheless, the Law recognises it as part of the attack upon a player\'s self-confidence and \\nas contributing to the increasing frustration at not being able to score. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Although such a ball could, in some ways and under some Playing Conditions), be \\nconsidered Wide, since it is out of the reach with a normal cricket shot, under the laws it is \\nto be called a No ball. A No ball call gives the striker extra protection from dismissal from \\nthis unfair delivery. Moreover, if the striker touches it, even inadvertently in trying to avoid \\nit, a Wide would be annulled, cancelling the one run penalty and replacement delivery, and \\nallowing the batsman potentially to be out Caught. Each such delivery, in addition to being a \\nNo ball, will be taken into consideration alongside bouncers towards the body, when \\ndeciding if they should be sanctioned, as follows. \\nWhen the umpire comes to the point that bouncers to a particular batsman are dangerous, \\nfor any of the above reasons, he/she will start and follow through the procedure in Law \\n41.6.3. The umpire should probably reach that point after the first bouncer bowled at the \\nbody of a batsman who is so inexperienced - perhaps a young player - that it is immediately \\nobvious the striker is unable to cope with it and is therefore in danger from the start. For \\nthe really skilful striker, scoring feely from such deliveries, the bowler may soon realise that \\nbowling bouncers is counter-productive and will stop doing so of his/her own accord. \\nHowever there may still come a time when the umpire feels this bowling has become unfair \\neven for an experienced batsman. Between these two extremes, for an ordinarily \\ncompetent or a fairly inexperienced batsman, the umpire must judge when the bowling is \\nbeginning to become dangerous to the striker. \\n \\nNotice that the sequence\' is for a particular bowler against the same batsman. That means \\nthe umpires must keep track of that particular bowler-batsman pair, if the bowler changes \\nends. A bowler who does so will carry his/ her record of bouncers to that batsman, \\ndangerous or over head-height, to the new end. However, if the umpire feels that bouncers \\nbeing bowled to a batsman are unfair because of his/her lack of skill, or have become unfair \\ndue to that batsman being worn down by persistent short bowling, then this will apply even \\nto a new bowler. This might be an over head height bouncer if they have become \\nunreasonable. If a delivery is dangerous to a batsman, it is dangerous whoever is bowling. A \\nsimilar delivery from a new bowler will still result in an immediate warning. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Once the warning procedure has started, that bowler will be suspended if he/she bowls a \\nfurther delivery to any batsman that the umpire considers is dangerous or unfair.  \\nReporting of the suspended bowler together with the captain will of course follow. \\n \\n2. Beamers (high non-pitching deliveries)  \\nEvery beamer is to be considered unfair - non-pitching deliveries above waist height are No \\nballs and 41.7.1 requires that they should always be called as such. \\nAssuming a non-pitching delivery is accidental, the umpire has two matters to take into \\nconsideration: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 first, the umpire must consider the height at which the ball would have passed the \\nstriker standing upright at the crease. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 secondly the umpire must decide if the delivery is dangerous. \\nThe ball may be difficult to judge for height if it is dropping as it reaches the striker. If the \\nstriker tries to play it well in front of the body, or has perhaps stepped down the pitch, it will \\nbe difficult to know how far it would have dropped by the time it reached the popping \\ncrease. An enquiring look from the bowler\'s end umpire to his/ her colleague, answered by a \\npre-agreed signal from the striker\'s end umpire, can be extremely helpful. However, it \\nshould be noted that it is still for the bowler\'s end umpire to make the decision. \\nIf the decision is taken that the delivery was above waist height, then it is a beamer and \\nunfair. The umpire will immediately call and signal No ball, whatever else he/she decides. \\nThen the umpire must make the second decision, on whether the delivery is dangerous. This \\nis not as straightforward as simply judging the pace and direction of the ball. Of course, \\nfaster deliveries are more likely to be dangerous, but there are many other considerations, \\nnot least the context of the delivery. For example, against a fast bowler, the batsman, \\nanticipating quick, short-pitched deliveries, might have his/her attention fixed on the pitch. \\nAn attempted slower-ball that loops out of the bowler\'s hand and hits the batsman on the \\nhead might be extremely dangerous, despite being slow, because it is so unexpected that \\nthe batsman does not \'pick it up\' out of the hand. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'That same looping delivery, bowled by a spinner who is constantly flighting the ball, comes \\nwithout the element of surprise and might not be dangerous. \\nSimilarly, deliveries directed at the batsman are more likely to be dangerous than those \\nwhich are wide of him/ her, but this is not a hard and fast rule in determining danger. An \\numpire may very well consider that a fast bowler who bowls one ball fast, high and well \\nwide of the batsman on the off side, followed by one that is fast, high and well wide of the \\nbatsman on the leg side, is becoming dangerous. The third such ball may be straight, and \\ncould be seriously dangerous to the batsman, and the bowler is clearly out of control. The \\nsecond such ball might, therefore, be considered dangerous in the eyes of the umpire even \\nif the first, in isolation, was not. \\nAnother factor to be considered is repetition. As with short-pitched bowling, even a very \\nskilful player might be worn down by a barrage of beamers. A batsman who is constantly \\nswatting such deliveries for six should not be disadvantaged with the removal of the \\nwayward bowler, but once it is clear that, due to their repetition, the deliveries have \\nbecome dangerous, the umpire should begin the warning process. \\nWhen deciding if a delivery is dangerous, the umpire must also consider the skill of the \\nstriker. A less skilful batsman may not be able to cope with the same deliveries as well as a \\nbetter batsman. \\nThe first time that the umpire identifies any delivery as dangerous, the warning procedure in \\n41.7.3 must be followed. Unlike for bouncers, once a bowler has been warned for a beamer, \\nthe process continues against any batsman. If that bowler bowls a second beamer at any \\nbatsman, that is enough for them to be suspended from bowling for the rest of the innings \\nunder 41.7.4. \\nHowever, the judgment of bouncers or beamers by any individual bowler and the associated \\nwarning and action procedures are separate. \\nFinally, if the umpire considers that a beamer was not an accident, but deliberately bowled, \\nthere is no warning. The captain must immediately remove the bowler from the attack, for \\nthe rest of the innings, at the direction of the umpire. This is a very serious offence and the \\numpires must decide if the bowling of such a delivery is a deliberate act. Context should also \\nbe helpful in determining that the delivery was deliberate. Such offences are thankfully very \\nrare and unlikely to happen without some indication of trouble beforehand. If the umpires \\nrealise there is bad feeling in the match, they should both watch and listen carefully for any \\nsigns of trouble and involve the captain of the relevant teams to prevent escalation to this \\nlevel if at all possible. Law 42 may well assist in this, as will be covered later: \\nReporting of a bowler and the captain for any suspension will of course follow. \\n \\n'"b'b"Deliberate front foot No ball \\nA new section has been added to the area of this Law covering dangerous bowling. Whereas \\nan accidental front foot No ball is penalised under Law 21, if a bowler deliberately over-\\nsteps - that is, deliberately bowls at a batsman from closer than the popping crease - then it \\nis likely to be dangerous. This is sometimes known as running through the crease\' and is \\noften borne out of frustration and as an attempt to unsettle the striker. There is no set \\ndistance to define this - the umpire must use his/ her judgment, based on the context of \\npreceding events, to decide if the over-stepping was done deliberately. The bowler is \\npenalised not only with a No ball, but this is considered a serious offence and the bowler is \\nalso immediately suspended from the attack in the same way as for a deliberate beamer. \\n \\nSuspending a bowler \\n \\nThere are several occasions in the Laws, most notably under dangerous and unfair bowling, \\nwhere a bowler needs to be suspended, either for the rest of the innings or, in the case of \\n41.3, for the rest of the match. There is a set procedure for any such suspensions, which \\numpires must be careful to follow: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 call and signal No ball (if required) \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 when the ball is dead, the umpire will direct the captain of the fielding side to \\nsuspend the bowler immediately from bowling. Note the umpires themselves never \\nsuspend a bowler \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 that bowler shall not be allowed to bowl again (either in the innings or the rest of \\nthe match, depending on the offence)  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the umpire then informs the other umpire of the reason for the suspension \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the umpire then informs the batsmen at the crease \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 as soon as practical, the umpire informs the batting captain \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If necessary, the over will be completed by another bowler, who had not bowled any \\npart of the previous over, nor will be allowed to bowl any part of the next over. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Time wasting \\n \\nIn recent times the rate of play has generally become slower, with over rates falling \\nnoticeably, so it is important for the umpires to remind captains of their responsibilities to \\nkeep play moving. Some general guidance on this has been covered in Part 1 of this book, \\nparticularly regarding discussions at the pre-match meeting with the captains. However, \\nthere will be times when umpires need more than just a quiet word to keep the game \\nmoving, because players are wasting time deliberately. These are addressed in 41.9. \\n \\n1. By fielders \\n \\nThis is usually a team activity rather than one individual. It is difficult to detect because the \\ncontributory factors, such as a conference between captain and bowler, fine adjustments of \\nthe field, the ball being returned to the bowler in a number of steps rather than in one big \\nthrow, and so on, are all legitimate. The umpire has to judge when they are being taken too \\nfar. It does not matter whether the slowness is a deliberate ploy to slow down play in the \\nhope of influencing the outcome of the game, or not. What matters is whether the \\nparticular activity is necessary and, if so, whether the time spent on it is more than is \\nreasonable. \\nThere are certain obvious points. A bowler with a long run up who waits until the ball is \\nreturned to him before starting the walk back to his/her mark is wasting time; another \\nfielder could be gathering the ball while the bowler is walking back. A captain who arranges \\nthat the same fielder is at deep fine leg at both ends is likely to be wasting time (sometimes, \\nespecially in T20 cricket, a captain may wish to position a specific fielder in a specific \\nposition - within reason, that is fair, as long as the fielder runs between the positions at the \\nend of each over). On the other hand, if a right handed and a left handed batsman are in at \\nthe same time, running a lot of singles, so that the sight-screen has to be moved virtually \\nevery ball, then slow progress is inevitable. This is something the umpires can discuss with \\nthe captains before the match so that someone is stationed by the screen so that moving it \\ncan be done with minimum delay. The umpire has to judge the situation and, whilst either \\numpire can intervene, if it is felt the timewasting has reached a point of sanction, it would \\nbe wise to obtain the agreement of his/her colleague before embarking on the punitive \\nmeasures for time wasting laid down in 41.9. Notice the specific instance of time wasting \\nwhile practising on the field. (See Law 26). \\n \\n'"b"b'The umpires must be clear when the timewasting takes place as following an initial but final \\nwarning, the Law differentiates between further timewasting not during an over, when 5 \\nPenalty runs are awarded, and during an over when the more serious sanction of the \\nsuspension of the bowler takes place (even if it was not the bowler who was wasting time). \\nIn both situations, reporting is required and so the umpires will need to have clear notes on \\nthe events that led to these sanctions. \\n \\n2. By batsmen \\n \\nAn over-riding principle is that a batsman should, within reason, be ready to face the ball by \\nthe time the bowler is ready to begin his/her run-up. Whilst no batsman should be \\npermitted to significantly delay the game by not being ready at this point under normal \\ncircumstances, the batsman is entitled to check his/her guard and look around to see where \\nthe fielders are. \\nBatsmen may need to re-tie their shoelaces, which will involve the removal and then \\nreplacement of gloves. It is reasonable for the striker to prod down a small divot on the \\npitch. If the field is being re-set, or the ball retrieved from beyond the boundary, a short \\nmid-pitch (not actually on the pitch!) discussion with the other batsman about strategy may \\nbe valid. \\nThe bringing on to the field of replacement gloves, a change of bat, or a cap for a helmet \\nmay well be reasonable, but the umpire has to judge when all these things are tactics to \\nwaste time. A check that the other umpire takes a similar view is wise. \\nSince many of the activities by which a batsman can waste time will happen before the ball \\ncomes into play, 41.10.2 makes provision for the umpire to award the 5 Penalty runs before \\nthe delivery, if that is appropriate The ball must of course be dead. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Field technique \\nNote that the umpires will obviously never want to waste time - however, they must be \\nvigilant not to delay the game unintentionally. This is crucial not only because to do so \\nwould be unfair, but also because if umpires are seen to be acting slowly it will reduce their \\ncredibility should they wish to enforce the Laws of timewasting later in the game. \\nIf an umpire does not need to switch sides when left and right handers are taking singles in \\norder to apply the Law, then he/she should not do so (particularly if the bowler is a spinner \\ntrying to move the game along quickly). That is not to say that it will never be necessary to \\nchange sides -it may well be as covered in positioning under Part 1 - but wherever possible it \\nshould not be the umpires delaying play. \\n \\nDamage to the pitch \\n \\nIt is unfair to cause deliberate or avoidable damage to the pitch. A player will be treated as \\ncausing avoidable damage if his/her presence on the pitch is without reasonable cause. \\nIt is not always realised that merely walking gently and calmly on the pitch - and not only \\nthe protected area but the whole pitch - will cause damage. Of course, in that case it will be \\nslight, but such slight damage will build up over time. The umpires are therefore to consider \\nthat a player is causing avoidable damage simply if he/she is on the pitch unnecessarily.  \\nThe umpires do not have to wait for damage to be visible. \'Avoidable\' and \'unnecessary\' are \\nimportant words. Of course, fielders have to be on the pitch if that is where the ball has to \\nbe fielded. The striker will actually stand on the pitch when waiting to receive a delivery and \\nwill certainly move on it in playing the ball. If he/she then runs, it is impossible to \\nimmediately get off the pitch from the point at which the ball was played. All this is \\nunavoidable damage. What is not allowed is fielders and batsmen being on the pitch \\nwithout reasonable cause. \\nThere are three cases of damage to the pitch under this Law. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"1. Bowler running on to the protected area \\nLaw 41.11 defines the protected area. Law 41.13 specifies that the bowler is not to run on \\nthis in following through, whether the ball has been delivered or not. The offence here is \\nnot in itself damaging the pitch, although that will be a consequence, it is running on the \\nprotected area in the follow-through. It should be noted that the bowler is not prevented \\nfrom entering the protected area to field the ball. It is only unnecessary running on the \\nprotected area that is prohibited. \\nThe umpire, however, must not divert attention away from the flight of the ball to watch \\nthe bowler\'s follow through. He/she will become aware of the bowler\'s position if it is not \\nfar enough out, since it will then be close to the umpire\'s own line of vision, watching the \\nball. The bowler may actually get in the way of that line of vision. Indeed, some slow \\nbowlers may finish their follow-through short of the protected area, and thus take up a \\nposition right in front of the umpire. This is not illegal, but it will make it difficult for an \\numpire to make certain decisions if their view is impeded, and is thus potentially harmful to \\nthe bowler. \\nIt will help with this judgment, if the umpire is standing back from the stumps at a distance \\nthat means the line of sight over the top of the stumps intersects the pitch at the exact start \\nof the protected area. This will not always be possible, depending on the line the bowler is \\ntaking, but where it is, it makes it far easier to judge if the bowler is encroaching into the \\nprotected area. \\nThis is a bowler only offence and there is an extra warning. The first is a caution, the second \\na final warning, These are issued to the bowler in person rather than the fielding captain, \\nalthough he/she along with the other umpire are informed of the offence. A third \\ntransgression results not in Penalty runs, but suspension from bowling for the rest of the \\ninnings, with the associated reporting of bowler and captain. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b" \\n \\n2. By fielders \\nHere it is not merely the protected area but the whole pitch that the fielders (including the \\nwicket-keeper) must not damage beyond what is unavoidable for necessary fielding action \\nLaw 41.12 sets out the details. An umpire-it can be either umpire who sees the action - will \\nhave to exercise considerable judgment in deciding whether a fierce turning on the heel is \\ndone in a genuine attempt to field a difficult ball or has been unnecessarily exaggerated in \\nthe hope of scarring the pitch. \\nThere is a mistaken belief by some players that if they have to cross from one side to the \\nother at the change of over, they will avoid damaging the pitch by jumping over it. Alas, this \\nis not so. If a fielder is near one end, then they should walk round behind the stumps. It is \\nalways better for fielders to walk around the pitch rather than across it However, if this is \\ngoing to waste time during an over, it is certainly better to walk gently across than trying to \\njump. There the fielder would do more damage by landing from a jump, since it will \\ninevitably be short of the 10 feet needed to clear the pitch. Crossing in these circumstances \\ncould be described as \'reasonable cause\\xe2\\x80\\x99. \\nRemember that the bowler is also a fielder and will also come under the provisions of this \\nsection of Law. A bowler could also be guilty, as a fielder, of damaging the pitch. certainly \\nafter the follow-through, but also during it, if either umpire considers he/she is deliberately \\nexaggerating his/her foot movements. It would be wise in this case for the umpire to check \\nthat his/her colleague shared the same opinion. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"If the umpire considers a member of the fielding side has breached this Law then they \\nshould caution the captain and, as this is a team offence, if there is any further breach by \\nany member of that side then, when the ball is dead, the procedure and sanctions in 41.12.3 \\nshould be applied. \\n \\n3. By batsmen \\nAs explained above, the striker cannot avoid standing and moving on the part of the pitch in \\nthe vicinity of his crease. He/she must, however, get off the pitch as quickly as possible if \\nattempting a run, and if the striker does enter the protected area in playing a shot, must \\nleave it as soon as the shot is complete. \\nThe introduction of Law 41.15, specifically prohibiting a striker from taking a guard within \\nthe protected area, or so close that encroachment is inevitable in playing at the ball, should \\nlimit the occasions when batsmen transgress. \\nNeither 41.14 nor 41.15 prevent the striker from stepping down the pitch, and going into \\nthe protected area, to play a particular ball. It is for either umpire to intervene if the striker \\ntakes up a position close to 5 feet in front of the popping crease, where the protected area \\nstarts, but it is likely that the striker\'s end umpire will be better sighted to judge. \\nIf the striker is in an illegal position during the bowler\'s run-up, Dead ball should be called \\nbefore the bowler enters the delivery stride. If the striker moves into such a position after \\nthe delivery stride has been entered the umpire will have to decide if it was in the course of \\nplaying the ball. If so, it is allowable but if not, the warning procedure will occur once the \\nball is dead, thereby giving the fielding side the chance of a dismissal. The initial \\ncontravention will bring a warning but repetition by any batsman during that innings will \\nresult in disallowance of runs and the award of 5 Penalty runs, as well as reporting of player \\nand captain. After the first warning, each incoming batsman must be informed. \\nThere is a tendency by the striker to run diagonally across the pitch in order to be in the \\nnormal position for a non-striker at the end of the run. That is illegal under Law 41.14, as it \\nis causing unnecessary damage to the pitch. Moreover, if the umpire forms the opinion that \\nit was done deliberately, possibly to impede fielding by the bowler or worse, to come \\nbetween the ball and either a fielder or the wicket itself, this would additionally be a case of \\nObstructing the field. See Law 37. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"The procedure laid down in Laws 41.14 and 41.15 are the same, but separate. Each allows \\nonly one warning before action but they are not aggregated so it is important for umpires to \\nbe sure under which Law the warning has been given. The warning lasts throughout the \\ninnings and will apply to any batsman. Although the batting captain is to be told at the first \\noffence, since it is a batsman who has transgressed, there may not be a suitable opportunity \\nto tell him/her for some time. Even if the captain has been informed, there is no guarantee \\nthat he/she has told the other batsmen. The umpire is therefore to tell each incoming \\nbatsman, even after the batting captain has been informed. If any batsman from that team \\ntransgresses again in that innings then the procedures under 41.14.3 or 41.15.3. as \\nappropriate, require that any runs are disallowed (though relevant penalties will be allowed) \\nand the batsmen are returned to their original ends. \\nSadly, there have been instances of tail-end batsmen deliberately ignoring the need to run \\noff the pitch, and even making unnecessarily fierce movements in playing at the ball, to \\nroughen the pitch in the hope of assisting their own bowlers in the next innings. Umpires \\nshould be aware of such practices. The non-striker has no need to be on the pitch at all. \\nThe question of wilfulness does not arise. The batsmen should not be on the pitch except as \\ndescribed above. However, if damage is wilful, that is more serious - and that will be \\nreflected in the umpires\' report after the match. \\n \\nUnfair running by batsmen \\n1. Non-striker leaving his her ground early \\nAlthough it is quite a different situation and there is no procedure towards any penalty, we \\ncan consider the non striker who leaves his/her ground too soon under the heading of \\n\\xe2\\x80\\x98unfair running by the batsmen\\xe2\\x80\\x99. \\n \\nThe Law compensates the fielding side for any advantage that the batsman may get from \\nnot being within his/her ground, by making the non-striker liable to being run out. From the \\nmoment the ball comes into play right up to the moment the bowler would normally release \\nthe ball, the non-striker is liable to be Run out by the bowler. Consequently, unless the non-\\nstriker remains within his/her ground until that moment, there is a risk of being dismissed. \\nThe umpire will have little warning that a run out is to be attempted and will certainly not \\nbe able to move round to square with the wicket and crease. However, it is usually fairly \\nobvious, given that the batsman is moving away from, rather than towards, the popping \\ncrease. \\n \\n"'b"b'There are those who feel that a bowler should not actually make the attempt on the first \\ntransgression, but should give a warning by stopping and indicating that he/ she could have \\nrun the non-striker out. This is a traditional courtesy, but is not obligatory. A non-striker, by \\nleaving early, is attempting to gain an advantage - it is they who are cheating, not the \\nbowler. Umpires should not be seen to discourage a bowler from fairly running out a \\nbatsman who does so. \\nIf the bowler does attempt a run out, either with the ball in hand or by throwing the ball at \\nthe stumps, and the attempt succeeds, then of course the non-striker is out. Should it fail, \\nthe umpire will call and signal Dead ball to ensure that no runs can accrue to the batting \\nside, nor can there be further action by the fielding side. If not clear, It will be helpful to the \\nscorers to tell them, as soon as practicable, what has happened. Whether the attempt at the \\nRun out is successful or not, the ball does not count as one in the over. \\nIt is rare, but possible, for a bowler to put down the wicket accidentally with the hand \\nholding the ball. Sometimes the ball is delivered and sometimes it is not. If the non-striker is \\nout of his/her ground when this happens, regardless of whether the ball is delivered or not, \\nhe/she will be Run out, even though the bowler has not made any deliberate attempt to do \\nso. If the non-striker is dismissed, the ball becomes dead as soon as the wicket is put down, \\nso it is not a No ball under Law 21.6 (Bowler breaking wicket in delivering the ball). If \\nhowever, the non-striker is not given out as above or the wicket is put down with anything \\nother than the ball, or the hand holding the ball, then 41.16 cannot apply and the non-\\nstriker is not out, but, if the ball is still delivered, it remains in play and would be a No ball. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"2. Batsmen stealing a run. \\nAlthough the ball is in play as soon as the bowler starts his/her run up, the batsmen are not \\npermitted to attempt runs until the ball is delivered. It should not be confused with the \\nprevious situation. It is not a case of one batsman leaving his/her ground too soon. To be \\ntrying to steal a run, both batsmen must be trying to cross and make good their ground \\nfrom end to end - this is a Law which is almost never broken, but, were it not to exist, the \\npractice could become common. Law 41.17 sets out the procedure It should be noted that \\nthe umpire must wait until the batsmen have crossed to call Dead ball. This ensures that: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 both batsmen are involved, confirming that a run is being attempted; \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the bowler has not taken up the option of trying to run out either batsman. If he/she \\nhad done so, before the batsman have crossed, action under 41.17 would not be \\nrequired. \\n \\nUnacceptable behaviour in ways not listed in the Laws  \\nLaw 41.2 empowers either umpire to call Dead ball and thus halt play, if he/she sees any act \\nwhich is considered unfair, even though it is not one of the misdemeanours specifically \\nstated in the Laws. It also outlines the procedure which then applies. \\nNote that 41.2 only applies to actions by players not covered by the Laws and, thus does not \\napply to incidences of players\' misconduct which would be covered by Law 42. As stated, \\nthis section of Law is another where either umpire can intervene if they consider an action \\nby any player is not covered by the Laws. The umpire is guided to delay that intervention if \\nthe non-offending side could be deprived of an advantage, such as taking a wicket or scoring \\nruns, particularly a boundary. Equally if the non-offending side could be disadvantaged by \\nlosing a wicket or having runs scored against them then an early call of Dead ball should be \\nmade. \\nThe first and final warning issued initially, is for the whole of the team for the rest of the \\nmatch and the captain is warned that a further offence will result in 5 Penalty runs, as will \\nany subsequent offences by that team at any time throughout the match. It should be noted \\nthat such a second offence is restricted to cases under this Law meaning, for example, that a \\nwarning under 41.2 followed by a first warning for damaging the pitch would not result in 5 \\nPenalty runs. \\nThe captain and player or players involved will be reported for any actions considered in \\nbreach of 41.2. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"LAW 42 PLAYERS\' CONDUCT \\n42.1 Unacceptable conduct \\n41.1.1 \\nThe umpires shall act upon any unacceptable conduct Four Levels of offence \\nand the corresponding actions by the umpires are identified as Level 1, Level 2, Level \\n3 and Level 4 offences in 42.2 to 42.5. \\n41.1.2 \\nIf either umpire considers that the conduct of a player at any time during the \\nmatch is unacceptable, the umpire concerned shall call and signal Dead ball This call \\nmay be delayed until the umpire is satisfied that i will not disadvantage the non-\\noffending side. \\n41.1.3 \\nThe umpire concerned shall report the matter to the other umpire and \\ntogether they shall decide whether misconduct has occurred. If so, they shall \\ndetermine into which of the Levels the conduct falls as set out in 42.2 to 42.5 below, \\nand then apply the related sanctions. \\n41.1.4 \\nFor each Level 1 to 4, if the offence is by a batsman the umpires shall \\nsummon the offending player\'s captain to the field. Solely for the purpose of this law \\nthe batsmen at the wicket may not deputise for their captain. \\n41.1.5 \\nFor each Level 1 to 4 \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Playing time shall be counted as lost from the call of Time to the call of Play, \\nexcluding intervals (Law 11) and suspensions of play (Law 2.8). \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the time for close of play on that day shall be extended by this length of time. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 If applicable, no overs shall be deducted during the last hour of the match \\nsolely on account of this time. \\n \\n42.2 Level 1 offences and action by umpires \\n42.2.1 \\nAny of the following action by a player shall constitute a level 1 offence: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 wilfully mistreating any part of the cricket ground, equipment or implements \\nused in the match  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 showing dissent at an umpire\'s decision by word or action \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 using language that in the circumstances, is obscene, offensive or insulting \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 making an obscene gesture \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 appealing excessively \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 advancing towards an umpire in an aggressive manner when appealing \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 any other misconduct, the nature of which is, in the opinion of the umpires, \\nequivalent to a Level 1 offence. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"42.2.2 \\nIt such an offence is committed, 42.2.2.1 to 42.2.2.6 shall be implemented as \\nappropriate, according to whether or not it is the first offence at any Level. \\n42.2.2.1 \\nThe umpire shall call Time, if necessary.  \\n42.2.2.2 \\nTogether the umpires shall summon and Inform the offending \\nplayer\'s captain that an offence at this Level has occurred. \\n42.2.2.3 \\nIf the Level 1 offence is the first offence, at any Level, by that \\nteam, the umpire shall \\n42.2.2.3.1 issue a first and final warning which shall apply to \\nall members of the team for the remainder of the \\nmatch. \\n42.2.2.3.2 warn the offending player\'s captain that any \\nfurther Level 1 offence by any member of his/ her \\nteam shall result in the award of 5 Penalty runs to \\nthe opposing team. \\n42.2.2.4 \\nIf the Level 1 offence follows an offence, at any Level, by that \\nteam, the umpire shall award 5 Penalty runs to the opposing team. \\n42.2.2.5 \\nAs soon as practicable the umpire shall call Play. \\n42.2.2.6 \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as \\npossible after the match to the Executive of the offending player\'s team \\nand to any Governing Body responsible for the match, who shall take such \\nfurther action as is considered appropriate against the captain, any other \\nindividuals concerned and, if appropriate, the team. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"42.3 Level 2 offences and action by umpires \\n42.3.1 \\nAny of the following actions by a player shall constitute a Level 2 offence: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 showing serious dissent at an umpire\'s decision by word or action \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 making inappropriate and deliberate physical contact with another player \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 throwing the ball at a player, umpire or another person in an inappropriate \\nand dangerous manner \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 using language or gesture to another player, umpire, team official or \\nspectator that, in the circumstances, is obscene or of a seriously insulting \\nnature  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 or any other misconduct, the nature of which is in the opinion of the umpires, \\nequivalent to a Level 2 offence  \\n42.3.2 \\nIf such an offence is committed, 42.3.2.1 to 42.3.2.6 shall be implemented. \\n42.3.2.1 \\nThe umpire shall call Time, if necessary. \\n42.3.2.2 \\nTogether the umpires shall summon and inform the offending player\'s \\ncaptain that an offence at this Level has occurred. \\n42.3.2.3 \\nThe umpire shall award 5 Penalty runs to the opposing team. \\n42.3.2.4 \\nThe umpire shall warn the offending player\'s captain that any future \\nLevel offence by any member of his/her team shall result in the award of 5 \\nPenalty runs to the opposing team. \\n42.3.2.5 \\nAs soon as practicable the Umpire shall call Play. \\n42.3.2.6 \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible \\nafter the match to the Executive of the offending player\'s team and to any \\nGoverning Body responsible for the match, who shall take such further action \\nas is considered appropriate against the captain, any other individuals \\nconcerned and, if appropriate, the team. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"42.4 Level 3 offences and action by umpires  \\n42.4.1 \\nEither of the following actions by a player shall constitute a Level 3 offence: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 intimidating an umpire by language or gesture  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 threatening to assault a player or any other person except an umpire. See \\n42.5.1. \\n42.4.2 \\nIf such an offence is committed, 42.4.2.1 to 42.4.2.8 shall be implemented. \\n42.4.2.1 \\nThe umpire shall call Time, if necessary  \\n42.4.2.2 \\nTogether the umpires shall summon and inform the offending player\'s \\ncaptain that an offence at this Level has occurred.  \\n \\n42.4.2.3 \\nThe umpires shall direct the captain to remove the offending player \\nimmediately from the field of play for a period in accordance with the \\nfollowing: \\n42.4.2.3.1 In a match where the innings are not limited to a number of \\novers, the player shall be suspended from the field of play for 10 \\novers. Any balls remaining in the over in progress at the time of \\nsuspension shall not count towards the overs for which the player is \\nsuspended. \\n42.4.2.3.2 In a match where the innings are limited to a number of overs, \\nthe player shall be suspended for one fifth of the number of overs \\nallocated to the current innings at its commencement. If. in \\ncalculating the length of the suspension, a part over results, it shall be \\nconsidered as a whole over. Any balls remaining in the over in \\nprogress at the time of suspension shall not count towards the overs \\nfor which the player is suspended. \\n42.4.2.3.3 If the offending player is a fielder, no substitute shall be \\nallowed for him/her. The offending player may return to the field of \\nplay after serving the period of suspension and may bowl \\nimmediately. \\n42.4.2.3.4 If a bowler is suspended mid over, then that over must be \\ncompleted by a different bowler who shall not have bowled the \\nprevious over nor shall he/she bowl the next over. \\n42.4.2.3.5 If the offending player is a not out batsman, he/she shall be \\nreplaced by another member of his/her team. The offending player \\nmay return to bat after having served the suspension only at the fall \\nof a wicket. If no batsman is available to bat during a batsman\'s \\nsuspension, the innings is completed. If the offending player does not \\ncontinue his/her innings for whatever reason, he/she is to be \\nrecorded as Retired - not out. \\n"'b'b"42.4.2.3.6 If the offending player is a dismissed member of the batting \\nside, the period of suspension will not commence until the start of the \\nnext innings. Furthermore, in these circumstances, the offending \\nplayer may not act as a runner during the innings when he/she was \\nsuspended. \\n42.4.2.3.7 Warn the offending player\'s captain that any future Level 1 \\noffence shall result in the award of 5 Penalty runs to the opposing \\nteam. \\n42.4.2.3.8 Any overs remaining to be served from a suspension shall be \\ncarried forward to the next and subsequent innings of the match A \\npart over at the end of the innings shall not count towards the overs \\nfor which the player is suspended. \\n42.4.2.4 \\nAs soon as practicable, the umpire shall  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the opposing team \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 signal the Level 3 penalty to the scorers  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 call Play \\n42.4.2.5 \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible \\nafter the match to the Executive of the offending player\'s team and to any \\nGoverning Body responsible for the match who shall take such further action \\nas is considered appropriate against the captain any other individuals \\nconcerned and, if appropriate, the team. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"42.5 Level 4 offences and action by umpires \\n42.5.1 \\nAny of the following actions by a player shall constitute a Level 4 offence: \\nthreatening to assault an umpire \\nmaking inappropriate and deliberate physical contact with an umpire \\nphysically assaulting a player or any other person \\ncommitting any other act of violence. \\n42.5.2 \\nif such an offence is committed. 42.5.2.1 to 42.5.2.5 shall be implemented. \\n42.5.2.1 \\nThe umpire shall call Time, if necessary \\n42.5.2.2 \\nTogether the umpires shall summon and inform the offending player\'s \\ncaptain that an offence at this Level has occurred. \\n42.5.2.3 \\nThe umpires shall direct the captain to remove the offending player \\nimmediately from the field of play for the remainder of the match and shall \\napply the following: \\n42.5.2.3.1 If the offending player is a fielder, no substitute shall be \\nallowed for him/her. He/she is to be recorded as Retired - out at the \\ncommencement of any subsequent innings in which his/ her team is \\nthe batting side.  \\n42.5.2.3.2 If a bowler is suspended mid over, then that over must be \\ncompleted by a different bowler who shall not have bowled the \\nprevious over nor shall be permitted to bowl the next over. \\n42.5.2.3.3 If the offending player is a batsman he/she is to be recorded as \\nRetired - out in the current innings, unless he/she has been dismissed \\nunder any of Laws 32 to 39, and at the commencement of any \\nsubsequent innings in which his/her team is the batting side. If no \\nfurther batsman is available to bat, the innings is completed. \\n42.5.2.3.4 Warn the offending player\'s captain that any future Level \\noffence shall result in the award of 5 Penalty runs to the opposing \\nteam. \\n42.5.2.4 \\nAs soon as practicable. the umpire shall \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 award 5 Penalty runs to the opposing team \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 signal the Level 4 penalty to the scorers \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 call Play \\n42.5.2.5 \\nThe umpires together shall report the occurrence as soon as possible \\nafter the match to the Executive of the offending player\'s team and to any \\nGoverning Body responsible for the match, who shall take such further action \\nas is considered appropriate against the captain any other individuals \\nconcerned and it appropriate the team. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'42.6 Captain refusing to remove a player from the field  \\n42.6.1 \\nIf a captain refuses to carry out an instruction under 42.4.2.3 or \\n42.5.2.3, the umpires shall invoke Law 16.3 (Umpires awarding a match), \\n42.6.2 \\nIf both captains refuse to carry out instructions under 42.4.2.3 or \\n42.5.2.3 in respect of the same incident the umpires shall instruct the \\nplayers to leave the field. The match is not concluded as in Law 129 \\n(Conclusion of match) and there shall be no result under Law 16 (The \\nresult). \\n \\n42.7 Additional points relating to Level 3 and Level 4 offences \\n42.7.1 \\nIf a player, while acting as wicket-keeper, commits a Level 3 or Level 4 \\noffence, Law 24.1.2 (Substitute fielders) shall not apply, meaning that only a \\nnominated player may act as wicket-keeper, even if another fielder becomes \\ninjured or ill and is replaced by a substitute. \\n42.7.2 \\nA nominated player who has a substitute or has a runner will suffer a \\npenalty for any Level 3 or Level 4 offence committed by that substitute or by \\nthat runner  \\n42.7.2.1 \\nWhen the offence is committed by a substitute, the nominated \\nplayer and substitute will each suffer the penalty defined in 42.4.2.3 or \\nin 42.5.2.3 as appropriate. Only the substitute will be reported as in \\n42.4.2.5 or 42.5.2.5. \\n42.7.2.2 \\nWhen the offence is committed by a runner, the batsman who \\nhas the runner and the runner will each suffer the penalty defined in \\n42.4.2.3 or in 42.5.2.3 as appropriate. The penalty for a Level 4 offence \\n(42.5.2.3) will apply to the runner for the remainder of the match, but in \\nonly the current innings to the batsman for whom the runner acted. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"The Preamble to the Laws sets out the manner in which players, coaches, officials and, in \\njunior matches, parents and teachers should approach the game of cricket. The Spirit of \\nCricket is not always easy to define, and the Preamble does not seek to do so prescriptively. \\nHowever, it does place respect, honesty and fair play at the centre of the game. Responsible \\nfor ensuring this is the case, are the captains - it is for them to make sure their players act \\nwithin the Spirit, and the Laws, of Cricket. \\nHowever, there are times when the players do not meet the standards laid down by the \\nPreamble. Unfortunately, there is evidence in cricket, from juniors to internationals, that \\nbehaviour has taken a turn for the worse. With that in mind, and after much deliberation \\nand consultation across the world, this new Law was introduced in 2017 to allow umpires to \\npunish poor on-field behaviour with strong, on field sanctions. \\nIt is hoped that umpires will rarely, if ever to have to use these sanctions. As much as \\npossible, the tone of a match should be set by the umpires\' management of it, by their \\ninteractions with the captains before and during play, and by a quiet word when needed. \\nHowever, a strong umpire will know that there are times when, despite all of the above \\nbeing performed perfectly, players still cross the line. That is when Law 42 should be used. \\nUmpires must learn from referees in other sports where such punishments are common and \\nhave been for decades, even centuries - to be neither scared of using these sanctions, nor \\ndesperate to show off their power. \\nEvery sanction, from Level 1 to Level 4, comes with a responsibility to report the player\'s \\nbehaviour - these on-field punishments do not replace the old system, they merely add to it. \\n \\nStopping the game \\nWhen an umpire - and it can be either umpire - identifies some act to be in breach of Law \\n42, they should not rush to intervene. Make sure not to inconvenience the team who have \\nnot committed the offence. For example, consider an LBW appeal that is turned down. The \\nball strikes the pad, and while the bowler appeals for LBW the ball runs away toward the \\nboundary. The umpire says not out, at which point the bowler shows dissent. If the umpire \\nwere to immediately call the ball Dead, before it had reached the boundary, the batting side \\nwould be denied four leg byes. The umpires should therefore be decisive, but calm, and not \\nrush to stop the game. \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b\'The same, conversely, is true if a batsman were to verbally abuse the bowler whilst running. \\nThe batsman has committed a Level 2 offence, but the punishment for that is only 5 Penalty \\nruns. The umpires should therefore wait to see if the fielding side run the batsman out \\nbefore declaring the ball Dead. Then, after consultation between the two, the relevant \\nprocess and the appropriate sanction can be applied, without disadvantaging the fielding \\nside, who have done nothing wrong. \\nOnce any relevant action that may benefit the non-offending side is over, the umpire calls \\nDead ball and must consult his/her colleague. The level of offence under Law 42, if any, is \\ndetermined by the umpires together. If only one umpire has seen or heard the offence, \\nconsultation may just be for that umpire to inform his/her colleague, rather than to \\ndetermine what has happened - the umpires will give a joint decision based on the view of \\nthe only umpire who saw the incident, having agreed on the level of the offence. Often, \\nhowever, both umpires will have slightly different views of the same incident, and can help \\neach other decide if an offence has occurred, and if so, at which level. \\n \\nDeciding the level of offence \\nThere are lots of offences covered specifically in each level of the Law, most of which are \\nperfectly obvious. For example, wilfully mistreating any part of the cricket ground, \\nequipment or implements" is not hard to classify. A batsman who smashes the gate on \\nhis/her way off the ground is clearly guilty of a Level 1 offence. \\nOthers though, are more complicated. When does dissent (Level 1) become serious dissent \\n(Level 2)? Is a batsman smashing over the wicket after being given out LBW wilful \\nmistreatment of equipment (Level 1) or serious dissent (Level 2)? Is the bowler showing \\nserious dissent (Level 2) or attempting to intimidate an umpire (Level 3)? This is for the \\numpires, together, to decide. There is, often, no definitively right or wrong answer - and it \\nwill always be helpful for the two umpires to talk frankly about their different views before \\ndeciding and then presenting a united front to both captains.  \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n\''b'b"Often, the decision will have an element of subjectivity For example, 42.2.1 describes \\nlanguage that \\xe2\\x80\\x98in the circumstances, is obscene, offensive or insulting\\xe2\\x80\\x99. This phrase is used \\nprecisely to tell the umpires to be sensitive to different cultures and attitudes, and to the \\nimpact of young players involved in the match. A comment or nickname may seem insulting \\nto a player or, if it is loud enough, a spectator not involved in the exchange. It is no excuse \\nto claim that the recipient of the call cares little about it. As circumstances change so must \\nan umpire\'s appreciation of insults. This means umpires need to be both aware of the \\ncontext, and flexible with their application of the Law. \\nLevels 1 and 2 have an \\xe2\\x80\\x98any other equivalent misconduct\\xe2\\x80\\x99 section, which, again, is left to the \\njudgment of the umpires. Just because an offence is not mentioned specifically in the Law, it \\ndoes not mean a player cannot be sanctioned for it under Level 1 or 2 of Law 42. \\nFurther guidance on differentiating between the offences in Level 42 can be found in the \\ntable at the end of this Law. \\nThe umpires together are required to judge the level of offence. They may disagree at first, \\nbut must come to a conclusion that satisfies both. When they determine that an offence has \\ntaken place, and its level, that is to be taken as their joint decision, whether or not one \\nprivately might have preferred a different outcome. \\nThe more serious offences in Level 3 and 4 which involve intimidation, threatening to \\nassault or, hopefully extremely rarely, actual assault should be easier to decide and by their \\nnature should be dealt with promptly and appropriately. \\n \\nDouble punishments \\nJust because a team has been punished for an offence in some other way under the Laws, it \\ndoes not mean they are protected from being punished again, from the same delivery under \\nLaw 42. This is most obviously the case for a batsman who has been given out LBW showing \\ndissent.
