He/she can still be punished under Law 42, despite already having been dismissed. \\nThe same is true for other offences. A batsman who deliberately collides with a fielder may \\nwell be out Obstructing the field. That does not protect the same batsman from a Level 2 \\noffence, for making inappropriate and deliberate physical contact with another player if the \\numpires consider the nature of the contact warrants it. The batsman should be given out \\nand his side also penalised Penalty runs. Similarly, if a fielder deliberately and aggressively \\nobstructs a batsman in running, the umpires would apply Law 41.5 to protect the batting \\nside and Law 42 to reprimand the fielder. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'Calling Time \\nThis is the only point in the game when the Laws require Time is to be called despite there \\nbeing no interval or Interruption - that is to differentiate it from other offences, which while \\nunfair are within the game. The offences under Law 42 are non-cricketing offences, and are \\nthus dealt with outside the usual game. Any time lost will be made up, as under Law 163, by \\nadding the lost Playing time on to the time that play is due to finish that day. \\nIt should be stressed, however, that if a Law 42 offence takes place shortly before an \\ninterval, and the time taken to deal with it runs into that interval (or interruption), then it is \\nnot the whole time between the calls of Time and Play that should be added. Only the \\nPlaying time which is lost (i.e. not time otherwise scheduled for an interval, nor time that \\nwould have been lost to another interruption) should be added on. \\n \\nSpeaking to the captains \\n \\nIn line with the responsibility of captains, it is the captain. not the offending player, who is \\nwarned by the umpire under Level 1 and 2. It is the captain, rather than the umpires, who \\nare to take any action against the player involved in a Level 3 or 4 offence - this is the same \\nprinciple as a captain removing a bowler from the attack for dangerous bowling (see page \\n363 in Law 41.8 explanation) and needs no further explanation. \\nHowever, if the sanction is against a batsman, his/her captain may well not be on the field. \\nThat means they need to be summoned, and the situation explained. \\nThis act alone should make the seriousness of the offence clear-it is the only time that the \\nbatsmen at the crease may not deputise for their captain. A huge fuss need not be made - \\nthe captain needs to know what has happened, what the sanction will be, and that it will \\nresult in the award of Penalty runs for any further offences - at any level - by the same team. \\nHe/she does not need to make their case the decision is made, he/she is merely being \\ninformed. The captain must then take any necessary action against his/her own player, \\nbefore returning to the pavilion. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"If the offending player happens to be the captain, whether of the fielding or batting side, \\nthen another nominated player will need to be summoned and informed of the breach and \\nimplications for future offences. If the captain has committed a Level 3 or 4 offence then the \\nother nominated player will have the responsibility to remove the captain from the field, \\nand will take over the responsibilities of the captaincy whilst the captain is suspended. \\nIf- and this is, obviously, a last resort - a captain refuses to carry out their duty to remove a \\nplayer, then umpires should, under 42.6, ask the captain if that means he/she and the team \\nare refusing to play. If the answer is no and the player leaves the field then hopefully no \\nmore needs to be said (although a report will still need to be made after the game). But if \\nthe offending player\'s captain persists in not removing the player then the match must be \\nawarded to the other side. \\nIn an extreme situation where players from both sides are involved in a Level 3 or 4 incident \\nat the same time and neither captain is prepared to remove his/her players from the field \\nthen, if both captains demonstrate a refusal to play, 42.6.2 is clear the umpires must stop \\nthe match with no result recorded. The umpires should obviously warn the captains of this \\npossibility before making such a decision. Furthermore, both captains and all the players \\ninvolved must be reported. \\n \\nFurther offences \\nThis is relatively self-explanatory, but the key thing to remember is that, after any offence \\nby a team, whatever the level, any further Level 1 offence shall be punished with Penalty \\nruns. Umpires are required to inform the captain of this. There is no mechanism for repeat \\noffences at any Level escalating to the next level. The team is at risk, however, of even the \\nsmallest offence costing them runs. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Calculating time off the field \\nln the case of a Level 3 offence, the umpires will need to know for how long the offending \\nplayer must be off the field, and will inform the captain of the offending team. \\nIn a timed game this is always 10 overs, In a limited overs name it is one fifth of the length \\nof the innings as determined at the start of the innings in question. \\nThis takes some explaining. If a game is scheduled to be 50 overs per side, and there have \\nbeen no interruptions before play starts, a player who is suspended in the first Innings will \\nbe off for 10 overs. If it then rains, and the game is shortened to say 20 overs per side, that \\nplayer, who is already off the field, does not see a change in his/ her penalty. Further, if \\nanother player is suspended during the first innings, they too will have to spend 10 overs off \\nthe field. It is the length of the innings when the innings started, not when the suspension \\nstarted that matters. However, in that same game, a player who is suspended in the second \\nInnings - which was, from its start, going to be 20 overs, would only be off the field for four \\novers. \\nSome players may complain this is unfair- the only sensible response to this is to remind \\nthem that any player who has committed an offence worthy of a Level 3 suspension is in no \\nposition to complain. \\nIf dividing the overs in the innings by 5 results in a part over the number is always rounded \\nup. Similarly, whatever the number of overs, the over in progress does not count. So, a \\nplayer suspended for 10 overs after two balls of an over, will actually serve 10.4 overs - \\nperhaps more if the suspension straddles the end of an innings, which also ends part-way \\nthrough an over - that part over would also not count. \\n \\nBowler suspensions \\nIf a bowler is suspended mid-over under Level 3 or 4 then another player must complete \\nthat over. The new bowler may not have bowled any part of the previous over, nor may \\nhe/she bowl any part of the next over. \\nIn limited overs cricket, all these part overs will count as a full over out of the bowler\'s \\npermitted bowling allowance (but not the suspension). \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"Batsmen suspensions \\nLevel 3 \\nWhen a not out batsman is suspended for a Level 3 offence then he/she can only resume an \\ninterrupted innings when all the following apply: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the overs for the suspension have expired \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 another wicket falls (but not upon a retirement) \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 there are still wickets to fall when the suspension is complete. \\nSuspended batsman not out \\nThe consequences for the suspended batsman vary depending on whether the suspended \\nbatsman was not out or had been dismissed. \\nIf, for example, in the first innings of a match, the number 6 batsman is suspended for 10 \\novers when 7 wickets are down and then the 8th and 9th wickets fall after a further 8 overs \\nhave been bowled. The suspended batsman will not be able to resume his/her innings. The \\ninnings is closed at however many runs have been scored for 9 wickets and the suspended \\nplayer is recorded as \'Retired - Not out. He/ she will then have to wait for the first two overs \\nof the next innings before being able to take the field. Had the 10 overs of the suspension \\nexpired before the 9th wicket fell, then the number 6 batsman would be able to resume \\nhis/her innings. \\nSuspended batsman dismissed \\nA batsman who has already been dismissed might commit a Level 3 offence. In this case \\ntheir suspension does not begin until the start of the next innings. \\nIf, for example, the same number 6 batsman as in the previous example, is given out LBW, \\nand in reaction to that decision commits a Level 3 offence, his/her suspension does not \\nbegin until his/her team are fielding (or, in a multi-day match, following on if required). \\nLevel 4 \\nIf the batsman has committed a Level 4 offence, irrespective of whether they are out or not, \\nand irrespective of when the innings is completed, he/she would not be allowed to retake \\nthe field in the match and would be recorded as \'Retired- Out (unless he/she has been \\ndismissed during that incident). \\nIn a two-innings match, if a player is suspended for a Level 4 offence during the first batting \\nor fielding innings then the second batting innings for that team will start on 0 runs for I \\nwicket with the offending player recorded Retired-Out for that innings \\n \\n"'b"b'Substitutes and Runners and Level 3 or 4 \\nWhilst 42.4.2.3.3 makes it clear that a player who is suspended for a Level 3 offence whilst \\nfielding is not allowed a substitute, it should also be noted that under 42.7.1 if a wicket \\nkeeper is suspended then only another nominated player is allowed to keep wicket and no \\nsubstitute is allowed to keep wicket for the duration of the suspension. \\nA player with a Level 4 penalty or a Level 3 penalty with suspension time still to serve cannot \\nact as a runner. \\nOn the other hand, if someone acting as a substitute is suspended for a Level 3 or 4 offence, \\nthen the player for whom they are substituting will suffer the same penalty, but only the \\nperson committing the offence will be reported. The substitute is the representative of the \\nplayer they are on for, and for the purposes of this Law they are treated as one. Both the \\nsubstitute and the player for whom they are substituting will be suspended. \\nHowever, it is slightly different for a runner. Because a runner is, by definition, another \\nnominated player, an offence by a runner would affect two players for an offence by one. \\nThus, if a runner commits a Level 4 offence, whilst the player acting as the runner will be \\nsuspended for the rest of the match the player for whom they are running will only be \\nsuspended for the rest of that innings. Again, it is only the runner him/herself who will be \\nreported. \\n \\nBefore Calling Play \\nAfter all of the above - calling Time, discussing the offence, calculating the suspension, \\ninforming the captains and, of course, signalling to the scorers both the Penalty runs and \\nlevel of offence if appropriate - the umpires should make a clear note of what they have \\ndone. Records made at the time are crucial for the preparation of the post-match report and \\nespecially if there is an appeal by the player and/or if any further action is to be taken. It \\nneed not be lengthy, but it should be accurate and clear. \\nThen, the umpires must calculate the time lost, and add any Playing time lost to the time for \\nclose of play. If in the last hour, no overs should be lost because of this interruption. The \\numpire should then call Play. \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"Level 1 \\nLevel 2 \\nLevel 3 \\nLevel 4 \\n1.1 Showing dissent at \\numpire\'s decision by \\nword or action \\n2.1 Showing serious dissent \\nat umpire\'s decision by word \\nor action \\n \\n \\n1.2 Appealing \\nexcessively \\n \\n \\n \\n1.3 Advancing towards \\nan umpire in an \\naggressive manner \\nwhen appealing \\n2.2 Throwing the ball at an \\numpire [or others-see \\nbelow] in an inappropriate \\nand dangerous manner \\n3.1 Intimidating an \\numpire by \\nlanguage or \\ngesture \\n41 Threatening to assault \\nan umpire. \\nor  \\n4.2 Making \\ninappropriate and \\ndeliberate physical \\ncontact with an umpire \\n1.4 Using language that. \\nin the circumstances, is \\nobscene, offensive or \\ninsulting, or  \\n1.5 Making an obscene \\ngesture \\n2.3 Using language or \\ngesture to \\nan umpire, player, official, or \\nspectator, that, in the \\ncircumstances, is obscene or \\nof a seriously insulting \\nnature \\n \\n \\n1.6 Wilfully mistreating \\nany part of the cricket \\nground, equipment, or \\nimplements used in the \\nmatch \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n2.4 Making \\ninappropriate and deliberate \\nphysical contact with \\nanother \\nplayer [NB with an umpire is \\nLevel 4] \\n3.2 Threatening to \\nassault a player or any \\nother person except an \\numpire [NB \\nthreatening to \\nassault an umpire is \\nLevel 4] \\n4.3 Physically assaulting a \\nplayer or any \\nother person [which Includes \\numpires who \\nwould already be covered by \\nphysical contact - see above \\nalthough this more serious] \\n \\n2.5 Throwing the ball at a \\nplayer [for umpire. See \\nabove], or another person in \\nan inappropriate and \\ndangerous manner \\n \\n4.4 Committing any other act of \\nviolence [could include umpires \\nsee also above] \\n1.7 Any other \\nmisconduct, the nature \\nof which is in the \\nopinion of the umpires, \\nequivalent to a Level 1 \\noffence \\n2.6 Any other misconduct \\nthe nature of which is, in the \\nopinion of the umpires \\nequivalent to a Level 2 \\noffence \\n \\n \\nNote: Offences are grouped by type to show the progression of seriousness across the Levels and the difference between levels when an \\numpire is involved. \\n"'b'b\'Meaning of Language: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\n"Dissent": expressing disagreement with an umpire\\\'s decision. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\n"Aggressive": behaving angrily, likely to confront or attack. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\n"Obscene": indecent or offensive. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\n"Inappropriate": unsuitable or unacceptable. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\n"Intimidation": to frighten, especially in order to force someone to do something  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\nAssault": to attack violently; to attempt to injure another person. \\nDissent (1.1) vs Serious dissent (1.2):  \\nWhether dissent\\\' falls under Level 1 or Level 2 is a matter of degree and depends on the strength, force and manner of the dissent. For \\nexample from a batsman in response to a LBW decision clearly and audibly - \\\'Ump you need the optician, said laughingly might be allowed, \\nbut "You must be joking\\\' said vehemently would be a Level 1 and You need to see a b**** optician, I hit the cover off that one\\\' said \\naggressively\\\' probably a Level 2. \\nLanguage and gestures (1.4 vs 2.3)  \\nThe difference between Level 1 and Level 2 is two-fold: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\nAt Level 2, the language or gesture is specifically aimed at an umpire, team official, player, or spectator \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\nAt Level 2, it is obscene (as it is at Level 1) or of a seriously insulting nature-again a matter of degree in which the umpire is \\nentitled to take into account the impact of what was said or gestured, on those to whom it was addressed. \\nSpecific protection of umpires  \\nNote carefully how the offences step up from one Level to another and how the protection of umpires is greater in terms of which Level of \\noffence is triggered, than for players and others. Thus: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\nAt Level 1 we begin with advancing towards the umpire aggressively or appealing excessively (e.g. particularly after the umpire \\nhas given his/her decision) \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\nLevel 2 moves up to throwing the ball at the umpire inappropriately and in a dangerous manner. There are only a few occasions \\nwhere it would be appropriate to throw the ball to the umpire so the test is easy and the offence is likely to turn on whether \\nthe throw was dangerous.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\nAt Level 3 the umpire is protected against being intimidated by language or gesture, with a view to attempting to influence the \\numpire\\\'s decision. The umpire should not feel frightened of doing his/her job properly. \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\nAt Level 4, the attack on the umpire is yet more serious and covers: inappropriate and deliberate physical contact (with no \\nrequirement for violence), as well as merely threatening an assault (a threat of violence) and actual assault (violence) and any \\nother acts of violence against umpires or any others. \\nProtection of players and other persons against ball throwing, physical contact, threats and assault/violence: \\nAgain these step up Level by Level but more slowly than for umpires.  \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 \\nLevel 2 protects against throwing the ball inappropriately and dangerously at others as well as against inappropriate and \\ndeliberate physical contact with another player (with no requirement for violence being involved). \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 \\nLevel 3 covers threatened assault (violence) of a player or another person (except an umpire because for umpires that\\\'s Level \\n4). \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 \\nLevel 4 covers physical assault (violence) and other acts of violence.  \\n\\xe2\\x80\\xa2 \\nIn relation to offences by players it is important to note two points: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\nEach Level of offence involves reporting to the sanctions imposed on match day are only the beginning--further \\nsanctions may be imposed on players in post-match disciplinary proceedings; \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 \\nSome offences arising under other Laws may also involve Offences under Law 42 e.g. deliberate obstruction by a \\nbatsman of a fielder or vice versa involving physical contact or violence and may trigger a Level 3 or 4 offence. \\n \\nNote that once the situation involves physical assault or other acts of violence, issues of overall player, umpire and spectator safety could \\nbe involved and may require more drastic actions than those prescribed by Law 42. \\n \\n \\n\''b"b' \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nPART 4 \\nThe Appendices  \\n \\n'"b'b"The Appendices are a vital part of the Laws of Cricket, although often overlooked. They \\nprovide clarity on certain aspects of the Laws, giving definitions and diagrams of key terms \\nand equipment. \\n \\nWhile rarely read in isolation, the Appendices should be used in conjunction with the Laws \\nand any serious student of the game - whether umpire, scorer, player or spectator - should \\nbe familiar with them. Where a question arises that the Laws do not address, the answer is \\noften to be found in these pages. \\n \\nAppendix A provides definitions of terms that are otherwise undefined in the Laws, while \\nAppendix B focuses on the bat and its specifications, and includes an diagram of the bat \\ngauge introduced in 2017. Appendix C illustrates Laws 6 and 7 giving clear dimensions for \\nthe Pitch and Crease markings, Appendix D does the same for Law 8 (The Wickets) and \\nAppendix E explains the legalities of the wicket keeper\'s gloves, as defined in Law 27.2. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'APPENDIX A \\nDefinitions and explanations of words or phrases not defined in the text \\nA. 1 The match \\nA.1.1 \\nThe game is used in these Laws as a general term meaning the Game of \\nCricket. \\nA.1.2 \\nA match is a single encounter (or contest) between two sides (or teams), \\nplayed under the Laws of Cricket. \\nA.1.3 \\nThe toss is the toss for choice of innings. \\nA.1.4 \\nBefore the toss is at any time before the toss on the day the match is \\nexpected to start or in the case of a one-day match, on the day the match is due to \\ntake place. \\nA.1.5 \\nBefore the match is at any time before the toss, not restricted to the day on \\nwhich the toss is to take place . \\nA.1.6 \\nDuring the match is at any time after the toss until the conclusion of the \\nmatch, whether play is in progress or not.  \\nA.1.7 \\nPlaying time is any time between the call of Play and the call of Time See \\nlaws 12.1 (Call of Play) and 12.2 (Call of Time). \\nA.1.8 \\nConduct of the match includes any action relevant to the match at any time \\non any day of the match. \\n \\nA.2 Implements and equipment \\nA.2.1 \\nImplements used in the match are the bat, the ball, the stumps and bails. \\nA.2.2 \\nExternal protective equipment is any viable item of apparel worn for \\nprotection against external blows. \\nFor a batsman, items permitted area protective helmet external leg guards (batting \\npads), batting gloves and, if visible, forearm guards For a fielder, only a protective \\nhelmet is permitted, except in the case of a wicket keeper for whom wicket-keeping \\npads and gloves are also permitted. \\nA.2.3 \\nA protective helmet is her made of hard material and designed to protect the \\nhead or the face or both. For the purposes of interpreting these Laws of Cricket, such \\na description will include faceguards. \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b'b"A.2.4 \\nEquipment -a batsman\'s equipment is his/her bat as defined above, together \\nwith any external protective equipment he/she is wearing. A fielder\'s equipment is \\nany external protective equipment that he/she is wearing. \\nA.2.5 \\nThe bat- the following are to be considered as part of the bat: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the whole of the bat itself.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the whole of a glove (or gloves) worn on the hand (or hands) holding the bat.  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 the hand (or hands) holding the bat, if the batsman is not wearing a glove on \\nthat hand or on those hands \\nA.2.6 \\nHeld in batsman\'s hand - Contact between a batsman\'s hand or glove worn \\non his/her hand, and any part of the bat shall constitute the bat being held in that \\nhand. \\n \\nA.3 The playing area \\nA.3.1 \\nThe field of play is the area contained within the boundary \\nA.3.2 \\nThe square is a specially prepared area of the field of play within which the \\nmatch pitch is situated. \\nA.3.3 \\nThe outfield is that part of the field of play between the square and the \\nboundary. \\n \\nA.4 Positioning \\nA.4.1 \\nBehind the popping crease at one end of the pitch is that area of the field of \\nplay, including any other marking objects and persons therein, that is on that side of \\nthe popping crease that does not include the creases at the opposite end of the \\npitch. \\nBehind, in relation to any other marking object or person, follows the same \\nprinciple. See the diagram in A.13. \\nA.4.2 \\nIn front of the popping crease at one end of the pitch is that area of the field \\nof play, including any other marking, objects and persons therein, that is on that side \\nof the popping crease that includes the creases at the opposite end of the pitch. \\nIn front of, in relation to any other marking, object or person, follows the same \\nprinciple. See the diagram in A.13. \\nA.4.3 \\nThe striker\'s end is the place where the striker stands to receive a delivery \\nfrom the bowler only insofar as it identifies, independently of where the striker may \\nsubsequently move, one end of the pitch. \\n \\n"'b'b"A.4.4 \\nThe bowler\'s end is the end from which the bowler delivers the ball. It is the \\nother end of the pitch from the striker\'s end and identifies that end of the pitch that \\nis not the striker\'s end as described in A.4.3. \\nA.4.5 \\nThe wicket-keeper\'s end is the same as the striker\'s end as described in \\nA.4.3. \\nA.4.6 \\nIn front of the line of the striker\'s wicket is in the area of the field of play in \\nfront of the imaginary line joining the fronts of the stumps at the striker\'s end; this \\nline to be considered extended in both directions to the boundary. See A.4.2. \\nA.4.7 \\nBehind the wicket is in the area of the field of play behind the imaginary line \\njoining the backs of the stumps at the appropriate end; this line to be considered \\nextended in both directions to the boundary. See A.4.1. \\nA.4.8 \\nBehind the wicket-keeper is behind the wicket at the striker\'s end, as defined \\nabove, but in line with both sets of stumps and further from the stumps than the \\nwicket-keeper. \\nA.4.9 \\nOff side/on side - see diagram in A.13  \\nA.4.10 \\nInside edge is the edge on the same side as the nearer wicket. \\n \\nA.5 Umpires \\nA.5.1 \\nUmpire - where the description the umpire is used on its own, it always \\nmeans \'the bowler\'s end umpire\' though this full description is sometimes used for \\nemphasis or clarity. Similarly the umpires always means both umpires. An umpire \\nand umpires are generalised terms. Otherwise, a fuller description indicates which \\none of the umpires is specifically intended. Each umpire will be bowler\'s end umpire \\nand striker\'s end umpire in alternate overs. \\nA.5.2 \\nBowler\'s end umpire is the umpire who is standing at the bowler\'s end (see \\nA.4.4) for the current delivery. \\nA.5.3 \\nStriker\'s end umpire is the umpire who is standing at the striker\'s end (see \\nA.4.3), to one side of the pitch or the other, depending on his/ her choice, for the \\ncurrent delivery. \\nA.5.4 \\nUmpires together agree applies to decisions which the umpires are to make \\njointly, independently of the players. \\n \\nA.6 Batsmen \\nA.6.1 \\nBatting side is the side currently batting, whether or not play is in progress.  \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"A.6.2 \\nMember of the batting side is one of the players nominated by the captain of \\nthe batting side, or any authorised replacement for such nominated player. \\nA.6.3 \\nA batsman\'s ground - at each end of the pitch, the whole area of the field of \\nplay behind the popping crease is the ground at that end for a batsman. \\nA.6.4 \\nOriginal end is the end where a batsman was when the ball came into play \\nfor that delivery. \\nA.6.5 \\nWicket he/she has left is the wicket at the end where a batsman was at the \\nstart of the run in progress . \\nA.6.6 \\nGuard position is the position and posture adopted by the striker to receive a \\nball delivered by the bowler. \\nA.6.7 \\nFor the purposes of these Laws, waist height is defined as the point at which \\nthe top of the batsman\'s trousers would conventionally be when he/she is standing \\nupright at the popping crease. \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"A.7 Fielders \\nA.7.1 \\nFielding side is the side currently fielding whether or not play is in progress. \\nA.7.2 \\nMember of the fielding side is one of the players nominated by the captain \\nof the fielding side, or any authorised replacement or substitute for such nominated \\nplayer. \\nA.7.3 \\nFielder is one of the 11 or fewer players who together represent the fielding \\nside on the field of play. This definition includes not only both the bowler and the \\nwicket-keeper but also nominated players who are legitimately on the field of play, \\ntogether with players legitimately acting as substitutes for absent nominated \\nplayers. It excludes any nominated player who is absent from the field of play, or \\nwho has been absent from the field of play and who has not yet obtained the \\numpire\'s permission to return. A player going briefly outside the boundary in the \\ncourse of discharging his/her duties as a fielder is not absent from the field of play \\nnor, for the purposes of Law 24.2 (Fielder absent or leaving the field of play), is \\nhe/she to be regarded as having left the field of play. \\n \\nA.8 Substitutes, Replacements and Runners \\nA.8.1 \\nA Substitute is a player who takes the place of a fielder on the field of play, \\nbut does not replace the player for whom he/she substitutes on that side\'s list of \\nnominated players. A substitute\'s activities are limited to fielding. \\nA.8.2 \\nA Replacement is a player who takes the place of a nominated player and \\nwho, thereby becomes a nominated player. A replacement\'s activities on the field of \\nplay are no more limited than those of any other nominated player. \\nA.8.3 \\nA Runner is a nominated player who runs for another nominated player of \\nhis/her own side who is batting and is unable to run. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"A.9 Bowlers \\nA.9.1 \\nOver the wicket / round the wicket - If, as the bowler runs up between the \\nwicket and the return crease, the wicket is on the same side as his/her bowling arm, \\nhe/she is bowling over the wicket. If the return crease is on the same side as his/her \\nbowling arm, he/she is bowling round the wicket. \\nA.9.2 \\nDelivery swing is the motion of the bowler\'s arm during which he/she \\nnormally releases the ball for a delivery. \\nA.9.3 \\nDelivery stride is the stride during which the delivery swing is made, whether \\nthe ball is released or not. It starts when the bowler\'s back foot lands for that stride \\nand ends when the front foot lands in the same stride. The stride after the delivery \\nstride is completed when the next foot lands, i.e. when the back foot of the delivery \\nstride lands again. \\n \\nA.10 The ball \\nA.10.1 \\nThe ball is struck/strikes the ball unless specifically defined otherwise, mean \\nthe balls struck by the bat\'/\'strikes the ball with the bat. \\nA.10.2 \\nRebounds directly/strikes directly and similar phrases mean without contact \\nwith any fielder but do not exclude contact with the ground. \\nA.10.3 \\nFull-pitch describes a ball delivered by the bowler that reaches or passes the \\nstriker without having touched the ground. Sometimes described as non-pitching. \\n \\nA.11 Runs \\nA.11.1 \\nA run to be disallowed is one that in law should not have been taken. It is not \\nonly to be cancelled but the batsmen are to be returned to their original ends. \\nA.11.2 \\nA run not to be scored is one that is not illegal but is not recognised as a \\nproperly executed run. It is not a run that has been made, so the question of \\ncancellation does not arise. The loss of the run so attempted is not a disallowance \\nand the batsmen will not be returned to their original ends on that account. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n"'b'b"A.12 The person \\nA.12.1 \\nPerson; A player\'s person is his/her physical person (flesh and blood) \\ntogether with any clothing or legitimate external protective equipment that he/she \\nis wearing except in the case of a batsman, his/her bat. \\nA hand, whether gloved or not, that is not holding the bat is part of the batsman\'s \\nperson No item of clothing or equipment is part of the player\'s person unless it is \\nattached to him/her. For a batsman, a glove being held but not worn is part of \\nhis/her person. \\nFor a fielder, an item of clothing or equipment he/she is holding in his/her hand or \\nhands is not part of his/her person. \\nA.12.2 \\nClothing - anything that a player is wearing including such items as spectacles \\nor jewellery that is not classed as external protective equipment is classed as \\nclothing, even though he/she may be wearing some items of apparel which are not \\nvisible for protection. A bat being carried by a batsman does not come within this \\ndefinition of clothing. \\nA.12.3 \\nHand for batsman or wicket-keeper shall include both the hand itself and the \\nwhole of a glove worn on the hand. \\n \\n \\n"'b"b'A.13 Off side/on side: in front of behind the popping crease \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'APPENDIX B \\nThe bat (Law 5) \\nB.1 General guidance \\nB.1.1 \\nMeasurements - All provisions in sections B.2 to R.6 below are subject to the \\nmeasurements and restrictions stated in the Law and this Appendix. \\nB.1.2 \\nAdhesives - Throughout, adhesives are permitted only where essential and \\nonly in minimal quantity \\nB.1.3 \\nCategories of bat - The specifications given below relate to Type A, B, C and D \\nbats unless specified otherwise. \\nB.1.4 \\n \\nB.2 Specifications for the Handle \\nB.2.1 \\nOne end of the handle is inserted into a recess in the blade as a means of \\njoining the handle and the blade. \\nThis lower portion is used purely for joining the blade and the handle together It is \\nnot part of the blade but, solely in Interpreting B.3 and B.4 below, references to the \\nblade shall be considered to extend also to this lower portion of the handle where \\nrelevant. \\nB.2.2 \\nThe handle may be glued where necessary and bound with twine along the \\nupper portion Providing Law 5.5 is not contravened, the upper portion may be \\ncovered with materials solely to provide a surface suitable for gripping Such covering \\nis an addition and is not part of the bat, except in relation to Law 5.6. The bottom of \\nthis group should not extend below the point defined in B.2.4 below. \\nTwine binding and the covering grip may extend beyond the junction of the upper \\nand lower portions of the hand, to cover part of the shoulders of the bat as defined \\nin B3.1 No material may be placed on or inverted into the lower portion of the \\nhandle other than as permitted above together with the minimal adhesives or \\nadhesive tape used solely for fixing these items, or for fixing the handle to the blade.  \\nB.2.3 \\nMaterials in handle - As a proportion of the total volume of the handle, \\nmaterials other than cane, wood or twine are restricted to one-tenth for Types A and \\nB and one-fifth for Type C and Type D. Such materials must not project more than \\n3.25 in/8.26 cm into the lower portion of the handle. \\nB.2.4 \\nBinding and covering of handle - The permitted continuation beyond the \\njunction of the upper and lower portions of the handle is restricted to a maximum, \\nmeasured along the length of the handle, of: \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 2.5 in/6.35 cm for the twine binding  \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 2.75 in/6.99 cm for the covering grip. \\n \\n'"b"b'B.3 Specifications for the Blade \\nB.3.1 \\nThe blade has a face, a back, a toe, sides and shoulders \\nB.3.1.1 \\nThe face of the blade is its main striking surface and shall be flat or \\nhave a slight convex curve resulting from traditional pressing techniques. The \\nback is the opposite surface. \\nB.3.1.2 \\nThe shoulders, sides and toe are the remaining surfaces, separating \\nthe face and the back. \\nB.3.1.3 \\nThe shoulders, one on each side of the handle, are along that portion \\nof the blade between the first entry point of the handle and the point at \\nwhich the blade first reaches its full width. \\nB.3.1.4 \\nThe toe is the surface opposite to the shoulders taken as a pair. \\nB.3.1.5 \\nThe sides, one each side of the blade, are along the rest of the blade, \\nbetween the toe and the shoulders. \\nB.3.2 \\nNo material may be placed on or inserted into the blade other than as \\npermitted in B.2.4, B.3.3. and Law 5.4 together with the minimal adhesives or \\nadhesive tape used solely for fixing these items, or for fixing the handle to the blade.  \\nB.3.3 \\nCovering the blade Type A and Type B bats shall have no covering on the \\nblade except as permitted in Law 5.4. Type C and Type D bats may have a cloth \\ncovering on the blade. This may be treated as specified in B.4 below. The cloth \\ncovering permitted for Type C and D bats shall be of thickness not exceeding 0.012 in \\n/0.3 mm before treatment as in B.4.1. Any materials referred to above, in Law 5.4 \\nand B.4 below, are to be considered as part of the bat, which must still pass through \\nthe gauge as defined in B.8. \\n \\nB.4 Protection and repair \\nB.4.1 \\nThe surface of the blade may be treated with non-solid materials to improve \\nresistance to moisture penetration and/or mask natural blemishes in the \\nappearance of the wood. Save for the purpose of giving a homogeneous \\nappearance by masking natural blemishes, such treatment shall not materially \\nalter the colour of the blade. \\nB.4.2 \\nMaterials can be used for protection and repair as stated in Law 5.4 and are \\nadditional to the blade. \\nNote however Law 5.6. \\nAny such material shall not extend over any part of the back of the blade except in \\nthe case of Law 5.4.1 and then only when it is applied as a continuous wrapping \\ncovering the damaged area. \\nThe repair material shall not extend along the length of the blade more than 0.79 \\nin 20 cm in each direction beyond the limits of the damaged area. Where used as a \\n'"b"b'continuous binding any overlapping shall not breach the maximum of 0.04 in/0.1 \\ncm in total thickness. \\nThe use of non-solid material which when dry forms a hard layer more than 0.004 \\nin/0.01 cm in thickness is not permitted. \\nAdditionally, for protection from damage, for Bat Types B, C and D, material may \\nbe inserted at the toe and/or along the sides, parallel to the face of the blade. \\nB.4.3 \\nPermitted coverings, repair material and toe guards, not exceeding their \\nspecified thicknesses, may be additional to the dimensions above, but the bat \\nmust still pass through the gauge as described in B.8. \\n \\nR.5 Toe and side inserts \\n  \\nThe wood used must not be more than 0.35 in/0.89 cm in thickness. \\n  \\nThe toe insert shall not extend from the toe more than 2.5 in/635 cm up the blade at \\nany point. \\n  \\nNeither side insert may extend from the edge more than 1 in/2.54 cm across the \\nblade at any point. \\n \\n8.6 Commercial identifications  \\n  \\nThese identifications may not exceed 0.008 in/0.02 cm in thickness. On the back of \\nthe blade they must occupy no more than 50% of the surface. On the face of the \\nblade, they must be confined within the top 9 in/22.86 cm, measured from where \\nthe bottom of the grip as defined in B.2.2 and B.2.4, would finish. \\n \\nB.7 Type D Bats \\n  \\nType D bats, as defined, shall comply with the size specifications and restrictions in \\nLaw 5 and this Appendix. The blade in addition may be  \\nB.7.1 laminated but using only wood and with no more than three pieces. \\nB.7.2 coloured, providing Law 5.5 is not contravened. \\n \\nB.8 Bat Gauge \\nAll bats that conform to the Laws of Cricket must meet the specifications defined in Law 5.7. \\nThey must also, with or without protective coverings permitted in Law 5.4, be able to pass \\nthrough a bat gauge, the dimensions and shape of which are shown in the diagram on the \\nfollowing page. \\n'"b"b' \\n \\n \\n \\n \\nBat Gauge Diagram (B.8) \\n \\nDimensions of aperture \\n \\nTotal depth: 2.68 in / 6.8 cm \\nWidth: 4.33 in / 11.0 cm  \\nEdge: 161 in / 41 cm  \\nCurve: 0.20 in / 0.5 cm \\n \\nNote: The curve of the lower edge of the aperture is an arc of a circle of radius 12.0 in / 30.5 \\ncm, whose centre is on the vertical centre line of the aperture \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'APPENDIX C \\nThe Pitch (Law 6) and The Creases (Law 7) \\n \\n \\n \\n'"b"b'APPENDIX D \\nThe wickets (Law 8) \\n \\nBails \\nFor Senior Cricket \\nOverall: 4.31 in / 10.95 cm \\n(a) 1.38 in / 3.50 cm \\n(b) 2.13 in / 5.40 cm \\n(c) 0.81 in / 2.06 cm \\n \\nStumps \\nFor Senior Cricket \\n(d) Height: 28 in / 71.1 cm \\n(e) Diameter: \\n                     max. 1.5 in / 3.81 cm \\n                     min. 1.38 in / 3.50 cm \\n \\nOverall width of wicket \\n(f) 9 in / 22.86 cm \\n \\n'"b"b'APPENDIX E \\nWicket-keeping gloves \\n \\nThe images show what is meant by: \\n \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 No webbing between the fingers \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 Single piece of non-stretch material \\nbetween finger and thumb as a means \\nof support \\n\\xef\\x82\\xa7 And, when a hand wearing the glove \\nhas the thumb fully extended, the top \\nedge being taut and not protruding \\nbeyond the straight line joining the top \\nof the index finger to the top of the \\nthumb. \\n \\n \\n \\n \\n'"
